### IN THE YEAR 1673.

<summary>This is the title page of Samuel von Pufendorf’s 1673 masterpiece, which provides a condensed guide to moral and political philosophy based on reason. It is a foundational text of the Enlightenment, establishing the "natural" duties people owe to themselves, others, and the state.</summary>

<meta>This page contains the formal dedication of the work. It follows the title page (represented by the mirrored image on the previous page). Samuel Pufendorf is dedicating this treatise on Natural Law to Gustav Otto Stenbock, a prominent Swedish noble and statesman who served as the Chancellor of Lund University at the time of the book's initial writing.</meta>

## *TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND*
## *MOST EXALTED HERO,*

## LORD

# GUSTAV
# OTTO
# STENBOCK,

### COUNT IN BOGESUND,
### FREE BARON IN
### KRONEBÄCK AND
### ÖRESTEN, etc.

### HIGH ADMIRAL OF THE REALM OF SWEDEN, <note>original: "Archithalasso." A high-ranking naval office, equivalent to Lord High Admiral.</note>
### AND CHANCELLOR OF THE CAROLINE 
### ACADEMY OF THE GOTHS, <note>original: "Academia Gothorum Carolina." This is the Latin name for Lund University, founded in 1666 and named after King Charles X Gustav.</note> etc.

### MY MOST GRACIOUS LORD.

<summary>This page is a formal dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock, a high-ranking Swedish official and Chancellor of Lund University. Such dedications were standard in the 17th and 18th centuries to secure patronage and political protection for scholarly works.</summary>

<keywords>Gustav Otto Stenbock, dedication, Swedish nobility, High Admiral, Lund University, Chancellor, patronage, 18th-century law</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock. The catchword "gra-" suggests the next page begins with "gradum" (step) or a similar word, continuing the thought about students entering the field of study.</meta>

### MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND
### MOST EXALTED COUNT,
### MOST GRACIOUS LORD,

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring ornate floral patterns, vines, and scrollwork.</image-desc>

*N*O small doubt was distracting my mind in different directions, as I wondered whether it would be sufficiently proper for such a tiny work to seek the patronage <note>original: "auspicia." In the 17th century, authors sought the "auspices" or protection of powerful nobles to validate their work and protect themselves from censorship.</note> of such an illustrious Name. For on one hand, the insignificance of this little book <term>libellus</term> <note>A "little book." Authors often used this term as a "modesty topes" to downplay the importance of their work when addressing a social superior.</note> caused me embarrassment, as it was capable of neither great genius nor splendor; since it contains only the first rudiments <term>rudimenta</term> <note>The basic principles or introductory elements.</note> of moral philosophy <note>original: "moralis philosophiæ." At this time, this field included what we now call ethics, political science, and law.</note>, which were mostly excerpted from my more extensive work <note>Pufendorf is likely referring to his massive 1672 volume, "On the Law of Nature and Nations."</note>. Although it may perhaps offer some use to those first [approaching] this discipline...

<summary>In this dedication, the author expresses humble hesitation about presenting such a short, introductory treatise to a man of Stenbock's high status. He explains that the book is a simplified summary of his much larger scholarly work on moral philosophy and law.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, patronage, moral philosophy, modesty, Samuel Pufendorf, introductory text, 17th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock. The word "gradum" (step) completes the sentence from the previous page regarding students entering their studies.</meta>

to those taking their first step <note>original: "gradum molientibus." This phrase completes the thought from the previous page regarding students beginning their education.</note>: thus, if I had to weigh here both your high rank <note>original: "fastigij." Literally a 'peak' or 'summit,' used here to denote high social or political standing.</note> and my own obligation, it did not seem that this work was sufficiently suited to either. On the other hand, your merits <term>merita</term> <note>In the 17th century, "merits" referred to significant services or achievements performed for the benefit of the state or the public.</note>—no less private than public—spurred my mind, which is most devoted to **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY**, to fear that I would be marked as an ingrate if I neglected any opportunity at all to testify how greatly I am bound to You. Nor do I speak now of those merits by which, through your exceptional deeds at home and abroad <note>original: "domi forisq;." A traditional Roman phrasing meaning both in domestic politics and in foreign military or diplomatic service.</note>, you have made your fatherland <term>patria</term> deeply indebted to You, and have long since dedicated Your Name to immortal glory. To recount these things with the dignity they deserve is the task of history; and while history [is occupied] with the most glorious deeds <note>original: "facinora." In Latin, this can mean 'crimes' or 'great deeds'; here it is used in the positive sense of 'achievements.'</note> of Your Nation...

<summary>Pufendorf explains that while his book might seem too small for a man of Stenbock's stature, he felt compelled to dedicate it to him to show gratitude for Stenbock's public and private services to the nation. He notes that Stenbock's great deeds at home and abroad are already a matter of historical record.</summary>

<keywords>patronage, gratitude, public service, Gustav Otto Stenbock, Swedish history, merits, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock. The text picks up from the previous page's final sentence, completing the word "facinora" (deeds/achievements).</meta>

[achieve]ments, and as it labors over weapons carried most successfully through so many regions, it always finds You to be a great part of such great affairs; Likewise, once there was a pause from wars, it admires You—having first been appointed to govern a most extensive province, and later to the protective guidance <term>moderamen</term> <note>original: "moderamine." In this context, it refers to Stenbock's role on the Regency Council during the minority of King Charles XI.</note> of the whole kingdom—flourishing no less in the arts of peace. It would be more fitting here to touch upon those things which have proceeded from **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY** toward this new Academy <note>The "recentem Academia" refers to the University of Lund, which had been recently founded in 1666.</note>, in which it was granted to me to settle my home and fortunes at the invitation of the Best and Greatest **KING**. This Academy can never sufficiently praise You according to Your merits as its most wise and equally most kind defender and supreme moderator, while among its advantages and honors...

<vocab>Academia, REGE, EXCELLENTIA, moderamine, gubernanda, deprædicare</vocab>

<summary>Pufendorf praises Stenbock for transitioning from a successful military career to a leader in civil governance and a protector of the newly founded University of Lund. He highlights his own gratitude for being invited by the King to join the faculty under Stenbock's oversight.</summary>

<keywords>Gustav Otto Stenbock, University of Lund, Charles XI, regency, patronage, arts of peace, academic administration</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock, following Pufendorf’s praise for Stenbock's transition from military leader to civil administrator and protector of the University of Lund.</meta>

...it finds You daily sailing <note>original: "velificantem." Pufendorf uses a nautical metaphor here to describe Stenbock's smooth and successful navigation through his duties.</note> through such a great mass of public affairs <term>negotiorum</term> without weariness. Indeed, with what veneration must I value those things which **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY** has particularly bestowed upon me? For others, the highest of their prayers is simply to become known to and approved by Men of such high standing <term>fastigij</term> <note>Literally "summit" or "pinnacle," referring to those at the top of the social and political hierarchy.</note>. But for me, Your grace has been so poured out and pleasing that I have experienced it as most kind <note>original: "benignissima."</note> on more than one occasion, both in promoting my interests and in warding off the assaults of the malevolent from me. Since to offer anything in return for these favors is far beyond the measure of my fortune, it is nonetheless necessary for me to at least fulfill my obligation through a humble devotion of spirit and an honest profession of so many favors <term>beneficiorum</term>; since this also is a trait of the greatest Men...

<sig>a 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: beni-</meta>

<summary>Pufendorf expresses deep personal gratitude to Stenbock for his protection against "malevolent" critics and for his ongoing patronage, acknowledging that he can only repay such high-level support with public devotion and professional loyalty.</summary>

<keywords>patronage, gratitude, public affairs, social standing, academic defense, Gustav Otto Stenbock</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock. Pufendorf is explaining that even though his gift (the book) is small, a noble patron like Stenbock will appreciate the spirit of gratitude in which it is offered.</meta>

*...kindness <note>original: "benignitas"</note> is such that it freely allows itself to be satisfied by the testimony of a grateful mind. And because it is customary for noble mortals to voluntarily add a mark of their esteem through a gracious interpretation of duty <note>original: "pietatis," here referring to the loyalty or "piety" a client owes a patron</note>, even when they are offered a meager tribute <note>original: "cultui"</note>; this too, the goodness of* **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY** *bids me to hope: that I might not seem to have offended against Your greatness, if I use such a small work <term>opusculo</term> <note>A diminutive form of "opus" (work), signaling the author's humility regarding his own book.</note> as an occasion to publicly express the most devoted spirit of my mind toward You. For it would be a long wait to expect some more splendid work from me, and one that might endure the ages; especially since talents are immensely chilled if they discover—while they labor to raise themselves above the common crowd—that envy and ignorance <note>original: "livorem & inscitiam"</note> exercise their teeth against them with impunity, [and of] leisure...*

<vocab>benignitas (kindness), contestatione (testimony/expression), mortalium (of mortals/men), pietatis (of duty/piety), opusculo (small work), devotissimi (most devoted), livorem (envy/malice), inscitiam (ignorance)</vocab>

<meta>catchword: ratio-</meta>

<summary>Pufendorf humbly asks Stenbock to accept this "small work" as a public token of his gratitude, noting that the harsh environment of academic envy often discourages scholars from attempting more ambitious projects.</summary>

<keywords>patronage, humility, academic rivalry, envy, gratitude, dedication, Gustav Otto Stenbock</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Gustav Otto Stenbock. In the previous section, Pufendorf expressed concern that academic envy and "leisure" (or the lack thereof) might hinder his work. Here, he concludes with the hope that the Patron's favor will reinvigorate his spirit, ending with a traditional blessing and signature.</meta>

*...no account is rendered. Yet my spirit will bloom with new vigor and cast off its rising weariness if I learn that this tribute has been accepted with a kindly face* <note>original: "placida fronte," literally "with a calm forehead," a common Latin idiom for a favorable or friendly reception</note> *by* **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY**; *and if, at the same time, you bid me to be secure in your favor and patronage for the future. Thus may* GOD, THE BEST AND GREATEST, <term>O. M.</term> <note>Abbreviation for "Optimus Maximus," a title originally used for Jupiter but adopted by Christian scholars to denote the Supreme Deity.</note> *preserve* **YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY** *flourishing and vigorous for many years, as an honor and benefit to the Fatherland, Your Most Splendid Family, and our young state!* <note>original: "novellæ reip." (reipublicæ). This likely refers to the "republic of letters" or, more specifically, the relatively new University of Lund, founded in 1666.</note>

**OF YOUR MOST ILLUSTRIOUS EXCELLENCY**

->the most devoted<-

Lund <note>original: "Londini Scanorum," the Latin name for Lund in Sweden, distinguishing it from London in England.</note>
January 23, <note>original: "X. Kal. Febr." (the tenth day before the Kalends of February), which corresponds to January 23rd.</note>
the year 1673.

SAMUEL PUFENDORF.

<summary>Pufendorf concludes his dedication by praying for his patron's continued health and favor, noting that such support is the primary cure for the "weariness" of scholarly labor. He dates the work from the University of Lund in 1673.</summary>
<keywords>patronage, Samuel Pufendorf, Lund, Sweden, dedication, 17th century, academic life, Gustav Otto Stenbock</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to the Patron (Gustav Otto Stenbock) to the direct address to the reader. While the previous page focused on seeking political protection for his work, this page explains the educational philosophy and purpose behind creating a shortened "compendium" of his larger theories on Natural Law.</meta>

# TO THE KINDLY READER
## GREETINGS.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'N' featuring a stork or pelican standing in a landscape within a square decorative border.</image-desc>
If a custom accepted by most learned men
had not taken on nearly the
force of law, it might have
seemed unnecessary to say
anything by way of a preface regarding the
purpose of this writing. The matter itself
speaks clearly enough: I have aimed at
nothing other than to set forth for <term>beginners</term> <note>original: "tironibus," a term for recruits or novices, here referring to students new to the study of law.</note> the
primary chapters of <term>natural law</term> <note>original: "juris naturalis," the universal system of rules and ethics believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason.</note> in a
brief and, I believe, clear <term>compendium</term> <note>A concise summary or handbook of a larger field of study.</note>.
My concern was that if students were to
plunge into the vast reaches of that discipline
without first gaining an elementary
knowledge, they might be deterred from the very
start by the sheer volume and difficulty of
the subject matter. At the same time, it also
seemed beneficial to the public that the
minds of studious youth be steeped in the kind
of <term>moral doctrine</term> <note>The study of ethics and the principles of right and wrong.</note> whose practical use
might be clearly seen in <term>civil life</term> <note>original: "vita civili," referring to life within a functioning society or state.</note>.
And although I have always considered it
a thankless task to condense the extensive
writings of others—and even more so my
own—into a summary, nevertheless, since
the authority of my superiors was added
to the task, I [did not think it would be
held] as a fault by wise men—

<meta>catchword: hi (beginning of "mihi")</meta>

<summary>Pufendorf introduces his handbook on natural law, explaining that he wrote this shortened version specifically for students to prevent them from being overwhelmed by his larger, more complex works. He emphasizes that moral education is essential for a productive life in society.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, education, compendium, moral doctrine, civil life, Samuel Pufendorf, legal training</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the reader. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, where the author was discussing the merits of creating a condensed "compendium" or summary of his larger legal theories for the sake of students.</meta>

...to be criticized, I think, because I wished to dedicate this labor solely to the **benefit of youth** <note>original: "juventutis utilitati"</note>; the favor shown to them ought to be so great that a work undertaken for their sake—even if it lacks genius or brilliance—should be judged unworthy by no one. Furthermore, no one with a **sound mind** <note>original: "sanum sinciput," literally a healthy "half-head," an idiom for common sense or a clear head.</note> would deny that principles of this kind are more suitable for the universal study of law than the elements of any particular **civil law** <note>original: "juris civilis," the specific legal code of a city or state, such as Roman Law, as opposed to universal moral law.</note>.

And these remarks might have sufficed for the present, had not certain people advised that it would not be out of place to preface some things which might help in understanding the nature of **natural law** <note>original: "juris naturalis"</note> as a whole, and in defining its limits more accurately. I undertook this all the more willingly because, in this way, the pretext is taken away from bothersomely curious men for extending their **feverish criticism** <note>original: "febriculosam suam censuram"; the author uses "febriculosa" to describe an unhealthy, agitated, or over-eager impulse to judge.</note> to this discipline, which is often separated from their own province as if by a boundary.

It is manifest, therefore, that from three sources, as it were, men are rec—

<summary>The author argues that simplified summaries are essential for students and that universal natural law provides a better educational foundation than specific state codes. He also signals his intent to define the boundaries of his field to protect it from meddling critics from other disciplines.</summary>
<keywords>legal pedagogy, student benefit, natural law, civil law, academic boundaries, Pufendorf, jurisdiction of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory argument about the sources of human duty. The previous page ended mid-word with "rec—", which is completed here as "recognition."</meta>

—cognition of their **duty** <note>original: "officij" (officium); here meaning a moral or legal obligation one is bound to perform.</note>, and what in this life should be done by them as honorable, or omitted as base; [they] draw [this] from the **light of reason** <note>original: "lumine rationis"; the innate human capacity to distinguish right from wrong without divine help.</note>, from **civil laws** <note>original: "legibus civilibus"</note>, and from the particular **revelation** <note>original: "revelatione"</note> of the **Divine Deity** <note>original: "divini Numinis"</note>.

From the first source flow the most common duties of man, especially those which make him **sociable** <note>original: "sociabilem"; a key concept in 17th-century law suggesting that humans are naturally inclined to live together in peace.</note> with other men; from the second flow the duties of man insofar as he lives subject to a particular and defined **state** <note>original: "civitati"</note>; from the third flow the duties of a **Christian man**.

From these, three separate disciplines arise: the first is that of **natural law** <note>original: "juris naturalis"</note>, common to all nations; the second is that of the **civil law** <note>original: "juris civilis"</note> of individual states, which is as varied, or can be, as the number of states into which the human race has divided.

The third is considered **Moral Theology** <note>original: "Theologia moralis"</note>, distinct from that part of Theology in which the things to be **believed** <note>original: "credenda"; referring to "dogmatic" or "systematic" theology which focuses on faith and creed rather than behavior.</note> are set forth. Each of these disciplines employs its own way of proving its doctrines, corresponding to its own principle—

<summary>The author identifies three sources of human duty—reason, state law, and divine revelation—which give rise to the distinct fields of natural law, civil law, and moral theology.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, civil law, moral theology, light of reason, divine revelation, sociability, duties of man</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Natural Law, Civil Law, and Moral Theology. The previous page ended mid-word with "ow...", which is completed here by "tem" to form the word "nature" <note>original: "indolem" or "naturam"; likely referring to the specific nature or character of each discipline.</note>.</meta>

...nature. In **natural law** <term>natural law (jus naturale): a system of ethics based on human reason and the inherent nature of things, rather than on specific legislation.</term>, it is asserted that something ought to be done because it is gathered through **right reason** <note>original: "rectam rationem"; the human capacity to think logically and reach moral truths.</note> to be necessary for **sociality** <note>original: "socialitatem"; the fundamental principle that humans must live cooperatively in society.</note> among men. The ultimate reason for the precepts of **civil law** <term>civil law (jus civile): the specific laws enacted by a particular state or ruler for its citizens.</term> is that the legislator has so decreed it. The **moral theologian** <note>original: "Theologus moralis"</note> rests in this as the final authority: that **GOD** has so commanded in the **Holy Scriptures**.

Just as the discipline of civil law presupposes natural law as a more general discipline, it follows that if something is contained within civil law about which natural law is silent, it should not be thought that the one contradicts the other. In a similar way, if certain things are handed down in Moral Theology that flow from **divine revelation** <note>original: "revelatione divina"; truths made known by God through scripture or grace that exceed human understanding.</note>—which our reason cannot reach and which natural law is therefore ignorant of—it would be very foolish <note>original: "imperitum"; literally "unskilled" or "ignorant."</note> to set one against the other, or to dream of some conflict between these disciplines. Conversely, if those things presupposed in the discipline of natural law, because [they are] investi- <note>The word is completed on the next page as "investigated" (investigantur).</note>

<summary>This page explains the distinct foundations of Natural Law (reason), Civil Law (the legislator's will), and Moral Theology (God's word), arguing that while they differ in scope, they do not contradict one another.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, civil law, moral theology, right reason, sociality, divine revelation, legislation, harmony of laws</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page completes the word "investigated" (investigari) from the previous sentence. It continues the discussion on the relationship between reason-based Natural Law and revelation-based Moral Theology, arguing that they are not in conflict but rather different ways of looking at the same truth.</meta>

...can be investigated; for that reason, those things are by no means opposed to what the **Holy Scriptures** deliver more clearly on the same matter, but are only conceived by way of abstraction <note>In this context, "abstraction" refers to the intellectual process of considering human nature on its own, setting aside what we know from the Bible to see what reason alone can discover.</note>. Thus, for example, in the discipline of **natural law** <term>natural law (jus naturale): a system of ethics based on human reason and the inherent nature of things, rather than on specific legislation.</term>, by abstracting from that knowledge which is drawn from **Holy Scripture** <note>original: "Sacra Scriptura"</note>, the condition of the first man is formed—however he was cast into the world—insofar as **reasoning** <note>original: "ratiocinatio"</note> alone can reach it. To oppose these findings to what the divine writings hand down regarding that same condition is truly the "juice of black cuttlefish" <note>original: "succus nigræ loliginis"; a classical idiom from the Roman poet Horace meaning pure malice or spiteful slander.</note>; it is mere "corrosive rust" <note>original: "ærugo"; another reference to Horace, signifying a poisonous or spiteful disposition.</note>.

Indeed, just as civil and natural law easily agree with one another, it seems somewhat more laborious to govern the boundaries between the same natural law and **moral theology**, and to define in which parts they chiefly differ. On this matter, I will set forth my opinion in a few words—not indeed by **Papal authority** <note>original: "autoritate Papali"; the author is clarifying that he does not claim the absolute infallibility or supreme power associated with the Pope.</note>, as if I were protected from all error by some privilege, nor as one who speaks from dreams sent by God, or...

<meta>catchword: irra-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that Natural Law uses human reason to "abstract" truths about humanity, which does not contradict Scripture but complements it. He then begins to define the difficult boundary between Natural Law and Moral Theology, disclaiming any personal infallibility.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, Holy Scripture, moral theology, human reason, abstraction, papal authority, logic vs revelation</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's personal disclaimer. He previously stated he does not write with "Papal authority." Here, he completes that thought by rejecting "private illumination" as a source of his work, positioning himself instead as a diligent scholar open to constructive criticism but dismissive of ignorant meddlers. He then transitions into the first formal distinction between Natural Law and Moral Theology: their differing sources.</meta>

...not as one who is animated by some irrational instinct or trust in a certain singular **illumination** <note>The author is distancing himself from "enthusiasts" or those who claim private, mystical revelation outside of reason or established scripture.</note>; but as one who has it at heart to **adorn the task** <note>original: "Spartam sibi commissam... exornare"; a reference to the classical Greek proverb "You have obtained Sparta, now adorn it," meaning to do one's duty in the station they have been given.</note> entrusted to him according to the measure of his own talent. 

In this way, however, just as I am ready to listen willingly to wise and **learned** men who suggest better ideas, and to correct my previously held views without stubbornness; so too do I care not a straw <note>original: "flocci faciam"; literally "to value at a tuft of wool," a common Latin idiom meaning to hold in no esteem or to not care at all.</note> for those censors who emulate **Midas** <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was given donkey ears by Apollo as a sign of his poor judgment; the author uses this to describe critics who lack the wisdom to judge his work.</note>, brazenly rushing into judgments on matters that do not concern them, and that whole nation of **busybodies** <term>busybodies (ardelionum): from the Latin "ardelio," referring to a meddler or someone who is pointlessly busy.</term>, whose character *Phaedrus* <note>A Roman fabulist who adapted Aesop's fables into Latin verse.</note> represents most charmingly: *Running about in a panic,* he says, *busy in idleness, panting for nothing, doing much yet doing nothing, a burden to themselves and most hateful to others.* 

The first distinction, therefore, by which these disciplines are separated from one another, results from the different **source** from which each derives its **doctrines** <note>original: "dogmata"</note>; a point which we have just now touched upon. From this it follows: if there are certain things which we are commanded by the **divine writings** to do or to omit, the necessity of which, however, [reason...]

<meta>This page continues the argument that certain legal and moral frameworks are only necessary because of human frailty. It transitions from discussing affirmative duties (like charity) to negative prohibitions (like the laws against murder), arguing that laws against crimes are redundant in a state where the impulse to commit them does not exist.</meta>

...yet these things are commanded in vain to those who are not subject to misery, poverty, and death. **Natural law** <note>original: "lex naturalis"</note> already commands us to be inclined toward forgiving injuries and reconciling peace. But such a command is useless among those who do not sin against the laws of **sociality** <term>socialitas: the fundamental duty or inclination of humans to live peacefully and cooperatively within a community.</term>. 

And this very point is also clearly perceived in negative precepts—those laws that forbid certain actions—which pertain to **natural law** rather than **positive law** <term>jus positivum: man-made laws established by specific governments or social contracts, as opposed to universal natural laws.</term>. For although any affirmative command virtually contains a prohibition of everything opposite to it (for example: he who is commanded to love his neighbor is, by that very fact, forbidden from doing to him anything that is repugnant to love), it nevertheless seems superfluous for these to be established by express decrees where no depraved desires drive men to commit such acts. 

To illustrate this point, we may look to the example of **Solon** <note>Solon (c. 630 – c. 560 BCE) was a famous Athenian lawmaker often cited by legal theorists for his wisdom.</note>, who refused to establish a punishment for **parricide** <term>parricidium: the murder of a parent or other near relative.</term> in his public laws. --- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of the two students with different dispositions. It argues that laws and prohibitions are necessary only for those inclined toward vice, whereas a person in a state of natural integrity would not require such constraints. This distinction is used to define the boundaries between natural law and moral theology.</meta>

...under the sharpest threat of punishment, he must be forbidden from running about, from playing dice, from selling his books, from **plagiarism** <note>original: *aliena vitula aret*; literally "plowing with another’s heifer," a metaphorical expression for using someone else's work or secrets as one's own</note> in composing his exercises, from **revelry** <note>original: *pergræcetur*; literally "to act like a Greek," a classical idiom for indulging in drinking, luxury, and a dissolute lifestyle</note>, and from seeking out prostitutes. 

If someone were to laboriously preach these same prohibitions to a boy of the first disposition <note>referring to the studious and modest boy mentioned on the previous page</note>, that boy would tell him to **keep a holy silence** <note>original: *εὐφημεῖν* (euphemein); a Greek term meaning to speak only auspicious words or to maintain a respectful silence during a ritual</note> and to sing such warnings to anyone rather than himself, since he is touched by no desire for such things. 

From this, I believe it is manifest that the "face" or character of **natural law** <term>juris naturalis: a system of law based on values intrinsic to human nature that can be deduced through reason, independent of the enacted laws of a specific state.</term> would be far different if one were to presuppose a state of human integrity <note>meaning a state of original perfection or innocence, as opposed to a fallen or corrupt state</note>. At the same time, since the limits of this discipline—by which it is separated from **moral theology** <term>Theologia moralis: the branch of theology that focuses on God’s laws regarding human behavior and the soul's salvation, distinct from secular or natural law.</term>—have been so clearly defined, this discipline will be in no worse a condition than **civil jurisprudence** <term>juris prudentiam civilem: the study and application of the laws of a specific city or state.</term>, medicine, **natural science** <term>scientiam naturalem</term>, or **mathematics** <term>mathesin</term>. Into these subjects, if some **uninitiated person** <note>original: *ἀμύητος* (amyetos); someone not "admitted to the mysteries" or lacking proper training in a specific field</note> should dare to burst, claiming for himself the right to judge without the consensus of the people, they do not hesitate, if this [occurs... <meta>catchword: nere (likely "occidere" or "occineret")</meta>]

<summary>The author concludes the analogy of the two students, explaining that laws against vice are unnecessary for those with a virtuous nature. He uses this to distinguish the study of natural law from moral theology, placing it on the same academic footing as medicine or mathematics.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, moral theology, pedagogy, academic discipline, human nature, plagiarism, civil jurisprudence, legal theory</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the reception of his work. Having previously distinguished between the virtuous student who needs no laws and the wayward one who does, he now applies a similar distinction to his readers and critics, citing a famous anecdote about the painter Apelles to warn against unlearned criticism.</meta>

...to remember what Apelles once said to Megabyzus <note>Megabyzus was a high-ranking Persian priest who visited the studio of the famous Greek painter Apelles; the story is a classic warning against non-experts giving unsolicited advice to professionals.</note>, when the latter attempted to lecture on I-know-not-what regarding the **art of painting** <term>ars pictoria: the technical and aesthetic discipline of painting, then considered a branch of high philosophy and geometry.</term>: "I beg you," he said, "be silent, lest the boys who grind the pigments <note>original: *melidem*; likely a reference to *miltos* or ochre, the raw materials handled by young apprentices in an artist's workshop</note> laugh at you for beginning to speak about things you have never learned." 

But we shall easily reach an agreement with good and wise men. As for malevolent and unlearned **detractors** <note>original: *obtrectatores*; those who habitually disparage or criticize the work of others out of malice or ignorance</note>, it is better to leave them to be tortured by their own envy; since it is certainly well established, and fixed by eternal law, that the **Ethiopian does not change his skin** <note>A proverbial expression, drawing on Jeremiah 13:23, used here to argue that a person’s fundamental nature—specifically the nature of a spiteful critic—is impossible to change.</note>.

<summary>The author dismisses unlearned and spiteful critics by citing an anecdote of Apelles and Megabyzus, arguing that while wise men will understand his work, the nature of a dedicated detractor is unchangeable.</summary>
<keywords>Apelles, Megabyzus, art of painting, professional expertise, envy, detractors, unchangeable nature, classical anecdotes</keywords>

# SAMUEL PUFENDORF
# ON THE DUTY OF MAN AND CITIZEN
### BOOK ONE.
### CHAPTER ONE.
# ON HUMAN ACTION.

->§. 1.<-

By <term>duty</term> <note>original: *officium*; in this context, it refers to a moral or legal obligation that a person is bound to perform.</note> we here mean a human action rightly shaped according to the requirement of an obligation and the dictates of laws. To understand this, it is necessary to first explain both the nature of human action and the nature of laws in general.

§. 2. By <term>human action</term> we do not mean just any movement proceeding from human faculties; we mean only that which is produced and directed by those faculties which the <note>original: *Creator O. M.* (Optimus Maximus); a title meaning "the Greatest and Best Creator," often borrowed from Roman descriptions of Jupiter and applied here to the Judeo-Christian God.</note> Greatest and Best Creator granted to the human race above the animals—namely, that which is undertaken with the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *intellectus*; the faculty of understanding or reasoning.</note> lighting the way and the <term>will</term> <note>original: *voluntas*; the faculty of choosing or deciding on a course of action.</note> making the decision.

§. 3. For it has been granted to man, not only that he might [perceive] the various things which occur in this universe—

<summary>This opening page introduces Samuel Pufendorf's definition of "duty" and "human action," establishing that true human behavior is distinct from animal instinct because it is guided by reason (intellect) and choice (will).</summary>
<keywords>duty, human action, law, obligation, intellect, will, natural law, Samuel Pufendorf</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the opening chapter of Book One. The previous page established that "human action" is defined by the use of the intellect and the will. Here, Pufendorf elaborates on how the intellect functions to compare ideas, foresee consequences, and choose between different options, asserting that every rational adult possesses a "natural light" of reason sufficient to understand basic moral duties.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>OF BOOK I.</header>

[occur], he may recognize them, compare them with one another, and by these opportunities form new notions for himself. But he can also foresee what he is about to do, and move himself toward its execution, and shape that same action according to a certain norm and a certain end, and gather what will result from it. Nay, he can even judge whether those things which have already been performed conform to the rule. Furthermore, not all of a man's <term>faculties</term> <note>original: *facultates*; the various powers of the mind and body.</note> move themselves perpetually or in a uniform manner; rather, some of these are stirred by an internal human impulse, and once stirred, they are tempered and directed. Finally, a man is not carried toward all objects indiscriminately, but he seeks some and is averse to others. Often, even if an object for acting is present, he suspends his movement; and when several objects are present, he chooses one and rejects the rest.

§. 4. Therefore, regarding the faculty of comprehending and judging things, which goes by the name of **intellect**, this must be held as certain above all else: that every man of mature age and sound mind possesses enough of a <term>natural light</term> <note>original: *naturalis luminis*; the innate human capacity for reason and moral discernment, often described by philosophers as a "light" that guides the mind without needing external revelation.</note> that—provided he applies <term>cultivation</term> <note>original: *cultura*; the process of education, training, or mental refinement.</note> and proper <term>meditation</term> <note>original: *meditatio*; here meaning deep, focused reflection or study.</note>—he can correctly understand at least those general precepts and principles which serve to lead this life honorably and peacefully; at the same time, he can judge that these principles are entirely consistent with <term>human nature</term> <note>original: *indoli humanae*; the inherent character or disposition of the human race.</note>. For unless this were the case, at least within the sphere of the hu- <meta>The text cuts off here; the catchword "mani" indicates the full word is "humani" (human).</meta>

<summary>Pufendorf describes the human intellect's ability to foresee consequences and make choices, arguing that every rational adult has a natural capacity (a "natural light") to understand basic moral principles through reflection.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues Pufendorf’s discussion of the human intellect as the foundation of moral action. On the previous page, he argued that while humans possess a "natural light" of reason, it requires cultivation. This page explains that if this capacity for understanding were not assumed, legal and moral accountability would vanish. He then categorizes different states of the conscience based on how clearly the mind perceives the reasons for its actions.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I</header>

[if the mind] were admitted, men could plead <term>invincible ignorance</term> <note>original: *ignorantiam invincibilem*; a legal and theological term for a lack of knowledge that a person could not have overcome even with reasonable effort.</note> for any of their crimes; for no one in a <term>human court</term> <note>original: *foro humano*; refers to earthly legal systems as opposed to the "divine court" of God's judgment.</note> can be accused of violating a rule that is beyond his strength to comprehend.

§. 5. When a person’s intellect has been correctly informed about what must be done or omitted, and in such a way that they can provide certain and indubitable reasons for their opinion, it is usually called a **right conscience** <note>original: *conscientia recta*; a conscience that is both certain and objectively correct.</note>. But when someone holds a true opinion regarding things to be done or omitted, yet cannot demonstrate it through reason themselves, but has drawn it from the course of social life, from habit, or from the authority of superiors—and yet lacks any reason that would more strongly persuade them of the contrary—it is usually called a **probable conscience** <note>original: *conscientia probabilis*.</note>. Most mortals are governed by this, since it is granted to only a few to know the causes of things.

§. 6. However, it often happens to some, especially in particular cases, that reasons present themselves on both sides; and they do not possess enough judgment to clearly discern which reasons carry more weight. This is what is usually called a **doubtful conscience** <note>original: *conscientia dubia*.</note>. Regarding this, the rule is as follows: as long as judgment remains in suspense as to whether something is good or evil, the action must be suspended. For if, before the doubt has been resolved, one devotes oneself to acting—

It establishes the moral rule that one should not act while the mind is in a state of doubt.</summary>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

[to end the doubt], it involves a purpose of sinning, or at least a neglect of the law.

§. 7. Often the human intellect also takes the false for the true; and then it is said to be in **error**. This is usually called **vincible error** <term>error vincibilis</term> <note>An error that could have been avoided if the person had used reasonable care and attention.</note>, when someone, by applying due attention and diligence, could have guarded against falling into it. But it is called **invincible** <term>error invincibilis</term> when someone, even after applying all the diligence that the standard of common life requires, could not have avoided it. However, such an error—at least among those who care to cultivate the light of reason and model their lives on integrity—is not usually found in the common rules of living, but only in specific private matters. For the general precepts of **natural law** <term>naturalis juris</term> <note>Universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason, such as the obligation not to harm others.</note> are plain; and he who enacts **positive laws** <note>original: *leges positivas*; specific statutes created by a government or legislator, as opposed to natural moral laws.</note> usually does, and should, make it his primary task to ensure they are known to his subjects. Therefore, in these matters, error does not arise except through **gross negligence** <note>original: *supinam negligentiam*; literally "supine negligence," referring to a lazy or extreme lack of care.</note>. But in specific dealings, it is easy for error to creep in against a person's will and without fault, concerning the object or other circumstances of the action.

§. 8. Where knowledge is simply absent, it is called **ignorance**. This is considered in two ways: first, according to how it affects an action; and second, according to whether it arose against one’s will or through some fault. In the first respect, ignorance is usually divided into **efficacious** <term>ignorantia efficax</term> and **concomitant** <term>ignorantia concomitans</term>. The former is that by which...

<summary>This page defines the legal and moral distinctions between vincible and invincible error, arguing that while general natural laws are clear to all, specific circumstances can lead to blameless ignorance. It begins to categorize types of ignorance based on their influence on human action.</summary>
<keywords>vincible error, invincible error, natural law, positive law, gross negligence, ignorance, moral accountability, legal theory</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 4 regarding the different types of ignorance and how they affect the morality of an action. It then transitions into the second faculty of the human mind: the Will.</meta>

# CHAPTER I

[...if the ignorance] had been absent, the current action would not have been undertaken. This is the kind which, even if it had been absent, the action would nonetheless have been undertaken. In the latter respect, ignorance is ***voluntary or involuntary***. The former is that which someone has either deliberately sought out, by rejecting the means of knowing the truth, or has allowed to creep upon them by not applying due diligence. The latter occurs when someone is ignorant of things they neither could know nor were bound to know. And this is again ***twofold***. For either someone could not cast off their ignorance in the present moment, yet they are nonetheless at fault <note>The author suggests that a person might be currently ignorant through their own prior negligence, making them responsible for their state.</note> for being in such a state. Or, they not only cannot overcome the ignorance in the present, but they are also not at fault for having fallen into such a condition.

§. 9. The other faculty, which is observed in humans uniquely as compared to the **beasts** <note>original: *brutis*; a common philosophical term of the era for animals, emphasizing their lack of rational choice.</note>, is called the ***will***; through which, as if by an internal impulse, a person moves themselves to act, and chooses what pleases them most, and turns away from what seems unsuitable. Therefore, man possesses from the will both that he acts ***spontaneously*** <term>sponte</term> <note>Meaning the person is the "author" of the action, acting from within rather than being forced by an external physical power.</note>—that is, so that he is not determined to act by some internal necessity, but is himself the author of his action—and that he acts ***freely*** <term>libere</term>—that is, so that when one object is presented, he can act or not act, and either choose or reject the same; or, when several objects are presented, choose one and reject the rest. Furthermore, since certain human actions are undertaken for their own sake,

A 3
<meta>catchword: which-</meta>

<summary>This page defines the distinction between voluntary and involuntary ignorance and introduces the human Will as the faculty that allows for spontaneous and free choice, distinguishing humans from animals.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the human will and the necessity of laws. It uses the analogy of two different types of students—one wayward and one virtuous—to explain how the "face" of natural law changes based on the perceived state of human nature (whether perfect or corrupt). It also asserts the independence of natural law as a formal discipline distinct from moral theology.</meta>

...must be forbidden under the sharpest threat of punishment: <margin>·</margin> that he not run about, nor play at dice, nor sell his books, nor **"plow with another’s heifer"** <note>An ancient idiom, here referring to the act of plagiarizing or using someone else's work to complete one's own school exercises.</note> in composing his exercises, nor live a life of debauchery <note>original: *pergræcetur*; literally "to act the Greek," a Roman idiom for self-indulgent or dissolute living.</note>, nor seek out harlots. If someone were to laboriously preach these same rules to a boy of the former [more virtuous] character, the boy would command him to **keep a holy silence** <note>original: *εὐφημεῖν* (euphemein); a Greek term meaning to speak only auspicious words or, more commonly, to maintain a respectful silence during a religious rite.</note>, and to sing those warnings to anyone rather than himself, since he is touched by no desire for such things. From this I believe it is clear that the face of **natural law** <term>juris naturalis</term> <note>The system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature rather than the rules of society.</note> would be far different if one were to presuppose a state of "integral" [unfallen] humanity. 

At the same time, since the boundaries of this discipline—by which it is separated from **moral theology** <term>Theologia morali</term> <note>The branch of theology that focuses on the divine communicative of God’s will regarding human behavior, often contrasted here with natural law derived from reason alone.</note>—have been so clearly marked, this discipline will be in no worse a condition than civil law, medicine, natural science, or **mathematics** <term>mathesin</term>. If some **uninitiated person** <note>original: *ἀμύητος* (amyētos); a Greek term for one who has not been initiated into the sacred mysteries, used here for someone lacking professional expertise.</note> should dare to break into these fields, claiming for himself the right to judge without the people’s vote, they do not hesitate, if this should happen...

<summary>The author argues that the application of natural law depends on whether human nature is viewed as perfect or corrupt, using a comparison of two schoolboys to illustrate the point. He also defends natural law as a distinct, professional discipline on par with medicine or mathematics, separate from moral theology.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, moral theology, human nature, discipline boundaries, plagiarism, ethics, academic expertise</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes a defense of the author's approach to natural law, contrasting the reception of his work among the wise versus the ignorant. It transitions from the previous page's discussion on the "face" of law to an anecdote about the Greek painter Apelles, illustrating that those who haven't studied a subject should not criticize it.</meta>

...deservedly, just as Apelles once did to Megabyzus <note>Apelles was a renowned painter of ancient Greece; Megabyzus was a high-ranking Persian priest of Artemis at Ephesus. This anecdote, recorded by authors like Pliny the Elder and Plutarch, serves as a warning against speaking with authority on subjects outside one's expertise.</note>, when the latter attempted to discourse on I know not what regarding the art of painting: "I beg you," he said, "be silent, lest the boys who grind the pigments <note>original: *melidem* (likely a variant of *miltum* or *melinum*); these were minerals like red ochre or white earth ground by apprentices to create paint.</note> laugh at you for beginning to speak of things you have not learned."

But with good and wise men indeed, we shall easily reach an agreement. As for those who are malevolent and unlearned detractors, it would be better to leave them to be tormented by their own envy; for it is certainly well known, and established by eternal law, that **"the Ethiopian does not change his skin."** <note>A proverbial expression (likely referencing Jeremiah 13:23) used here to suggest that a person’s inherent nature or deep-seated ignorance is often unalterable.</note>

# SAMUEL PUFENDORF’S
# ON THE DUTY OF MAN
## AND CITIZEN

## CHAPTER ONE.
# ON HUMAN ACTION.

->§. I.<-

By *duty* <note>original: *Officium*; the core concept of the book, referring to the moral and legal obligations an individual has toward others and the state.</note> we here mean a human *action* <note>original: *actio*</note> that is rightly shaped according to the measure of an obligation in compliance with the dictates of laws. To understand this, it is necessary to first discuss both the nature of human action and laws in general.

§. 2. By *human action* we do not mean every movement proceeding from human faculties; but only those which are produced and directed by those powers which the Creator, Most Good and Most Great <note>original: *Creator O. M.* (Optimus Maximus), a traditional Latin epithet for God, emphasizing supreme benevolence and power.</note>, has granted to mankind above the beasts—namely, those actions undertaken with the *intellect* <note>original: *intellectu*; the power of the mind to understand and reason.</note> shining the light before them and the *will* <note>original: *voluntate*; the power of the mind to choose or decide on a course of action.</note> deciding the course.

§. 3. For it has been granted to man not only to [perceive] the various things which occur <note>The Latin sentence is interrupted here; the catchword indicates the word "occurrunt" (occur) continues on the next page.</note> in this universe—

<meta>catchword: occur,</meta>

<meta>This page continues Pufendorf's analysis of human action, transitioning from the basic perception of the world to the sophisticated internal processes of the intellect and will. It establishes the "natural light" of reason as the foundation for moral accountability.</meta>

# BOOK I.

[occur]red <note>Completing the word *occurrunt* from the previous page: "...things which occur in this universe."</note>, he is able to know them, compare them with one another, and on these occasions form new notions for himself. But he is also able to look forward to what he is about to do, move himself toward its execution, and conform that action to a certain **norm** <note>original: *norma*; a standard or rule of conduct.</note> and a certain end, while gathering what results will follow. Moreover, he can judge whether those things already completed agree with the rule. Nor do all of man's faculties move themselves perpetually or in a uniform manner; rather, some of these are excited by an internal impulse of man, and once excited, are tempered and directed. Finally, man is not carried toward all objects indiscriminately, but seeks some and turns away from others. Often, even if an object for acting is present, he suspends the motion; and when several objects are present, he chooses one and rejects the rest.

->§. 4.<-

Regarding the faculty of grasping and judging things, which goes by the name of *intellect* <note>original: *intellectus*</note>, this must be held as certain before all else: that for any man of mature age and sound mind, there remains enough of a **natural light**, as it were <note>original: *naturalis velut luminis*; the innate human capacity for reason and moral discernment, often contrasted with divine revelation.</note>, so that with the application of cultivation <note>meaning education or social refinement</note> and proper **meditation** <note>original: *meditatione*; careful, systematic reflection or study.</note>, he can rightly grasp at least those general precepts and principles which make for leading this life honestly and peacefully. At the same time, he can judge that these principles are indeed consistent with **human nature** <note>original: *humanæ indoli*; the natural disposition or character of mankind.</note>. For unless this were true, at least within the sphere of the human court <note>original: *fori humani*; the realm of human law and social judgment, as opposed to the internal "court" of conscience or the divine judgment of God.</note>—

<meta>Catchword: [hu]man</meta>

<summary>Pufendorf explains how the human intellect allows individuals to compare ideas, plan future actions, and evaluate past behavior according to rules. He asserts that all mentally sound adults possess a "natural light" of reason sufficient to understand the basic moral principles necessary for a peaceful social life.</summary>

For in a **human court** <note>original: *foro humano*; referring to civil or legal judgment as opposed to the internal judgment of the soul.</note>, no one can be accused of violating a rule that is beyond their power to understand.

# CHAPTER I

A 2

->§. 5.<-

When a person’s intellect has been correctly informed about what must be done or avoided—and specifically in such a way that it can provide certain and undoubted reasons for its judgment—it is usually called a **right conscience** <note>original: *conscientia recta*</note>. But when someone holds a true opinion regarding what should be done or avoided, yet cannot demonstrate it through reason himself—instead deriving it from the course of civil life, from custom, or from the authority of superiors—and yet lacks any reason that would more strongly persuade him of the opposite, it is usually called a **probable conscience** <note>original: *conscientia probabilis*</note>. By this, the greatest part of mortals is guided; for indeed, it is granted to only a few to know the causes of things.

->§. 6.<-

Nevertheless, it not infrequently happens to some—especially regarding specific individual cases—that reasons present themselves from both sides, and they do not possess such strong **judgment** <note>original: *judicium*</note> that they can clearly discern which reasons carry more weight. This is what is customarily called a **doubtful conscience** <note>original: *conscientia dubia*</note>. Regarding this, the rule is as follows: as long as judgment hangs in the balance as to whether something is good or evil, the action must be suspended. For if one sets themselves to acting before the doubt has been resolved...

...to settle the judgment, it implies a purpose of sinning, or at least a neglect of the law.

->§. 7.<-

Often, the human intellect also takes the false for the true; in such a case, it is said to be in **error**. This error is usually called *vincible* <note>original: *vincibilis*; literally "conquerable," referring to a mistake that could have been avoided with proper effort.</note> when a person could have avoided falling into it by applying due attention and diligence. It is called *invincible* <note>original: *invincibilis*; an unavoidable mistake.</note>, however, when someone could not avoid it even after applying all the diligence that the standard of common life requires. Among those who care to cultivate the light of reason and to lead an honorable life, such error does not usually occur regarding the general rules of living, but only regarding specific, individual affairs. For the general precepts of **natural law** <note>original: *naturalis juris praecepta*; universal moral principles discovered through reason rather than through written codes.</note> are plain; furthermore, he who enacts **positive laws** <note>original: *leges positivas*; specific laws established by a government or ruler.</note> usually can and should ensure that they are made known to his subjects. Therefore, in these matters, error does not arise except through "supine" <note>Extreme or lazy negligence.</note> negligence. But in individual business matters, it is easy for error to creep in against one's will and without fault, concerning the object or other circumstances of the action.

->§. 8.<-

Where knowledge is simply absent, it is called **ignorance** <note>original: *ignorantia*</note>. This is considered in two ways: first, as it relates to an action; second, according to whether it arose against one's will or through some fault. Regarding the first aspect, ignorance is usually divided into *efficacious* <note>original: *efficacem*; ignorance that is the direct cause of the action.</note> and *concomitant* <note>original: *concomitantem*; ignorance that exists alongside an action but did not necessarily cause it.</note>. **Efficacious ignorance** is that by which...

->CHAPTER I<-

...if it had been absent, the present action would not have been undertaken. This other kind is that which, even if it had been absent, the action would nevertheless have been undertaken. From a later perspective, ignorance is either **voluntary** or **involuntary**. The former is that which someone either deliberately sought out by rejecting the means of learning the truth, or allowed to creep in by failing to apply the required diligence. The latter occurs when someone is ignorant of things they neither could know nor were required to know. And this is again **twofold**. Either someone cannot currently shed their ignorance, yet they are at fault for being in such a state <note>This refers to "vincible" ignorance, where the person is responsible for their lack of knowledge because of past laziness or neglect.</note>; or they not only cannot overcome the ignorance for the time being, but are also not at fault for having fallen into such a condition.

->§. 9.<-

The other faculty, which is observed in humans specifically above the level of the beasts, is called the **will**; it is through this that a person moves themselves toward action as if by an internal impulse, choosing what is most pleasing to them and turning away from what seems unsuitable. From the will, therefore, a human derives the capacity to act **voluntarily** <note>original: *sponte*; acting of one's own accord rather than by reflex or instinct.</note>—that is, not being determined by some internal necessity to act, but being the author of their own action—and also to act **freely**—that is, so that when a single object is proposed, they can choose to act or not act, and to select or reject it; or, when several objects are proposed, they can choose one and reject the rest. Furthermore, since some human actions are undertaken for their own sake...

### BOOK I.

...certain [objects], insofar as they serve to achieve something else—that is, some have the character of an *end*, while others have the character of *means* <note>In this philosophical context, an "end" (finis) is the ultimate goal or purpose, while "means" (media) are the tools, steps, or instruments used to reach that goal.</note>. The will is occupied with the end in such a way that, once the end is recognized, it first approves it, then effectively moves itself to achieve it, and as it were, tends toward it, more or less intensely; once that end is attained, the will rests and peacefully enjoys it. The means, however, are first appraised, then those which seem most suitable are chosen, and finally they are put to use.

§. 10. Just as a human is considered the author of their actions primarily for this reason—because they undertook them voluntarily—it must especially be observed regarding the will that its spontaneity <note>original: *spontaneitas*; the quality of acting from one’s own internal impulse rather than being forced by an external power.</note> must certainly be asserted, at least concerning those actions for which a person is usually held accountable in a human court <note>original: *in foro humano*; refers to the legal and social sphere of responsibility where humans judge one another, as opposed to a divine or internal forum.</note>. Where, however, no spontaneity at all is left to the human, then the person themselves is not considered the author of the action, but rather whoever imposes the necessity upon them, to which the unwilling person accommodates their limbs and strength.

§. 11. Furthermore, although the will always seeks "the good" in general and turns away from "evil" in general, a great variety of desires and actions is nonetheless seen among individual humans. This arises from the fact that not all goods and evils appear to a person in their pure form, but are instead mixed together—good things with bad, and bad things with good. And

### CHAPTER I.

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: fori</meta>

...because different objects uniquely affect different aspects of a human being. For example, some affect the self-esteem <note>original: *aestimationem*; the value or regard one holds for oneself</note> a person has, some affect the external senses, and some affect self-love <note>original: *amorem sui*</note>, through which one strives to preserve oneself. From this, it follows that a person perceives certain things as **becoming** or **honorable** <note>original: *decora*</note>, others as **pleasant** <note>original: *jucunda*</note>, and still others as **useful** <note>original: *utilia*</note>. And just as each of these makes a strong impression on a person, it likewise causes that person to lean or incline toward it specifically. Added to this, in most people, is a particular inclination <note>original: *inclinatio*; a natural leaning or tendency</note> toward specific things, or an aversion to them. Hence it happens that regarding almost any action, there appear simultaneously various forms of good and evil—some real, some merely apparent <note>original: *species bonorum & malorum, verorum aut adparentium*</note>. Some people possess a greater discernment <note>original: *perspicacia*; the ability to see things clearly or "through" appearances</note> to distinguish between these than others. Thus, it is not surprising that one person is drawn toward something that another finds utterly repulsive.

§. 12. Yet the human will is not always found in a state of perfect balance <note>original: *aequilibrium*</note> regarding every action, as if an inclination toward one side or the other arose solely from its own internal impulse after everything had been maturely weighed. Instead, a person is very frequently propelled toward one side rather than the other by external "weights" <note>original: *momenta*; factors or influences that tip the scales of decision</note>. For even if we set aside that common human tendency toward wrongdoing <note>original: *prava proclivitatem*; a leaning toward what is crooked or morally wrong</note>, the origin and nature of which [belongs to] another... <note>The text cuts off here as it prepares to move to the next page or topic, likely referring to theology or a different branch of philosophy.</note>

# BOOK I.

...action, it is the task of the legal sphere <note>original: *fori*; referring here to the "human court" or the jurisdiction of human law as opposed to the internal conscience</note> to examine it. First, a **peculiar disposition of character** <note>original: *peculiaris ingenij dispositio*</note> grants the will a certain singular inclination, by which some people are made very prone toward a certain type of action. This is observed not only in individual men, but even in entire nations. This disposition seems to be produced by the character of the climate and the sun surrounding us, and the balance of the "humors" <term>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—which ancient and medieval medicine believed determined a person’s health and temperament</term> in our bodies. These humors result from one's lineage, age, diet, health, the nature of one's studies, and similar causes. Likewise, it is influenced by the physical structure of the organs which the mind uses to perform its functions, and other similar factors.

Regarding this, it must be noted that—besides the fact that a person can significantly blunt and alter their temperament by taking care—however much force is attributed to this natural disposition, it must not be judged to be so powerful that it necessarily drives a person to violate **natural law** <note>original: *legis naturalis*; the universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature</note>. This is especially true as far as natural law is practiced in the human court; in that setting, wicked desires are not punished as long as they remain internal and do not result in an outward act. Therefore, even if "nature returns with a vengeance" <note>original: *natura recurrat, furcâ licet expulsâ*; a reference to the Roman poet Horace’s famous line: "You may drive out nature with a pitchfork, yet she will always return."</note>, it can still be prevented from producing external, vicious actions. The difficulty encountered in overcoming such an inclination is compensated for by the splendor of the praise that awaits the victor here. However, if the mind is shaken by such impulses that no reason can restrain them from breaking out;

# CHAPTER I.

...there is a way, however, that they <note>referring to natural inclinations or desires mentioned on the previous page</note> can be satisfied, as it were, without sin <note>original: *citra peccatum*; meaning without violating moral or divine law</note>.

§. 13. The frequent repetition of actions of the same kind also strongly inclines the will toward certain actions; the resulting inclination is called ***habit*** <note>original: *consuetudo*; in this context, it refers to a settled or regular tendency or practice that becomes difficult to give up</note>. Indeed, through this habit, it comes about that an action is undertaken willingly and readily, so that when the object of that action is present, the mind seems to be drawn toward it, or when it is absent, the mind desires it most vehemently. Regarding this, the following must be observed: just as there seems to be no habit that a person cannot cast off again by applying diligence, so too no habit is powerful enough to twist the mind so far that a person is not capable of restraining at least the external acts toward which they are rushed, here and now. And because it was within a person's power to contract such a habit in the first place, it follows that even if the habit makes actions easier to perform, the value of good actions is not diminished by it, nor is the malice of wicked ones made lighter. Rather, just as a good habit increases a person's praise, so a wicked one increases their shame.

§. 14. It also matters a great deal whether a peaceful tranquility <note>original: *placida tranquillitas*; a state of mental calm often associated with Stoic philosophy</note> remains in the mind, or whether the mind is shaken by certain movements called ***emotions*** <note>original: *affectus*; often translated as "passions" or "affects," referring to strong feelings like anger, fear, or lust that can cloud judgment</note>. Regarding these, it must be held that however violent they may be, a person can be superior to them through the proper use of reason, and their force can be kept at least within the final...

<meta>catchword: mum (part of the word "ultimum," meaning "final" or "outermost")</meta>

<summary>This page discusses how the repetition of actions creates habits and how emotions influence the human will, arguing that individuals remain morally responsible because reason can still govern external behavior.</summary>
<keywords>habit, will, emotions, reason, moral responsibility, virtue, vice, tranquility, self-control</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the influence of internal movements on human behavior. It completes a sentence from the previous page regarding the "emotions" (affectus) and how they might be restrained before they lead to an external action.</meta>

->### BOOK I.<-

...to halt the first impulse of the act <note>original: *mum actum sistere*; likely completing "emotionum actum sistere," meaning to stop the motion or impulse of the emotions</note>. Furthermore, since some emotions are stirred by the appearance of good, and others by the appearance of evil, and they urge us either to acquire something pleasing or to avoid something troublesome: it is consistent with human nature that mortals show more favor or leniency toward the latter than the former—all the more so if the evil that triggered them was hostile and intolerable. For it is considered far more tolerable to go without a good thing that is not essential for preserving one's nature than to suffer an evil that leads toward the destruction of that nature.

§. 15. Finally, just as there are certain diseases <note>original: *morbi*</note> that completely remove the use of reason <note>original: *ratio*</note> either permanently or for a time, it is also common among many nations for people to voluntarily bring upon themselves a kind of short-lived disease that significantly disturbs the use of reason. By this we mean ***drunkenness*** <note>original: *ebrietatem*</note>, arising from certain types of drinks or even certain vapors. This state drives and disturbs the blood and the spirits <note>referring to "animal spirits," which early modern thinkers believed were the physical fluids or vapors that transmitted messages between the mind and the body</note> with violent motion, and makes people prone to lust especially, as well as anger, recklessness, and excessive joy. Thus, many people seem as if they are carried outside themselves through drunkenness, having put on a completely different character than the one they were seen to have when sober. Yet, just as this state does not always strip away all use of reason, because it was voluntarily sought out, it is more likely to bring hatred rather than favor upon the actions committed while under its influence.

§. 16.

->### CHAPTER I.<-

§. 16. Furthermore, human actions are usually called ***voluntary*** <note>original: *voluntaria*</note> insofar as they proceed from and are directed by the will. Conversely, if actions are undertaken knowingly but while the will is resisting, they are called ***involuntary*** <note>original: *invitæ*</note>, taking this term in its narrower sense. For when the term is used more broadly, it also includes those actions committed through ***ignorance***. In this context, however, "involuntary" signifies the same thing to us as ***compelled*** <note>original: *coactum*</note>; namely, when someone is forced by a stronger external force to employ their limbs in such a way that they signal their aversion and disagreement through signs, and especially through the physical resistance of the body. 

In a less proper sense, actions are also called involuntary when, under the pressure of a grave necessity, something is chosen and undertaken as the lesser evil—an act from which the person would otherwise have vehemently recoiled if they were not placed in that state of necessity. Such actions are commonly called ***mixed*** <note>original: *mixtas*; a classic philosophical category, such as throwing cargo overboard during a storm to save a ship</note>. These share a commonality with spontaneous actions in that the will, given the current circumstances, certainly chooses them as the lesser evil. However, they align to some extent with involuntary actions regarding their effect, because they are usually either not <term>imputed<gloss>To 'impute' an action means to legally or morally assign the responsibility for that act to the person who performed it.</gloss></term> to the agent at all, or at least not as severely as spontaneous actions are.

§. 17. Furthermore, regarding human actions that proceed from and are directed by the intellect and the will, this is their primary characteristic: that they can be ***imputed*** to a person—that is, that a person can rightly be regarded as the author of those actions, and [held] to render... <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "dam" indicates the next page begins with the completion of "reddendam," likely "reddendam rationem," meaning "to render an account."</note>

<summary>This page defines the categories of human actions—voluntary, involuntary (compelled), and "mixed"—based on the level of consent from the will. It introduces the fundamental principle of "imputation," or the legal and moral assignment of responsibility to an individual.</summary>

<keywords>voluntary actions, involuntary actions, compulsion, mixed actions, necessity, imputation, moral responsibility, human agency</keywords>

->## BOOK I.<-

...[is] bound to give an account of the same; and that the effects which arise from them redound upon that person. For there is no closer reason why any action can be <term>imputed<gloss>From the Latin 'imputari'; to legally or morally assign the responsibility for an act to the person who performed it.</gloss></term> to someone than that it proceeded from them knowingly and willingly, whether directly or indirectly; or because it was within their power that it should happen or not happen. 

Hence, it is held as a primary axiom in the <term>moral disciplines<gloss>The study of ethics, law, and human conduct.</gloss></term> concerning the <term>human forum<gloss>The arena of human judgment and legal courts, as distinguished from the 'divine forum' or God's judgment.</gloss></term>; that a person can be called to account for those actions which it is within their power to either perform or not perform. Or, what amounts to the same thing: that any action capable of human direction, which is within someone's power to do or not do, can be imputed to them. Thus, conversely, no one can be considered the author of an action which was not within their power, neither in itself nor in its cause.

§. 18. From these premises, we shall form several specific propositions, from which it may be clear what can be imputed to each person, or of which action and outcome each can be considered the author. 

I. Actions committed by another, as well as the operations of any other things, and even any events, cannot be imputed to someone else, except insofar as that person is able and bound to regulate them. Indeed, nothing is more common among men than for one person to be entrusted with directing the actions of another <note>This refers to relationships like parents and children, or masters and servants</note>. Here, therefore, if some action is committed by the other person, regarding which the director neglected what was within their power to do; that action will be imputed...

<summary>This page establishes the foundational rule of moral accountability: a person is only responsible for actions that they knowingly choose and have the power to perform or prevent. It also introduces the concept of 'vicarious responsibility,' where a superior may be held accountable for the actions of a subordinate if they failed to properly direct them.</summary>
