->### CHAPTER I<-

...[it will be imputed] not only to the one who committed the act directly, but also to that person who, despite having the duty and the possibility of providing direction, omitted some necessary action. This matter, however, generally has its own limits and boundaries, so that the term *possible* must be understood with a certain tempering and in a moral sense. For although one person may be subject to another, the <term>liberty<gloss>From the Latin 'libertas'; the inherent freedom of the human will.</gloss></term> of the subject is not extinguished; they are still able to resist the direction of the superior and move in a different direction. Nor does the nature of human life allow for a person to be perpetually attached to another, as if fastened to them, in a way that allows them to observe every single movement.

Therefore, if a person has done everything that the nature of the supervision assigned to them required, yet something was nevertheless committed by the other, the act shall be imputed to the perpetrator alone. Similarly, since humans exercise <term>dominion<gloss>From the Latin 'dominium'; legal or rightful power and control over property or creatures.</gloss></term> over animals, whatever damage is caused by those animals to another person's detriment will be imputed to the owner, provided that the owner neglected something regarding their required <term>custody<gloss>The duty of care or 'custodia' in protecting others from one's property.</gloss></term> and <term>circumspection<gloss>Watchful caution or vigilance.</gloss></term>. 

Likewise, any evils that happen to another can be imputed to the person who did not remove the cause or opportunity for those evils when they were able and obligated to do so. Furthermore, since it is within the power of humans to promote or suspend the operations of many natural things, whatever benefit or harm is caused by these things will be imputed to those humans, according to how much their work or neglect contributed to the final outcome. Sometimes also, outside the usual order, such events are imputed to someone which otherwise—

# BOOK I.

**§. 19. II.** Whatever qualities exist or do not exist in any person, which it was not in their power to have or not have, cannot be <term>imputed<gloss>From 'imputatio'; the process of assigning moral or legal responsibility to an individual.</gloss></term> to them—except to the extent that they failed to supply a natural defect through diligence, or failed to assist their native strengths. Thus, since no one can provide themselves with sharp wits or physical strength, no one can be held responsible on those grounds, except regarding how much <term>cultivation<gloss>Latin: 'cultura'; the training, education, or refinement of one's natural abilities.</gloss></term> they applied or neglected to apply. 

Thus, rough manners are counted as a fault not in a rustic peasant, but in a city-dweller or a courtier. From this, we must judge as highly absurd any reproaches for qualities whose cause was not in our power; for example: short stature, an unattractive appearance, and the like.

**§. 20. III.** Those things done through <term>invincible ignorance<gloss>Latin: 'ignorantia invincibilis'; a lack of knowledge that a person could not have overcome through reasonable effort.</gloss></term> cannot be imputed. Because we cannot direct an action where the light of the intellect does not shine before us; and it is presupposed that the person was neither able to acquire such light, nor at fault for being unable to do so. Indeed, even in common life, <note>original: "τὸ posse"; the Greek article 'the' with the Latin verb 'to be able', meaning 'the capacity' or 'the power'</note> **the capacity** to act is understood in a moral sense to mean that faculty, skill, and vigilance...

# CHAPTER I.

**§. 21. IV.** Ignorance, as well as error, regarding the laws and the **duty** <gloss>Latin: 'officium'; the moral or legal obligation assigned to an individual's role.</gloss> enjoined upon anyone does not free them from **imputation** <gloss>Latin: 'imputatio'; the legal or moral act of holding someone responsible for their deeds.</gloss>. For he who enjoins laws and duties upon another is usually able and obliged to ensure that these reach the notice of the subject. Furthermore, the laws and rules of duty are usually—and should be—adapted to the subject's capacity; everyone ought to be solicitous in learning and retaining them. Hence, he who is the cause of their own ignorance will also be held bound to answer for those actions which flow from that ignorance. <note>This principle asserts that "ignorance of the law is no excuse," provided the law was properly publicized and understandable.</note>

**§. 22. V.** To one who lacks the **opportunity** <gloss>Latin: 'occasio'; the set of external circumstances required to perform an action.</gloss> for acting through no fault of their own, what they have not done is not imputed. Now, opportunity is seen to embrace these four things:
1. That the object of the action be ready at hand.
2. That a convenient place be present, where we cannot be hindered by others or punished with some evil.
3. That an opportune time be present, in which more necessary business must not be performed, and which is likewise opportune for others who concur in the action.
4. That, finally, the natural powers for acting be available.

For since an action cannot occur without these things, it would even be absurd if something were imputed to someone for which the opportunity to act was lacking. Thus, a **physician** <gloss>Latin: 'medicus'.</gloss> cannot be accused of laziness if no one is sick. Thus, it is not granted to be **generous** <gloss>Latin: 'liberalis'.</gloss> to one who is himself in need. Thus, one cannot be accused of having **buried his talent** <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is punished for hiding his money rather than investing it.</note> who, although seeking a position properly—

**§. 23. VI.** It also cannot be **imputed** <gloss>Latin: 'imputari'; the legal or moral act of holding someone responsible for their actions.</gloss> to anyone that they did not perform those things which exceed their own strength, nor those things which can neither be prevented nor accomplished by their power. Hence it is commonly said that **there is no obligation toward the impossible** <gloss>Latin: 'impossibilium nulla esse obligatio'.</gloss>. It must be added, however: provided that the person has not, through their own fault, worn down or lost the strength to perform. For in such a case, he can be treated exactly as if he still possessed that strength; otherwise, there would be an easy way to evade any somewhat laborious obligation by voluntarily ruining one's own power to act. <note>This principle prevents people from intentionally incapacitating themselves (e.g., getting drunk or injuring themselves) to escape a duty.</note>

**§. 24. VII.** Furthermore, those things which a person suffers or does **under compulsion** <gloss>Latin: 'coacte'; forced or coerced against one's will.</gloss> cannot be imputed. For it is understood that to cast off or escape such things is placed beyond one's own strength. Now, we are said to be "compelled" in two ways. First, when someone stronger applies our limbs through **violence** <gloss>Latin: 'violentiam'; physical force.</gloss> to do or suffer something. Second, if a more powerful person threatens some grave evil close at hand—and has the immediate ability to carry it out—unless we voluntarily move ourselves to do something, or abstain from doing it. In that case, unless we are expressly bound to redeem at the cost of our own misfortune that which is being inflicted upon another, the one who imposes that necessity upon us shall be considered the author of the **deed** <gloss>Latin: 'facinoris'; refers to the act or crime committed under duress.</gloss>...

### CHAPTER I.

...be held <note>The text begins mid-word, completing a thought from the previous page regarding those forced into action by others.</note> [accountable]; to us, however, that deed can no more be **imputed** than a slaughter can be attributed to a sword or an axe. <note>This is a classic legal argument: a person under absolute physical duress is merely a "tool" of the one applying the force, and therefore holds no moral or legal responsibility for the outcome.</note>

**§. 25. VIII.** The actions of those who lack the **use of reason** <gloss>Latin: 'rationis usu'; the cognitive ability to distinguish right from wrong.</gloss> are not imputed. This is because they are unable to clearly distinguish what is being done, or to compare their actions to a standard. To this category belong the actions of **infants** <gloss>Latin: 'infantum'; in a legal sense, children before they reach the "age of reason."</gloss>, before the use of reason begins to exert itself a bit more distinctly. For if they are rebuked or beaten because of certain actions, this is not done because they have deserved a punishment properly so-called in a **human court** <note>The "human court" (forum humano) refers to civil and criminal legal systems, as opposed to the "divine court" or one's own conscience.</note>, but by way of simple correction and discipline; this is so they do not cause trouble to others through such things, or so they do not contract a wicked habit. Likewise, the actions of the **insane**, the **mentally deranged**, and the **delirious** are not considered "human" actions <note>In this context, a "human action" (actio humana) is a technical term for an act performed with intent and free will. Acts done without these are "actions of a human" but not "human actions."</note>, provided that this illness came upon them through no fault of their own.

**§. 26. IX.** Finally, those things which someone seems to do in **dreams** cannot be imputed; except insofar as, having lingered with delight on such thoughts during the day, he has impressed their images deeply on his mind: although even these are very rarely considered in a human court. For otherwise, the **imagination** <gloss>Latin: 'phantasia'; the mental faculty that produces images while sleeping.</gloss> in sleep is like a boat floating without a pilot, such that it is not within a person's power to control what kind of visions it produces.

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

...it may sometimes happen that an action is not **imputed** <gloss>Latin: 'imputetur'; held to one's moral or legal account.</gloss> at all to the one who directly performed it, but rather to another who used that person merely as an instrument. However, it is more common that the action is imputed simultaneously to both the one who performed it and the other who contributed to it, whether by doing something or by failing to act. <note>The author is categorizing degrees of complicity in an act, distinguishing between the person who physically does the deed (the "perpetrator") and those who influence or assist them.</note>

This occurs primarily in three ways: either the second party is considered the **principal cause** <gloss>Latin: 'principalis causa'; the primary driver or mastermind.</gloss> of the action and the **perpetrator** <gloss>Latin: 'patrator'; the one who physically carries out the deed.</gloss> is considered the secondary cause; or both proceed, as it were, with an equal step; or the second party is the secondary cause and the perpetrator is the principal cause.

To the **first class** belong those who, through their authority, influenced another to do something; those who provided the required consent without which the other could not have acted; and those who could and should have prohibited the act, yet failed to do so.

To the **second class** belong those who command or hire someone to commit a crime; those who provide assistance; those who offer harbor and defense; and those who could and should have brought aid to the victim of the injury, but did not.

To the **third class** are referred those who give specific advice; those who praise and encourage before the crime is committed; those who, by their example, incite others to sin; and similar cases.

## CHAPTER II.
# ON THE RULE OF HUMAN ACTIONS,
### or on Law in General.

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II.</header>

B 2

->§. I.<-

Because human actions depend upon the **will** <gloss>Latin: 'voluntate'</gloss>, and the wills of individuals are not always similar to one another, but different people usually tend toward different things; therefore, to establish order and **decorum** <note>original: 'decorem'; meaning social harmony or the fitness of things.</note> in the human race, it was necessary for some **standard** <gloss>Latin: 'normam'</gloss> to exist, according to which those wills might be regulated. Otherwise, if in such great freedom of will, and such great diversity of inclinations and pursuits, everyone acted—without reflection toward a certain standard—whatever came into his mind, the greatest confusion could not fail to exist in the human race.

§. 2. That standard is called ***Law*** <gloss>Latin: 'Lex'</gloss>, which is a decree by which a **superior** <note>In this legal framework, a 'superior' is anyone with the authority to command others, whether by divine or civil right.</note> obligates his subject to arrange his actions according to that superior's prescription.

§. 3. So that this definition may be better understood, we must unfold what **obligation** <gloss>Latin: 'obligatio'</gloss> is; from where it arises; who can receive an obligation; and who can impose the same on another. Commonly, therefore, an **obligation** is called a **bond of law** <gloss>Latin: 'vinculum juris'</gloss>, by which we are bound by necessity to perform something. Specifically, through it, a kind of **bridle** <note>original: 'frænum'; the author uses the metaphor of a horse's bridle to describe how law restrains and directs human liberty.</note> is cast upon our liberty, so that although the will may in fact tend in a different direction, it nevertheless finds itself imbued with an internal sense, as it were, so that it is forced to acknowledge that it has not acted rightly unless the performed action conforms to the prescribed standard. And so—

<meta>catchword: and so;</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter II, defining Law as a necessary standard to prevent social chaos and explaining the nature of legal obligation as an internal restraint on human freedom.</summary>
<keywords>Law, obligation, human will, bond of law, superior and subject, social order, vinculum juris, lex</keywords>

BOOK I.
20

...theless, and therefore, if any evil should happen to a person on that account, he should judge that it happened to him not undeservedly; since, if he had followed the **standard** <gloss>Latin: 'normam'</gloss> as was right, it would have been possible to avoid it.

§. 4. *Moreover, there is a twofold reason why man is capable of receiving an **obligation** <gloss>Latin: 'obligationem'</gloss>:* one indeed, because he has a **will** <gloss>Latin: 'voluntatem'</gloss>, which can turn itself in different directions, and thus can also adapt itself to a standard; the other, because man is not immune from the power of a **superior** <note>original: 'superioris'; in this context, a superior is any authority—divine or human—with the right to command.</note>. For where the powers of any agent are bound by nature to a uniform way of acting, there a **free action** <gloss>Latin: 'libera actio'</gloss> is expected in vain: and a standard is prescribed to such a one to no purpose, since he can neither understand it nor adapt himself to it. If, however, anyone does not acknowledge a superior, by that very fact no one exists who can rightfully impose necessity upon him. And even if he should observe a certain method in acting, and constantly abstain from certain actions, he is understood to do this not from any obligation, but from his own **pleasure** <gloss>Latin: 'beneplacito'</gloss>. It follows, therefore, that he is **capable of obligation** <gloss>Latin: 'capax obligationis'</gloss> who both has a superior and can know the prescribed standard, and has a will flexible in different directions; which, nevertheless, when a standard has been prescribed by a superior, feels that it does not rightly depart from it. It is evident that man is endowed with such a nature.

§. 5. *Obligation is introduced into the mind of man—*

## CHAPTER II.

21
B 3
<meta>catchword: actu</meta>

...obligation properly comes from a **superior** <note>original: 'superiore'; in this framework, a superior is anyone with the justified right to limit another's actions.</note>, that is, someone who not only has the power to inflict some evil upon those who resist them, but who also has just causes for demanding that the freedom of our **will** <gloss>Latin: 'voluntatis'</gloss> be limited according to their own **discretion** <gloss>Latin: 'arbitrio'</gloss>. For when these qualities exist in someone, once they have signaled what they desire, it is inevitable that a fear tempered by reverence arises in the human mind. The fear arises from their power, while the reverence comes from considering the reasons which, even if fear were removed, ought to persuade someone to embrace that person's will.

For a person who can offer no reason why they wish to impose an **obligation** <gloss>Latin: 'obligationem'</gloss> on me against my will, other than sheer **force** <gloss>Latin: 'vires'</gloss>, might indeed terrify me; I may judge it better to obey them for a time to avoid a greater evil by escaping their reach. But once that fear is removed, nothing further prevents me from acting according to my own choice rather than theirs. Conversely, if someone has reasons why I ought to obey them, but lacks the **power** <gloss>Latin: 'viribus'</gloss> to impose any evil upon me, I can neglect their orders with impunity—unless someone more powerful steps in to assert that their trampled authority must be upheld.

Now, the reasons why someone may rightly demand to be obeyed by another are as follows: if the subject has received significant benefits from them; if it is clear that the superior wishes the subject well and can provide for them more effectively than the subject can for themselves; and finally, if...

<note>The text here lists the three traditional pillars of legitimate authority: gratitude for benefits, the superior's goodwill, and their superior wisdom or foresight.</note>

<summary>This page defines a 'superior' as an authority who possesses both the power to punish and a justified right to command. It distinguishes between mere coercion, which produces only temporary fear, and legitimate authority, which inspires a lasting sense of duty through reverence and reason.</summary>

## BOOK I.

<meta>catchword: recte</meta>

...actually claims for himself the direction of this person; and finally, if someone has voluntarily subjected themselves to another and consented to their direction.

§ 6. Furthermore, in order for a **law** <term>lex: a rule of action prescribed by an authority</term> to exert its power upon the minds of those for whom it is enacted, a *knowledge of both the legislator and the law itself* is required. For no one will be able to offer obedience if they know neither whom they ought to obey nor to what they are bound. And indeed, the knowledge of the **legislator** <gloss>lawgiver</gloss> is very easy to attain. For it is established from the **light of reason** <note>A common Enlightenment phrase referring to the human capacity for logic and moral intuition without needing divine revelation.</note> that the author of the natural laws is the same as the author of the universe. Nor can a citizen be ignorant of who holds **sovereign power** <gloss>Latin: 'imperium'</gloss> over them.

Furthermore, how natural laws become known will be explained shortly. **Civil laws** <note>Laws created by human governments, as opposed to natural laws inherent in human nature.</note>, however, come to the knowledge of subjects through a **promulgation** <note>original: 'promulgationem'; the formal process of making a law known to the public</note> that is performed publicly and clearly. In this process, two things must be certain: first, that the law has as its author the one who holds the **supreme power in the state** <gloss>Latin: 'summum in civitate imperium'</gloss>; and second, what the actual meaning of the law is.

The first point is known if the ruler either proclaims the law with their own voice, or signs it in their own hand, or if it is done through their delegates. The authority of these delegates is questioned in vain if it is clear that this task is connected with the office they hold in the **state** <gloss>Latin: 'civitate'</gloss> and that they are regularly employed for this purpose; if those laws are brought into use in the courts; and if they contain nothing that detracts from the sovereign power. As for the meaning of the law...

<summary>This page establishes that for a law to be binding, the subject must clearly know who the lawgiver is and what the law requires. It distinguishes between the author of natural law (God/the Creator) and the author of civil law (the sovereign), noting that the latter must publicly announce laws through official channels.</summary>

<keywords>sovereignty, promulgation, civil law, natural law, knowledge of the law, state authority, legal obligation</keywords>

### CHAPTER II.

23

...[so that it] may be correctly understood, it is the duty of those promulgating it to use as much clarity as possible. If any obscurity should occur in the laws, its clarification must be sought from the **legislator** <gloss>the lawgiver</gloss>, or from those who have been publicly appointed to administer the **law** <term>jus: here referring to the system of justice or legal rulings</term> according to the statutes.

§ 7. There are two parts to every **perfect law** <term>lex perfecta: a legal rule that is considered complete because it includes both a directive and a penalty for non-compliance</term>: one, by which it is defined what is to be done or what is to be omitted; the other, by which it is indicated what evil is proposed for the person who neglects the precept or commits the forbidden act. For just as—due to the depravity of the human spirit, which loves to incline toward the forbidden <note>original: 'in vetita tendere amantis'; a likely allusion to the Roman poet Ovid's observation that humans naturally desire what is prohibited</note>—it is useless to say, *do this*, if no evil will await the one who does not do it; so it is absurd to say, *you shall be punished*, unless a cause that deserves punishment has preceded it. 

Thus, the whole force of the law consists in signifying what the superior wants us to do or not do, and what penalty has been established for violators of the law. Moreover, the **power of obliging** <gloss>Latin: 'vis obligandi'</gloss>, that is, of bringing an intrinsic necessity <note>The internal moral or rational feeling that one must obey a rule.</note>, and the **power of coercing** <gloss>Latin: 'vis cogendi'</gloss>, or driving one toward the observation of the laws through penalties, properly resides in the legislator and in the person to whom the custody and execution of the laws has been entrusted.

§ 8. That which is enjoined upon anyone through laws must not only be within the power of the person for whom the laws are made, but must also bring some utility either to that person themselves or to others. For just as wanting to demand something from someone under the threat of punishment which is...

§ 9. Although a law regularly encompasses all the subjects of a legislator to whom the subject matter of the law applies, and whom the legislator did not intend to be exempt from the start: nevertheless, it sometimes happens that someone is specifically released from the obligation of the law. This is what they call **dispensing** <gloss>original: *dispensare*</gloss>. Just as, however, only he who possesses the power of making and repealing laws can grant a dispensation: so one must take care lest, by granting dispensations indiscriminately and without the most serious reasons, the authority of the laws be undermined, and an occasion for envy and indignation <note>The author suggests that if laws are applied inconsistently without good reason, it creates social unrest and resentment among those who are still forced to obey.</note> be provided among the subjects.

§ 10. Nevertheless, **equity** <gloss>original: *equitas*</gloss> differs greatly from dispensation; it is the correction of that in which the law is deficient because of its universality; or a proper interpretation of the law, by which it is shown from **natural reason** <term>naturalis ratio: the human capacity to understand moral truths through logic and common sense, independent of specific written statutes</term> that some specific case is not included under a universal law, because otherwise something absurd would result from it. For because all cases can neither be foreseen nor expressed due to their infinite variety; therefore the **judges** <gloss>Latin: *judices*</gloss>, whose role it is to apply the general decrees of the laws to specific cases, ought to except from the law those cases which the **legislator** <gloss>the lawgiver</gloss> himself would have excepted, if...

...[the legislator] would have been present, or had foreseen such cases.

§ 11. Furthermore, from their relationship and conformity to the **moral norm** <term>norma moralis: the standard or rule, such as a law, against which human behavior is measured to determine if it is right or wrong</term>, human actions receive certain qualities and names. Indeed, those actions about which the law settles nothing in either direction are called *licit or permitted*. Although sometimes in civil life—where not everything can be cut to the quick <note>The author uses a metaphor suggesting that civil laws cannot perfectly regulate every tiny detail of human conduct without being overly intrusive or impossible to enforce.</note>—those things are also called "licit" for which no punishment has been established in a human court, even if they are in themselves contrary to natural **decency** <gloss>original: *honestati*</gloss>. Furthermore, those actions that agree with the law are called *good*, while those that differ from it are called *bad*. But for an action to be good, it is necessary that it agree with the law in every respect; for it to be bad, it is enough if it fails in only one respect <note>This reflects a classic principle of moral philosophy: for something to be truly good, the intent, the action, and the circumstances must all be right; but a single flaw in any of these makes the action "bad."</note>.

§ 12. **Justice** <gloss>original: *Justitia*</gloss>, however, is sometimes an attribute of *actions*, and sometimes of *persons*. When justice is attributed to a *person*, it is commonly defined as the constant and perpetual will to give to each their own <note>This is a famous definition from Roman law (specifically the jurist Ulpian). It suggests that justice isn't just a single act, but a consistent character trait.</note>. Indeed, a man is called "just" who delights in doing just things, who applies himself to justice, or who endeavors in every matter to do that which is just. An unjust man, on the contrary, is one who neglects to give each his own, or thinks that it should be measured not by his duty, but by his immediate advantage. Thus, it is possible for there to be not a few unjust actions of a just man, and vice versa. For the just man does just things because of the command of the law, and unjust things only through...

->BOOK I.<-

->26<-

...weakness; whereas an unjust person does just things only because of the punishment attached to the law, and unjust things out of the malice of their soul <note>The author distinguishes between a good person who might fail out of human frailty and a malicious person who only follows the law to avoid being caught.</note>.

§ 13. When, however, **justice is attributed to actions**, then it is nothing other than the right application of those actions to a person. And a just action is one which is applied from **deliberate choice** <gloss>original: *proæresi*</gloss>, or by one who is acting knowingly and willingly, to the person to whom it is owed. Thus, the justice of actions differs from goodness in this most especially: that while goodness simply denotes agreement with the law, justice additionally involves a relationship to those toward whom the action is performed. For this reason, justice is also called a "virtue toward others."

§ 14. Not everyone organizes the division of justice in the same way. The most widely accepted is the division into **universal and particular**. The former [universal] is spoken of whenever any duty is performed toward others, even those that could not be demanded by force or by initiating a legal action in court <note>This refers to "imperfect duties," like charity or gratitude, which are morally right but often not legally enforceable.</note>. The latter [particular], however, is when those things are provided to someone which they could demand by their own right. This, in turn, is usually divided into **distributive and commutative**. The former [distributive] rests on an agreement entered into between a society and its members concerning the sharing of loss and profit in proportion <term>pro rata: according to a calculated share or proportion based on one’s contribution or status</term>. The latter [commutative], however, rests on a bilateral contract <note>A contract where both parties have mutual obligations to one another, such as a buyer and a seller.</note>, especially concerning things and actions that enter into commerce.

<meta>catchword: §. 15.</meta>

### CHAPTER II. 27

§. 15. Once it is understood what justice is, it is easy to conclude what injustice is as well. Here it should be observed that an unjust action—one undertaken with **deliberate intent** and through which a **perfect right** <note>A "perfect right" refers to a claim that is legally enforceable, such as a debt or property ownership, as opposed to a moral "imperfect right" like a plea for charity.</note> owed to another is violated, or something they possessed by such a right (regardless of how it was acquired) is infringed upon—is properly called ***injury*** <note>original: *injuria*</note>. This occurs in three ways: 
1. If someone is denied what they could demand as their own right (this does not apply if the thing was owed merely out of humanity or a similar virtue); 
2. If something is taken away which they held by their own right, where that right was valid against the person taking it; 
3. Or if some evil is inflicted upon another which we had no authority to inflict. 

Furthermore, for an "injury" to truly exist, **deliberate intent** and the **malice** of the person performing the act are required. Where these are absent, the harming of another is called a ***misfortune*** <note>original: *infortunium*</note> or a ***fault*** <note>original: *culpa*</note>. This is considered more or less serious depending on how heavy or light the sluggishness and negligence were that led the person to, as it were, run into the other.

§. 19. With respect to its author, Law is divided into ***divine*** and ***human***; the former is established by God, while the latter is established by men. But if law is considered according to whether it has a necessary and universal agreement with humanity, it is divided into ***natural*** and ***positive*** <note>"Positive law" refers to laws that are "posed" or "placed" by a specific authority (like a king or a legislature), which may vary between different times and places, as opposed to universal Natural Law.</note>. Natural law is that which so agrees with the rational and social nature of man that an honorable and peaceful society could not exist among the human race without it. For this reason, it [is known] through... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

justice, injustice, injury, misfortune, fault, sluggishness, negligence, divine law, human law, natural law, positive law

## CHAPTER III.
# ON NATURAL LAW.

->§. 1.<-

What the character of natural law is, what its necessity is, and which precepts it consists of in this mortal condition, is most clearly understood if one examines the nature and disposition of man more deeply. For just as it contributes greatly to the accurate knowledge of civil laws <note>original: *legum civilium* - the laws of a specific state or city-state.</note> to have properly understood the state of the commonwealth, as well as the customs and interests of the citizens, so too, once the common disposition and state of mortals is perceived, it will be easily apparent by which laws their safety and well-being <note>original: *incolumitas*</note> are maintained.

§. 2. Therefore, man has this in common with all living creatures endowed with senses: that he holds nothing dearer than himself, and he strives [to care for] himself...

<summary>This page defines the difference between natural law (inherent in human reason) and positive law (decreed by a legislator) and begins an inquiry into how natural law is discovered by studying human nature.</summary>

to preserve himself in every way; so that he strives to acquire those things which seem good to him, and to repel those that are evil. This affection <note>original: *affectus* — here meaning a natural urge or driving sentiment.</note> is generally so great that all other instincts yield to it. And whoever goes to attack a man's safety, that man cannot help but turn away from him with such aversion that even after the threatened evil has been driven away, hatred and a desire for revenge usually still remain.

§. 3. But in this regard, man appears to be in a worse condition than the beasts, because hardly any other animal is followed from birth by such great weakness <note>original: *imbecillitas* — in this context, it refers to the physical helplessness and vulnerability of a human infant compared to a newborn animal.</note>; so much so that it would be like a miracle if anyone reached a mature age unless the help of other men were added. Since now, even among so many inventions created for human necessity, many years of careful instruction <note>original: *informatio* — the process of being "formed" or educated.</note> are required so that one might be able to provide food and clothing <note>original: *victum et amictum*</note> for himself by his own efforts <note>original: *proprio marte* — literally "by his own Mars," a classical idiom for relying on one's own strength or skill.</note>. Let us imagine a man who has reached a robust age without any care or cultivation coming from other men, who possesses no knowledge except what has sprouted, as it were, spontaneously from his own intellect, and who is abandoned in solitude <note>original: *solitudo*</note> without any help or fellowship from others. Truly, one would find hardly a more miserable animal. Speechless, of course, and naked, with nothing left to him but to pull up herbs and roots, or to gather fruits growing wild, and to quench his thirst at a spring, a river, or whatever pool he happens upon,

§. 4. Yet this animal, who is so mutually useful, suffers from not a few vices and is endowed with a no less great capacity for doing harm; these qualities make any association with him quite precarious <note>original: *ancipitem* — literally "two-headed" or "doubled-edged," meaning the relationship is uncertain or risky.</note> and require great caution, lest one receive evil from him instead of good. First of all, there is found in man a generally greater inclination toward harming than in any of the beasts <note>original: *brutorum* — referring to non-human animals, often called "brutes" in this period because they lack human reason.</note>. For animals are driven to ferocity almost exclusively by the desire for food or for mating <note>original: *Veneris* — literally "of Venus," the goddess of love, used here to mean sexual instinct.</note>, both of which they can satisfy with relatively little trouble. 

And once that desire is quieted, they are not easily stirred to anger or to harming others unless someone provokes them. But man is an animal ready for lust at any time; he is pricked by its stings far more frequently than seems necessary for the preservation of the species. §. 6. Finally, within the human race, one must consider that remarkable variety of dispositions <note>original: *ingeniorum varietas* — the diversity of character, intellect, and temperament found among people.</note>, which is not seen in individual species of brute animals; for they have almost identical inclinations and are led by nearly equal feeling and appetite <note>original: *appetitu* — natural drive or instinct.</note>. But among men, there are as many opinions as there are heads, and to each their own way of thinking is beautiful. Nor are all driven by a simple or uniform desire <note>original: *cupidine*</note>, but by a desire that is manifold and varied in its mixture. Indeed, one and the same man often appears different from himself; what he craved at one time, he shrinks from at another. There is no less variety in pursuits, customs, and inclinations toward exercising the vigor of the mind—a variety now seen in almost infinite ways of life. Lest men collide with one another because of these differences, there is a need for careful balancing <note>original: *temperatura*</note> and moderation <note>original: *moderatione*</note>.

§. 7. ### CHAPTER III.

...the condition <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: "and unable to be saved without the condition..."</note> that he be sociable; that is, that he be joined with those like himself, and conduct himself toward them in such a way that they find no justified reason to harm him, but rather wish to preserve and promote his well-being.

§. 8. Therefore, the rules of this <term>sociability</term> <note>original: *socialitas* — the fundamental duty to live peacefully and helpfully in society.</note>, or those rules which teach how one ought to behave so as to exist as a beneficial member of human society, are called ***natural laws***.

§. 9. With these points established, it is clear that ***this is the fundamental natural law:*** Every person must, as much as lies within them, cultivate and maintain sociability. From this it follows—since whoever wills an end also wills the means without which that end cannot be achieved—that all things which necessarily and universally contribute to that sociability are understood to be commanded by <term>natural right</term> <note>original: *jure naturali*</note>; conversely, those things which disturb or break that sociability are understood to be forbidden. All other precepts are merely specific applications <note>original: *subsumtiones* — the act of categorizing specific behaviors under a general rule.</note> under this general law, the clarity of which is suggested to humans by that innate <term>natural light</term> <note>original: *lumen naturale* — the inherent human capacity for reason and moral intuition.</note>.

§. 10. Furthermore, even if these precepts have an obvious usefulness, in order for them to obtain the force of law, it is necessary to presuppose that God exists and governs all things by His **providence**; and that He has commanded the human race to observe these **dictates of reason** <note>original: *rationis dictamina*</note> as laws promulgated by Him through the power of that innate light. For otherwise...

->### BOOK I.<-

...they <note>Completing the sentence from page 33: "in order for them to obtain the force of law, it is necessary that they be followed by a superior power..."</note> might perhaps be observed with a view toward their usefulness, much like the instructions prescribed by doctors for maintaining health. However, they would not be observed as **laws**; for a law necessarily assumes a superior, and specifically one who has actually undertaken the governance of another.

§. 11. That God is the author of <term>natural law</term> <note>original: *lex naturalis* — the moral framework humans can discover through reason.</note> is demonstrated by <term>natural reason</term> <note>original: *ratio naturalis* — the human capacity for logical thought without the aid of divine revelation.</note> in this way: if we focus strictly on the current condition of mortals, without considering whether their original state was different or where that change may have come from. Since human nature is so constituted that the human race cannot survive without a **sociable life**, and since the human mind is found to be capable of understanding the concepts that serve this purpose; and since it is clear that not only the human race—like all other creatures—owes its origin to God, but also that God embraces this world, however it is currently constituted, under the guidance of His <term>providence</term> <note>original: *providentia* — the belief in God's ongoing care and direction of the universe.</note>: it follows that God wills for humans to use those powers which they feel within themselves (and which are unique to them compared to the beasts) for the preservation of their nature. Thus, human life is meant to be distinct from the lawless life of animals. Because this distinction cannot be achieved unless the natural law is observed, it is understood that man is obligated by God to keep this law. It is seen as a means not invented by human choice, nor from their whims <note>original: *libidine* — referring to arbitrary human desire or impulse.</note>...

<summary>This page argues that natural laws are more than just helpful advice; they are divine mandates. It asserts that because God created humans as social beings with the unique capacity for reason, He intends for us to live by these laws to distinguish ourselves from "lawless" animals.</summary>

§. 13. *The division of duties that are incumbent upon man from **natural law** <term>natural law</term> <note>original: *lex naturalis* — a system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature rather than from the rules of society.</note> seems most conveniently established according to the objects toward which they are to be exercised. For this reason, a man cannot dispose of certain things concerning himself according to his own absolute whim, partly so that he can be a fit worshiper of the divine Power, and partly so that he can be a convenient and useful member of human society.

# CHAPTER IV.
## ON THE DUTY OF MAN TOWARD GOD, or concerning Natural Religion.

->§. 1.<-

The duty of man toward God, as far as it can be investigated by natural reason, *is completed in two parts:* both that we think rightly about God, and that we adjust our actions to His will. Thus **natural religion** <term>natural religion</term> <note>original: *religio naturalis* — a system of belief about God based on human reason and the observation of nature rather than on sacred texts or miracles.</note> consists of propositions that are both *theoretical* and *practical*.

§. 2. Among those things which every man must believe about God, the first of all is that he be certainly persuaded that *God exists*, i.e., that there truly is some highest and first Being, upon whom this universe depends. This is what wise men demonstrate from the subordination of causes, which they insist must ultimately rest upon some first cause, as well as from motion, and from the contemplation of the **mechanism of this universe** <note>original: *machinæ hujus universi* — a common Enlightenment-era metaphor comparing the cosmos to a complex clockwork device designed by a creator.</note>, and similar arguments.

For since the entire human race has been in a kind of perpetual possession of this conviction, it was necessary, if anyone wished to attack it, that he not only solidly destroy all the arguments by which God is proved to exist, but also bring forth more plausible reasons for his own assertion. At the same time, since it has been believed up to now that the welfare of the human race is contained in that conviction, it would further be necessary for him to show that the human race is better served by atheism than by retaining a sound worship of the **Deity** <note>original: *Numinis* — refers to the divine power or will of God.</note>. Since this can in no way be done, the impiety of all those who attempt to tear this down in any way is most detestable and should be restrained with the most severe punishments.

§. 3. The second point is, *that God is the creator of this universe.* For since it is manifest from reason that all these things did not exist from themselves, it is necessary that they have some highest cause. This itself is what we call God. From this it follows that those are mistaken who repeatedly boast of ***Nature*** as if it were the ultimate cause of all things and effects. Those are also in error who believe that God is something perceived by the senses, and especially the ***stars***. For the very substance of these things proves that they are not the first principle, but originated from something else.

Nor do those think any less unworthily of God who call Him the ***soul of the world*** <term>soul of the world</term> <note>original: *anima mundi* — an ancient philosophical concept suggesting that the universe is a living being with a rational spirit. The author argues this makes God merely a "part" of the universe rather than its independent creator.</note>. For the "soul of the world"—whatever that is actually supposed to be—denotes a part of the world. But how could a part of a thing be the cause of that same thing, that is, be something prior to it? However, if by "soul of the world" one understands that first and invisible principle upon which the force and motion of all things depend, then an obscure and figurative name is merely being substituted for a plain word. From this it also appears that ***the world is not eternal***. For it is contrary to the nature of something that has a cause to be eternal. And whoever asserts that the world is eternal denies its cause, and thus denies God Himself.

§. 4. The third point is, *that God exercises governance both over the entire world and over the human race.* This is clearly evident from the wonderful and constant order seen in this universe. §. 5. *The fourth point is, that no attribute belongs to God which involves any imperfection.* For since He is the cause and origin of all things, it would be absurd for any creature of His to be able to conceive in its mind some perfection that was absent from God. Indeed, since His perfection surpasses the grasp of such a tiny creature in infinite ways, it is more fitting to express that perfection in **negative** rather than **positive** terms <term>negative terms</term> <note>original: *terminis negativis* — This refers to "Apophatic theology," the idea that we can better describe what God is *not* (e.g., infinite/not-finite, immortal/not-mortal) than what He *is*, because His nature exceeds human language.</note>. Therefore, things that denote anything *finite or determined* are by no means to be attributed to God; because something greater can always be attributed to the finite. Furthermore, every determination and shape involves boundaries and limits. Indeed, He must not be said to be distinctly and fully comprehended or conceived by our imagination, or by any faculty of our soul; because whatever we are able to conceive distinctly and fully in the mind, that thing is finite. Nor indeed, because we call *God infinite,* do we hold a full concept of Him in our minds: since "infinite" properly does not denote something in the thing itself, but rather an inability in our own mind; as if we were saying that we do not grasp the magnitude of His essence. Therefore, it is also not correctly said that *He has parts, or is some kind of "whole";*

### CHAPTER IV.

...because these are attributes of finite things. Nor that He is contained *in any place*; because this implies limits and boundaries of magnitude. Nor that He *moves, or rests*; for both of these suppose being in a place. Likewise, things that denote some pain or *passion* <note>In this philosophical context, "passion" (from the Latin *passio*) refers to the state of being acted upon or changed by an external force, which would imply God is not self-sufficient.</note> cannot be properly attributed to God; (I say *properly*. For where such attributes are read of God, it is done humanly <note>original: *ἀνθρωποπαθῶς* (anthropopathōs) — an ancient Greek theological term for describing God using human emotions or bodily traits</note>, in terms of the effect produced, not the internal feeling): such as anger, repentance, or mercy. Likewise, those things that denote lack and the absence of some good: such as appetite, hope, longing, and desirous love. For these involve need, and therefore imperfection: since it cannot be understood how one would long for, hope for, or desire anything, unless he lacks or is in need of it. Thus, even when *intellect, will, knowledge, and the acts of the senses—vision, hearing*—are attributed to God: these must be understood in a far more sublime way than the way they exist in us. For the will is a rational appetite; however, appetite presupposes the absence and the need for a suitable thing. And understanding and sensation in humans involve "passion," being impressed by objects upon the organs of the body and the powers of the soul. This is a sign of a power dependent on another, and consequently not most perfect. Finally, it also contradicts divine perfection if more than *one God* <note>original: *plures uno Dij* — literally "gods more than one"</note> were to be spoken of. For besides the fact that the wonderful harmony of the world [demonstrates] only one...

### BOOK I.

...implies a governor; even God would then be finite, if there were several of equal power not dependent upon Him. Thus, it involves a contradiction for many infinite things to exist. Since this is the case, it is most consistent with reason, when expressing the attributes of God in any way, to use names that are either **negative**, such as infinite, incomprehensible, immense <note>original: *immensus* — in this philosophical context, it means "unmeasurable" or lacking spatial boundaries, rather than just "very large."</note>, eternal (that is, lacking beginning and end); or **superlative**, such as best, greatest, most powerful, most wise, etc.; or **indefinite**, such as good, just, creator, king, lord, etc. These are used in the sense that we do not so much wish to say distinctly *what* He is, as to profess our admiration and obedience through whatever expression we can; this is a sign of a humble soul, and of a soul honoring Him as much as it is able.

§. 6. **The practical propositions of natural religion <note>Natural religion refers to truths about God that can be discovered through human reason and observation of the world, without relying on supernatural revelation or scripture.</note> concern partly the internal, and partly the external worship of God. Internal worship of God consists in His honor.** Honor, moreover, is the opinion of another's power joined with goodness. Namely, the soul of a human being ought to conceive, as much as it can, a reverence toward Him from the consideration of God's power and goodness. From this it follows that one ought to **love** Him, as the author and giver of all good; to **hope** in Him, as the one upon whom all our future happiness also depends; [to submit to] His will... <note>The text cuts off here at "volun-"; the catchword "tate" indicates the next page begins with the completion of the word "voluntate" (will).</note>

§. 7. **External worship of God** <term>External worship: the outward expression of religious belief through actions, speech, and rituals, as opposed to internal thoughts or feelings.</term> consists primarily in these things: that man **gives thanks to God for so many good things** received from Him. That he expresses God's will through his own actions as much as possible, or rather, that he obeys Him. That he admires and celebrates His greatness. That he pours out prayers to Him for obtaining good things and warding off <note>original: *averruncandis* — a term often used in Roman religion for the averting of calamities or "turning away" evil omens.</note> evils; for prayers are signs of hope, and hope is an acknowledgment of divine goodness and power. That he speaks of God considerately: for that is a sign of fear <note>In this context, "fear" implies profound reverence or awe rather than terror.</note>; and fear is a confession of power. From which it follows, that **one must not use God's name rashly or in vain**; for both are thoughtless. **One must not swear where there is no need**; for it is in vain. **One must not dispute curiously or petulantly about the nature and government of God**: for from this nothing else follows than that we wish to measure God according to the standard of our own reason <note>original: *modulum nostræ rationis* — the author warns against trying to limit the infinite nature of God to what the human mind can logically grasp.</note>. §. 8. Indeed, it is certain that the effect of this **natural religion**, considered strictly and in accordance with the current condition of men, is limited within the sphere of this life; it is by no means sufficient for obtaining eternal salvation. For human reason <term>Human reason: the capacity for logical, rational, and analytic thought, independent of divine inspiration.</term>, left to itself, does not know that the depravity seen in the faculties and inclinations of man came about through human fault <note>The author is referring here to the concept of Original Sin, which he argues cannot be known through logic alone, but only through the Bible.</note>, and deserves God’s offense and eternal destruction. Therefore, the necessity of a **Savior**, His office and merit, as well as the promises made by God to the human race and other things dependent on them, are hidden from reason; it is established from the divine scriptures <note>original: *divinis literis* — literally "divine letters," a common scholarly term for the Bible or Holy Writ.</note> that eternal salvation comes to men through these things alone.

§. 9. Furthermore, it will be worthwhile to weigh a little more distinctly the usefulness that religion produces in human life, so that it may be clear that it is truly the ul- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword *timum* indicates the word is "ultimate" or "furthest."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of religion's role in society. ### CHAPTER IV

the most sacred and firmest bond of human society. For in **natural liberty** <term>Natural liberty: the state of humans before or outside of organized government, where individuals are guided only by their own reason and the law of nature.</term>, if you remove the fear of the divine Power, when anyone relies on their own strength, they will inflict whatever they please on the weaker ones. They will regard honesty, shame, and faith as empty words, and will not be driven to do right by anything other than the feeling of their own weakness. Furthermore, if religion were removed, the internal stability of states would always be uncertain. To keep citizens in their duty, the fear of temporal punishment, the faith pledged to superiors and the glory of keeping it, and even gratitude for being defended from the miseries of the **natural state** <term>Natural state: a philosophical concept describing the hypothetical condition of humanity without political authority or civil laws.</term> by the help of the supreme power, would by no means suffice. For then that saying would truly have a place: *He who knows how to die, knows not how to be forced* <note>original: *qui mori scit, cogi nescit* — a classical sentiment suggesting that a person who does not fear death cannot be coerced by any earthly power.</note>. Since for those who do not fear God, nothing can be feared more than death. Anyone capable of despising death could attempt anything against those in power. 

As for why someone would want to do so, a cause would hardly be lacking; for instance, to avoid the inconveniences that seem to weigh on them from another's rule, or to gain for themselves the advantages that accompany the possessor of power. This is especially true when someone could easily judge that they are acting by right—either because the one currently in power seems to be managing the **state** <term>State: the Latin *res publica* refers to the commonwealth or the public affairs of the political community.</term> badly, or because they hope that they themselves would rule far better. Moreover, an opportunity to attempt such things could easily be offered, with the king [exposing] his life...

### BOOK I.

if [the ruling power] does not guard with enough caution—(and in such a state of affairs, who will guard the **guards** <term>Guards: from the Latin *custodes*. The author is referencing the famous question, "Who will guard the guards themselves?" (original: *quis custodes ipsos custodiet*).</term> themselves?)—or if many conspire together, or if an external war rushes in and enemies are invited to join their side. Furthermore, citizens would be most prone to inflicting injuries on one another. For since in the **civil court** <term>Civil court: the *forum civili*, the public venue where legal cases are judged based on evidence and testimony.</term> judgment is pronounced according to what is documented and proven, all **crimes** <term>Crimes: *scelera*, referring to wicked acts or moral outrages.</term> and outrages from which profit is likely to come would be regarded as proofs of cleverness—in which one might take pride—provided they could be committed secretly and without witnesses. No one would practice works of **mercy** <term>Mercy: *misericordia*, the compassionate treatment of those in distress.</term> or **friendship** <term>Friendship: *amicitia*, here implying the social bonds and mutual duties between citizens.</term> unless there was a certain hope of glory or gain. From this it would also follow that, while no one could place solid **trust** <term>Trust: *fiduciam*, the reliance on another's integrity or word.</term> in another's word once **divine punishments** <term>Divine punishments: *poenis divinis*, the belief that God punishes the wicked in this life or the next.</term> are removed, individuals would live anxious lives in perpetual fear and suspicion, lest they be deceived or harmed by others. But both those **ruling** <term>Rulers: *imperantes*, those who hold the supreme power in a state.</term> and those **subject** <term>Subjects: *subjecti*, the citizens or people living under the authority of a ruler.</term> to them would be little inclined to perform noble and glorious deeds. For rulers, bound by no bond of conscience, would hold all duties and even **justice** <term>Justice: *justitiam*, the administration of law and fairness.</term> itself as for sale, and would seek private advantage in everything through the **oppression of citizens** <term>Oppression of citizens: *oppressio civium*, the abuse of power to crush or exploit the people.</term>. Just as they would always fear rebellion from the citizens, they would understand their own safety to be placed solely in rendering the people as weakened as possible. Citizens, on the other hand, fearing the oppression of those ruling...

<header>CHAPTER IV.</header>

...ts <note>The text continues from the previous page, completing the word *subiectos* (subjects).</note>, would never cease looking for opportunities to rebel; nor would they trust one another any less, fearing each other mutually. Indeed, even spouses, upon the rise of even a slight quarrel, would suspect one another of being killed by poison or some other secret method. An equal danger would threaten from the **household** <term>Household: *familia*, which in this context includes not just immediate family but also domestic servants and enslaved people living under the same roof.</term>. Since without religion there would also be no **conscience** <term>Conscience: *conscientia*, internal moral judgment. The author argues that without a divine judge, there is no internal mechanism to feel guilt.</term>, it would not be easy to detect secret crimes of this kind; for these are usually betrayed through the restlessness of conscience and terrors that break out into external signs. From this it is apparent how much it concerns the human race to block all paths to **atheism** <note>original: "atheismo"</note>, lest it grow strong; and at the same time, what **madness** <note>original: "vecordia"</note> drives those who believe it contributes to a reputation for **political wisdom** <term>Political wisdom: *prudentiæ politicæ*. This is likely a critique of "Machiavellian" thought, where appearing irreligious or cynical was sometimes seen as a sign of a "shrewd" ruler.</term> if they appear inclined toward impiety.

## CHAPTER V.
### ON THE DUTY OF MAN
#### Toward Himself.

->§. 1.<-

Although a deeply ingrained **self-love** <note>original: "amor sui"</note> compels a person to take anxious care of themselves and to provide for their own advantages in every way—so much so that from this perspective it seems superfluous to devise any obligation—nevertheless, in another respect, a person is certainly bound to observe certain things regarding themselves. For since not for himself

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...on <note>This completes the word "person" from the previous page.</note> is not born for himself alone, but has been adorned by the Creator with such excellent **endowments** <term>Endowments: *dotibus*, referring to natural talents, intellect, and spiritual gifts bestowed by God.</term> so that he might both celebrate His glory and exist as a suitable member of human society. From this, man is bound to conduct himself in such a way that he does not allow the Creator's gifts to perish through neglect, and contributes something to human society to the best of his ability <note>original: "pro virili"</note>. Thus, although one's own ignorance is a great disgrace and loss to anyone, a student is rightly punished <note>original: "vapulat," literally meaning to be flogged or beaten, a common historical reference to school discipline.</note> by a teacher if he neglects to learn the skills of which he was capable.

->§. 2.<-

Furthermore, since man consists of two parts, the **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note> and the **body**, of which the former takes the role of the ruler and the latter the role of the servant or instrument—so that we utilize the command of the mind and the service of the body—care must be taken for both, but especially for the soul. Indeed, the **mind** <note>original: "animus"</note> must be formed above all to properly sustain a social life, and must be imbued with a sense and love of duty and **integrity** <term>Integrity: *honesti*, referring to moral rectitude or that which is honorable and virtuous.</term>. Then, according to each person's capacity and fortune, something must be learned so that no one becomes a "useless weight upon the earth," burdensome to themselves and troublesome to others. But one must also choose an honorable way of life in good time, according to the impulse of one's **character** <term>Character: *genii*, the natural inclination or "spirit" of a person.</term>, the ability of body and mind, the condition of one's birth, worldly goods, the authority of parents, the commands of civil rulers, opportunity, or necessity.

->§. 3.<-

Since, however, the soul is sustained by the supports of the body, its strengths must be maintained by suitable nourish—

->§. 4.<-

Since, however, no one gave life to themselves, but it is to be held as a gift from God <note>original: *Dei beneficio*</note>, it is also apparent that no power belongs to a man over his own life to the extent that he may end it at his pleasure; rather, one must certainly wait until he is called away by Him who placed us in this **station** <note>A common Stoic and legal metaphor comparing life to a military post where a soldier must remain until relieved.</note>. # BOOK I.

Indeed, it is even permissible to undergo such danger voluntarily, provided that more serious reasons do not hold one back, and there is hope that this will result in the safety of others—and provided these others are worthy of being redeemed at such a high price. For it would be foolish to join someone in death in vain, or for an excellent man to seek death for a worthless man <note>original: *pro nauci homine*, referring to a person of no value or "not worth a nut."</note>. ->§. 8.<-

Otherwise, indeed, all the goods which nature or industry has brought forth for us would be granted in vain if it were not permitted to oppose force against another who unjustly invades them; and the honest would be exposed as ready prey to the wicked, if the former were never supposed to oppose force against the latter. Thus, to completely outlaw violent self-defense would lead to the destruction of the human race.

§. 7. Nevertheless, it is not always permissible to resort to extremes whenever an injury is threatened; rather, *safer remedies must first be attempted*; for example, blocking the attacker's path, staying within fortified places, or warning the attacker to desist from his fury. Just as it is the mark of a prudent man to endure through *patience with a minor injury*, if it can be done conveniently, and to waive some of his rights rather than expose himself to greater danger by an untimely opposition of violence; especially when that which is attacked can be easily repaired or compensated. But where my safety cannot be secured in this or a similar way, it will be permissible to attempt even extreme measures to preserve it.

§. 8. ### CHAPTER V

...must first be considered; whether one lives in *natural liberty* <note>original: *naturali libertate*; a philosophical concept describing a state where no man-made laws or governments exist</note>, subject to no mortal, or whether one lives subject to *civil authority* <note>original: *imperio civili*</note>. In the former state <note>the state of nature</note>, where someone persists in inflicting an injury <note>original: *injuriam*</note> and, not being led by repentance <note>original: *pœnitentia*</note> for their wicked attempt, refuses to return to cultivating peace <note>original: *pacem*</note> with me, I will be able to repel this person even by killing them. 

And this is true not only if he attacks my life, but even if he only attempts to wound or hurt me, or even to snatch away my property while my body remains safe. For I have no guarantee <note>original: *cautum*</note> that he will not move from these smaller injuries to greater ones; and he who declares himself an enemy <note>original: *hoſtẽ*</note> is no longer protected by any right that would prevent me from repelling him by any means necessary. Indeed, human life would then be "unsociable" <note>original: *inſociabilis*</note> if it were not permitted to use extreme measures against someone who does not cease to continue even moderate injuries. In such a situation, every modest man would be forced to serve as a perpetual laughingstock <note>original: *ludibrium*</note> to every wicked person. 

Furthermore, in this state, I can not only repel a danger attempted in the present, but once that threat is driven off, I can pursue the invader until I have been sufficiently secured <note>original: *cautum*</note> against him for the future. 

*Regarding this security, it is to be understood thus:* If someone, having attempted an injury, is spontaneously led by repentance to ask for forgiveness <note>original: *veniam*</note> and offers restitution <note>original: *reſtitutionẽ*</note> for the damage, I ought to return to a state of favor with him once his word is accepted. For it is a firm sign of a reformed mind to repent and seek forgiveness of one's own accord. But he who only then displays repentance—

§. 9. And indeed, this is permitted only so that the danger may be driven off; however, vengeance and the security against being harmed in the future <note>original: *cautio de non offendendo*</note> are left to the judgment of the magistrate.

§. 10. Furthermore, it is permissible to undertake defense both against one who intends my destruction with malicious intent <note>original: *dolo malo*</note>, and against one who does so by mistake; for example, if someone driven by insanity should attack me, or because he believes me to be someone else with whom he has an ongoing feud. For it is enough that the other person has no right to attack or kill me, nor is there any obligation on my part to undergo death in vain.

§. 11. Regarding the time within which defense can rightly be made, it must be held as follows. Those who both live in natural liberty <note>original: *naturali libertate*; the philosophical state of humans before the invention of government</note>, although they can and should assume that others will observe the duties of natural law <note>original: *legis naturalis*</note> toward them; nevertheless, because of the wick— <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *malitiam* or "wickedness."</note>

# CHAPTER V.

Truly, that suspicion, arising from the common malice of men, is not sufficient for me to be able to spontaneously <note>original: *ultro*; acting of one's own accord or preemptively</note> overwhelm another with arms under the pretext of my own defense, not even if I see his power <note>original: *potentiam*</note> growing excessively; especially where he has increased that same power through harmless industry or by the kindness of fate <note>original: *benignitatem fati*; good fortune or luck</note> without the oppression of others. 

Indeed, if someone shows not only the capability of doing harm but also the will to do so—not against me, but against another—I cannot for that reason immediately invade him spontaneously on my own behalf, unless I am bound by a treaty <term>treaty (foedere): a formal agreement or alliance between parties or nations</term> to assist the other person who is being unjustly attacked by a more powerful one. It is beneficial to do this even more promptly if it is probable that, once the other is overwhelmed, he will turn against me as well, and use his previous victory as a tool for the next. 

But where it clearly appears that another is already occupied with inflicting violence upon me, even if he has not yet fully revealed his attempts, it will be permitted to immediately begin a violent self-defense <note>original: *violentam sui defensionem*</note> and seize the one preparing the harm; provided there is no hope that, if warned in a friendly manner, he would cast off his hostile mind, or if such a warning [would be harmful] to our affairs, <meta>The text continues on the next page with the word: harmful (noxia)</meta>

# BOOK I.

[the threat] is about to become harmful. Hence, in this situation, he must be considered the <term>aggressor (aggreſſore): the person who initiates an attack or threat</term> who first conceived the will to harm another and prepared himself to carry it out; conversely, the favor of <term>defense (defenſionis): the act of protecting oneself</term> will accompany the person who, using speed, overwhelms the one moving more slowly. For defense does not require one to receive the first blow, nor merely to dodge and repel the blows that are aimed.

§. 12. But in <term>states (civitatibus): organized political communities under a government</term>, by no means is such a wide margin allowed for self-defense. For here, even if someone understands that a fellow citizen is preparing to inflict <term>violence (vis): the use of physical force to harm</term> upon them, or is spreading terrible threats, it will not be permitted to strike them first; instead, the matter must be brought before the common ruler, and security <note>original: *cautio*; a legal guarantee or protection</note> should be sought from them. Truly, when someone is already being attacked by another and is reduced to such straits that there is no opportunity to beg for the help of a <term>magistrate (magiſtratus): a civil officer or judge</term> or of other citizens; only then will it be permitted to use even the most extreme measures against the invader to repel the violence. This is allowed, however, not with the intention that <term>vengeance (vindicta): punishment inflicted in retaliation</term> for the injury be exacted through <term>slaughter (cædes): the act of killing</term>, but only insofar as life cannot be rescued from the present danger without such killing. 

*The beginning of that time in which one can kill another with impunity for their own defense starts from this point: when the aggressor displays the will to attack my life, and, equipped with the strength and instruments for harming, is already within...*

### CHAPTER V.

<sig>D 5</sig>

[insofar as the threat] has reached that point from which he can truly do harm, also calculating that window of time which is necessary if I prefer to act first rather than be acted upon. <note>The author is discussing the "preemptive" element of defense—at what exact moment a person is allowed to strike to prevent being killed.</note> Even though, because of the <term>agitation of the mind (perturbationem animi): the state of fear or panic caused by sudden danger</term> which such great peril excites, a moderate excess of force is not punished in a <term>human court (foro humano): a secular legal tribunal, as opposed to a strict moral or divine judgment</term>. *The duration of <term>blameless defense (inculpatæ tutelæ): defense that is legally and morally justified</term> lasts until the aggressor has been repelled, or withdraws of his own accord, or is moved by repentance at the very moment of the deed, or because the outcome of his attempt has failed; so that he can no longer cause harm for the present, and we have the opportunity to withdraw to safe places.* For the <term>avenging of injury (ultio injuriæ): the punishment of a past wrong</term> and <term>security (cautio): measures taken to prevent future harm</term> for the future belong to the care and power of the <term>civil authority (imperij civilis): the government or state</term>.

§. 13. Although it has been said that one should not rightly rush to <term>killing (cædem): the taking of a life</term> where the danger can be repelled by a more convenient way, nevertheless, because of the *agitation of the mind* which imminent danger usually produces, things are not usually judged with extreme precision <note>original: *ad vivum reſecari*; literally "cut to the quick," meaning to apply the law with absolute, unforgiving rigor</note>; for a person trembling in such danger is not able to look around for every way of escape as accurately as one who considers the matter with a calm mind. From this it follows that just as it is reckless to voluntarily leave a safe place to confront a challenger; so too, if another person attacks me while I am in an open place, I am not strictly bound to flee, unless there is a refuge nearby into which I can withdraw without danger; nor am I always bound to <term>retreat (cẽsim ire): to move backward or yield ground while facing an opponent</term>. [...and because in flight] one’s <note>original: "nudum tergum ostendendum" — literally "the naked back must be shown."</note> back must be exposed, and on both sides the danger of a slip or fall looms; and once you have been driven from your stance, it is not easily recovered. Furthermore, just as a person is not excluded from the <term>favor of defense (favore defensionis): the legal benefit or protection granted to those acting in self-defense</term> simply because they went out into public of their own accord to attend to their business—even though they would have been safe from danger had they stayed home—so too the same protection is *not* enjoyed by one who, having been challenged to a <term>duel (duellum): a pre-arranged combat between two people, often illegal under civil law</term> by another, is so hard-pressed after appearing that he must either <note>original: "transfodiat" — pierce through or stab</note> kill his opponent or perish himself. For since the laws forbid entering into such a danger in the first place, no account is taken of that peril to excuse the killing.

§. 14. That which is granted for the *defense of life* is also granted for the *defense of limbs*, such that he is held innocent who kills a violent attacker who perhaps intended only to <term>mutilate (mutilandi): to cut off or permanently disable a body part</term> a limb or inflict a <term>serious wound (grave vulnus): an injury that causes significant harm without necessarily being intended as fatal</term>. For by nature we especially abhor mutilation and serious injury; and the loss of a limb, particularly a <note>original: "nobilioris" — literally "more noble," referring to essential organs or limbs like hands or eyes</note> more vital one, is sometimes valued as being not much less than life itself. Furthermore, it is not known in advance whether death might follow from the mutilation and the wound. Moreover, to endure such a thing exceeds the common endurance of men, to which the laws do not regularly tend to obligate anyone—especially for the sake of a <note>original: "hominis malitiosi" — a person acting with "malice aforethought" or wicked intent</note> malicious man.

§. 15. But also, what is permitted for the sake of life is judged to be permitted for *chastity* <note>original: "pudicitia" — this term refers to sexual integrity, modesty, or the right to refuse sexual violation</note>: since indeed no greater [outrage...]
