### CHAPTER V.

[...no greater] outrage can be inflicted upon an honorable woman than if someone attempts to snatch her <term>chastity</term> from her against her will—an integrity by which the honor of her sex is most highly valued—and forces her to bear offspring for an enemy from her own blood. <note>The author argues that rape is not just a personal violation but a fundamental "outrage" because it forces a woman to bear children for an aggressor, which in the legal thought of the time was seen as a profound disruption of natural and family order.</note>

§. 16. Furthermore, the defense of property among those living in <term>natural liberty (naturali libertate): the condition of human beings before the establishment of formal governments or civil laws</term> may even extend to the killing of the invader, provided the property is not of such a nature as to be worthless. For certainly our life cannot be preserved without property; and he who attacks property through injustice declares a hostile intent just as much as he who attacks life. However, in <term>civil states (civitatibus): organized societies under a government</term>, where stolen goods can be recovered through the help of a <term>magistrate (magistratus): a civil officer or judge</term>, this is generally not permitted—except in the case where the person who comes to snatch our things cannot be brought to trial. From this perspective, it is lawful to kill <term>robbers (praedones): those who use violence or highway robbery</term> and <term>night-thieves (fures nocturnos)</term>. <note>The law distinguishes between daytime theft (where a thief might be identified and sued) and nighttime theft, where the immediate danger and lack of witnesses make lethal self-defense more legally permissible.</note>

§. 17. Thus far we have spoken of the defense made by those who are attacked by others through injustice. However, he who was the first to harm another can only then rightly defend himself—and in that defense harm the other again—after he has been touched by <term>repentance (poenitentia)</term> and has offered <term>reparation (reparationem)</term> for the damage caused and <term>security (cautionem): a legal guarantee or assurance</term> against future harm, and the injured party has rejected this through bitterness of spirit and persists in seeking <term>vengeance (vindictam)</term> by his own hand. <note>This is a crucial nuance: if an original aggressor tries to make amends and the victim refuses, continuing to attack out of pure revenge, the original aggressor regains the right to defend their own life.</note>

<meta>This page continues the legal and moral discussion regarding self-preservation. While the previous page focused on the specific right to kill in defense of life, limb, chastity, or property, this page transitions into a broader philosophical and legal analysis of "Necessity" as a general exemption from the standard obligations of the law.</meta>

### BOOK I.

...in [various] cases, one is deemed to be exempt from the obligation of common laws. For this reason, it is said that *necessity* has no law. <note>original: "necessitas lege carere dicitur." This is a famous legal maxim (necessitas non habet legem) suggesting that in extreme emergencies, the normal rules of society may be suspended to ensure survival.</note> Truly, since a human being is driven with such intense fervor toward self-preservation, it is not easily presumed that such an obligation has been placed upon them to which their own safety must give way. 

For although not only God, but also—where the gravity of the matter demands—<term>civil authority (imperium civile): the power of the state or government to command its citizens</term> can impose such a rigid obligation upon us that death should be faced rather than departing from it even by a "fingernail’s breadth" <note>original: "latum unguem" - a Latin idiom meaning a very small distance or a tiny amount; the author is saying the law can sometimes be absolute.</note>, nevertheless, such a rigid obligation of laws is not always presumed. For those who enacted these laws, or who introduced certain institutions among men—since they certainly intended to promote the safety or benefit of people through them—are generally judged to have kept in view the condition of human nature, and how a human being cannot help but recoil from and repel those things which lead toward their own destruction. 

Hence, as a rule, laws—especially <term>positive laws (leges positivae): man-made laws enacted by a specific government or society, rather than universal laws of nature</term>—and any human institutions are judged to exclude cases of necessity. That is, they do not bind us when their observance would be accompanied by an evil that destroys human nature or exceeds common human endurance; unless that specific case is expressly included, or is part of the very nature of the matter. Therefore, necessity does not truly cause a law to be directly violated or a sin to be <meta>catchword: committed</meta>

<summary>This page explores the legal doctrine of Necessity, arguing that most laws—particularly those made by humans—implicitly include an exception for life-threatening situations because the drive for self-preservation is a fundamental part of human nature.</summary>
<keywords>necessity, self-preservation, positive laws, civil authority, legal obligation, human nature, exemptions</keywords>

### CHAPTER V.

...to be admitted: but from the benevolent intent of the legislators, and simultaneously from a consideration of human nature, it is presumed that a **case of necessity** <note>original: "casum necessitatis" — a legal situation where an individual is forced to break a law to prevent a greater harm or to save their own life.</note> is not contained under a law conceived in general terms. The matter will be clarified by one or two examples.

**§. 19.** Although in other circumstances a person does not have a <term>right (jus): a legal or moral entitlement</term> over their own limbs such that they may mutilate or destroy them at their own whim; it will nevertheless be permitted to cut off a limb seized by an incurable disease, lest the whole body perish, or the parts still healthy be dragged down, or the use of other limbs be hindered by a useless appendage.

**§. 20.** If, in a shipwreck, more people should leap into a **lifeboat** <note>original: "scapha" — a small boat or skiff used for emergencies.</note> than it can carry, and the boat does not belong to any one person by a specific right: it seems that those who are to be thrown overboard should be **chosen by lot**. <note>original: "sorte ducendi" — the practice of "drawing lots" or using a random method of selection to ensure fairness in desperate situations.</note> And if anyone refuses to undergo the danger of the lot—acting as one who seeks the destruction of everyone—he can be thrown overboard outside of the lot.

**§. 21** If two people fall into an immediate danger to life, in which both are destined to perish, one is allowed to do something by which death is hastened for the other (who would have perished anyway), so that he himself may be safe. For example: If I, being skilled in swimming, had fallen into deep waters with another who is unskilled in swimming, and he held me in a tight embrace, and I did not have enough strength to carry him out of the water with me: I will be able to violently push such a person away from me, lest I be suffocated together with him, even if he could have been supported by me in some way for a very short time. # BOOK I.

So also, when two are fleeing and an enemy follows threatening death, one person—either by closing a gate behind them or by destroying a bridge—can leave the other in mortal danger if both cannot be saved at the same time.

§. 22. *Out of **necessity** <term>necessitas: a legal state where survival justifies breaking a rule</term>, the danger of death or great **injury** <note>original: "læsionis"</note> can be threatened against another indirectly. This is done in such a way that it is not our specific purpose to harm them, but merely to undertake such an action for our own **preservation** <note>original: "conservatione"</note> from which harm might probably be inflicted upon them; even though we would prefer to satisfy our necessity by any other means, and we moderate the injury itself as much as is in our power.* For example, if a stronger person pursues me with the intent to kill, and someone happens to be in the narrow way through which I must flee; if, after being warned, they do not move aside—or if the narrowness of the time or place does not allow for it—it will be permitted to knock them down and continue the **flight** <note>original: "fugam"</note> over them as they lie there, even if it seems they will be severely harmed by that impact. This applies unless I am bound to that person by a special **obligation** <note>original: "obligatione"</note>, such that I ought to undergo the danger myself for their sake. But if the person standing in the way of the flight cannot move, despite being warned—for instance, if it is an infant or a person with a disability <note>original: "claudus" — literally "lame" or "limping"</note>—the one who falls <note>The OCR ends with "fal-", likely the beginning of "falsus" or "fallit," or perhaps "calcavit" (stepped upon).</note> over them will at least deserve an excuse...

### CHAPTER VI.

...rather, he strives for this instead, than by weaving delays to offer his body to the enemy. But truly, if someone should petulantly or inhumanely obstruct me and refuse to grant a path for escape, they may even be directly shoved and knocked down. Furthermore, those to whom injury is brought about through these cases ought to bear it as a stroke of fate <note>original: "fatalem calamitatem," literally a fatal or fated calamity</note>.

§. 23. If someone, through no fault of their own, should find themselves in extreme want of food or of clothing necessary against the cold, and has not been able to obtain these from others who are wealthier and have an abundance—neither through prayers, nor payment, nor by offering their own labor so that they might grant them voluntarily—then, without the crime of **theft** <note>original: "furti"</note> or **robbery** <note>original: "rapina"</note>, they may take them by force or in secret. This is especially true if they have the intention of providing the value of those goods when the opportunity arises. For a wealthy person was bound by the duty of **humanity** <term>humanitas: the moral and social obligation to treat others with kindness and provide aid</term> to help one placed in such necessity. Although, as a rule, things owed out of humanity can by no means be snatched away by force, yet extreme **necessity** <term>necessitas: a legal state where survival justifies an action that would otherwise be a crime</term> ensures that such things can be claimed no less than those owed by a "perfect obligation" <note>In legal philosophy, a "perfect obligation" is a debt or duty that can be legally enforced, whereas a "duty of humanity" is usually only a moral one.</note>. It is necessary, however, that the person in need first tries all other paths so that they might meet their needs with the owner's consent; nor should the owner be held by an equal necessity, or be about to fall into the same state. Furthermore, restitution must be made, especially where the other person's fortunes do not allow them to give such a thing away for free.

§. 24.

<summary>This page explores the limits of the "plea of necessity," arguing that a person in extreme, life-threatening poverty may legally take food or clothing from the wealthy if all other appeals fail. It balances this right with the requirement that the person in need must intend to pay the owner back and must not cause the owner to fall into the same state of want.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the state of necessity, specifically regarding the destruction of another's property to save one's own, and begins Chapter VI, which transitions from general duties to specific obligations between all human beings, starting with the duty not to cause harm.</meta>

-> BOOK I. <-

§. 24. Finally, it appears that **necessity** <term>necessitas: a legal state where urgent survival or preservation justifies an action that would otherwise be illegal</term> regarding our own affairs grants permission *to destroy the property of others;* provided, however, that the danger to our property arose through no fault of our own; that the danger cannot be dispelled in a more convenient way; and that we do not destroy someone else's more valuable property for the sake of our own less valuable property. We must also provide for the appraisal <note>original: "aestimationem," referring to the financial value</note> of that property, if it would not have perished otherwise. Alternatively, we should share in the loss if the other person's property was destined to perish along with our own, but was sacrificed to save ours. Almost all *maritime laws* <note>original: "leges nauticae," the historical legal codes governing seafaring and trade</note> follow this principle of equity. Thus, if a fire breaks out and threatens my house, it is permissible to tear down a neighbor's house, provided that those whose houses were saved by this action compensate the neighbor for their loss in proportion to the benefit received.

### CHAPTER VI.
# ON THE DUTY OF EVERYONE
# TOWARD ANYONE ELSE,
## and first, on not harming others.
### § I.

Next follow the *duties which a human being must practice toward other human beings.* Some of these arise from a *common obligation,* **which the Creator willed all human beings, as such, to be bound by among themselves. Others, however, proceed from a specific institution introduced or accepted by humans, or from** * a specific acquired status of persons.* The former are to be practiced by anyone toward anyone; the latter, however, are not

### CHAPTER VI.
-> (continued) <-

...except toward certain persons, based on a specific condition or status. Thus, you could call the former *absolute* duties, and the latter *hypothetical* <term>hypothetica: duties that are "conditional," meaning they only apply once a person enters into a specific agreement or social role, like a contract or a marriage</term>.

§. 2. Among absolute duties, or those of anyone toward anyone else, *the first place is held by this: that no one should harm another.* Indeed, this is the broadest of all duties, encompassing all humans as such. It is also the easiest, since it consists of a simple refrain from action <note>The author suggests that "not doing" something is easier than "doing" something</note>, except when passions struggling against reason must occasionally be restrained. Moreover, it is the most necessary duty, because without it, the social life of humans could in no way exist. For I can live peacefully with one who confers no benefit upon me, or who does not even exchange common duties with me, provided they do not harm me in any way. Indeed, from the vast majority of mortals, we desire nothing more than this; benefits are usually shared only among a few. But with one who harms me, I can in no way live in peace; since nature has implanted in each person such a tender love for themselves and their possessions, that they cannot help but repel in every way someone who attempts to bring injury <note>original: "noxam," meaning physical harm or legal damage</note> to these things.

§. 3. Furthermore, by this very duty, not only are those things protected which are present in someone by nature—such as life, the body, limbs, chastity, and liberty—but also whatever things are acquired by some institution...

Thus, whatever becomes ours by any legitimate title <term>legitimo titulo: a legal right or recognized claim to ownership</term>, this precept forbids it from being taken away, corrupted, damaged, or withdrawn from our use in whole or in part. Hence, by this very command, any crimes by which harm is brought to others are understood to be forbidden; such as murder, wounding, beating, robbery, theft, fraud, and violence, whether inflicted directly or indirectly, mediately or immediately, and other similar acts.

§. 4. From this it further follows: *If any injury <term>læsio: physical or legal harm</term> is inflicted or damage <term>damnum: financial or material loss</term> is caused to someone by another in any way that can rightly be imputed to them, it must be repaired by that person as much as possible.* For otherwise, the precept that no one should be harmed, nor damage be given to anyone, would be empty if, once someone has actually been harmed, they were forced to swallow the loss for free, while the one who caused the harm could enjoy the fruit of their injustice <note>original: "injuriæ," referring to a wrongful act that violates another's rights</note> securely and without making repayment. Nor would the wickedness of mortals ever refrain from harming one another without the necessity of restitution; nor would it be easy for the person who suffered the damage to settle their mind toward living in peace with the other as long as they have not obtained reparation for it.

§. 5. *Although damage <note>original: "damnum"</note> might properly seem to pertain to the injury of things;* it is nevertheless understood by us here so broadly as to denote every injury, corruption, diminution, or removal of that which is already ours, or the interception of that—

### CHAPTER VI.

But if those things are intercepted which were owed only by an imperfect obligation <term>obligatio imperfecta: a moral or social duty, such as charity or gratitude, which cannot be legally forced</term>, it is not considered a "damage" that must be compensated. For it would be inappropriate to count as a loss the failure to receive such things, or to demand compensation <note>original: "pensationem"</note> for things which I could only expect from another as a voluntary favor <note>original: "ultronei beneficij"</note>, and which I cannot count among my possessions before I have actually received them.

§. 6. Furthermore, under the name of "damage" falls not only our property, or what is owed to us, which is harmed, lost, or intercepted; but also the ***fruits*** <note>original: "fructus": in a legal sense, this refers to any products, profits, or advantages derived from an asset, such as crops from land or interest from money</note> which come from the thing itself, whether they have already been gathered, or are still expected, provided the owner was going to gather them—after deducting, however, the expenses that were necessary for gathering those fruits. Moreover, the valuation of expected fruits is increased or decreased according to whether they were closer to or further from the conclusion of an uncertain harvest <note>original: "proventus"</note>. Finally, whatever subsequently flows from an injury as if by natural necessity is also considered as part of a single damage.

### BOOK I.

...not only directly and *by oneself*, but also *through others*. Damage caused directly by someone can also be imputed to another, because they contributed something to that deed, either by doing something or by not doing what they ought to have done. Sometimes, among several people who concurred in the same deed, one is considered the ***principal*** <term>principalis causa: the primary actor or main cause of an event</term> cause, while another is in the place of an ***accessory*** <term>accessorij: those who assist or contribute to a deed without being the main actor</term>; at other times, they all walk with equal step <note>original: "pari passu ambulant"; meaning they share equal responsibility and standing in the matter</note>. Regarding these, it must be observed that they are held to the reparation of damage <note>original: "reparationem damni"</note> only if they were truly the cause of the damage and brought some significance to the whole damage or a part of it. 

However, where someone has not contributed any actual labor to the act itself by which damage was caused, nor brought it about that the act was undertaken beforehand, nor shared in any part of the profit <note>original: "emolumenti"</note>; such a person, although they may be bound by some sin <note>original: "peccato"; here referring to moral guilt or religious transgression rather than legal liability</note> on account of that act, will nevertheless not be held to the restitution of the damage. Examples include those who rejoice in others' misfortunes, or those who praise or excuse a damage once it has been committed; those who wish for it before it happens, or who favor or flatter the actor while it is being done.

§. 8. Where several have concurred in one act from which damage has flowed, he who—by command or by some other means involving necessity—drove others to the deed shall be held responsible in the first place. The perpetrator of the crime, who was not permitted to refuse his service <note>original: "ministerium detrectare non licuit"; likely referring to a servant or soldier under strict orders</note>, shall be regarded only as an instrument. But he who approached the crime without necessity...

It establishes that restitution is only required when a person's actions or commands truly contributed to the resulting loss.</summary>

### CHAPTER VI.

<sig>E 3</sig>

...takes place, he himself will be held responsible in the first place, then the others who contributed anything to the **deed** <term>facinus: a deed or act, often used in a legal sense to denote a crime or transgression</term>. This is so, however, that if the reparation of the **damage** <term>damnum: a loss or injury to person, property, or status</term> has been made by the primary parties, the secondary parties are exempt from it (which is different in the case of punishments <note>original: "pœnis"; the author notes that while financial restitution can be satisfied by one person, criminal punishment usually applies to all individuals involved</note>). If several people have committed a deed through **conspiracy** <term>conspiratio: a secret agreement or plot between two or more people to perform an illegal act</term>, all are held responsible for each person's share, and each individual is held for the whole; so that if everyone is caught, each one ought to contribute a proportional share to repair the damage. Where the rest have escaped and only one is caught, he will be held responsible to pay for everyone. Moreover, where some of those caught are not able to pay, those who are wealthy will be held responsible for the **whole amount** <term>in solidum: a legal term meaning "for the whole," where a single debtor can be held liable for the entire debt of a group</term>. But if several people have concurred in the same deed without a conspiracy, and it can be clearly discerned how much each one contributed to the loss, each shall only be held to pay for that which resulted from his own actions. Yet if one person has paid the whole amount, the others are freed from the reparation of the damage.

§. 9. Not only is the person who harmed another through malicious intent held to the reparation of damage, but also the one who did so, beyond their direct intention, through **negligence** <term>negligentia: a failure to exercise the care that a reasonably prudent person would exercise</term> *or fault*, which was easy to avoid. For this is no small part of **sociality** <term>socialitas: the principle of living together in a community; a core concept in natural law theory regarding the duties humans owe each other</term>, to act so carefully that our daily conduct does not become fearsome or intolerable to others. Indeed, one is often bound by a specific obligation to apply careful **diligence** <term>diligentia: the standard of care and attention required to avoid harming others</term>. Nay, even the law...

# BOOK I.

...the **slightest fault** <term>levissima culpa: the most minor degree of negligence, where someone fails to exercise the extreme care that a very cautious person would have shown</term> can be sufficient to require the necessity of **reparation** <term>reparatio: the act of restoring something to its original state or paying for its loss</term>, provided that the nature of the business does not, as it were, voluntarily reject the most exact diligence; or if the fault lies more with the one who receives the **damage** <term>damnum: a loss or injury to one's property or person</term> than with the one who causes it; or unless a great upheaval or the circumstances of the business do not allow for accurate foresight; for example, if someone in the heat of battle, while brandishing weapons, happens to injure someone standing very close to him.

§. 10. *However, he who has caused injury through a **fortuitous event** <term>casus fortuitus: an accidental occurrence or "act of God" that could not have been predicted or prevented</term> and without any fault of his own, is not bound to make reparation.* This is because, since nothing has been committed that can be blamed on him, there is no reason why he who acted without intention should have to pay for a **misfortune** <note>original: "fatale malum"; literally a "fateful evil," referring here to an unavoidable accident</note> any more than the other person who suffered it.

§. 11. It is also consistent with **natural equity** <term>naturalis æquitas: a sense of justice derived from reason and the nature of human relationships</term> *that if a person under our authority <note>original: "homo noster"; literally "our man," referring in this context to a servant or slave</note> has caused damage to another without our own fault, we should either compensate for it ourselves or hand that person over to the injured party.* For a **servant** <term>servus: a person in a state of legal servitude</term> is certainly naturally liable for the reparation of damage caused by himself. However, since the servant does not possess goods from which reparation can be made, and since his person belongs to his **master** <term>dominus: the head of a household or owner</term>, it is indeed fair that the master either repairs the damage or surrenders the servant <note>This refers to the legal concept of "noxal surrender," where an owner avoids further liability by giving the person or animal that caused the harm to the victim</note>. For otherwise, a servant would be given license to injure everyone according to his whim, if reparation for damage could be obtained neither from him—who has nothing, and does not even own his own person—nor from his master. For even if mostly because of the injury [to] the master—

### CHAPTER VI.

...the owner himself might wish to punish the person with lashes or imprisonment, the injured party can in no way be satisfied by that <note>original: "læso inde haudquidquam satisfieri potest"; meaning that punishing a servant doesn't provide financial or material compensation to the victim</note>.

§. 12. Furthermore, the same principle seems equitable regarding *our animals*: when they have caused damage to another—even without our fault and having been moved spontaneously against the nature of their kind—the owner must either repair the damage or hand over the animal. For if I had been injured by an animal living in natural liberty, I could certainly have repaired my loss in whatever way possible by capturing or killing it; it seems this right surely could not have been taken away by another person’s **ownership** <term>dominium: legal right of property or ownership</term>. And since the owner derives profit from that animal, while I have suffered a loss from it—and because the restoration of a loss is a far more favorable claim than the acquisition of profit—it appears right to demand of the owner of the animal that he repair the damage caused, or, if the animal is not worth that much to him, that he surrender it as compensation for the harm done <term>noxæ deditio: the legal practice of surrendering the person or animal that caused damage to the victim as a way to settle the liability</term>.

§. 13. Thus, he who has caused *damage to another without **malicious intent*** <term>dolus malus: the deliberate intention to deceive or cause harm</term> ought to offer reparation of his own accord and testify that malice was absent from his part, so that the injured party does not regard him as an enemy and, in turn, attempt hostile acts. However, he who has injured another *through malice* must not only offer reparation for the damage of his own accord, but also show **repentance** <term>pœnitentia: a regret for one's actions coupled with a change of mind</term> for his deed and ask for forgiveness. In turn, the injured party, once reparation is obtained, is bound to grant pardon to the one who is repentant and seeking it, and to return to a state of friendship with him. For he who

On this account, vengeance is also condemned by **natural law** <term>lex naturalis: a system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature rather than rules of society</term> when it has no other purpose than to cause pain to those who have harmed us, and to satisfy our own spirits with their suffering. Indeed, it is fitting for mortals to be all the more inclined toward forgiving mutual offenses, as they themselves frequently violate the laws of the **Supreme Divinity** <note>original: "supremi Numinis"; referring to God</note>, and thus they themselves have daily need of forgiveness.

### CHAPTER VII.
## ON RECOGNIZING THE NATURAL
## Equality of Men.
### §. I.

Man is an animal not only most studious of his own preservation, but one in whom is implanted a certain delicate self-esteem. If anything is taken away from this, he is often no less disturbed than by an injury inflicted upon his body or his possessions. Indeed, within the very word "human" <note>original: "hominis"</note>, a certain inherent dignity is judged to exist. The final and most effective argument used to blunt the insolent insults of others is this: "Surely I am not a dog, but a human being just as you are" <note>original: "utiq; non canis sum, sed æque homo atq; tu"</note>. Since, therefore, human nature belongs equally to all,

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Chapter VII, building on the idea that human dignity requires mutual respect. It establishes the "second duty" of natural law: the requirement to treat others as natural equals.</meta>

->[E 5]<-

and no one would willingly or could be joined in society with another by whom they are not at least valued as an equal human being and a sharer of a common nature. Therefore, in the second place among the duties of any person toward any other, it is held: *That everyone should value and treat every other human being as naturally equal to themselves, or as an equal human.*

### § 2. 
Moreover, this equality of men consists not only in the fact that the strengths of adult humans are nearly equal—to the point that even the weaker can bring death to the stronger through treachery, or with the help of skill and the handiness of weapons—but also in this: that although one person may be endowed by nature with various gifts of mind and body over another, the precepts of **natural law** <term>lex naturalis: a universal system of justice believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</term> must nonetheless be practiced by them toward others, just as they expect the same from others. Nor should more license be granted to such a person for harming others. 

Just as, conversely, a stingy nature or a meager fortune does not, by itself, condemn anyone to a worse condition regarding the enjoyment of **common right** <term>jus commune: the basic legal and social entitlements shared by all members of a community</term> than others possess. Rather, whatever things one person can demand or expect from another, others also owe the same to them, all else being equal <note>original: "cæteris paribus"</note>; and whatever rule of law one establishes for others, it is most fitting that they follow it themselves. *For the obligation to cultivate a **social life** <term>vita socialis: the cooperative and organized way of living together that distinguishes humans from solitary animals</term> with other men binds all people equally; nor to one...*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the second duty of natural law: the recognition of natural equality among all human beings. It transitions from the physical and mental capacities of men to the moral and religious arguments for equality.</meta>

# BOOK I.

it is no more permissible to violate the **natural laws** <term>leges naturales: universal rules of conduct derived from reason and the shared nature of humanity</term> in one person than in another. Even if common arguments illustrating this equality are not lacking; for instance, that we all descend from the same stock, are born, nourished, and die in the same way; and that God has guaranteed a stable and unshakable fortune to no one. Just as the teachings of the Christian religion, in seeking the favor of the Divine <note>original: "Numinis"</note>, recommend not nobility, power, or riches, but sincerity of piety, which can be found as much in the humble as in those placed in an illustrious position.

### §. 3. 
Furthermore, from this equality it follows: that he who wishes to use the labor of others for his own advantage ought, in turn, to devote himself to their use as well. For he who demands that others indeed serve him, while he himself desires to remain always exempt, surely considers others to be unequal to himself. Hence, just as those who easily permit the same things to everyone that they allow for themselves are most suitable for **society** <term>societas: the bond of mutual cooperation and fellowship between humans</term>: so those are clearly unsociable who, while thinking themselves superior to others, want everything to be permitted to themselves alone; they claim honor for themselves above others and demand the principal share of things held in common, even where they possess no exceptional right over the rest. Whence also this is among the common duties of **natural law** <term>jus naturale: the system of justice based on the inherent dignity of the person</term>; *that no one, to whom a special right has not been acquired, should claim more for himself than for others, but should permit others to enjoy an equal right with himself.*

<summary>This page establishes that human equality is rooted in a shared biological nature and religious standing, leading to the moral requirement of reciprocity in social life. It concludes that no one should claim more rights than they are willing to grant others unless they have a specific, earned legal justification.</summary>

<keywords>natural equality, reciprocity, Christian religion, social duty, natural law, human dignity, fairness</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural equality from the previous page, moving from the general principle of recognizing others as equals to the practical application of this principle in distributing rights and property.</meta>

# CHAPTER VII.

### § 4. 
This same equality also shows how one ought to behave when **rights among others** <term>jus inter alios: the legal or moral principles governing the distribution of things between multiple parties</term> are to be distributed by him; namely, that he should treat them as equals, and grant nothing to one over the other except based on the merits of the case. For if it is done otherwise, the one who is sidelined is affected by both insult and injury, and the **natural dignity** <term>dignatio: the inherent worth or status granted to every human being by nature</term> given to him is taken away. From this it follows that a common resource ought to be divided into equal portions among equals by right. 

Where a thing does not allow for division, those who have an equal right ought to use it in common; and this should be done, if the quantity of the thing permits, as much as each person wants. But if the resource does not allow for that, they should then use it in a predefined way and proportionately to the number of those using it. For no other way can be thought of to observe equality. However, if the thing can neither be divided nor held in common, its use should either be alternating, or if even this is not successful, or if an equivalent cannot be provided to the rest, the thing must be awarded to one person by **lot** <note>original: "fortem" / "fors", referring to the practice of random selection to ensure fairness when resources are scarce</note>. For in cases of this kind, no more suitable remedy than **lot** <note>original: "fors"</note> can be found; because it removes any suspicion of contempt, and for the person it does not favor, it takes nothing away from their dignity.

### § 5. 
One sins against this duty through **pride** <term>superbia: the vice of overvaluing oneself while devaluing others</term>, by which someone prefers themselves to others for no reason, or for an insufficient one, and treats them as if they were unequal...

<meta>This page concludes the final section of the previous chapter (likely Chapter V, despite the previous page's label as Chapter VII) regarding the limits of property rights in emergencies. 64
OF BOOK I.

§ 24. Finally, it appears that **necessity** <term>necessitas: the legal and moral principle that extreme, life-threatening circumstances can justify actions that would normally be considered crimes or civil wrongs</term>, when involving our own affairs, grants us the indulgence of **destroying the property of others** <note>original: "res alienas perdendi"</note>. This is permissible, however, only if the danger to our own property arose through no fault of our own; if the danger cannot be turned away by any more convenient means; and if we do not destroy another person's more valuable item to save our own less valuable one. Furthermore, we must provide an appraisal of the item’s value and pay for it, provided that the item would not have perished anyway. If the other person’s property was destined to perish along with our own, but its loss ends up saving our property, we should share in the cost of the damage. This **fairness** <note>original: "aequitatem"</note> is generally followed by **maritime laws** <term>leges nauticae: specifically referring to the "Lex Rhodia de Jactu," which dictated that if cargo is jettisoned to save a ship, the loss is shared proportionately by all who benefited</term>. Similarly, if a fire breaks out and threatens my house, it is permissible to tear down a neighbor’s house, provided that those whose houses were saved by this action compensate the neighbor for his loss proportionally.

# CHAPTER VI.
## ON THE DUTY OF ANY PERSONS TOWARD ANY OTHERS,
### and first, on not harming others.

->§ 1.<-

Next follow the **duties which must be performed by a human being toward other human beings.** Some of these arise from the **common obligation by which the Creator willed all humans, as such, to be bound to one another.** Others, however, flow from a **specific institution introduced or accepted by humans, or from a certain acquired status of men.** The former must be performed by anyone toward anyone else; the latter, however, not <note>The text ends here with the catchword "nis," likely continuing as "nisi" (unless) on the following page.</note>

# CHAPTER VI.

...except toward certain persons, based on a specific condition or status. Hence, you might call the former **absolute duties** <note>original: "absoluta"</note> and the latter **hypothetical duties** <term>hypothetica: duties that only apply if certain conditions are met, such as entering into a contract or a specific social role like fatherhood or citizenship</term>.

->§ 2.<-

Among the absolute duties, or those which any person owes to any others, the first place is held by this: **that no one should harm another** <note>original: "ne quis alterum laedat"</note>. Indeed, this duty is the broadest of all, as it encompasses all humans simply as human beings. It is also the easiest to fulfill, since it consists of the mere cessation of action <note>meaning you fulfill this duty simply by *not* doing something harmful</note>, unless those passions which struggle against reason must occasionally be restrained. Moreover, this duty is the most necessary, because without it, the **social life** <term>socialis vita: a central concept in Natural Law referring to the peaceful, organized coexistence of humans</term> of mankind could in no way exist. For I can still live peacefully with someone even if he bestows no benefit upon me, or if he does not even exchange the commonest courtesies with me, provided that he harms me in no way. Indeed, from the vast majority of mortals, we desire nothing more than this; for favors and benefits are generally shared only among a small circle of people. But with a person who harms me, I can in no way live in peace. **Nature** has implanted in everyone such a tender love of self and of one’s own things that a person cannot help but use every means to repel anyone who attempts to bring harm to them.

->§ 3.<-

By this very duty, protection is provided not only for those things which belong to someone by **nature** itself—such as life, the body, limbs, **chastity** <note>original: "pudicitia," referring here to bodily integrity and sexual honor</note>, and **liberty**—but also for whatever things, by some human institution...

Thus, by whatever lawful title something becomes ours, this precept forbids that it be taken away, corrupted, harmed, or withdrawn from our use in whole or in part. Hence, by this very fact, all crimes by which harm is inflicted on others are understood to be forbidden; such as murder, wounding, beating, robbery, theft, fraud, and violence, whether inflicted directly or indirectly, immediately or through some medium, and other similar acts.

->§. 4.<-

Furthermore, it follows from this: **If harm has been inflicted or damage caused to someone by another in any way that can rightly be imputed to them** <note>imputed: legally or morally attributed to a person as their responsibility</note>, **that damage must be repaired by that person as much as possible.** For otherwise, the command not to harm anyone or cause damage would be pointless if, when someone is actually harmed, they were forced to swallow the loss for nothing, while the one who caused the harm could enjoy the fruit of their injustice safely and without making a refund. Nor would the depravity of mortals ever refrain from mutual harm without the necessity of **restitution** <term>restitutio: the legal and moral obligation to restore what was taken or to compensate for a loss</term>; nor would it be easy for the victim of the damage to settle their mind toward living in peace with the other person as long as they have not obtained **reparation** <term>reparatio: the process of making amends for a wrong or injury</term> for it.

->§. 5.<-

Although "damage" <note>original: "Damnum"</note> might seem strictly to refer to the harming of physical things, it is understood by us here so broadly as to denote every harm, corruption, decrease, or taking away of that which is already ours, or the interception

### CHAPTER VI.

<sig>E 2</sig>

...of that which we ought to have by a **perfect right** <term>jure perfecto: a legal right that is enforceable and certain, rather than a mere moral expectation</term>; whether it was given by nature, or assigned by a subsequent human act or law; or finally, the omission or denial of some performance which another was bound to provide to us from a **perfect obligation** <term>obligatio perfecta: a duty that corresponds to a legal right and can be compelled by law</term>. But if those things are intercepted which were owed only out of an **imperfect obligation** <note>original: "obligatione duntaxat imperfecta"; an ethical duty, such as charity or gratitude, which cannot be legally forced</note>, it is not considered as **damage caused** <note>original: "damnum datum"</note> for which compensation is required. For it would be inappropriate to count the failure to receive such things as a loss, or to demand compensation for things which I could only expect from another as a voluntary favor, and which I cannot count as my own until I have actually received them.

->§. 6.<-

Furthermore, under the name of "damage" falls not only our property, or what is owed to us, which is harmed, lost, or seized; but also the **fruits** <term>fructus: the profits, offspring, or products derived from an asset, such as crops from land or interest on money</term> that come from the thing itself, whether they have already been collected or are still anticipated, provided that the owner would have collected them: deducting, however, the expenses that were necessary for gathering those fruits. The valuation of anticipated fruits is increased or decreased depending on how close or far they were from the conclusion of that uncertain outcome. Finally, whatever follows from an **injury** <note>original: "læsione"</note> as if by natural necessity is also counted as part of the total damage.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VI, section 6, regarding how damage is calculated and who is held responsible for it. The previous page established that "damage" includes both lost property and lost profits (fructus).</meta>

### BOOK I.

...not only directly and *by oneself*, but also *through others*.
Damage directly caused by one person can also be attributed to another <note>original: "imputari"; in a legal sense, to be held responsible for an action even if not the physical actor</note>, because they contributed something to that act, either by doing something or by failing to do something they were obligated to do. Sometimes among several people who participated in the same act, one is considered the **primary cause** <term>principalis causa: the main person or instigator who caused an event to happen</term>, while another is in the position of an **accessory** <note>original: "accessorij"; those who provide assistance or support to the main actor</note>; sometimes they all "walk at an equal pace" <note>original: "pari passu"; meaning they share equal responsibility and legal standing</note>. Regarding these individuals, it must be observed that they are only held to the reparation of the damage if they were truly a cause of the damage and contributed some weight toward the whole damage or a part of it. However, where someone did not contribute any real effort to the act itself by which the damage was caused, nor brought it about beforehand so that it would be undertaken, nor shared in any part of the profit; even though they might be bound by some sin on the occasion of that act, they will not be held to the **restitution of the damage** <term>restitutio damni: the legal requirement to make amends or pay back the value of what was lost</term>; such as those who rejoice in others' misfortunes, who praise or excuse a **damage** <note>original: "damnum"</note> already committed, who wish for it before it happens, or who favor or flatter the perpetrator while it is happening.

->§. 8.<-

*Where several have participated in a single act from which damage has resulted, the person held responsible in the first place will be the one who, by command or another method involving some necessity, drove others to the deed. The perpetrator of the **crime** <term>facinus: a wicked deed or criminal act</term>, who was not permitted to refuse their **service** <note>original: "ministerium"; the performance of a task or duty, often in a subordinate role</note>, will be considered merely as an instrument. One who approaches the crime without necessity—*

<meta>catchword: sit,</meta>

<summary>This page explains how legal liability for damage is shared among multiple people, distinguishing between those who contribute to the harm and those who merely approve of it. It establishes that only those who actively contribute to or profit from the harm are legally required to pay restitution, while the instigator is held most responsible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and moral discussion of liability for damages. The previous page discussed how an individual can be responsible for damage caused by others if they contributed to the act as an accessory or instigator.</meta>

### CHAPTER VI.

...it happens, he himself will be held responsible in the first place, then the others who contributed anything to the **deed** <note>original: "facinus"; refers here to an injurious act or offense</note>. However, this is done in such a way that if the reparation for the damage has been made by the primary parties, the secondary parties are immune from the same obligation. (Note that this is different in the case of **punishments** <note>In criminal law, every participant might be punished individually, but in civil law, once the victim's loss is paid for, the debt is settled.</note>.) If several people committed a deed through a **conspiracy** <note>original: "conspirationem"; a mutual agreement to act together</note>, all are held liable for each individual, and each individual for all. Thus, if everyone is caught, each individual should contribute a proportional share to repair the damage. Where only one person is caught and the others have escaped, this person will be held responsible to pay for everyone. Furthermore, if some of those caught are not able to pay, the wealthy participants will be held liable for the **whole amount** <term>in solidum: a legal principle where a single party can be held responsible for the entire obligation if the other parties cannot pay</term>. But if several people joined in the same deed without a conspiracy, and it can be clearly distinguished how much each person contributed to the damage, each will be held responsible for paying only for the damage that proceeded from themselves. If one person pays the full amount, the others are freed from the reparation of the damage.

§. 9. A person is held to the reparation of damage not only if they harmed another with **malicious intent** <note>original: "dolo malo"; a legal term for deliberate, deceitful intent to cause harm</note>, but also if they did so through **negligence or fault** <note>original: "negligentiam seu culpam"</note> that was easy to avoid, even if they did not directly intend it. For it is no small part of **sociality** <term>socialitas: the principle that human beings are naturally inclined toward and obligated to maintain a peaceful, functioning society</term> to act so circumspectly that our daily interactions do not become a source of fear or become intolerable to others. Indeed, a person is often bound to exercise careful diligence because of a specific obligation. Furthermore, even the **lightest...** <note>The text continues on the next page with "levissima," likely referring to the "lightest fault" or negligence.</note>

->### BOOK I.<-

...slightest fault <note>original: "levissima culpa"</note> can be enough to require the necessity of making reparation, provided that the nature of the situation does not, as it were, inherently reject the most exact diligence; or that the fault does not lie more with the person who receives the damage than with the one who causes it; or unless a great disturbance or the circumstances of the business do not allow for accurate circumspection; for instance, if someone in the heat of battle, while brandishing weapons, should wound someone standing very close to him.

§. 10. However, one who has caused harm through **accidental misfortune** <note>original: "casum fortuitum"</note> and without any fault of their own is not bound to make reparation. Because since nothing was committed that can be blamed on them, there is no reason why the one who acted against their will should pay for a fatal evil any more than the other person who suffered it.

§. 11. It is also consistent with **natural equity** <term>equity: a principle of fairness used to provide justice where the strict application of the law might be too harsh</term> that if **our servant** <note>original: "homo noster"; literally "our man," referring here to a servant or an enslaved person under one's legal power</note> has caused damage to another without fault on our part, we should either compensate for the damage ourselves or surrender that person to the injured party. For a servant is naturally liable for the reparation of damage they have caused. Since, however, the servant does not possess property from which reparation can be made, and their body belongs to the master, it is surely fair that the master either repair the damage or surrender the servant. Otherwise, a servant would be given license to harm everyone according to their whim, if reparation for the damage could be obtained neither from the servant, who has nothing—and does not even own himself—nor from the master. For even if, especially on account of the injury, the master...

### CHAPTER VI.

...[even if the master] should wish to punish him with lashes or imprisonment, the injured party can in no way be satisfied by that <note>The author argues that private punishment of a servant by a master does nothing to compensate the victim of the servant's actions.</note>.

§. 12. Furthermore, the same principle seems fair regarding our **animals**: when they cause damage to another—even without any fault on our part, and even if they were moved to act spontaneously in a way contrary to the nature of their species <note>original: "contra naturam generis"; this refers to an animal acting unusually aggressively for its kind</note>—the owner should either repair the damage or surrender the animal <term>noxae deditio: a legal principle where an owner can escape further liability by handing over the specific person or animal that caused the harm</term>. For if I had been injured by an animal living in natural liberty, I could certainly have recovered my loss in some way by capturing or killing it; it seems this right could not have been taken away simply because someone else now claims ownership of that animal. Moreover, since the owner derives profit from that animal, while I have suffered a loss from it—and because the law favors the reparation of loss far more than the acquisition of profit—it is clear that one may rightly demand the owner repair the damage or, if the animal is not worth that much to him, hand it over as compensation for the harm.

§. 13. Thus, whoever has caused *damage to another without malicious intent* <note>original: "dolum malum"; a technical legal term for deliberate intent to deceive or harm</note> ought to offer reparation of their own accord and testify that they bore no malice. This prevents the injured party from viewing them as an enemy and attempting to retaliate in kind. However, he who has injured another *through malice* <note>original: "per malitiam"</note> must not only offer to repair the damage of his own accord, but must also show repentance for his deed and ask for pardon. In turn, once reparation has been obtained, the injured party is bound to grant pardon to the one who is repentant and seeking it, and to return to a state of grace with him. For he who...

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...is not willing to be satisfied with reparation of the damage and repentance, but goes so far as to seek vengeance with his own hand <note>original: "vindictam utiq; manu sibi quæsitum it"; this refers to "extrajudicial" or private revenge outside the legal system</note>, he does nothing else than indulge the bitterness of his own mind, and thus breaks the peace among men for a trivial reason. On this account, vengeance is condemned also by **natural law** <term>natural law: a system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature rather than the rules of society</term>, which has no other end in view than to cause grief to those who have harmed us, and to satisfy our own mind with their pain. Mortals ought to be the more prone to forgive mutual offenses the more frequently they themselves violate the laws of the **Supreme Being** <note>original: "supremi Numinis"; referring to God as the author of natural law</note>, and thus daily stand in need of forgiveness themselves.

### CHAPTER VII.
# ON ACKNOWLEDGING THE NATURAL
## EQUALITY of Men.

->§. 1.<-

Man is an animal not only most eager to preserve himself, but also one in whom is implanted a certain *delicate self-esteem*. If anything is taken away from this self-esteem, he is usually no less moved than if harm were done to his body or his property. Indeed, in the very word "man" there is judged to be a certain dignity <note>original: "dignatio"; a sense of worth or status inherent to being human</note>, so that the final and most effective argument by which the insolent insults of others are beaten back is held to be this: "I am certainly not a dog, but as much a man as you." Since, therefore, human nature belongs equally to all,

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

...and no one would willingly wish or be able to associate with someone by whom he is not esteemed at least as an equal man and a sharer in a common nature. Therefore, in the **second** place among the duties of any person toward any other, it is held: *That everyone should value and treat every other person as being by nature equal to themselves, or as an equal man.* <note>original: "æque homo"; literally "equally a man," implying that the status of being human is a shared baseline regardless of social rank.</note>

->§. 2.<-

This equality of men consists not only in the fact that the physical strength of adult humans is nearly equal—at least to the extent that even a weaker person can bring death to one who is stronger, whether by ambush or through skill and the clever use of weapons—but also in the fact that, although one person may be endowed by nature with various gifts of mind or body above another, the precepts of **natural law** <term>natural law: a system of universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</term> must nonetheless be observed by him toward others, just as he expects the same from them. No greater license is granted to him to inflict injuries upon others. Conversely, nature’s stinginess toward a person, or a meager fortune, does not in itself condemn them to a worse condition regarding the enjoyment of **common right** <note>original: "communis juris"; the basic legal standing or rights shared by all members of humanity</note> than others. Rather, whatever one person may demand or expect from another, others also—all things being equal—owe to that same person; and whatever rule of law a person establishes for others, it is most fitting that he himself abide by it. *For the **obligation** <note>original: "obligatio"</note> to cultivate a social life with other humans binds all people equally; nor to one...*

<summary>This page argues that human equality is a fundamental duty of natural law, based not just on the shared physical vulnerability of humans but on the reciprocal obligation to treat others as equals in right and dignity.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental equality of all human beings. Having established that humans share a common nature, the text now addresses why this equality must be respected regardless of one's physical or social advantages, grounding the argument in both common experience and religious teachings.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...no more is it permitted to violate the **laws of nature** <term>laws of nature: the moral rules governing human conduct discovered through reason</term> against one person than against another. Even common-sense arguments illustrating this equality are not lacking; for instance, that we all descend from the same stock <note>A reference to the common origin of humanity, likely reflecting the biblical account of Adam and Eve.</note>, and are born, nourished, and die in the same manner; and that God has guaranteed to no one a stable and unshakable fortune. Just as the decrees of the Christian religion, in preparing us for the favor of the Divine <note>original: "Numinis"; a term referring to the power or will of God.</note>, recommend not nobility, power, or wealth, but rather a sincerity of piety, which can be found as much in a humble person as in one placed in an illustrious position.

->§. 3.<-

Furthermore, it follows from this equality that he who wishes to use the labor of others for his own benefit must, in turn, make himself available for their use as well. For he who demands that others indeed serve him, while he himself wishes always to be exempt, surely considers others to be unequal to himself. Thus, just as those who easily permit the same things to everyone that they allow to themselves are most suitable for social life; so too are those clearly unsociable who, while thinking themselves superior to others, want everything to be permitted to them alone, and claim honor for themselves above others, and the lion's share of things held in common, where they possess no exceptional right over the rest. Whence it is also among the common duties of natural law: *that no one, for whom a special right has not been acquired, should claim more for himself than for others, but should permit others to enjoy an equal right with himself.*

<meta>catchword: §. 4.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that natural equality requires reciprocity: if we expect others to serve us or follow moral laws, we must do the same for them. It reinforces this by noting that social status and wealth do not grant a higher standing in the eyes of God or the laws of nature.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, equality, reciprocity, Christian religion, piety, social duty, common rights</keywords>

### CHAPTER VII.

->§. 4.<-

This same equality also shows how one ought to behave when *the right among others* is to be distributed by him; namely, that he should treat them as equals, and grant nothing to one over the other beyond the merits of the case. For if it were otherwise, he who is passed over is affected by both insult and injury, and the dignity <term>dignity: original 'dignatio', referring to the inherent worth or status granted to a person by nature</term> given by nature is taken away from him. From this it follows that a common thing ought to be divided by right into equal portions among equals. Where a thing does not admit of division, those who have an equal right ought to use it in common; and that, if the quantity of the thing <note>original: "quantitas re"; likely "res" (the thing) in the original manuscript.</note> allows, as much as each person wishes. But if the thing does not allow for that, they should then use it in a prescribed manner and proportionately to the number of users. For no other way of observing equality can be devised. But if the thing can neither be divided nor held in common, its use should be either alternating, or if even this does not work, or an equivalent cannot be provided to the rest, the thing must be awarded to one by *lot* <note>original: "sortem"; the practice of deciding by chance to ensure fairness when resources are indivisible.</note>. For in cases of this kind, no more suitable remedy than *lots* can be found; for it removes any suspicion of contempt, and takes nothing away from the dignity of the person whom it does not favor.

->§. 5.<-

One sins against this duty through *pride*, by which a person, for no reason or for an insufficient one, prefers himself to others, and treats them as if they were unequal—

# BOOK I.

...despises them in comparison to himself. *We say "for no reason."* <note>original: "Nulla de causa dicimus"</note> For where someone has rightly acquired a right for himself, which gives him preference over others, he can correctly exercise and defend that right, yet without empty arrogance <term>arrogance: original 'fastum', a haughty display of superiority</term> or contempt for others. Just as, conversely, anyone rightly grants to another the preference and honor that is owed to them. Furthermore, a certain honorable *humility* <note>original: "humilitas"</note> is always a companion to true generosity <term>generosity: original 'generositas', here meaning noble-mindedness or greatness of soul</term>, which consists in the reflection we make upon the weakness of our nature and the errors we might have committed in the past, or could commit hereafter, which are no smaller than those that others can commit. Through this, it is brought about that we prefer ourselves to no one, considering that others can use their free will <term>free will: original 'libero arbitrio', the capacity for self-governance</term> just as well as we can, a faculty in which they are equally powerful. The legitimate use of this will is the only thing a human being can truly consider his own, and the only thing by which he can value or despise himself. However, to exalt oneself for no reason is indeed a ridiculous vice <note>original: "vitium"</note>; both because it is foolish in itself to make much of oneself for nothing, and because it treats everyone else as fools, as if they would judge things without any reason.

->§ 6.<-

One sins even more if one shows *contempt toward others* through external signs, deeds, words, facial expressions, laughter, or any kind of insult <note>original: "contumelia"</note>. This sin is to be considered all the worse because the minds of others are more harshly provoked to anger and reveng—

CHAPTER V.

For indeed, through this, glory and reputation are violated—the integrity and strength of which form the basis of all the soul's pleasure.

## CHAPTER VIII.
### ON THE COMMON DUTIES OF HUMANITY.

->§ 1.<-

Among the duties of anyone toward anyone else, and those which must be performed for the sake of common sociality <term>sociality: original 'socialitatem', the natural inclination of humans to live in community and mutual support</term>, *this is placed in the third position: that every person should promote the utility of another, as far as they conveniently can.* For since nature has established a certain kinship among human beings, it is not enough merely to have not harmed or despised others; rather, those things must also be bestowed upon others, or mutually shared, through which mutual benevolence <note>original: "benevolentia"</note> among humans may be nourished. We benefit others either *indefinitely* or *definitely*; and this is done in such a way that either *nothing is lost to us* or *something is lost*.

->§ 2.<-

One promotes the advantages of others *indefinitely* <note>original: "Indefinite", meaning in a general or unspecified way, rather than a specific act for a specific person</note> who properly cultivates his own soul and body so that useful actions may proceed from him toward others; or if, through the ingenuity of his skill, he discovers such things,

Hence, those must be judged to sin against this duty who learn no honorable skill, but pass through life in silence, holding their soul only in place of salt <note>original: "animam pro sale habent." This is a classical idiom suggesting that some people have no more purpose than a pig, whose soul (life-force) was thought to exist only as salt to keep its meat from rotting until it could be eaten</note> to keep from decaying; they are born merely to be a number and to consume the fruits of the earth. Also included are those who, content with the wealth left by their ancestors, believe they may safely sacrifice to idleness because of the industry <term>industry: 'industria', meaning hard work and purposeful activity</term> of others has already produced the means by which they live. And those who brood alone over riches they have found, but provide no part for their own kin. Likewise, those who, like pigs, gladden no one except by their death. And other such idle weights upon the earth of that same sort <note>original: "id farinae", literally "of that same flour/batch"</note>.

->§ 3.<-

Conversely, to those who set out to deserve well of the human race <note>original: "mortalium genere"</note>, others owe this: that they do not envy them, nor throw obstacles in the way of their noble endeavors. Furthermore, if there is no other way to repay them in kind, they should at least strive to promote the celebration of their memory and their fame, which is the principal fruit of their labors.

->§ 4.<-

Especially, however, to be unwilling to dispense to others those benefits which can be provided without our own loss, trouble, or labor, is considered a detestable malice and inhumanity. These are usually called ***matters of harmless utility*** <term>res innoxiae utilitatis: acts of kindness that benefit the receiver while costing the giver absolutely nothing</term>, or things which profit the recipient but do not burden the giver. ### CHAPTER VIII.

...[faithful counsel] to give to one who is deliberating, and to kindly show the way to one who is wandering. Thus, if someone should no longer wish to possess a certain thing because of an excessive supply, or because keeping it is bothersome to them, why should they not prefer to leave it intact so that it might provide use to others—provided they are not enemies—rather than destroying it? In this way, it is not right to waste food <note>original: "nutrimentum"</note> when we are satisfied, nor to block or hide a spring when we have drunk our fill, nor to destroy the signs of navigation or travel after we have used them. To this category belong the modest alms <note>original: "stipes", referring to small donations or coins given to the poor</note> of the wealthy contributed to the needy; likewise that kindness <term>humanity: 'humanitas', the moral duty to treat others with kindness and respect as fellow human beings</term> which is shown for a just cause to travelers, especially where some calamity has overtaken them; and other acts of that kind.

->§ 5.<-

A more sublime degree of kindness is to confer something upon another for free out of unique benevolence, consisting of expense or laborious effort, by which their needs may be relieved or some remarkable advantage may be secured for them. These are called **benefits** in the highest sense <note>original: "κατ' εξοχὴν" (kat' exochen), a Greek term meaning preeminently or par excellence</note>, and they are the most suitable material for earning distinguished praise, provided that greatness of soul <note>original: "magnitudo animi"</note> and prudence have properly tempered them. Their distribution and manner are generally governed by the condition of both the giver and the receiver. In these matters, it must be observed above all else that our kindness does not harm those very people to whom it seems to be done, nor others; and secondly, that the kindness should not be greater

### BOOK I.

...than one's means; second, so that the gift is granted according to the worth <term>dignitas: the social standing or moral merit of a person</term> of each recipient, and especially to those who have deserved well; and finally, according to how much each person needs our help, while also observing the degrees of relationship between people. We must also consider what is most necessary for each individual, and what they can or cannot achieve with or without our assistance. The manner of giving also adds much grace to benefits; therefore, let us give with a cheerful face, promptly, and with an open declaration of goodwill.

->§ 6.<-

In turn, there ought to exist in the person who has received a benefit a **grateful spirit** <note>original: "gratus animus"</note>, by which they show that the gift was welcome to them. For this reason, the recipient favors the giver and seeks an opportunity to return like for like, or even more, as far as is possible. For it is not strictly necessary that we return exactly the same value as what was given; often, eagerness and effort alone fulfill the obligation. This is provided, however, that there is nothing that can be rightly held against the one who claims to have given a benefit. For if someone pulled me out of the water, I owe them nothing if they had thrown me into it in the first place.

->§ 7.<-

Furthermore, because benefits are so well-suited to binding the hearts of men together, it is all the more fitting for the one who has received them to apply themselves to showing gratitude. At the very least, we should not allow someone who, trusting in us, was the first to do us a kindness, to be in a worse condition because of that act; nor should the benefi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword indicates the word is 'beneficium' (benefit/kindness).</note>

### CHAPTER VIII.

...we should only accept a benefit with the intention of striving to ensure the giver does not rightfully regret giving it. For if, for a specific reason, we do not wish to be particularly obligated to someone, it was permissible to skillfully refuse the offered benefit. Indeed, if there were no necessity to return such a favor, a person would act against reason if they recklessly threw away their own possessions or granted a benefit that they foresaw would be wasted. In this way, all beneficence <term>beneficentia: the act of doing good or being kind</term> and trust among men would be removed, and along with it all benevolence; nor would there be any mutual aid among them, nor any starting point for winning over the goodwill of others.

->§. 8.<-

*Although there is no <term>injury: a legal violation of rights</term> in an ungrateful mind by itself, the name of an "ingrate" is nonetheless considered more foul, more odious, and more detestable than that of "unjust."* For it is considered a sign of a very base and degenerate mind to declare oneself unworthy of the judgment that another has made regarding one's own integrity; it suggests that a person cannot even be moved to feel a sense of humanity by benefits—which can soothe even the brute beasts. In the civil court <note>original: "foro civili"</note>, however, a <term>legal action: a formal lawsuit</term> <note>original: "actio"</note> is not usually granted for simple ingratitude, or in cases where someone simply forgets a benefit and neglects to return it when given the opportunity. For the best part of a benefit is lost if a *legal action* were granted, as if it were a debt for a specific sum of money; for it would then begin to be considered a loan. And since it is a most honorable thing to return grace, it will cease to be so splendidly honorable if it becomes a necessity. Finally, to this...

...is unwilling to rest content with the reparation of damage and repentance <term>reparatione damni: the legal or moral act of making amends for a loss</term>, but goes about seeking revenge by his own hand, he does nothing else but indulge the bitterness of his own mind, and so breaks the peace among men for a pointless reason. For this reason, revenge <note>original: "vindicta"</note> is also condemned by natural law <term>naturalis lex: universal moral principles discovered through reason rather than through written statutes</term> if it has no purpose other than to cause pain to those who harmed us and to satisfy our own minds with their suffering. Mortals should be even more inclined to forgive mutual offenses because they themselves so frequently violate the laws of the Supreme Deity, and thus they themselves need forgiveness every day.

## CHAPTER VII.
# ON RECOGNIZING THE NATURAL
## Equality of Men.

->§. 1.<-

Man is an animal not only most devoted to his own preservation, but one in whom is implanted a certain delicate self-esteem. If anything is taken away from this esteem, he is often no less provoked than by harm done to his body or his property. Indeed, the very word "human" is judged to carry a certain dignity <term>dignatio: a sense of inherent worth or status</term>. Thus, the final and most effective argument used to blunt the insolent insults of others is this: "I am certainly not a dog, but as much a human being as you." Since human nature <note>original: "natura humana"</note> belongs equally to everyone...

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

...and no one would willingly want or be able to be associated with another by whom he is not valued at least as an equal human being and a sharer of a common nature. Therefore, in the second place among the duties of any person toward any other, it is held: *That each person should value and treat every other human being as naturally equal to himself, or as an equal human being.*

->§. 2.<-

Moreover, this equality of men consists not only in the fact that the physical strength of adult human beings is roughly equal—to the extent that even a weaker person can bring death to a stronger one through ambush, or with the help of skill and the handling of weapons—but also in this: that although one person may be endowed by nature with various gifts of mind or body over another, nevertheless the precepts of natural law <term>lex naturalis: universal moral principles discovered through reason rather than through written statutes</term> must be exercised by him toward others just as he himself expects the same from others. Nor should more license be granted to such a person to inflict injuries upon others. Just as, on the other hand, no person is condemned by a "stingy" nature or a meager fortune to be of a lower condition regarding the enjoyment of common right <term>jus commune: the shared legal and moral protections available to every member of the human race</term> than others. Rather, what one person can demand or expect from another, others also—all things being equal <note>original: "cæteris paribus"</note>—owe to that same person; and whatever right a person establishes over others, it is most fitting that he himself should abide by it. *Indeed, the obligation to cultivate a social life <term>vita socialis: the organized, cooperative existence of humans in a community</term> with other human beings binds all men equally;* nor to one...

<summary>This page defines human equality not as equal physical ability, but as an equal moral standing under natural law. It argues that no amount of talent or strength grants a person the right to mistreat others or escape their social obligations.</summary>
