<meta>This page continues the argument from page 73 regarding the fundamental equality of human beings. It concludes the thought that because we share a common nature, the obligations of natural law apply to everyone equally, regardless of their social standing or physical strength.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...social bond <note>Picking up the sentence from the previous page: "Indeed, the obligation to cultivate a [social bond]..."</note> is no more permitted to violate natural laws in one person than in another. Nor are popular reasons lacking to illustrate this equality; for instance, that we all descend from the same stock, are born, are nourished, and die in the same manner; and that God has guaranteed no one a stable and unshaken fortune. Similarly, the decrees of the Christian religion, in order to gain the favor of the Deity, do not recommend nobility, power, or riches, but rather sincerity of piety, which can be found as much in a person of humble station as in one of illustrious rank.

§. 3. Furthermore, it follows from this equality: that he who wishes to use the labor of others for his own benefit must, in turn, make himself available for their use. For he who demands that others serve him, while he himself wishes to remain forever exempt from service, surely considers others to be unequal to himself. Hence, just as those who readily allow the same rights to all that they allow to themselves are most fit for society, so those who consider themselves superior to others are clearly unsociable <term>insociabiles: individuals whose behavior or attitudes make them unfit for living in a peaceful, cooperative community</term>. These individuals want everything to be permitted to them alone, arrogating honor to themselves above others, and claiming the lion's share of things held in common where they possess no special right <term>jus eximium: a specific, exceptional legal privilege that sets one above the common standard</term> over the rest. For this reason, it is among the common duties of natural law <term>lex naturalis: the universal moral rules understood through human reason</term>: *that no one, who has not acquired a specific right, should claim more for himself than for others, but should instead permit others to enjoy an equal right alongside himself.*

<meta>Catchword: §. 4</meta>

# CHAPTER VII. 75

§. 4. This same equality also shows how one ought to behave when a right is to be distributed *among others*; namely, that he should treat them as equals, and grant nothing to one over the other beyond the merits of the case <note>original: "merita causæ"; referring to the specific legal or moral facts that might justify a different treatment</note>. For if it is done otherwise, the one who is disregarded is affected by both insult and injury, and the dignity <term>dignatio: the natural worth or status granted to every human being by virtue of their shared humanity</term> given to him by nature is taken away. From this it follows that a common thing should rightfully be divided into equal portions among equals. Where the thing does not admit of division, those who have an equal right ought to use it in common; and that, if the quantity of the thing permits, as much as each person wants. But if the thing does not allow for that, they should then use the thing in a predefined manner, and proportionately to the number of users. For no other way can be devised to observe equality. However, if the thing can neither be divided nor held in common, its use should be alternating; or, if even this does not work, and an equivalent cannot be provided to the others, the thing must be awarded to one by *lot* <term>fors: a method of deciding by chance, such as drawing straws or casting dice, to ensure fairness when resources cannot be shared</term>. For in cases of this kind, no more convenient remedy than the *lot* can be found; because it removes any suspicion of contempt and, for the one it does not favor, it takes nothing away from their dignity.

§. 5. One sins against this duty through *pride*, by which someone, for no reason or for an insufficient one, prefers himself to others, and treats them as if they were unequal...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 75 regarding the social obligations arising from natural equality. HEADER: BOOK I.

...[from the previous page: takes nothing away from them, but] looks down on them before himself. *We say for no reason.* For when someone has rightfully acquired a right for himself, which gives him preference over others, he can correctly exercise and defend it, yet without empty pride or contempt for others. Just as, conversely, anyone should rightly grant to another the preference and honor that is due to them. For the rest, a certain honorable *humility* is always a companion to true nobility of spirit <term>generositas: in this context, a "noble-mindedness" or greatness of soul that recognizes one's own limits</term>. This humility consists in the reflection we make upon the weakness of our nature <note>original: "infirmitate naturæ nostræ"</note> and the errors we might have committed in the past, or may commit in the future—errors which are no smaller than those others might commit. Through this reflection, it is brought about that we do not prefer ourselves to anyone else, considering that others can use their free will <term>liberum arbitrium: the capacity of a human being to choose their own actions</term> just as well as we can, a faculty in which they are equally capable. The legitimate use of this free will is the only thing a person can truly claim as their own, and the only standard by which they can value or despise themselves. To puff oneself up for no reason is truly a ridiculous vice; both because it is foolish in itself to think highly of oneself for nothing, and because it treats everyone else as fools, as if they would value him without any rational cause.

§. 6. An even greater offense is committed if someone shows *contempt* toward others through outward signs, such as deeds, words, facial expressions, laughter, or any kind of insult. This transgression is to be judged all the more severe because the minds of others are more harshly driven toward anger and re- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "ciscen-" indicates the next page begins with "ulciscendum," referring to revenge or seeking redress.</note>

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>

...[are] incited to a desire for revenge <note>original: "vindiciscendi libidinem"</note>. To such an extent that many are found who would prefer to expose their life to present danger, and even more to break off peace with others, than to suffer an unavenged insult. For through such an insult, one's glory and reputation are violated—and it is upon the integrity and vigor of that reputation that all pleasure of the soul depends.

## CHAPTER VIII.
# ON THE GENERAL DUTIES OF HUMANITY.

-> §. 1. <-

Among the duties of any person toward any other, which must be practiced for the sake of common sociability <term>socialitas: the inherent drive and necessity for human beings to live peacefully in a community</term>, this is placed in the third position: *that everyone should promote the utility of another as much as they conveniently can.* For since nature has established a certain kinship among men, it is not enough merely to have not harmed or despised others; rather, certain benefits must also be bestowed upon others, or mutually shared, so that mutual benevolence <note>original: "benevolentia"</note> among men may be nourished. We benefit others either *indefinitely* or *definitely*; and we do so *either in such a way that nothing is lost to us, or something is.*

§. 2. One promotes the advantages of others *indefinitely* when they properly cultivate their own soul and body, so that useful actions might proceed from themselves toward others; or if, through the ingenuity <term>solertia: a combination of skill, cleverness, and practical wisdom</term> of their talent, they discover such things...

§. 3. In turn, others owe this to those who resolve to serve the human race well: that they do not envy them, nor cast obstacles in the way of their noble efforts. Furthermore, if there is no other way to do deeds equal to theirs, they should at least help promote the celebration of their memory and fame, which is the chief fruit of such labors.

# CHAPTER VIII.

...to give faithful counsel <note>The original text "filium" is likely an error for "consilium" (counsel), as confirmed by the vocabulary list at the bottom of the page.</note> to one who is deliberating, and to kindly show the way to one who is wandering. Thus, if someone no longer wishes to possess a certain thing, either because of an excessive abundance or because its safekeeping has become a burden, why should he not prefer to leave it intact so that it may be of use to others (who are not enemies) rather than destroying it? Likewise, it is not right to waste food when we are full, nor to block or hide a spring when we have drunk our fill, nor to destroy markers of navigation or travel after we have used them. To this category belong the modest donations <term>stipes: small offerings or alms often given in a religious or charitable context</term> given by the wealthy to the needy; likewise that kindness which is shown for a just cause to travelers, especially when some calamity has overwhelmed them; and other things of this sort.

**§. 5.** A higher degree of humanity <term>humanitas: the virtue of kindness, philanthropy, and civilization</term> is to bestow something upon another for free out of singular benevolence, consisting of expense or laborious effort, by which their necessities may be relieved or some distinguished benefit may be secured for them. These are called, preeminently <note>original: "κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν" (kat’ exochēn), a Greek phrase meaning "par excellence" or "above all others"</note>, *benefits* <term>beneficia: voluntary acts of kindness or favors</term>, which are the most suitable material for earning distinguished praise, provided that greatness of soul <term>magnitudo animi: the virtue of being "great-souled" or magnanimous</term> and prudence have properly tempered them. Their distribution and measure are generally governed by the condition of both the giver and the receiver. In these matters, this must be observed above all: that the kindness does not harm those very people whom it seems to benefit, nor others; and furthermore, that the kindness is not greater

### CHAPTER VIII.

...receive a benefit <note>The text begins mid-word with "-cium," completing "beneficium" from the previous page.</note>, unless with the intention of striving to ensure that the giver does not rightfully regret his gift. For if, for some specific reason, we do not wish to be particularly indebted to someone, it is permitted to skillfully decline the offered kindness. Indeed, if there were no necessity to return a favor, a person would act against reason if they recklessly threw away their own goods, or if they conferred a favor that they foresaw would be wasted. In such a case, all beneficence and trust among men would be abolished, along with all benevolence; there would be no more mutual help given freely, nor any starting point for establishing goodwill.

**§. 8. *Although there is no "injury" <term>injuria: in a legal sense, a violation of another's rights that can be prosecuted</term> in an ungrateful mind in and of itself*:** the name of an "ingrate" is nonetheless considered more foul, more hateful, and more detestable than that of an "unjust" person. For it is judged to be the mark of a truly base and degenerate soul for a man to declare himself unworthy of the high opinion another held regarding his integrity; it suggests he cannot be moved even by benefits—which even soothe wild animals—to feel a sense of human kindness. In a civil court <term>forum civile: the arena of public law and litigation</term>, however, no legal action <term>actio: the right to sue or seek a legal remedy</term> is usually granted for simple ingratitude—that is, where someone merely forgets a favor or neglects to return it when the opportunity arises. For the best part of a benefit <term>beneficium: a voluntary act of kindness or favor</term> is lost if a legal action is allowed as if it were a debt of a specific sum of money; it would then begin to be a loan rather than a gift. Furthermore, while it is a most honorable thing to return gratitude <term>gratia: the virtue of thankfulness and reciprocity</term>, it ceases to be so magnificently honorable if it becomes a legal necessity. Finally, to this...

And for that very reason, I gave a benefit—that is, I did not stipulate <term>stipulatus: a formal verbal contract in Roman law where one party asks and the other promises</term> for the repayment of what I gave—so that there might be an opportunity for the other person to show that they returned gratitude out of love for what is honorable, and not out of fear of human punishment or compulsion; and so that I myself might appear to have given not out of hope for profit, but for the sake of practicing humanity <term>humanitas: the virtue of kindness and philanthropic spirit</term>, for which I did not wish to have legal security for its return. Truly, he who not only fails to repay a kindness but also returns evil to his benefactor instead must be punished more severely for this act, for it demonstrates a more foul malignancy of soul.

## CHAPTER IX.

### ON THE DUTY OF CONTRACTING
### PARTIES in general.

### §. 1.

A transition occurs from absolute duties to hypothetical ones, as if through intermediate agreements <term>pacta: agreements or contracts that create specific obligations</term>; for indeed, all duties, besides those already listed, seem to presuppose an express or tacit agreement. Therefore, in this section, we must discuss *the nature of agreements* and what must be observed by those entering into them.

### §. 2. Moreover, it was necessary for agreements between men <note>The text ends here as a catchword for the following page: "mines" (homines).</note>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from absolute duties (those owed to everyone regardless of circumstances) to hypothetical or conditional duties that arise when people enter into specific relationships or agreements. It follows the conclusion of the previous chapter on gratitude.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

...that [contracts] are entered into among men is sufficiently clear. For although the duties of humanity <term>humanitas: the innate kindness and concern for others that arises from our shared human nature</term> spread far and wide through human life, nevertheless all things which men might usefully receive from one another cannot be derived from that single source alone. For not everyone possesses such a goodness of character <note>original: "ingenii bonitas"</note> that they would wish to perform everything by which they can benefit others solely out of humanity, without a well-founded hope of receiving something equal in return. Frequently, too, the things that can come to us from others are of such a nature that we cannot, without embarrassment <note>original: "salva fronte," literally "with a safe forehead," an idiom meaning without blushing or feeling shame</note>, demand that they be provided to us for free. It is also often unbecoming of our station or our person to owe such a favor to another. Thus, for the most part, one person may be unable to give, or we ourselves may be unwilling to receive, unless that person receives something equal from us in exchange. Finally, it is not uncommon for others to be unaware of the specific ways in which they might serve our interests. 

Therefore, so that mutual duties between men—which are the fruit of sociality <term>socialitas: the fundamental principle in natural law that humans are meant to live in cooperative society</term>—might be exercised more frequently and according to certain fixed rules, it was necessary for men themselves to come to an agreement regarding the performance of those services which they could not with certainty promise themselves from others based on the law of humanity alone. Thus, it had to be determined in advance what each person should perform for another, and what they, in turn, should expect and might rightfully demand. This very determination is accomplished through promises and agreements <term>pacta: formal or informal contracts that create specific legal or moral obligations between parties</term>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of contracts and agreements. This page establishes the fundamental rule of natural law regarding these agreements.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

...it is a natural obligation: *that everyone keep their word, or fulfill their promises and agreements.* <note>original: "ut quilibet fidem datam servet, seu promissa & pacta expleat"</note> For if it were otherwise, the greatest part of the utility <term>utilitas: the practical benefit and well-being that arises from social cooperation</term> which is suited to arise for the human race from the mutual exchange of labor and resources would perish. And unless there were a necessity to keep promises, it would not be permitted in any way for a person to firmly build their own plans upon the support of other men. Indeed, from broken faith, the most just causes for complaints and war are liable to sprout up. For where I have performed something according to an agreement, and the other party breaks their faith, my property or my labor has been lost in vain. But even if I have performed nothing yet, it is still troublesome for my plans and intentions to be disturbed; since I could have looked after my affairs in another way, if that person had not offered their help to me. And it is beneath one’s dignity to be made a laughingstock because I believed the other party was a prudent and good man.

§. 4. It must be observed, moreover, that those things which are owed from the duty of humanity alone differ most especially from those things which are owed from an agreement or a perfect promise <term>promissum perfectum: a binding promise that gives the receiver a legal or moral right to compel its fulfillment</term>; in that the former <note>duties of humanity, like charity</note> are indeed rightly requested and honorably performed; but when the other person neglects to perform them voluntarily, I can only complain of their inhumanity, lack of feeling, or harshness. I cannot, however, compel them to perform by my own force, nor by the authority of a superior. Yet this compulsion is permitted when those things owed from a "perfect" promise or an agreement are not voluntarily performed.

### CHAPTER IX.

From this it also follows that we have an *imperfect right* to the former <note>referring to duties of humanity like charity, mentioned on the previous page</note>, but a *perfect right* to the latter <note>referring to formal agreements</note>: just as we are said to be *imperfectly* bound to the former, and *perfectly* bound to the latter.

§. 5. We give our word either *through a solitary act* or <term>unilateral act</term> <note>original Greek: "μονόπλευρον" (monopleuron), meaning one-sided</note>, or *through a reciprocal act* or <term>bilateral act</term> <note>original Greek: "δίπλευρον" (dipleuron), meaning two-sided</note>. For sometimes only one person binds themselves to perform something; but at other times two or more people mutually obligate themselves to perform something. The first act is called a *gratuitous promise*, and the second a *pact* <term>pactum: a mutual agreement or contract where both sides exchange obligations</term>.

§. 9. A *promise* can be divided into *imperfect* and *perfect*. The former is when we, who are promising, certainly wish to be bound; however, we do not give the other person a right to demand it, but rather we do not wish to be forcefully compelled to fulfill our promise. For example: if I should phrase a promise this way; "I have seriously intended to perform this or that for you, and I ask that you believe me." In this way, I seem to be bound more by the law of truthfulness <note>original: "lex veracitatis"</note> than by the law of justice, and I prefer to be seen as acting out of an impulse of my own consistency and seriousness <note>original: "gravitatis"</note> in performing my duty, rather than because of someone else's right. To this category belong the pledges of powerful or influential men, by which they promise someone their recommendation, intercession, promotion, or vote—not merely through empty words of honor, but seriously. Yet, they by no means wish these things to be demanded from them as if by some right, but entirely [dependent] on their own hu—

[...others]<note>The text begins mid-word, completing the thought from the previous page regarding circumstances.</note>, and other circumstances, it is clearly gathered. Thus, a **tacit pact** <term>pactum tacitum: an agreement not stated in words but implied by actions or the nature of the situation</term> often adheres to a principal agreement, flowing from the very nature of the business itself. It is also very common that in pacts, certain tacit exceptions and necessary conditions must be understood as implied.

§. 10. However, for someone to be able to consent clearly, the **use of reason** <note>original: *usus rationis*</note> is required to the extent that they understand the business at hand, whether it is suitable for them, and whether it can be performed by them; and having weighed these things, they are able to express their consent through sufficient signs. From this it follows that the promises and pacts of **infants** <note>In a legal context, this refers to young children who have not yet reached the "age of reason."</note>, as well as those of the **insane and the mad** (except where the madness is distinguished by lucid intervals), are null and void. The same must be pronounced regarding the promises of **drunkards**, if the drunkenness has already proceeded so far that reason is completely overwhelmed and put to sleep. For it cannot be held as a true and deliberate consent if someone—even in a most momentary and unthinking impulse—inclines toward something or gives certain signs that would otherwise express consent, at a time when the mind was, as if by a drug, displaced from its seat. Moreover, it would be shameless to wish to demand such a promise, especially one joined with a great burden. Indeed, if someone sought out that drunkenness and, having observed the other's easy-going state, craftily elicited a promise, they would not be immune from the charge of **deceit and fraud** <note>original: *dolus* and *fraus*</note>. But those who, once the hangover has passed...

§. 11. Furthermore, how long the *weakness of reason* <note>original: *imbecillitas rationis*</note> lasts in *children*, preventing the formation of an **obligation** <term>obligatio: a legal or moral bond that requires a person to perform a specific act</term>, cannot be accurately defined by **natural law** <note>original: *lex naturalis*; the universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature</note>; for judgment matures sooner in some and later in others. But that must be judged from each person's daily actions. Even though in most states, civil laws have established a certain general time limit here. Just as it has been beneficially accepted in some places that they should find it necessary to employ the authority of others more prudent [than themselves] in contracting obligations, until that unadvised impulse of **youth** is judged to have cooled down. For that age—even when it understands the business being conducted—is carried away by a violent and almost shortsighted impulse, and is easy in making promises, full of hope, seeking a reputation for generosity, prone to ambitiously cultivating friendships, and knows not how to be suspicious. Hence, he who preys upon the easy nature of that age is hardly free from deceit; he wishes to enrich himself through the losses of younger people, which they do not know how to foresee or estimate because of their **weakness of judgment**.

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of how "error" (mistake) affects the validity of a contract or promise. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, detailing scenarios where a person might have made a promise based on a false assumption.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>
<sig>F 5</sig>

...had supposed [something], without the consideration of which I would not have made the promise, naturally the **promise** <term>promissio: a voluntary pledge to do or give something in the future</term> will have no force. For the promisor consented not absolutely, but under a condition; and since that condition did not materialize, the promise is also null. 

2. If I was driven by an error into making a **pact** <term>pactum: an informal agreement or bargain</term> or **contracting** <term>contrahendum: the formal act of entering into a legal obligation</term>, and I discover the error while **matters are still unchanged** <note>original: *re integra*; a legal state where no action has yet been taken and the parties can still be returned to their original positions</note> and before anything has yet been performed, it is surely fair that the opportunity to **withdraw** <note>original: *pœnitendi*; literally "of repenting," used here in the legal sense of having a "right of rescission" or changing one's mind before a deal is finalized</note> be granted to me. This is especially true where I declared, while entering the agreement, what motive was driving me, and where the other party suffers no loss from my withdrawal, or I am ready to compensate them for it. 

But when the matter is no longer "unchanged," and the error only becomes apparent when the agreement has already been fulfilled, either in whole or in part, the one who made the mistake cannot withdraw from the agreement, except as far as the other party is willing to allow out of kindness. 

3. Where an error occurs concerning the very object being agreed upon, the agreement is invalidated not so much because of the error itself, but because the conditions of the agreement were not satisfied. For in agreements, the object of the agreement and its qualities must be known; without such knowledge, a **clear consensus** <note>original: *liquidus consensus*; a transparent and fully informed agreement</note> cannot be understood to exist. Therefore, when a defect is discovered, the party who would have been harmed can either withdraw from the contract, compel the other to make up for the defect, or even demand compensation for damages, in cases where **deceit** <term>dolus: intentional fraud or trickery</term> or **fault** <term>culpa: negligence or failure to take proper care</term> on the part of the other person was involved.

<header>BOOK I.</header>

§. 13. Now, if anyone has been led to make a promise or enter into an agreement through the **deceit** <term>dolus: intentional trickery, craftiness, or fraud used to deceive another party</term> and malicious fraud of another, the matter is to be handled as follows: 

1. If a third party has employed deceit, without the collusion of the person with whom we are making the agreement, the transaction shall remain valid. However, we may seek to recover from the person who employed the deceit the amount of our interest in not having been deceived <note>original: *id quod intererat*; this refers to the "indemnity" or the difference in value between the actual state of affairs and what was represented</note>.

2. If someone has by their own **malicious deceit** <note>original: *dolo suo malo*</note> provided the cause for me to promise them something or to agree with them, I am in no way obligated to them by that act. 

3. If someone has approached the agreement voluntarily and with a clear intention, but during the transaction itself—for example, concerning the object, its qualities, or its value—deceit has intervened, the pact will be **vitiated** <note>original: *vitiosum*; meaning the contract is legally flawed or "voidable"</note> to the extent that it is at the discretion of the deceived party either to dissolve the agreement entirely or to demand compensation for the **injury** <note>original: *læsionis*; in this context, the economic loss suffered due to the unfair terms</note>. 

4. Those things which do not pertain to the essence of the transaction, and were not expressly designated, do not void an act that is otherwise properly framed; this is true even if one party perhaps thought about them silently while agreeing, or if their opinion was confirmed by **cunning** <note>original: *astu*</note> up until the contract was concluded.

§. 14. **Fear** <term>metus: in legal terms, duress or the apprehension of a present or future danger</term> which intervenes regarding promises and pacts is understood in two ways: either as a **probable suspicion** <term>suspicio: a rational belief or apprehension</term> that we will be deceived by the other party—either because such a vice dwells in their character, or because they have clearly enough signaled their malicious intent—or as a **vehement [shaking] of the mind...** <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page; the catchword indicates the next word relates to the "agitation of the mind" caused by fear</note>.

### CHAPTER 9.

...unless by terror, arising from a threatened grave evil, we are willing to enter into a promise or agreement. Regarding the *former* kind of fear, the following must be held: 

1. He who trusts in the promises and agreements of one for whom the **scruple of good faith** <term>fidei religio: the religious or moral obligation to keep one's word, often tied to one's reputation or religious standing</term> is generally worth nothing, acts imprudently indeed; but for that cause alone, the agreement is not rendered void. 

2. Where an agreement has already been entered into, and no new signs of threatened deception emerge, it will not be permitted to shrink back from the agreement under the pretext of **faults** <note>original: *vitiorum*; referring here to moral defects or a known history of dishonesty</note> that were known before the agreement was made. For the same cause that did not prevent someone from making an agreement ought not, likewise, to prevent them from fulfilling it. 

3. Where, after an agreement has been entered into, most certain signs emerge that the other party is acting so as to mock me <note>i.e., to defraud or trick me</note> once I have performed my part first, I cannot be forced to perform until I have been **secured** <note>original: *cautum sit*; meaning that legal guarantees or collateral have been provided</note> against that deception.

§. 15. Regarding the *latter* kind of fear, these rules are to be observed: 

1. Agreements that are entered into because of fear inflicted by a third party are valid. For in this case, certainly, no **fault** <note>original: *vitium*</note> resides in the other party that would prevent them from being able to acquire a **right** <note>original: *jus*</note> against me from the agreement; and having dispelled a fear threatened by another is certainly worthy of **compensation** <note>original: *pensatione*</note>. 

2. Those agreements which are entered into out of fear or **reverence** <note>original: *reverentia*</note> for a legitimate command, or because of the **authority** <note>original: *auctoritaté*</note> of those to whom we are greatly indebted, are valid. 

3. Agreements, to...

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...agreements that a person is forced into by the unjust violence of the very individual to whom they are promising or with whom they are **contracting** <note>original: *paciscitur*; the catchword on the previous page "pacifci-" completes this word</note> are invalid. For the injury that someone inflicts upon me by instilling an unjust fear makes that person incapable of acquiring any legal right against me through that specific action. Furthermore, since a person is otherwise required to make amends for damage they have caused, the obligation is considered canceled through **offsetting** <term>compensatio: a legal principle where two debts or obligations cancel each other out</term> if that which ought to be immediately restored is simply not paid in the first place.

§. 16. Furthermore, it is necessary that **consent** <term>consensus</term> be **mutual** <note>original: *mutuum*</note>, not only in formal contracts but also in promises; thus, it is required that not only the person making the promise agrees, but also the person to whom the promise is made. For when this consent is missing, or when the recipient refuses to accept the offered promise, the thing promised remains in the possession of the promisor. For a person who offers their property to another neither wishes to force it upon an unwilling recipient, nor to treat it as **abandoned** <note>original: *pro derelicta*; property cast away by the owner with the intent of relinquishing ownership</note>. Therefore, if the other party does not accept, the promisor loses none of their rights over the offered object.

However, if a **request** <term>rogatio: a formal petition or inquiry asking for something</term> preceded the promise, that request is considered to remain in effect unless it has been expressly revoked; in such a case, the acceptance is understood to have been made in advance. This is only true, however, if the offer matches the request. For if the offer differs, an express acceptance is required; often, my needs are not met unless I receive exactly as much as I requested.

§. 17. Regarding the **subject matter** <note>original: *materiam*</note> of promises and agreements, it is required that what we promise or...

<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

**we contract** <note>original: *paciscimur*; this completes the sentence started on page 92</note> *must not be beyond our powers, nor should we be prohibited from performing it by any law*: for otherwise we make promises either foolishly or dishonestly. From this it follows that no one can bind themselves to do that which is impossible for them. But if a matter, which was considered possible at the time the agreement was entered into, later becomes impossible by some accident without the fault of the contracting party, the agreement will be void if performance has not yet begun <term>re integra: a legal state where no action has yet been taken to fulfill a contract</term>. Where something has already been performed by the other party, it must be restored, or an equivalent must be provided in its place. Even if this cannot be done, the greatest effort must be applied to ensure the other party suffers no loss. For in agreements, we look primarily to that which was expressly agreed upon; where we cannot obtain this, it is sufficient that an equivalent be presented: at the very least, we must take every precaution that we do not suffer damage. However, a person who through fraud or grave fault has deprived themselves of the power to perform is not only held to their utmost effort, but may also be punished with an additional penalty as a substitute for the failure to fulfill the obligation.

§. 18. It is also clear *that we cannot be obligated to perform an unlawful act.* For no one can validly bind themselves further than they have the power to do so. But a person who prohibits an action by law certainly takes away the power of undertaking it and of accepting an obligation to perform that same act. For it is surely contradictory that, from an obligation through

...laws <note>original: *leges*; completing the sentence from page 93</note> confirmed that one must necessarily do something which, by those same laws, must be omitted. Thus, he indeed sins who promises unlawful things; but he sins twice who performs them. From this it is also gathered that *promises ought not to be kept when they would be harmful to the person to whom they are made*; for it is prohibited by natural law <term>natural law: a philosophy that certain rights or values are inherent by virtue of human nature and universally cognizable through human reason</term> for anyone to inflict evil upon another, even if that person foolishly desires it. Therefore, if an agreement regarding an immoral matter <note>original: *rem turpem*; often referring to acts against public or religious morals</note> has been entered into, neither party shall be bound to fulfill it. Moreover, even where a shameful deed has been performed by one party, the other will not be obligated to pay the agreed-upon reward. However, what has already been given for that purpose cannot be reclaimed, unless perhaps fraud or <term>enormous loss: a legal concept where a contract is rescinded because the price is so unfair it constitutes a grave injury</term> <note>original: *enormis laesio*</note> has occurred.

§. 19. Finally, it is also manifest that we promise or bargain in vain regarding the property of others, insofar as those things are not our own but are subject to the direction and will of another. But if I have promised this: that I will give my best effort so that another (whom it is assumed I cannot command by authority) might perform a task, then I am bound to work in every morally possible way—that is, as much as another can honestly demand of me and as much as the standards of civil life allow—to persuade the other to perform. ### CHAPTER IX.

...way <note>completing the phrase "morally possible way" from page 94</note> until the right has expired. For he who, by prior promises or agreements, has already transferred his right to another, certainly has no such right remaining anymore that he can confer upon a third party. And with no trouble, all promises and agreements could be made void if it were permitted to enter into another agreement in which things contrary to the first were arranged, and which could not be fulfilled at the same time as the first. Upon this is founded that well-worn maxim: *the first in time is stronger in right* <note>original: *qui prior tempore, potior jure*; a fundamental legal principle that older claims or rights take precedence over newer ones</note>.

§. 20. Furthermore, regarding promises, it is especially to be observed that they are usually conceived either ***purely and absolutely***, or ***under a condition***; that is to say, so that the validity of the promise is deferred to some event depending on chance or human will. Moreover, conditions are either ***possible*** or ***impossible***. The former are subdivided into ***casual or fortuitous***, which are not within our power to make exist or not exist; ***potestative or arbitrary***, which are within the power of the one to whom the promise is made to make exist or not exist; and ***mixed***, whose fulfillment depends partly on the will of the one upon whom it is conferred, and partly on chance. Furthermore, ***impossible*** conditions are such either ***physically*** or ***morally***; that is, some things cannot be done by us through the nature of things, and some should not be done through laws and decency. And indeed, impossible conditions, if we follow a natural simplicity of interpretation, make the promising speech negative <note>original: *orationem promittentem faciunt negativam*; meaning that if a promise is based on something that cannot happen, the promise is effectively void or considered a "no"</note>.

BOOK I.

<meta>catchword: alone</meta>

...the law <note>completing the thought from page 95 regarding impossible conditions</note>, even if it is possible for laws to decree that if such conditions are attached to a serious business matter, they should be treated as if they were never added; this is done so that men are not mocked by legal acts that would otherwise yield no result.

§. 21. Finally, we make promises and agreements not only by ourselves, but also through the intervention of other men, whom we appoint as ***intermediaries*** and ***interpreters*** <note>original: *internuncios* and *interpretes*; in this context, these refer to agents or proxies who act on another's behalf</note> of our will. When these agents act in good faith according to the mandates they received from us, we are validly bound to those who dealt with them as if they were acting in our place.

§. 22. And these are the <term>absolute duties</term> <note>These are duties that apply to all humans regardless of their specific social or political status, such as the duty not to harm others.</note> of man, as well as those that serve as a transition to others. The remaining duties presuppose some human ***institution*** <note>original: *institutum*; referring to systems established by human agreement rather than natural law alone</note>, supported by a general pact and introduced into the human race, or perhaps some particular state. Of these institutions, three are primarily identified: ***speech***, ***ownership of things*** <note>original: *dominium rerum*; the concept of private property</note> and their ***price***, and ***human sovereignty*** <note>original: *imperium humanum*; the authority of government or social hierarchy</note>. We must now explain each of these, as well as the duties that result from them.

# CHAPTER X.

...from this faculty alone, they have gathered that man was destined by nature to lead a social life. Therefore, concerning the legitimate use of this [faculty] which is beneficial to human society, <term>natural law</term> <note>original: *lex naturalis*; the universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</note> dictates this duty to men: *that no one should deceive another through speech, or through other signs established for expressing the thoughts of the mind.*

§ 2. But so that the nature of speech may be more deeply understood, it must be known that regarding speech—whether expressed by the living voice or by written letters—a twofold obligation arises. ***One*** is that those who use the same language are bound to apply a specific word to a specific thing, according to the usage of each language. For since neither vocal sounds nor specific formations of letters signify a particular thing by their nature (otherwise all languages and styles of writing would have to be the same), it is necessary—lest the use of language be in vain if everyone called things by whatever word they pleased—that a <term>tacit agreement</term> <note>original: *tacitam conventionem*; an unspoken or implied understanding between people that allows communication to function</note> has existed among those who use the same language to denote a specific thing by a specific word and no other. For unless there is a concerted effort toward a uniform application of words, it is impossible to understand the thoughts of the mind from another’s speech. Hence, by the force of this agreement, everyone is bound to use words in common speech as the accepted usage of that language requires. From this it also follows that although the thoughts of the

-># BOOK I.<-
->98<-

...[even if they] could differ from speech, nevertheless in human life everyone is deemed to have intended that which their words convey, even if perhaps the internal intention of the mind departs from them. For since this intention cannot be established except through <term>signs</term> <note>original: *signa*; refers to the words, writing, or gestures used to communicate internal thoughts</note>, every use of speech would be rendered useless if the internal thought of the mind—which anyone could fabricate for themselves as they please—could nullify what the signs convey in common life.

§ 3. *The second obligation that enters into speech consists in this: that one ought to reveal the feelings of one's mind to another through speech in such a way that the other can clearly understand them from it. For since a person is able not only to speak but also to remain silent, and since one is not always bound to disclose what one considers in the mind before just anyone:* therefore it is necessary that a specific obligation exists which brings upon someone the necessity both to speak and to speak in such a way that the other understands the feelings of our mind. Moreover, that obligation arises *either from a specific <term>agreement</term> <note>original: *pacto*; a formal or informal contract or arrangement between parties</note>, or from a common precept of <term>natural law</term> <note>original: *legis naturalis*; the universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</note>, or from the nature of the present business for the contracting of which speech is employed.* For often it is expressly agreed with someone that they should reveal the feelings of their mind to me regarding some business; for example, if I engage someone as a teacher for myself in some discipline. Often also some precept of natural law [directs] my knowledge to another...

<summary>This page argues that for social stability, individuals must be held to the outward meaning of their words rather than secret intentions, and it identifies the specific conditions—such as contracts or natural law—that obligate a person to speak truthfully.</summary>
<keywords>communication, intention, natural law, social contract, speech, signs, agreement, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on the moral necessity of communication and begins a nuanced exploration of when a person is permitted to withhold the truth or use figurative language. It continues the argument that the obligation to speak is tied to the rights of the listener and the specific circumstances of the interaction.</meta>

-># CHAPTER IX.<-

...[the law] orders one to communicate, so that in this way they might benefit the other, or that I may avert harm from them, or at least not provide a cause or occasion for them to receive harm. Finally, sometimes the present business, which has been undertaken with another, cannot be settled unless I reveal my judgment on it; as occurs concerning the celebration of <term>contracts</term> <note>original: *contractus*; formal or informal legal agreements involving mutual obligations</note>.

§. 4. Since, however, it does not always happen in practice that every one of my thoughts must be signified to another under one of these headings; it is clear that I am not bound to indicate anything else to another through my speech, except those things which they have a <term>perfect or imperfect right</term> <note>original: *jus perfectum aut imperfectum*; in natural law theory, a "perfect right" is one that can be legally or forcefully claimed, while an "imperfect right" is a moral claim—like the claim to a person's charity—that cannot be strictly compelled</note> to understand from me. And thus, I am rightly able to <term>dissimulate</term> <note>original: *dissimulare*; to hide or disguise one's true thoughts, feelings, or intentions</note> by keeping silent—even if I am questioned intensely—about things that the other has no right to know from me, nor am I held to that by any obligation.

§. 5. On the contrary, because <term>speech</term> <note>original: *sermo*; refers here to the faculty of discourse and the use of words</note> was invented not only for the sake of others, but also for ourselves: therefore, where some utility of my own is involved, and the right of no one else is harmed, it will be permitted for me to form my speech in such a way that it expresses something different from that which I am revolving in my mind.

§. 6. Finally, because those to whom we speak are often so disposed that if they were to perceive the matter through simple and open speech, it would result in their own detriment, and it would not be permitted for us to achieve the good end which we intend: therefore, in these cases, it will be permitted to use <term>feigned and colored speech</term> <note>original: *ficto coloratoq; sermone*; this refers to the use of metaphors, figures of speech, or "white lies" intended to guide a listener toward a good outcome without revealing a potentially harmful truth</note>.

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

**Vocabulary:** to communicate, contracts, to dissimulate, speech, perfect right, imperfect right, and with colored speech.

<summary>This page defines the limits of the moral obligation to tell the truth, stating that one is only required to reveal their thoughts when the listener has a legitimate right to that information. It justifies the use of silence, concealment, or even "colored speech" when it serves a useful purpose or protects the listener from harm.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, ethics of communication, truth-telling, dissimulation, perfect rights, imperfect rights, social contracts, figurative speech</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ethics of communication. Having established that the obligation to speak depends on the listener's rights, the author now defines "moral truth" and "lying" based on these obligations rather than just the literal accuracy of words.</meta>

-># BOOK I.<-

...and which does not directly represent our opinion and intention to our listeners. For whoever wishes and is obligated to benefit someone is certainly not bound to do so in a manner that would fail to achieve their intended goal.

§. 7. From these points, we can gather what *truth* consists of—a pursuit for which good men are so highly praised. It is essentially this: that our words properly represent the <term>thoughts of our mind</term> <note>original: *animi nostri sensa*; literally "senses of our mind," referring to our internal intentions and beliefs</note> to another person who has a right to understand them. This applies when we have either a <term>perfect or imperfect obligation</term> <note>original: *obligatio perfecta aut imperfecta*; a perfect obligation is a legal duty, while an imperfect obligation is a moral or social duty</note> to reveal those thoughts to them. This is done so that the listener may either gain some rightful benefit from understanding our mind, or to ensure they are not unfairly harmed by being given a different meaning than the truth.

From this, it is also clear in passing that a lie is not always committed even when one deliberately says something that does not perfectly match the facts or our own private thoughts. Thus, <term>logical truth</term> <note>original: *veritas logica*; the correspondence between words and objective facts</note>, which consists of the agreement of words with things, does not coincide in every respect with <term>moral truth</term> <note>original: *veritas moralis*; the ethical duty to communicate honestly based on the rights of the listener</note>.

§. 8. *A lie*, by contrast, occurs when a person’s speech deliberately presents an opinion that is different from what they actually think, specifically in a situation where the listener has a right to know the truth and we have a duty to ensure they understand our true mind.

<meta>catchword: §. 9.</meta>

<summary>This page defines truth and lying within the framework of natural law, distinguishing between "logical truth" (factual accuracy) and "moral truth" (fulfilling the duty to inform those who have a right to know).</summary>
<keywords>truth, logical truth, moral truth, lying, obligation, rights of the listener, communication ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the ethical use of fictional speech and moves into the obligations associated with taking oaths. It follows from the previous page's definition of "moral truth," arguing that the morality of a statement depends on whether the listener has a right to the truth.</meta>

->### CHAPTER X.<-

->G 3<-

§. 9. From what has been said, it is clear that a person does not incur the <term>stain of lying</term> <note>original: *mendacij notam*; the moral guilt or reputation of being a liar</note> if they use fictional speech or stories with children or similar people for the purpose of their better instruction, insofar as such listeners are not capable of understanding the <term>naked truth</term> <note>original: *nudae veritatis*; the literal facts without simplification or metaphor</note>. 

The same applies to those who employ a fictional story for a good purpose that could not be achieved plainly. For instance, if an innocent person must be protected, an angry person calmed, a grieving person soothed, a fearful person encouraged, or a reluctant person persuaded to take medicine. It also applies if someone's stubbornness must be broken, a malicious plan thwarted, or if the <term>secrets of the state</term> <note>original: *arcana reip.*; literally "secrets of the republic," referring to confidential government matters</note> and plans—which it is in the public interest to keep from others—need to be shielded by false rumors to ward off meddlesome curiosity. Finally, it applies if we deceive an enemy with stories as a <term>stratagem</term> <note>original: *strategemate*; a military trick or ruse</note> when we already have the right to harm them by open means.

§. 10. On the other hand, if someone is truly bound to clearly express their inner thoughts to another, they do not escape guilt if they tell only a part of the truth, or deceive the other with ambiguous speech, or keep a certain <term>mental reservation</term> <note>original: *tacita restrictio*; a controversial practice where a speaker adds a silent qualification to their words to avoid telling a "logical" lie while still deceiving the listener</note> that deviates from common usage.

# ON THE DUTY OF THOSE TAKING OATHS.

§. I.

<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of truth and deception to the specific moral and legal weight of oaths. It establishes a definition of an oath as a "religious assertion" that invokes divine witness to ensure honesty when human oversight is insufficient.</meta>

# BOOK I.
<page-num>102</page-num>

Through an <term>oath</term> <note>original: *jusjurandum*; a formal promise or statement of fact invoking a deity</note>, a significant confirmation is deemed to be added to our speech and to all the actions in which speech plays a part. *It is indeed a <term>religious assertion</term> <note>original: *assertio religiosa*; a statement made sacred by its appeal to the divine</note> by which we renounce divine mercy or call down divine punishment upon ourselves unless we speak the truth.* While the all-knowing and all-powerful witness and avenger is invoked, a presumption of truth is aroused because no one is believed to be so recklessly impious as to dare to petulantly call down the gravest indignation of the <term>Deity</term> <note>original: *Numinis*; referring to divine power or the presence of God</note> upon themselves. From this, the duty of those swearing is understood to be this: *that they approach the taking of the oath with reverence, and religiously observe what has been sworn.*

§. 2. Furthermore, the purpose and use of an oath consists chiefly in this: that men are more firmly bound to tell the truth, or to keep a promised agreement or pact, by the fear of an all-knowing and all-powerful Divine Being. Through the oath, they invoke His vengeance upon themselves if they knowingly deceive, in cases where fear of other men does not seem effective enough; for they might hope to despise or evade human strength, or to deceive human knowledge.

§. 3. Because, truly, besides the Divinity nothing is all-knowing or all-powerful, it follows that an oath is absurdly framed by means of any thing that lacks the character of divinity, in the sense that such a thing—

<summary>This page defines an oath as a religious act where an individual invokes God as a witness and judge, effectively trading their hope for divine mercy for the credibility of their word. It argues that oaths are necessary tools for social stability when human laws and oversight are unable to guarantee honesty.</summary>

# CHAPTER XI.
<page-num>103</page-num>

...is invoked as a witness and an avenger of <term>perjury</term> <note>original: *perjurium*; the act of lying under oath or breaking a sworn promise</note>. Even if it is frequent that in oaths *a specific thing is named by which one swears*, it is done in this sense: that God, if the swearer should deceive, may exert His vengeance upon that specific thing, as it is something most dear and highly valued by the person swearing.

§. 4. In *oaths, the formula* by which God is *described*—while being invoked as witness and avenger—*must be adapted to the belief* or religion that the *one swearing* holds concerning God. For it is in vain that someone is compelled to swear by a God in whom they do not believe, and whom they therefore do not fear. Yet no one considers themselves to be swearing by God under any other formula or name than that which is contained in the precepts of their own religion (that is, what is true religion in the opinion of the swearer). From this it also follows that one who swears by false gods, whom they nevertheless hold to be true, is certainly bound; and if they deceive, they truly commit perjury. This is because, under whatever specific concept they held, they had a general notion of the <term>Deity</term> <note>original: *Numen*; divine power or the presence of God</note> before their eyes; and thus, by knowingly committing perjury, they violated the reverence due to the divine majesty as much as was in their power.

§. 5. For the <term>obligation</term> <note>original: *obligatio*; a moral or legal bond that constrains a person to a certain course of action</note> of an oath, it is required that *it be undertaken with a deliberate mind.* Therefore, someone will not be bound by an oath who has *uttered the oath by simply reciting it*, or who has led another in the formal words of an oath spoken in the first person. But one who [presents] the appearance of one seriously intending to swear...

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...displayed <note>referring to the outward expression of an oath from the previous page</note>, he shall certainly be bound, whatever he might have been thinking in his mind while taking the oath. For otherwise, all use of oaths—and indeed every method of binding oneself through visible signs—would be removed from human life, if a person could use their silent intention to prevent the natural effect of an act which was established specifically to produce that effect.

§. 6. Oaths do not, in themselves, produce a new and unique <term>obligation</term> <note>original: *obligatio*; a moral or legal bond</note>; rather, they are added to an already valid obligation as a kind of *accessory bond*. For whenever we swear, we assume there is something already required of us, and by failing to perform it, we provoke divine punishment upon ourselves. This would be absurd unless it were already unlawful not to perform what is assumed; thus, it implies we were already obligated beforehand. From this it follows that acts which possess an inherent flaw that prevents an obligation from forming do not become binding simply because an oath is added to them. Just as a previously valid obligation is not canceled by a subsequent oath, nor is a right acquired by another person through that obligation taken away. For this reason, it is in vain for someone to swear that they will not pay a debt owed to another. Nor will an oath be binding where it is clear that the swearer assumed a certain fact to be true which, in reality, was not—and if they had not believed it, they would never have sworn; especially where they were led into er-

# CHAPTER XI.

...ror <note>Completing the word "error" from the previous page.</note> has been led. Nor does he who compels me to swear through unjust fear <note>duress or coercion</note> acquire a right for himself to demand anything from me rightfully based on that oath. Likewise, an oath shall have no force regarding the performance of anything unlawful; nor, indeed, regarding the omission of any good act which is commanded by divine or human laws. Finally, an oath does not change the nature and substance of a <term>contractual promise</term> <note>original: *promissi pactivè*; a promise made as part of a formal agreement</note> to which it is added. For this reason, it is useless to swear to impossible things. Also, a promise made with a condition is not changed into an absolute or pure one simply by an oath. And just as with any other promise, acceptance <note>The requirement that the receiving party must agree to the promise for it to be binding.</note> is equally required in a sworn promise.

§. 7. However, oaths achieve this effect because of the included **invocation of God**, whom no one can deceive by craft, and whom no one can mock without being punished. Consequently, not only is a more terrible punishment thought to await the person who breaks a sworn promise than one who breaks an unsworn one, but it also ensures that all **deceit and legal trickery** <note>original: *dolus & cavillatio*</note> must be excluded from the transactions to which the oaths are attached.

§. 8. Nevertheless, oaths are not always to be interpreted broadly, but *sometimes strictly*, if the subject matter seems to require it; for instance, if the oath was taken to the detriment of someone else, and the oath was added not so much to a promise as to threats. §. 9. Hence, it is also his duty to draft the words of the oath, and indeed as clearly as possible; so that he may indicate how he understands them, and the one swearing may agree that he correctly perceives that meaning. These words must then be clearly expressed by the latter, so that he cannot quibble with or evade them by any means <note>The author emphasizes that the "intent of the receiver" is the standard for interpreting an oath to prevent the swearer from using mental reservations or clever wordplay to escape their duty.</note>.

§. 10 The **division of oaths** is conveniently taken **from the use** to which they are applied in daily life. For some are added to promises and agreements, so that they may be observed with greater religious devotion. Others, however, are used to confirm someone’s assertion regarding an **uncertain fact** <term>factum illiquidum</term> <note>original: *factum illiquidum*; a legal term for a fact or debt that is not yet clearly established, proven, or "liquidated"</note>, and where the truth cannot be more conveniently investigated by other means. Such an oath is required from **witnesses**, or those who are considered to have knowledge of someone else's deed. Sometimes even those who are in dispute with one another...

## CHAPTER XII.

# ON THE DUTY CONCERNING THE ACQUISITION
# of the Ownership of Things.

-> §. 1. <-

Since the condition of the human body is such that it requires external things to be taken in and is nourished by them, and must be protected against those things which tend to destroy its framework; and since this life can also be rendered more comfortable and pleasant by many things: it is from this safely concluded that it is surely the will of the Supreme Governor <term>Moderator</term> <note>original: *Moderator*; referring to God as the ruler and sustainer of the universe</note> of the universe that man should apply other creatures to his own use; even to the point of destroying many of them. Nor does this hold true only regarding plants <term>vegetabilia</term> and things that perish without sensation, but even regarding harmless animals <term>animalia</term> which, although they may die with pain, are nevertheless killed and consumed by man for the sake of food without sin.

§. 2. Furthermore, from the beginning, all these things are understood to have been *set before men by God as if in common* <note>original: *velut in medio hominibus expositæ*; literally "placed in the middle," referring to the state of "original negative community" where everything was available to everyone before the invention of private property</note>, so that they belonged no more to one person than to another. This was done, however, in such a way that humans might arrange for their distribution as the condition of the human race and the requirements of peace, tranquility, and the preservation of good order seemed to demand. From this, while the human race was still comprised of only a few...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on judicial oaths and begins a new chapter on the foundations of property rights, arguing that God intended for humans to use and even consume other creatures for their survival and comfort. It introduces the concept that the world was originally "placed in the middle" as common property to be organized by human reason for the sake of social order.</summary>

<keywords>property rights, ownership, natural law, divine providence, animal consumption, original community of goods, judicial oaths, Dominium</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 107 regarding the transition from a state of nature, where all things were held in common, to the establishment of private property. It resolves the catchword from the previous page, completing the word "sustenance" or "maintenance" (alimonium).</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...so that [human life] might be sustained <note>original: *subsisteret*; the catchword *limi-* from the previous page completes *alimonium*, referring to the nourishment or maintenance of life</note>, it was agreed *that those specific things which anyone had seized* with the intention of applying them to their own use *should indeed become theirs, and should not be snatched away by another.* However, the actual bodies <note>original: *corpora*; here referring to the physical sources or substances of things, such as the land itself, rather than the fruit or wood taken from it</note> from which those things were produced remained in common, belonging to no one in particular. But afterward, as the number of humans increased and cultivation began to be applied to those things from which man’s food and clothing are produced, the *substances of things themselves were also divided among men, and a proper portion was assigned to each* in order to avoid quarrels and to introduce good order. This was done with the added agreement that *those things which were left in common during the first division of property could thereafter become the property of whoever first claimed them for himself.* Thus, by the will of the Divine Power <term>Numine</term> <note>original: *Numine*; referring to God’s providence or divine will</note>, and through the prior consent of men and at least a tacit agreement <term>pacto tacito</term>, *the ownership of things, or dominion,* was introduced.

-> §. 3. <-

*Ownership <term>dominium</term> is a right by which the substance, as it were, of a thing belongs to someone in such a way that it does not belong to another in the same manner as a whole <term>in solidum</term>.* <note>original: *in solidum*; a legal term meaning "for the whole," implying that full ownership is exclusive and cannot be held entirely by two separate people at once</note> From this it follows that, concerning things that belong to us as our own, we may dispose of them as we please and exclude all others from their use; except in cases where others have acquired a specific right from us through agreements. Although it often happens in states <note>original: *civitatibus*; referring to civil societies or organized political communities</note> that ownership is not always held unimpaired by everyone, but [is limited] by certain...

<summary>This page explains the historical and legal transition from communal sharing to private property (dominium). It argues that property was established by human consent and divine will to prevent conflict as the population grew and agriculture began.</summary>

<keywords>dominium, private property, right of first discovery, tacit agreement, natural law, civil society, division of resources</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and philosophical discussion from page 108 regarding the nature of ownership. It completes the sentence from the previous page, which argued that ownership is a right exclusive to an individual, though it may be restricted by law or agreement.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...[same manner, but is] circumscribed by limits through civil authority, or by the arrangement and agreement of men among themselves. When one thing belongs to many people in an undivided way and in the same manner, that thing is called **common** <term>communis</term> to those many.

§. 4. Just as things did not pass into private **ownership** <term>proprietas</term> all at once, but gradually and as the needs of the human race seemed to require, so it was not necessary that every single thing become private property. Instead, to maintain the peace of the human race, some things could—and indeed should—remain in a state of primitive community. For those things which are useful to humans but are nevertheless inexhaustible—so that they can be open to the use of everyone without making that use any less available to individuals—it would be both pointless and foolish to try to divide them. Examples include the light and heat of the sun, the air, flowing water, and similar things. To this category also belongs the vast **Ocean** <term>Oceanus</term>, situated between great continents, at least in those parts most distant from the shores. Since the ocean is not only more than sufficient for the shared use of all, but it is also morally impossible <note>original: *moraliter fit impossibilis*; meaning that it is practically and legally beyond the capacity of a single nation to enforce exclusive control over the open sea</note> for any one people to guard it. Where a thing is of such a nature that others can in no way be barred from its use, to divide it or make it the private property of anyone is not only unnecessary, but also likely to provide grounds for empty disputes.

<meta>catchword §. 5.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that while property rights are necessary for order, certain resources like air, sunlight, and the high seas must remain common property because they are inexhaustible and cannot be physically or morally occupied by a single group.</summary>
<keywords>common property, private property, ownership, natural law, freedom of the seas, inexhaustible resources, civil authority, social contract</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal nature of property, moving from the definition of common versus private property to the specific legal mechanisms by which one becomes an owner. It completes the introductory thoughts on the history of property and begins the formal classification of the "modes of acquisition."</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

§. 5. *Modes of acquiring ownership are either **original** or **derivative**.* Through the former, property is introduced into a thing for the first time. Through the latter, an **ownership** <term>dominium</term> already established passes from one person to another. The former modes are again either **simply** so, by which ownership is acquired over the physical body of a thing; or **relatively** <note>original: *secundum quid*; referring to the acquisition of something as an addition to existing property</note>, by which some increase is joined to a thing we already own.

§. 6. After the division of the ownership of things was accepted among men, it was agreed that whatever did not fall under that primary division should belong to the **occupant** <term>occupans</term>—that is, to the one who first physically grasped those things with the intention of keeping them for themselves <note>original: *animo sibi habendi*; a central legal concept where physical possession must be paired with the mental intent to own</note>. Thus, today, the only original mode of acquiring ownership over the body of a thing is **occupation** <term>occupatio</term>. In this manner, deserted regions are acquired which no human has ever claimed for themselves; these become the property of the person who first enters them with the intention of keeping them, provided they apply cultivation to them and establish certain boundaries as to how far they wish their property to extend. However, where a **community** <term>cœtus</term> of many people has occupied some part of the land together, it is most common for a specific portion to be assigned to each individual from that group; what remains is considered to belong to the whole community. Through occupation, one also acquires wild beasts, birds, and fish living in the sea, rivers, or lakes; likewise, if anything from the sea...

<vocab>ownership, occupation, modes of acquisition, property, community, original, derivative</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the two primary ways to acquire property: original acquisition (taking something for the first time) and derivative acquisition (transfer from a previous owner). It identifies "occupation" as the primary original method, applicable to deserted lands and wild animals.</summary>

# CHAPTER XII.

...are usually cast onto the shore; provided, however, that by the **civil authority** <note>original: *imperium civile*; referring to the laws of the state or government</note> the common capture of such things is not forbidden, or assigned to a specific person. It is necessary, if these things are to become ours, that we physically seize them and bring them under our power.

Things are also acquired by **occupation** <term>occupatio</term> <note>the legal process of taking possession of something that currently has no owner</note> in which the **ownership** <term>dominium</term>, to which they were previously subject, has been clearly extinguished. For instance, if things are thrown away with the intention that they no longer be ours; or those which we lose against our will at first, but later regard as **abandoned** <term>res derelictæ</term>. To this also belongs **treasure** <term>thesaurus</term>, or money whose owner is unknown; this goes to the finder <term>inventio</term>, where it has not been otherwise arranged by civil laws.

§. 7. Furthermore, many things that are subject to ownership do not always remain in the same state, but *expand their substance through various increases;* external **additions** <term>incrementa</term> accrue to some; others produce *fruits*; for many, value increases through a *form* imposed by **human industry** <term>industria humana</term>. All these things can be included under the term *accessions* <term>accessio</term>; and they are divided into two classes. For some arise without human intervention from the nature of those things alone: others are produced entirely or in part by the act and industry of humans. Regarding these, the rule is this: that whoever is the owner of the thing, the accessions and any benefits also belong to that same person;

BOOK I.

And he who has created a new form from his own material shall be the owner of that form.

§. 8. It frequently happens, however, that others, through a contract or some other means, have sought for themselves the right to derive a specific benefit from our property, or even to prevent us from using our property in every possible way. These rights are usually called **servitudes** <term>servitutes</term> <note>original: *servitutes*; these are legal rights to use someone else's land for a specific purpose, such as a right of way</note>. They are divided into **personal** <term>personales</term>, where the benefit from another's property goes directly to the person themselves; and **real** <term>reales</term>, where the benefit is taken from another's property by means of our own property <note>often called 'praedial' servitudes; for example, a right of way that stays with a piece of land regardless of who owns it</note>. Included among the personal servitudes are **usufruct** <term>usufructus</term> <note>the right to use and enjoy the profits of another's property</note>, **use** <term>usus</term>, **habitation** <term>habitatio</term> <note>the right to live in another's house</note>, and the **services of slaves** <term>operae servorum</term>. Real servitudes are again divided into **servitudes of urban estates** <term>praediorum urbanorum</term> and **rural estates** <term>rusticorum</term>. The urban ones are, for example, the servitude of **bearing a weight** <note>original: *oneris ferendi*; the right to rest a building's weight on a neighbor's wall</note>, of **lights** <term>luminum</term>, **not obstructing lights** <note>original: *ne luminibus officiatur*; a rule preventing a neighbor from blocking your windows</note>, **view** <term>prospectus</term>, **receiving dripping water** <term>stillicidii recipiendi</term> <note>the right to let rainwater run off your roof onto a neighbor’s land</note>, etc. The rural ones are, for example, a **footpath** <term>iter</term>, **driving cattle** <term>actus</term>, a **road** <term>via</term>, an **aqueduct** <term>aquaeductus</term>, **drawing water** <term>aquae haustus</term>, **driving livestock to water** <term>pecoris ad aquam appulsus</term>, the **right of pasturage** <term>jus pascendi</term>, etc. Almost all of these were introduced because of the requirements of being neighbors.

§. 9. Among the **derivative** <term>derivativos</term> modes of acquisition <note>ways of getting property from a previous owner</note>, some are those by which property is passed to another by **disposition of the law** <term>dispositione legis</term>; others are those by the **act of the previous owner** <term>facto prioris domini</term>. These occur so that either the **entirety of someone's goods** <term>bonorum alicujus universitas</term> or a **specific portion of them** is transferred.

§. 10. The **entirety of goods** <term>universitas bonorum</term> passes by law through the death of the previous owner in **intestate successions** <term>successionibus ab intestato</term> <note>when a person dies without leaving a valid will</note>. For since it is both contrary to the common inclination of men, and to the peace of the human
