### CHAPTER XII.

...it is not at all beneficial for anyone that the goods which someone sought with so much labor while they lived should be considered abandoned <term>abandoned property</term> <note>original: *pro derelictis*; in Roman law, this refers to property voluntarily given up by its owner, which then becomes available to the first person to claim it</note> upon the owner’s death and left open to any occupant. Therefore, guided by reason, it has been accepted among all nations that if a person has made no disposal of their own goods, their property should pass to those whom they are judged to hold most dear according to the common affection of mankind. These are generally those who descend from us <note>i.e., children and grandchildren</note>, and next to them, others joined to us by blood, each according to their degree of closeness <term>proximity</term> <note>original: *propinquitatis*; the legal measure of how closely two people are related by blood</note>.

And although some people exist who, either because of favors received or a specific affection, love certain strangers more than their own blood relatives; nevertheless, for the sake of peace, it was decided not to consider the feelings of a few individuals, but rather to follow the common inclination of mortals and observe a very simple method of succession, one not subject to any complicated disputes. Such disputes would certainly arise if benefactors and friends could compete with those who rely on the right of blood. If anyone truly wished to prefer benefactors or friends over relatives, they should have explicitly made arrangements regarding that matter <note>referring to the necessity of writing a last will and testament</note>.

**§. 11.** From these points it follows that the closest heirs to anyone are their own children, whom nature has both carefully entrusted to their parents to be nourished and educated, and to whom each parent is considered to...

->### BOOK I.<-

...[that they] wished to provide most generously, and to leave what remains to those [their children] above all. By children, however, we primarily understand those who are born from a legitimate marriage. For both reason itself, the propriety of civil life, and the laws of more refined nations favor these over natural children <note>original: *naturalibus*; children born outside of a legal marriage, who often had fewer inheritance rights in historical legal systems</note>. These rules, however, only hold firm unless a father, for sufficient reasons, has refused to recognize someone as his son, or unless he has disowned <note>original: *abdicaverit*; the formal legal act of disinheriting or renouncing a child</note> the same due to some most atrocious wickedness. Under the term "children" are also included those of further degrees <note>i.e., grandchildren and great-grandchildren</note>, whom the grandfather is bound to support if their own parents are deceased. Thus, it is most equitable that they should compete for the inheritance of the grandfather along with their paternal and maternal uncles; this is so that, on top of the calamity of a father’s early death, they are not also excluded from their grandfather's inheritance. Where descendants are lacking, it is right that the goods of the deceased children should pass back to the *parents*. For those who have no surviving children or parents, their brothers will succeed them. Where these are also lacking, the heir will be whoever is closest to the deceased by blood. Although, to avoid the very frequent lawsuits that can arise here, and at the same time so that the matter may be properly adjusted for the benefit of the commonwealth <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: *reipublice*; the organized political community or state</note>, in most cities the order of succession is found to be accurately arranged by law. It is safest for private individuals to follow this established order, unless grave causes compel them to make specific arrangements <note>referring to the creation of a private will to bypass the standard legal order of inheritance</note>.

# CHAPTER XII. 115

§. 12. By the act of the previous owner, the entirety of a person's property is transferred upon death through a **will** <note>original: *testamentum*</note>. Indeed, this practice is accepted by most as a certain solace for our mortality: that a living person can arrange to transfer his goods, in the event of his death, to the person he loves most. Furthermore, while it seems to have been the custom in the most ancient times for people to openly name their heirs when death was imminent and to hand over the estate to them from hand to hand, many nations later favored a different kind of will for weightier reasons. Specifically, this allowed people to signify their last will at whatever time they pleased, either openly or privately in writing. This could be changed at the owner's whim, and the legal rights for the named or written heirs would only come into existence at the moment the testator <note>the person making the will</note> breathed his last. And although such last wills rightly enjoy great legal favor, they must nevertheless be regulated according to the requirements of family obligations and the utility of the state <note>original: *civitatum*</note>. For this reason, laws are accustomed to provide rules for how wills must be structured. Anyone who departs from these legal arrangements cannot complain that his personal wishes were not taken into account.

§. 13. Between living persons, property passes by the act of the previous owner either **for free** or through a **contract**. Transfers of the first kind are called **donations** <note>original: *donationes*; gifts given without expectation of compensation</note>. Contracts shall be discussed in the sections below.

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of property transfer. Having previously covered voluntary transfers—such as wills, donations, and contracts—the author now examines how property can be transferred involuntarily (as punishment or in war) and introduces the concept of legal prescription (usucapio).</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

§. 14. Sometimes property is also transferred **against the will of the previous owner** <note>original: *invito priore domino*</note>. This occurs within states primarily by way of punishment; for those condemned for crimes, sometimes all their goods, and sometimes a specific portion of them, are seized and applied either to the public treasury or to the injured party. Likewise, in war, property is seized from the unwilling by an enemy superior in strength and is acquired by the captor. However, the previous owner does not lose the right to recover these items by equal force, until they have renounced all claim to them through subsequent peace treaties.

§. 15. Finally, a unique method of acquisition is **prescription** <note>original: *usucapio*; a legal concept where ownership is gained through long-term possession</note>. Through this, someone who has obtained possession of a thing in **good faith** <note>original: *bona fide*; believing they have a right to the item</note> and with a **just title** <note>original: *justo titulo*; a legitimate legal reason for possession, such as a purchase or inheritance</note>, and has held it for a long, quiet, and uninterrupted time, is eventually regarded as the absolute owner of that thing. Consequently, they can turn away the former owner if that person later wishes to reclaim it. 

The reason this law was introduced was twofold: partly because a person who neglects to claim their property for a long time is judged to have abandoned it <note>original: *pro relicto*</note>, since opportunities to reclaim it are rarely lacking over a long period; and partly because it is in the interest of public peace and tranquility that the ownership of things eventually be placed beyond dispute. This is especially true because it seems far more grievous to be deprived of something acquired in good faith after possessing it for a long time, than it is to forever go without something lost long ago, the longing for which has long since faded.

## CHAPTER XIII.
# ON THE DUTIES WHICH RESULT FROM THE OWNERSHIP
### of things in themselves.

->§. 1.<-

From the introduction of ownership <term>ownership (dominium): the legal right to possess, use, and exclude others from a resource</term>, these duties have arisen among men. 1. *That everyone is bound to allow another (provided they are not an enemy) to enjoy their own things in peace, and they must not endure to damage, misappropriate, or seize them for themselves by force or fraud.* By this very principle, thefts <note>original: *furta*</note>, robberies <note>original: *rapinae*</note>, and similar crimes directed at the property of others are forbidden.

§. 2. 2. *When the property of another comes into our hands without any crime on our part, and in our good faith <note>original: *bona fide*; meaning the person honestly believes the item is theirs or was acquired legally</note>, and we still have it in our possession, we are bound to ensure, as far as lies within us, that it returns to the possession of its legitimate owner.* However, we are not bound to restore that thing at our own expense; and if we have incurred any expenses in preserving it, we may rightly claim them back, or retain the item until those costs are paid. Furthermore, we are only actually bound to restitution <note>original: *restituendum*; the act of returning something to its rightful owner</note> once it becomes known to us that the thing belongs to another. For then we ought to make it known that the item is in our possession, and that we are not the cause of why...

However, regarding what we have acquired through a just title <note>original: *justo titulo*; a valid legal basis for possession, such as a purchase or inheritance</note>, we are not bound to call it into doubt ourselves, nor to publicly announce it as if to see if anyone wants to claim the thing for themselves. And this duty outweighs particular contracts and provides an exception to them. For example: if a thief, without my knowledge of the theft, should deposit a stolen item with me, and later the true owner of that same item appears, the item must be restored to him, and not to the thief.

§. 3. 3. *But if someone else's property, obtained in good faith <note>original: *bona fide*; meaning the person honestly believed they had the right to the item</note>, has been consumed, it is our duty to restore to the owner only as much as we have become enriched by it:* lest we gain a profit from someone else’s undeserved loss.

§. 4. From these points, the following conclusions are further drawn: 1. A possessor in good faith is not bound to any restitution *if the thing has perished*; because the thing itself is no longer in his possession, nor is there any profit derived from it.

§. 5. 2. A possessor in good faith is bound to restore not only the thing itself, but also any *remaining fruits* <term>fruits (*fructus*): the natural or legal products of a property, such as crops from a field, offspring of animals, or interest from capital</term>. For naturally, to whomever the thing belongs, the fruits belong to the same person. It shall be permitted, however, for the possessor to deduct all expenses incurred for the thing and its cultivation which allowed the fruits to be produced.

§. 6. 3. A possessor in good faith is bound to the restitution of the thing and of the *consumed fruits*; provided that he would have consumed just as much otherwise, and he is able to recover the price of the evicted thing <note>original: *rei evictae*; property legally reclaimed by its rightful owner from a current possessor</note> from the person who sold or gave it to him. §. 7. 4. A possessor in good faith <note>original: *B. f. This is because he no longer possesses the thing, nor has anything replaced the thing in his holdings.

§. 8. 5. If a possessor in good faith should give to another a piece of property belonging to a third party which had been given to him, he is not liable; unless he was entirely obligated by some duty to give away that same amount anyway. In that case, it will be considered a profit to have spared his own property <note>meaning he saved his own money by using the other person's item to fulfill a debt or obligation</note>.

§. 9. 6. If a possessor in good faith has alienated <note>transferred or sold to another</note> someone else’s property that was acquired by an onerous title <note>original: *oneroso titulo*; a legal term for an acquisition that cost the possessor something, such as a purchase</note> in any way whatsoever, he is not liable, except to the extent that he made a profit from it.

§. 10. 7. A possessor in good faith is bound to restore someone else’s property even if it was *acquired by an onerous title*, and he cannot demand back from the true owner what he paid for it, but only from the person from whom he received the thing. This holds unless the owner probably could not have regained possession of his property without some expense, or if he has voluntarily promised a <gloss>reward for finding</gloss> <term>reward for finding</term> <note>original Greek: *μήνυτρον* (mīnytron)</note>.

§. 11. He who *finds* someone else’s property, which likely fell away against the owner’s will, cannot collect it with the intention of withholding it from the owner who is searching for it. However, where the owner does not appear, he may rightly keep it for himself.

### CHAPTER XIV.
## ON PRICE.

->§. I.<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Chapter XIV, transitioning from the legal obligations of a possessor to the economic principles of value and price. It explains why a system of pricing became necessary once private property was established and trade began.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
->BOOK I.<-

After the introduction of private ownership <note>original: *dominia*</note>, it was found that not all things were of the same nature, nor did they provide the same use for human needs. Furthermore, not every person possessed the abundance of things they might desire for their own use. Consequently, it soon became an established practice among men to exchange things with one another. However, because it very often happened that things of a different nature or use had to be transferred, it was necessary to ensure that neither party was harmed by such an exchange. Therefore, by human agreement, it became necessary to assign a certain quantity to things, according to which they could be compared and made equal to one another. This same thing occurred regarding actions <note>services or labor</note>, which people decided not to perform for the benefit of others for free. That quantity is commonly known by the name of ***price*** <note>original: *pretij*</note>.

§ 1. Price is divided into ***common*** and ***eminent***. The former is considered in things and actions or labor that enter into commerce, based on the degree to which they bring some use or pleasure to humans. The latter, however, is seen in ***money*** <note>original: *numo*; referring to coinage or currency</note>, insofar as it is understood to virtually contain the price of all things and labor, and to provide a common measure for them.

§. 3. *The foundation of common price* in itself is that fitness of a thing or labor by which it can contribute, either directly or indirectly, to the necessities of human life, and toward making that life more comfortable or pleasant. Hence, we are accustomed to calling things that provide no use at all "of no price." Yet, there are certain things...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XIV regarding the concept of price. While the previous page established the necessity of assigning value to goods and labor for the sake of fair exchange, this page focuses on things that are excluded from this system of valuation.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIV.</header>

...most useful to human life, for which it is understood that no fixed price is set; either because they are and ought to be free from ownership <note>original: *dominio*</note>, or because they are unfit for exchange and thus set aside from trade, or because in trade they are never considered as anything other than an appendage to another thing. But also, when divine or human law has removed certain actions from commerce, or forbidden them to be performed for a fee, it is understood that the price has also been taken away from them. Thus, because the upper parts of the air, the ether <note>In older science, the substance filling the space of the universe beyond the clouds</note>, the celestial bodies, and the vast ocean are exempt from human ownership, no price can be assigned to them. Likewise, there is no price for a free person <note>original: *liberi capitis*</note>, because free people do not enter into commerce. Likewise, the sunny light of the sun, the clear and pure air, the pleasant face of the earth (insofar as it merely feeds the eyes), the wind, shade, and similar things considered in and of themselves have no price; since humans cannot enjoy such things without the use of the earth. Yet these very things carry great weight in increasing or decreasing the price of regions, fields, and estates. Likewise, it is unlawful to sell sacred actions for a price, to which some moral effect has been assigned by divine institution; a crime which they call **simony** <note>The act of buying or selling ecclesiastical privileges, such as pardons or benefices</note>. And a judge wickedly holds **justice** for sale.

§. 4. Now, the reasons why [the price] of one and the same thing...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XIV regarding why some things have a price and others do not. ...the reasons why the price may be ***increased or decreased***, and why one thing is preferred over another—even though the latter may seem to have an equal or greater use in human life—are various. For in this matter, the necessity of a thing or the nobility of its use do not always hold the primary place. On the contrary, we see that those things which human life can least do without are often the most inexpensive <note>original: *viliſſima*; literally "the vilest" or "cheapest"</note>; this is because, not without the singular providence of God, nature pours forth a bountiful supply of them.

Therefore, **rarity** primarily contributes to the *increase* <note>original: *intenfionem*; an intensification or raising of the price</note> of price; this rarity is valued even more when things are brought from remote places. From this, the ambitious luxury of men has set enormous prices on many things that human life could very easily do without—for example, pearls <note>original: *unionibus*</note> and gemstones. However, the prices of things for daily use are increased most significantly when their rarity is combined with necessity or want.

Besides rarity, the prices of **manufactured goods** <term>artificialium: objects created by human skill and artifice rather than found in nature</term> are usually increased by the subtlety and elegance of the craftsmanship they display, and sometimes by the reputation of the artist, as well as the difficulty of the work, the scarcity of craftsmen and laborers, and similar factors.

The price of **labor and services** is increased by their difficulty, the skill required, their utility, and necessity; the rarity, dignity, and freedom of those performing the work; and finally the reputation of the craft itself—whether it is considered noble or ignoble. The opposites of these factors tend to decrease the price. Finally, sometimes cer-

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XIV regarding the determination of prices. ### CHAPTER XIV.

...[some]thing is valued highly, not universally, but by individuals out of a peculiar affection <note>The text begins mid-word, likely completing a term regarding how an object is esteemed.</note>; for instance, because the person from whom the item came is very highly regarded by us, and the object was given to express that person's affection; or because we have become accustomed to it; or because it serves as a monument to a remarkable event; or because, by its help, we turned away a great misfortune; or because it was fashioned by our own hands. This is usually called **sentimental value** <term>pretium affectionis: literally the "price of affection," referring to the subjective value an object holds for a specific person regardless of its market price</term>.

§. 5. However, regarding the definition of the prices of individual things, other factors have also traditionally been considered. Among those who live together in a state of **natural liberty** <term>naturali libertate: the condition of humans before the establishment of a formal government or civil laws</term>, the prices of individual things are defined in no other way than by the agreement of the contracting parties. This is because they are free to part with or acquire whatever they wish, and they have no common master to regulate their trade. 

But in **civil states** <note>original: *civitatibus*; organized political communities or nations</note>, the prices of things are defined in two ways: first, by the decree of a superior or by law; second, by the common estimation and judgment of men, or the **custom of the market** <term>usus fori: the "use of the forum," meaning the prevailing market rate</term>, along with the consent of those who are entering a contract with one another. The former is usually called the **legal price** <note>original: *pretium legitimum*</note>, and the latter the **common price** <note>original: *pretium vulgare*; the standard market value</note>. Where a legal price has been established for the benefit of buyers (which is the more frequent case), it is not permitted for sellers to demand more; though they are not prohibited from accepting less if they so choose. Thus, where the wages of labor—

...[it] is rare for it to be publicly fixed <note>original: *taxata*; refers to a price or wage set by law or authority</note> in favor of those who hire; a worker is not permitted to demand more, even if he is not forbidden from accepting less.

§. 6. Indeed, the **common price** <term>vulgare pretium: the standard market rate when not strictly regulated by law</term>, which is not fixed by laws, has a certain latitude within which more or less can and usually is given and received, as was agreed between the contracting parties. This, however, almost always follows the **custom of the market** <term>usum fori: literally "usage of the forum"; the prevailing market rates or standards</term>. In this context, account is usually taken of the labor and expenses that merchants commonly incur in transporting and handling goods; as well as how the item is bought or sold—whether in large quantities or in small portions <note>referring to the difference between wholesale and retail pricing</note>. 

The common price also sometimes changes suddenly due to the abundance or scarcity of buyers, money, or goods. For a scarcity of buyers and of money (arising from some specific cause) along with an abundance of goods reduces the price. Conversely, a large number of buyers <note>original: *emturientium*; those eager to buy</note> and of money, along with a rarity of goods, increases the price. Likewise, it tends to lower the price if the goods are searching for a buyer <note>i.e., the seller is eager or desperate to sell</note>. On the other hand, the price is increased when the seller is approached and asked, though they would not have sold otherwise. Finally, it is also usually considered whether one offers cash <note>original: *paratam... pecuniam*; literally "ready money"</note> or delays payment to a later date; for even the time allowed for payment is part of the price <note>an early legal recognition that the "time value of money" justifies a higher price for credit than for cash</note>.

### CHAPTER XIV.

...[it was found] that this common price alone was not enough to manage human affairs and the expansion of trade. For at that time, commerce consisted solely of barter <term>permutatio: the direct exchange of one good or service for another without a medium like money</term>, and the labor of others could not be hired in any other way than through mutual labor or the delivery of a physical item. But once we began to desire such a variety of things for our convenience or pleasure, it was certainly not easy for everyone to possess the kind of things that another would want to exchange for, or which were equivalent to the other's goods. 

Furthermore, in refined societies, where citizens are distinguished by various social classes <note>original: *variis statibus*; referring to the different ranks, professions, and economic roles in a developed city</note>, many types of people necessarily exist who—if that simple exchange of goods and labor were the only system in place—would be completely unable to sustain their lives, or could do so only with great difficulty. Hence, it seemed right to most nations that desired a more comfortable lifestyle to impose, by a certain agreement, an **eminent price** <term>pretium eminens: an "exalted" or formal value placed on a medium of exchange (money) to represent the worth of all other things</term> upon a specific item. The common prices of all other things would be measured against this item, and their value would be "virtually" contained within it. Thus, through the intervention of that one item, anyone could conveniently purchase any saleable thing and carry out any trade or contract.

§. 8. For this purpose, most nations chose to use **nobler metals** <note>original: *metalla nobiliora*; referring primarily to gold and silver, valued for their rarity and beauty</note> that were not very common. For these are of a very compact substance, such that they are not easily worn down by use, and they allow for division into many minute...

BOOK I.

...[allow for division into] small parts. Furthermore, these same materials are easy to keep safe and to handle; and because of their rarity, they can be equivalent in value to many other things. Occasionally, out of necessity—and among certain nations due to a lack of metals—other things have been used in place of money.

§. 9. Furthermore, within states, *it is the responsibility of the sovereign rulers to define the value of money*; and for this reason, public marks are usually stamped upon it. However, in defining this value, one must respect the common estimation of neighboring nations, or those with whom we conduct trade. For otherwise, if a state sets too high a value on its coins, or if it does not properly balance their material <note>This refers to the "fineness" or purity of the gold or silver content in the coinage.</note>, it will greatly hinder <term>sufflaminabit: literally "to put a brake on"; meaning to obstruct or clog the flow of trade</term> the commerce of its citizens with foreigners—at least those transactions which cannot be settled through the simple exchange of goods. For that very reason, a change in the value of money should not be made lightly, unless the highest necessity of the state <note>original: *reipublicae*</note> requires it. This is true even if, as the abundance of gold and silver grows, the value of money tends to decrease on its own when compared to the price of land and the things that depend on it.

# CHAPTER XV.

->§. 1.<-

An agreement <note>original: *Pactum*</note> in its general kind is the consensus and convention of two or more people toward the same shared intent. But because simple *pacts* are frequently distinguished from *contracts*, the difference seems to consist mainly in this: that *contracts* is the name given to *pacts* which involve things and actions that enter into commerce, and therefore presuppose the ownership and prices of things. But conventions entered into concerning other matters are designated by the common word "pacts." Even so, among some of these, the names of pact and contract are granted interchangeably.

§. 2. Contracts can be divided into *beneficial* and *onerous*. The former bring some advantage to one of the contracting parties for free; such as a *mandate* <note>original: *mandatum*; a commission where one person performs a service for another for free</note>, a *loan for use* <note>original: *commodatum*</note>, or a *deposit* <note>original: *depositum*; placing an object in the care of another for safekeeping</note>. The latter <note>onerous contracts</note> bind both parties to an equal burden; for in these, something is provided or given for the purpose of receiving just as much in return.

§. 3. All onerous contracts, however, have this feature: that *equality* must be observed in them, or that each of the contracting parties should obtain just as much as the other. Where inequality has occurred, a right arises for the party who received less to demand that what is lacking be supplied, or even to break off the contract entirely. This, however, is especially the rule in civil states <note>meaning organized societies with established legal systems</note>, where the practice

### BOOK I.

To find and determine this equality, however, it is required that both of the contracting parties are aware of the thing itself being contracted, along with its qualities that carry weight in the transaction. From this, it follows that the person who is to transfer an item to another via contract must disclose not only the valuable qualities of the item, but also its deficiencies and defects. Without this disclosure, a just price cannot be established. However, it is not necessary to disclose external circumstances that do not directly affect the thing itself. Furthermore, defects that are already known to both parties do not need to be pointed out. Anyone who knowingly buys something defective has only themselves to blame <note>original: *habet quod sibi imputet*; a legal expression meaning the person must bear the consequences of their own choices or negligence</note>.

§. 4. Moreover, equality must be maintained in these contracts to such a degree that, even if nothing was intentionally hidden, if an inequality is discovered later—even without any fault of the parties (for example, if a defect was hidden or there was a mistake in the price)—it should be corrected. A deduction should be made from the person who received more, and an addition made for the one who has less. Though, to prevent a flood of lawsuits, civil laws <note>the specific laws of a state or city</note> hardly provide a remedy in these cases except for <term>enormous losses</term> <note>original: *enormibus laesionibus*; a legal principle where a contract can be rescinded or corrected if the price is severely unfair, typically if the price is less than half the true value</note>; for other matters, the law bids everyone to be diligent in their own affairs.

§. 5. *Beneficial contracts are primarily of three types: the <term>mandate</term> <note>a commission where one person performs a service for another for free</note>, the <term>loan for use</term> <note>original: *commodatum*; a free loan of a specific item that must be returned exactly as it was</note>, and the <term>deposit</term> <note>placing an object in the care of another for safekeeping without payment</note>.* Man-

### CHAPTER XV.

# Of Gratuitous Contracts

[a mandate] is given, when someone voluntarily undertakes to manage the business of another at the request and commission of that person. This happens in a *twofold way*: either a specific method for handling the matter is prescribed to the person, or the matter is left to their own judgment and skill. In this contract, one must act with the highest degree of good faith and diligence; for almost no one entrusts a task to anyone except a friend, or someone of whom they have the highest opinion. Conversely, the <term>mandatary</term> <note>The person who accepts the commission or task</note> must be kept free from loss regarding expenses incurred for the matter entrusted to them, as well as from damages they suffered because of the mandate, provided those damages specifically arose from the matter itself.

§. 6. A <term>gratuitous loan</term> <note>Latin: *commodatum*; a loan where the specific item must be returned, rather than a loan of money or grain</note> is when we grant the use of our property to someone for free. In this case, it must be observed that the person must preserve and handle the same item carefully and with the utmost diligence. They should not use it for other purposes, nor for any longer than the lender granted. Furthermore, they must return it unharmed and in the same condition it was received, except for what was lost through ordinary wear and tear. If the item was granted for a set period of time, and in the meantime the owner begins to have a great need for it due to some circumstance unforeseen at the time the loan was made, it must be returned to the owner upon request without any delay <note>original: *citra tergiversationem*; literally "without turning one's back" or making excuses</note>. However, if the borrowed item is lost by a chance and unforeseen accident without any fault on the part of the <term>borrower</term> <note>Latin: *commodatarius*</note>, the value of the item does not have to be paid [to the owner].

<note>Vocabulary from the original Latin: **Mandate** (commission), **Gratuitous loan** (loan of a specific item), **Contract**, **Good faith**, **Diligence**, **Mandatary** (the one performing the task), **Loss/Damage**, **Usage**, **Owner/Master**, **Borrower**.</note>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...where the same item would have perished anyway even in the owner's possession. Otherwise, it seems fair for the <term>borrower</term> <note>Latin: *commodatarium*; the person who receives the item for use</note> to pay the estimated value of the item, since the owner would not have lost it had he not been kind toward the other person. Conversely, if any useful or necessary expenses were incurred for the loaned item—beyond those costs that normally and regularly accompany the use of the thing—the owner must refund that amount.

§. 7. A <term>deposit</term> <note>Latin: *depositum*</note> occurs when we entrust our property, or anything relating to us in any way, to the good faith of another so that they may guard it for free. In this arrangement, it is required that the entrusted thing be guarded diligently and be restored whenever the <term>depositor</term> <note>The person who hands over the item for safekeeping</note> wishes; unless that restoration would be harmful to the owner or others, in which case the return should be delayed for that reason. 

It is not permitted to use the deposited item without the owner's consent, especially if the item would become worse in any way through use, or if the owner has an interest in the item not being seen. If anyone dares to do this, they shall be responsible for any risks or dangers the item encounters because of that use. Furthermore, it is not permitted to remove the deposited item from the bindings, containers, or wrappers in which it was enclosed by the depositor. 

While it is a very shameful thing—indeed, more foul than theft—to deny that a deposit was ever made, it is far more shameful if someone denies an <term>emergency deposit</term> <note>Latin: *depositum miserabile*; a deposit made under duress or "miserable" circumstances</note>, which is one made due to the danger of fire, a collapsing building, or a riot. In turn, the expenses incurred for the deposited item must be refunded by the depositor...

# CHAPTER XV.

§. 8. Among <term>onerous contracts</term> <note>Latin: *contractus onerosos*; agreements where both parties give and receive something of value, as opposed to "gratuitous" or free gifts</note>, the oldest is <term>exchange</term> <note>Latin: *permutatio*; also known as barter</note>, which was almost the only way commerce was conducted before the invention of money. In this arrangement, a thing is given on both sides in return for an equivalent thing. Even today, following the invention of money, a type of exchange remains especially common among merchants where things are not simply compared to each other, but are first valued in money and then delivered in place of money. However, a different kind of transaction from the contract of exchange is <term>reciprocal donation</term> <note>Latin: *reciproca donatio*</note>; in this case, it is not necessary to observe strict equality between the items given.

§. 9. <term>Buying and selling</term> <note>Latin: *emptio venditio*</note> is the contract by which ownership of a thing, or a right equivalent to it, is acquired in exchange for money. Its simplest form is when, once the price has been agreed upon, the buyer immediately offers the price and the seller immediately offers and delivers the goods. Frequently, however, it is agreed that the goods be delivered immediately, but the price is to be paid after a certain interval of time. Sometimes the price is agreed upon, but the delivery of the thing or goods is set to occur at a certain fixed date. In such cases, it seems consistent with <term>equity</term> <note>Latin: *æquitas*; fairness and natural justice</note> that the thing remains at the risk of the seller until that date has passed. But if, after that date has passed, the buyer is in <term>default</term> <note>Latin: *mora*; a legal delay or failure to perform an obligation on time</note>—meaning they did not take care to have the item delivered to them—the loss of the thing shall thereafter fall upon the buyer. It is also common to add various additional pacts to the contract of buying and selling, such as an <term>adjudication to a date</term> <note>Latin: *addictio in diem*; a clause allowing the seller to cancel the sale if a better offer is received within a certain timeframe</note>, by which a thing is sold in such a way that it is permitted...

->### BOOK I.<-

This refers to a clause known as *in diem addictio*.</note> ...to the seller to accept a better condition, offered by another party within a certain time. Also the ***forfeiture clause*** <note>Latin: *lex commissoria*; a provision where a sale is cancelled and the buyer loses their deposit if the full price is not paid by a specific deadline</note>, when it is so agreed that if the price is not paid by a certain day, the thing is considered unbought. Likewise, the ***right of redemption*** <note>Latin: *retractus*</note>, or an agreement for buying back, which is either conceived so that if the price is offered within a certain time, or at any time, the buyer is bound to restore the thing to the seller; or so that if the thing is offered back, the seller is bound to restore the price; or so that if the buyer is about to sell it again of his own accord, the original seller is admitted to buy it before all others: which is also called the <term>right of first refusal</term> <note>Latin: *jus protimiseos*, from the Greek for "priority in purchase"</note>. So also it is frequent that from estates sold, the seller reserves some small part for himself, or a certain use of it. There is also a type of purchase which they call ***in bulk*** <note>Latin: *per aversionem*; a "lump sum" purchase of a group of items without individual valuation</note>, when many things of different prices are bought together, not valued individually, but appraised jointly and as if in a mixed heap. In that method of selling which is called an ***auction*** <note>Latin: *auctio*</note>, the thing is finally knocked down to that person who, among several bidders, has offered the most. Finally, there is even a type of purchase by which not a certain thing, but only a probable ***hope*** <note>Latin: *spes*; in legal terms *emptio spei*, such as buying the "cast of a net" from a fisherman before he pulls it up</note> is bought; in which a element of a gamble is mixed: so that neither the buyer, if the hope fails, nor the seller, if the result should be much greater than expected, ought to complain.

§. 10. ***Leasing and hiring*** <note>Latin: *locatio conductio*; the Roman law category covering the renting of goods, the hiring of services, or the contracting of specific pieces of work</note> is the contract by which the use of a thing, or labor, is assigned to someone for a fee. In which...

### CHAPTER XV.

Although it is customary for the <term>payment</term> <note>original: "mercede"; in this context, it refers to either wages for labor or rent for a property.</note> to be agreed upon in advance; nevertheless, if anyone offers their labor or the use of their property to another without a previously defined price, they are considered to expect as much as common usage or the fairness of the <term>hirer</term> <note>Latin: *conductor*; the person who pays for the use of a thing or the labor of another.</note> shall allow. Regarding this contract, it must be observed that if the thing leased is completely destroyed, from that time forward the hirer is no longer held to the payment or rent. 

But if the thing leased has a certain and defined use, just as the <term>owner</term> <note>Latin: *dominus*; the lessor or landlord.</note> ought to provide it so that it is fit for that use, so too if the property suffers any damage, the hirer deducts from the rent as much as the use of the thing has decreased. However, if the yield of the leased thing is uncertain and contains some element of <term>risk</term> <note>Latin: *alea*; literally "a game of dice," used here to mean the speculative nature of an investment or harvest.</note>, then just as an overflowing harvest is to the hirer's profit, so a meager one is to his loss. Under strict law, nothing should be deducted from the rent on account of <term>barrenness</term> <note>Latin: *sterilitatem*; a failed harvest or lack of productivity.</note>, especially since the barrenness of one year is usually compensated by the abundance of another. 

An exception is made if the events that hindered the yield were quite rare, and of such a <term>risk</term> that the hirer is presumed not to have considered taking it upon himself. In such cases, it is indeed equitable that they should serve to reduce or remit the rent. Furthermore, just as the one who has leased a thing to another is bound to ensure that it is fit for use and to bear the necessary expenses, so the hirer is bound to use it in the manner of a <term>good head of a household</term> <note>Latin: *bonus pater familias*; a standard legal benchmark for a person acting with appropriate care, diligence, and responsibility.</note>, and to repair what has been damaged by his own fault...

->**BOOK I.**<-

...if the price of labor <note>original: "rarum"; though the transcription reads "rarum," the context suggests "mercede" or "pretium" (the price/wage) from the previous page's discussion on labor.</note> is publicly <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "taxata"; refers to a price ceiling or a legally mandated rate set by authorities.</note> in favor of those who hire, the laborer is not allowed to demand more, although he is not prohibited from accepting less.

§. 6. Indeed, the common market price, which is not fixed by laws, has a certain range within which more or less can and usually is given and received, according to what was agreed upon between the contracting parties. This, however, almost always follows the <term>market custom</term> <note>Latin: *usus fori*; literally "the usage of the forum," referring to the prevailing standards and practices of the local marketplace.</note>. In this context, account is usually taken of the labor and expenses that merchants commonly incur in transporting and handling goods; as well as whether the item is bought or sold in large quantities or <term>at retail</term> <note>Latin: *minutatim*; literally "in small pieces" or "bit by bit."</note>. Sometimes the common price also changes suddenly due to the abundance or scarcity of buyers, money, or goods. For a scarcity of buyers and money (arising from some specific cause) and an abundance of goods decreases the price. Conversely, a crowd of <term>eager buyers</term> <note>Latin: *emturientium*; a specific grammatical form expressing a strong desire or "itching" to buy.</note> and a plenty of money, combined with a rarity of goods, increases the price. Likewise, it tends to decrease the price if the goods must seek out a buyer. On the other hand, the price is increased when the seller is specifically requested to sell, being otherwise unwilling to do so. Finally, it is also customary to consider whether someone offers <term>ready money</term> <note>Latin: *paratam pecuniam*; cash on the spot.</note> or defers payment to a later date; since <term>time is part of the price</term> <note>Latin: *dies sit pars pretij*; an early legal recognition of the time value of money—that a payment today is worth more than a payment in the future.</note>.

§. 7. Furthermore, after mortals departed from their primeval simplicity and introduced various kinds of <term>gainful activity</term> <note>Latin: *quaestus*; refers to profit-making ventures, trade, or occupations intended to accumulate wealth.</note>, it was easily perceived...

<page-num>125</page-num>

# CHAPTER XIV.

<meta>The opening word "sum" likely completes "perspectum" or "compertum" from the previous page, meaning "it was perceived" or "it was found."</meta>

...perceived, that the common price alone was not sufficient for conducting the business of men and for expanding commerce. For at that time, trade consisted solely of <term>barter</term> <note>Latin: *permutatione*; the direct exchange of goods for goods.</note>, and the labor of others could not be hired out in any way other than through mutual labor or the delivery of a physical thing. But after we began to desire such a variety of things for our convenience or pleasure, it was certainly not easy for everyone to possess the kinds of items that another might want to exchange for their own, or which were equivalent in value to the other person's goods. Furthermore, in civilized states, where citizens are distinguished by various social ranks, it is necessary that many types of people exist who, if that simple exchange of goods and labor were still in force, would plainly be unable to sustain their lives, or could do so only with great difficulty. Hence, to most nations who favored a more refined way of life, it seemed best by a certain agreement to impose an <term>eminent price</term> <note>Latin: *pretium eminens*; a technical term in early modern economic theory referring to the "official" or universal value assigned to money, which acts as a standard for all other "common" prices.</note> upon a specific thing. Against this standard, the common prices of all other things would be measured, and within it, those same values would be virtually contained. Thus, with that specific thing intervening as a medium, anyone could acquire for themselves any saleable item and could conveniently carry out any trade or contract.

§. 8. To this end, most nations chose to employ the more *noble metals*, which are not found everywhere <note>literally "not very common."</note>. For these are substances of a very compact nature, such that they cannot easily be worn down by use, and they allow for division into many small...

<summary>This page explains the historical and social transition from a barter economy to a money-based economy, arguing that "eminent price" (currency) was necessary to sustain complex societies and various social classes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 125 regarding the nature of money. The previous page ended describing how money acts as a medium of exchange; this page begins by explaining why certain materials (metals) are chosen for this purpose due to their physical properties.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

admit of division into small parts. They are no less suitable for storage and handling; and because of their rarity, they can be equivalent in value to many other things. Nevertheless, sometimes out of necessity, and among certain nations due to a lack of metals, other things have been used in place of coins <note>Latin: *numi*; referring here to any standardized currency.</note>.

§. 9. Furthermore, in established states, *it is the responsibility of the supreme rulers to define the values of coins*; and for that reason, public marks <note>official seals or images stamped onto currency to guarantee its weight and purity</note> are usually imprinted upon them. However, in defining this value, the common estimation of neighboring nations, or those with whom we conduct trade, must be taken into account. For otherwise, if a state assigns too high a value to its coins, or if it does not properly temper the quality of their material <note>Latin: *materiam... rite non temperaverit*; referring to the debasement of currency or the mixing of base metals with gold/silver</note>, it will greatly hinder <note>original: *sufflaminabit*; literally "to clog a wheel," meaning to obstruct or slow down progress</note> the commerce of its citizens with foreigners—at least that trade which cannot be settled by the simple <term>barter</term> of goods. For that very reason, a change in the value of coins should not be made lightly, unless the highest necessity of the state <note>Latin: *reipub.*, short for *respublica*</note> requires it. This is true even though, as the supply of gold and silver increases, the value of coins in comparison to the price of land and the things that depend on land usually tends to decrease of its own accord.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of money and currency value on page 126 to the legal and ethical framework of agreements. It introduces the definitions of pacts and contracts, categorizing them by their purpose and the requirement for fairness.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XV.</header>

->§. 1.<-

A pact <note>original: *pactum*</note>, in its general sense, is the consent and agreement of two or more people toward the same shared intention. However, because simple *pacts* are frequently distinguished from *contracts*, the difference seems to consist primarily in this: *contracts* are the name given to those agreements that concern things and actions entering into commerce <note>referring to trade, buying, and selling in a marketplace</note>, and thus they presuppose ownership and the price of things. Agreements entered into concerning other matters are designated by the common term "pacts." Nevertheless, among some of these, the names of "pacts" and "contracts" are used interchangeably.

§. 2. Contracts can be divided into those that are *beneficial* <note>original: *beneficos*; also known as gratuitous contracts where only one side benefits</note> and those that are *onerous*. The former bring some advantage to one of the contracting parties for free, such as a <term>mandate</term> <note>a commission where one person asks another to perform a service for free</note>, a <term>commodatum</term> <note>a "loan for use," where an item is lent for free to be returned later</note>, or a <term>deposit</term> <note>keeping someone’s property safe without charge</note>. The latter <note>the "onerous" contracts</note> bind both parties to an equal burden; for in these, something is performed or given for the purpose of receiving just as much in return.

§. 3. Furthermore, all onerous contracts have this requirement: that *equality* <note>original: *æqualitas*</note> must be observed within them, meaning that each of the two contracting parties should obtain just as much as the other. Where an inequality occurs, the party who received less acquires a right to demand that what is lacking be supplied, or even to break off the contract entirely. This principle, however, holds most weight in civil states <note>established societies with formal legal systems</note>, where the practice of the...

<summary>This page defines the legal distinction between pacts and contracts, categorizing contracts into beneficial (gratuitous) and onerous (reciprocal) types. It emphasizes the necessity of "equality" or fair exchange in contracts involving trade.</summary>
<keywords>contracts, pacts, beneficial contracts, onerous contracts, mandate, deposit, equality in exchange, legal ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 127 regarding "onerous" contracts, where both parties must receive equal value. It transitions from the general requirement of equality to the specific obligation of disclosing defects and the legal remedies for unfair pricing.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

...ties, or the prices of things are defined by the market <note>original: *fori*</note> or by law. To find and determine this equality, it is required that for both contracting parties, the thing itself being traded—along with its qualities that have any significance here—must be known. Therefore, he who is to transfer a thing to another through a contract must disclose not only the valuable qualities of the item, but also its deficiencies and defects <note>original: *vitia*</note>. Without this, a just price cannot be established. However, it is not necessary to disclose circumstances that do not directly affect the thing itself. Furthermore, defects already known to both parties do not need to be pointed out. And if someone knowingly purchases something defective, he has only himself to blame.

§. 4. Furthermore, equality must be applied in these contracts to such an extent that, even if nothing was concealed, if an inequality is later discovered—even without the fault of the contracting parties—it must be corrected. For example, if a defect was hidden or there was an error in the price, the excess must be taken from the one who has more and given to the one who has less. Although, to avoid a massive number of lawsuits, civil laws <note>the laws of the state, as opposed to natural or moral law</note> hardly provide a remedy here except for cases of extreme loss <term>enormibus læsionibus</term> <note>a legal principle where a contract can be overturned if the price is so unfair—usually less than half the value—that it constitutes an "enormous injury"</note>; otherwise, the laws command everyone to look after their own affairs.

§. 5. *There are three primary beneficial contracts: a mandate, a loan for use, and a deposit.* A mandate <note>original: *mandatum*; a contract where one person performs a service for another for free</note>...

...is established, when someone undertakes the business of another for free <note>original: *gratis*</note>, with that person requesting and entrusting the task. This occurs in a **twofold** manner: either the specific method of carrying out the matter is prescribed for him, or it is left to his own judgment and skill. In this contract, one must act with the highest degree of good faith and diligence; for indeed, hardly anyone entrusts a mandate <note>a "mandate" in this sense is a legal commission where one person acts on behalf of another</note> except to a friend, or someone of whom he has the highest opinion. Conversely, the agent <term>mandatarius</term> <note>the person performing the task</note> must be kept free from loss regarding the expenses incurred for the matter entrusted to him, as well as from any damages he suffered because of the mandate, provided they arose specifically from the business itself.

§. 6. A **Gratuitous Loan** <term>commodatum</term> <note>a "commodatum" is a specific type of loan where an item is lent for use for free and the exact same item must be returned</note> is that by which we grant the use of our property to someone for free. Regarding this, it must be observed that the borrower should preserve and handle the item solicitously and with the greatest care. He must not use it for purposes other than those the lender granted, nor for longer than agreed. He must return it unharmed and in the same condition it was received, except for what was lost through ordinary wear and tear. If the item was granted for a fixed time, but in the meantime the owner begins to have a great need for it—due to some event that was unforeseen at the time the loan was made—it must be returned to the owner upon request without any hesitation. However, if the loaned item is lost through a chance accident or unforeseen event without any fault of the borrower <term>commodatarius</term>, the value of the item does not have to be paid,

§. 7. A **Deposit** <term>depositum</term> <note>original: *Depositum est*; a contract where one person stores property for another for free</note> is that by which we entrust our property, or something belonging to us in any way, to the good faith of another, so that he may guard it for free. In this, it is required that the entrusted thing be diligently guarded, and be restored whenever it pleases the depositor; unless that restoration would be harmful to the owner or others, and for that reason, it ought to be delayed. Nor is it permitted to use the deposited item without the owner's consent, especially if it would be made worse in any way through use, or if it is in the owner's interest that it not be seen. And if anyone should dare to do this, he shall be liable for any risks the item falls into because of that use. Nor is it permitted to remove the deposited item from those bonds or containers in which it was enclosed by the depositor. While it is very shameful, and more foul than theft, to deny <note>i.e., to refuse to return or acknowledge</note> a deposit; it is much more shameful if someone denies a **necessary deposit** <note>original: *depositum miserabile*; literally a "miserable" or "pitiable" deposit, made in times of emergency when there is no choice of person to trust</note>, such as one made because of the danger of fire, a building's collapse, or a riot. Conversely, expenses made on the deposited item are to be refunded by the depositor,

# CHAPTER XV. #

§. 8. Among **onerous contracts** <term>contractus onerosos</term> <note>legal agreements where both parties provide something of value, creating a mutual obligation or "burden"</note>, perhaps the most ancient, and the only one by which commerce was managed before the invention of money, is **exchange** <term>permutatio</term> <note>commonly known as barter</note>, by which an equivalent thing is given by both sides for another thing. Even today, after the invention of money, a type of exchange is especially common among merchants in which things are not simply compared to one another, but are first valued in money, and then delivered to each other in place of money. However, a **reciprocal donation** <term>reciproca donatio</term> is a different kind of business from a contract of exchange; in this, it is not necessary that equality [of value] be observed.

§. 9. **Buying and selling** <term>emptio venditio</term> is the contract by which ownership <term>dominium</term> of some thing, or a right equivalent to it, is acquired for money. Its simplest form is when, once the price <term>pretium</term> has been agreed upon, the buyer offers and hands over the price, and the seller the goods <term>mercem</term>, immediately. Frequently, however, it is agreed that the goods be delivered immediately, but the price is to be paid after a certain interval. Sometimes an agreement is reached regarding the price, but the delivery of the thing or goods must be made at a certain deadline. Where it seems consistent with equity, the thing is at the risk of the seller <note>original: *periculo venditoris*; meaning the seller bears the loss if the item is destroyed or damaged</note> before that deadline has passed: but if, after it has passed, the buyer has been in delay <note>original: *in mora*; a legal term for a failure to perform an obligation on time</note> in seeing that it is delivered to him, the loss of the thing shall subsequently fall upon the buyer. Various agreements are also usually added to the contract of buying and selling: such as an **adjudication to a date** <term>addictio in diem</term> <note>a clause allowing a seller to cancel a sale if a better offer is received within a specific timeframe</note>, by which a thing is sold in such a way that it is permitted

Likewise, the **forfeiture clause** <term>lex commissoria</term> <note>a provision where a sale is voided if the price is not paid by a specific date</note>, when it is so agreed that if the price is not paid by a certain day, the thing is considered unbought. Likewise, the **right of redemption** <term>retractus</term>, or a "pact of selling back," which is drafted either so that if the price is offered within a certain time, or whenever it is offered, the buyer is bound to restore the thing to the seller; or so that if the thing is offered back, the seller is bound to restore the price; or so that if the buyer should voluntarily decide to sell it again, the original seller is admitted to buy it before all others: which is also called the **right of first refusal** <term>jus protimiseos</term> <note>from the Greek word for "preference"</note>. It is also common that, out of estates sold, the seller reserves for himself some small portion, or a certain use of it. There is also a type of purchase which they call **by the bulk** <term>per aversionem</term> <note>a "lump sum" sale where items are not counted individually</note>, when many things of different prices are bought not by being valued individually, but appraised together and as if in a jumble. In that manner of selling called an **auction** <term>auctio</term>, the thing is finally awarded to the one who, out of several bidders, has offered the most. Finally, there is also a type of purchase where not a specific thing, but only a probable **hope** <term>spes</term> is bought <note>such as paying a fisherman for whatever his next cast of the net brings in</note>; in which an element of chance <note>original: *aleae*; literally "the roll of the dice"</note> is mixed in, so that neither the buyer (if the hope fails) nor the seller (if the yield is far above expectations) ought to complain.

§. 10. **Hiring and leasing** <term>locatio conductio</term> <note>a contract where one party provides the use of an object or their labor in exchange for payment</note> is that by which the use of a thing, or a person’s labor, is assigned to someone for a fee. In which...

->### CHAPTER XV.<-

<p>Although it is customary to agree on the **payment** <term>merces</term> <note>original: *mercede*</note> in advance, if someone grants their labor or the use of their property to another without a previously defined price, they are deemed to expect as much as common practice or the fairness of the **hirer** <term>conductor</term> <note>original: *conductoris*; the person paying for the use of the service or object</note> suggests. Regarding this **contract** <note>original: *contractum*</note>, it must be observed that if the **thing leased** <term>res locata</term> perishes completely, from that time the hirer is no longer held to the payment or **rent** <term>pensio</term> <note>original: *pensionem*</note>. But if the thing leased has a certain and defined use, just as the owner must provide it so that it is fit for that use, so if it suffers some damage, the hirer deducts from the rent as much as the usefulness of the thing has diminished. But if the yield of the leased thing is uncertain and contains some element of **risk** <note>original: *aleæ*; literally "the roll of the dice"</note>, as an abundant yield is to the hirer's gain, so a poor one is to his loss; and by strict law, nothing should be deducted from the rent due to **barrenness** <note>original: *sterilitatem*; referring here to a failed harvest</note>, especially since the barrenness of one year is usually compensated by the **abundance** <note>original: *ubertate*</note> of another. Unless the events that interrupted the yield were quite rare, and it is presumed that the hirer did not contemplate taking that risk upon himself. For it is indeed equitable that these should count toward reducing or waiving the rent. Furthermore, just as the one who has leased something to another is bound to ensure that it is fit for use and to bear the necessary expenses, so the hirer must use the same in the manner of a **good head of a household** <term>bonus pater familias</term> <note>the legal standard of care expected from a prudent and responsible person</note>, and what by his own fault...</p>

**Vocabulary:** payment, hirer, contract, the thing leased, rent, risk, barrenness, abundance, good head of a household.

But one who has hired someone’s **continuous labor** <note>original: *continuam operam*; long-term employment</note> acts inhumanely if, when that person is made unable to work for a short time due to illness or another accident, they either dismiss them entirely from their duties or deduct anything from their **payment** <term>pensio</term> <note>original: *pensionem*; here referring to a regular wage or salary</note>.

§. II. In a **contract of a loan** <term>mutuum</term> <note>original: *contractu mutui*; a specific legal category of loan where the item is consumed and replaced by an equivalent, such as money, grain, or oil</note>, a **fungible thing** <term>res fungibilis</term> is given to someone on the condition that, after an interval of time, they return the same kind in the same quantity and quality. Those things which are usually given in a loan are called *fungible*, or things that "function" within their own class, because any item of that class can take the place of another in such a way that whoever receives the same kind, quantity, and quality is said to have received the same thing they gave. These same things are determined and specified by weight, number, and measure; in this respect, they are also commonly called **quantities**, as distinguished from **specific objects**. Furthermore, something is given as a loan either for free, so that no more is received than was given, or with some profit, which is designated by the term ***interest*** <term>usura</term> <note>original: *usurae*; in this period, the term referred to any interest on a loan, though today "usury" implies excessive or illegal interest</note>. This has nothing in it contrary to natural law, provided it is mod- <note>The text likely continues to say "moderate" or "limited by law."</note>

# CHAPTER XV.

->[...justifi]able,<- and corresponds to the profit that the other person makes from the money or other thing lent; and to my own loss or **lost profit** <term>lucrum cessans</term> <note>original: *lucro cessanti*; a legal term referring to profits that were prevented from being realized because the owner did not have access to their property</note> which I incur from the absence of my property; and provided it is not demanded from poor men, for whom a **loan** <term>mutuum</term> takes the place of **charity** <term>eleemosyna</term>.

§. 12. In a **contract of partnership** <term>contractu societatis</term> <note>original: *contractu societatis*; a legal agreement where parties combine resources for a common goal</note>, two or more people contribute money, property, or labor among themselves for the purpose that whatever profit results from it may be divided among each individual **proportionally** <term>pro rata</term>; and if any loss is sustained, it is borne by each individual in a similar proportional manner. In such a partnership, just as it is one's duty to apply good faith and diligence; so too, one ought not to withdraw from it at an inopportune time or to the **deception** <term>fraudem</term> of a partner. Furthermore, once a partnership is dissolved, each person receives back what they put in, after the profit or loss has been deducted. 

But if one person has contributed money or property, and another has contributed labor, one must consider how the contribution was made. For when the labor of the one is concerned only with managing or selling the other's money or property, the shares of the profit are defined according to the ratio of the benefit of the money or property to the value of the labor; and the **principal** <term>sors</term> <note>original: *sors*; the capital or initial investment</note> remains safe and at the risk of the person who contributed it. However, when labor is expended toward **improving** <term>melioratio</term> a thing contributed by the other party, the laborer is understood to have a share in the thing itself, in proportion to the improvement. But where a partnership of all goods is entered into, just as individual partners must faithfully contribute what they gain; so...

# BOOK I.

->[...with the use of the property,]<-

conversely, each individual must be supported from the common pool in proportion to their status. Moreover, when a **partnership** <term>societas</term> is dissolved, the division of goods is made in proportion to those things which each individual brought into the partnership at the start; no consideration is given to whose specific property produced the profit or loss for the group, unless it was otherwise agreed upon.

§. 13. *There are also several contracts that involve an element of chance.* <note>original: *aleam*; literally "a die" or "gambling," referring here to the legal concept of risk or aleatory contracts</note> Among these you might include **wagers** <term>sponsiones</term>, when one person affirms and another denies the occurrence of some event that is not yet known to either party, with a certain price deposited by both sides which goes to the person whose claim is found to match the outcome. To this category belong all kinds of games in which one competes for a prize. Among these, however, those involving a contest of intellect, dexterity, skill, or strength contain less of an element of chance. In some, intellect and chance exert themselves with equal force. In others, finally, chance predominates. Nevertheless, it is for the rulers of the state to decide to what extent it is in the public interest or for the benefit of individuals that contracts of this kind be tolerated. To this relates the **lottery** <term>lotaria</term>, when several people buy something with pooled money and then decide by lot who alone should receive the whole thing. Likewise the **urn of fortune** <term>olla fortunæ</term> <note>a historical term for a raffle or "lucky dip," where participants draw tickets from a jar</note>, when, after a certain number of inscribed and blank tokens or slips of paper have been cast into an urn, the opportunity to draw them out is purchased for a price...

<meta>catchword: hen- (continuing "extrahendi")</meta>

### CHAPTER XV.

...of drawing <note>completing the word "prehendi" from the previous page</note>, so that the person drawing the lot receives whatever the inscription on that lot indicates. Related to these types of contracts is **insurance** <term>assecuratio</term>, or a contract for avoiding and guaranteeing against risk. In this arrangement, a person accepts a certain sum of money and, in exchange, takes upon themselves and guarantees against the risks that goods will face while being transported to other locations. Thus, if it happens that these goods perish, the insurer is bound to restore their value to the owner.

§. 14. For greater stability and security, **guarantees** <term>fidejussiones</term> and **pledges** <term>pignora</term> are frequently added to contracts. In a **guarantee** <note>original: *fidejussio*; a form of suretyship where a third party takes responsibility for a debt</note>, another person—who is judged suitable by the creditor—takes upon themselves the obligation of the **primary debtor** <term>principalis debitor</term> as a backup, as it were. In this way, if the debtor does not pay, this person steps into their place; however, whatever they have paid out must be refunded to them by the primary debtor. Although a guarantor cannot be held responsible for a greater sum than the primary debtor, there is nothing to prevent the guarantor from being held more strictly to the agreement than the debtor; for indeed, more trust may have been placed in the guarantor than in the debtor. Naturally, however, the primary debtor should be called upon before the guarantor, unless the guarantor has taken the obligation entirely upon themselves; such a person is usually called an **assumer of debt** <term>expromissor</term> <note>original: *expromissor*; a legal term for someone who replaces the original debtor, effectively releasing them from the obligation</note>. But if several people have acted as guarantors for one person, each one is to be approached only for their proportionate share—unless perhaps one of them is unable to pay, or there is no opportunity to approach them.

<summary>This page defines the legal foundations of insurance (assecuratio) and suretyship (fidejussio), explaining how risk is transferred and how third-party guarantors secure a debt.</summary>

<keywords>insurance, risk management, guarantees, suretyship, primary debtor, debt assumer, liability, legal contracts</keywords>

# BOOK I.

...of the debt. For then the rest <note>original: *reliqui*; referring to the remaining co-guarantors or sureties</note> will be burdened with this person's portion.

§. 15. Often, a certain thing is also usually handed over or assigned by a debtor to a creditor to secure a debt, under the name of a **pledge** <term>pignus</term> or a **mortgage** <term>hypotheca</term>, until the debt has been paid off. The purpose of this is not only so that the debtor is urged to pay by the desire to recover their own property, but also so that there is something readily available from which the creditor may be paid. For this reason, pledges are usually worth as much as, or more than, the debt itself.

Furthermore, the things offered as pledges are either **fruit-bearing** <note>original: *fructuosæ*; referring to property that produces income or yield, such as a field or a rental property</note> or **barren** <note>original: *steriles*; assets that do not produce income, such as a piece of furniture or jewelry</note>. Regarding the former, an **antichretic agreement** <note>original: *pactum ἀντιχρήσεως* (antichrēseōs); a contract where the creditor enjoys the use or profits of the property in place of interest payments</note> is often added, whereby the creditor receives the fruits of that pledge in place of interest. Regarding the latter, however, a **forfeiture clause** <note>original: *lex commissoria*</note> is used—namely, so that the pledge passes to the creditor if payment is not made within a certain time. This is not naturally unfair in cases where the pledge is not worth more than the debt and the interest for the intervening time, or if any surplus is returned to the owner.

Just as the creditor must return the pledge once payment is made, so too in the meantime do they owe it no less care than they give their own property. Furthermore, where an antichretic agreement has not been added, and if the item is of such a nature that it is worn down by use, or if the debtor has any interest in it, the creditor cannot use the item against the debtor's will. A **mortgage** <term>hypotheca</term> differs from a **pledge** <term>pignus</term> in that the latter is established by the physical delivery of the item, while the former consists of a bare assignment without the item being delivered.

**pledge, mortgage, antichretic agreement, forfeiture clause, debtor, creditor**

# CHAPTER XVI.
## THE WAYS IN WHICH obligations arising from agreements are discharged.
### §. 1.

Among the ways of discharging obligations <term>solvendi obligationes</term> arising from agreements, whereby the duties stemming from them completely expire, the most natural is the *fulfillment or payment of that which was agreed upon*. Although the person who owes the debt is normally bound to pay it themselves; nevertheless, if the performance is made by someone else in the name of the person who contracted the obligation, the obligation is dissolved; for it generally makes no difference to the law by whom the fulfillment is carried out. However, this is provided that the person who pays on behalf of another—without the intent of making a gift <note>original: *animo non donandi*</note>—can recover what they have spent from the original debtor. Furthermore, payment must be made to the person to whom it is owed, or to someone they have delegated to receive the debt in their name. And finally, that very thing which was agreed upon must be performed or paid: nothing else should be given in its place, and it must be the whole thing, not damaged, not just a part, and not divided, provided likewise at the place and time agreed. Although often...

<meta>This page continues Chapter XVI on the discharge of obligations. It follows the discussion of payment (solutio), picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's observation that while payment should ideally be exact, circumstances may vary.</meta>

->LIBRI I.<-

the kindness of the creditor or the weakness of the debtor might compel the extension of the deadline for payment, or its division, or even the acceptance of one thing in place of another.

§. 2. Obligations are also discharged by *compensation* <term>compensatio; the set-off of a debt</term>, which is the balancing of credit and debt against each other; or, when a debtor is released for the reason that the creditor himself, in turn, clearly owes him an object of the same kind and of the same value. For since, especially in the case of fungible goods <term>res fungibiles; items that can be replaced by identical units, such as grain or money</term>, an equal amount is considered the same thing; and indeed, where there is a mutual debt, the same amount would immediately have to be returned to me as I myself received. Therefore, to avoid useless circuits of payment, it is most convenient for each party to discharge the obligation by retaining what is their own. It is clear, however, that properly only fungible goods of the same kind can be compensated, for which the time of payment is currently present or has passed. Other things or performances of a different nature cannot be compensated, unless perhaps they are both resolved into their estimated value—that is, into money.

§. 3. An obligation is also discharged by *condonation* <term>condonatio; the forgiving of a debt</term> or the remission of the one to whom something was owed, and whose interest it was that the obligation be fulfilled. This is usually done either expressly, through the use of signs testifying to consent—for instance, by giving a formal release <term>acceptilatio; a ritual or formal discharge of a debt</term>, or by the returning or destruction of written bonds <term>chirographis; handwritten debt notes</term>—or tacitly, if the creditor himself hinders the performance or is the cause of why that which is owed to him cannot be provided.

§. 4. Obligations are also customarily dissolved,
