# CHAPTER XVI.

those obligations which consist of performance by both parties may be dissolved while the matter is still untouched <note>original: "re adhuc integra," a legal phrase meaning before any part of the contract has been performed</note>, by *mutual dissent*; unless positive laws <note>Man-made laws or statutes, as opposed to natural law.</note> have forbidden it. However, where something has already been performed by one party, there must follow either a forgiveness of that performance or a compensation so that the loss is made up for in some other way.

§. 5. Furthermore, an obligation is not so much fulfilled as it is broken off by the *treachery* <term>perfidia; a breach of faith or failure to keep a promise</term> of the other party. For when one party does not perform what was agreed upon, the other is not bound to perform what they had undertaken in consideration of that performance. Indeed, the specific terms of performance in agreements are included as conditions for those that follow, as if it were said: "I will perform, provided that you have performed first."

§. 6. Obligations also expire when either the person who was to perform, or the person who was to receive the performance, has *changed the status* <note>Legal standing or condition, such as citizenship or family role.</note> upon which those obligations were solely founded.

§. 7. Obligations expire by the passage of time itself, if their duration was limited to a specific point in time; unless that duration has been extended by an express or silent agreement of the parties. It is necessary, however, that during that time the power to demand the fulfillment of the obligation actually existed.

§. 8. Finally, obligations are dissolved by *death* when they are rooted in the specific person of an individual. For once the subject <note>The individual person who carries the obligation.</note> is removed, it is necessary that their legal attributes <note>original: "accidentia," a philosophical term for qualities or obligations that cannot exist without a subject to hold them.</note> are also extinguished. Often, however, the obligation of the deceased [continues] in their survivors...

In this case, the creditor's consent is required, but not necessarily that of the third-party debtor, whom I can delegate to another even without their knowledge or against their will. For it makes no difference to whom a person pays, but it matters greatly from whom a person demands a debt.*

## ON INTERPRETATION.

->§. 1.<-

It is indeed the case that in those matters commanded by authority, no one is bound further than the ruler intended; nor is anyone held to more than they themselves intended in those things they freely commit to. §. 2. Regarding common words, this is the rule: words are regularly to be understood in their own proper and well-known meaning, which has been imposed upon them not so much by grammatical accuracy, or analogy, or the logic of their derivation, as by common usage; in whose hands resides the judgment, the right, and the standard of speaking <note>original: "quem penes arbitrium est, & jus, & norma loquendi," a famous quote from Horace’s *Ars Poetica* emphasizing that language is defined by how people actually use it</note>.

§. 3. *Technical terms* <note>original: "Vocabula artium," literally "words of the arts," referring to specialized vocabulary used in specific professions or sciences</note> are to be explained according to the definitions of the experts in each craft. However, if technical terms are defined differently by different people, it helps to prevent legal disputes <note>original: "ad lites præscindendas"</note> to express in common words exactly what we mean by that term.

§. 4. *Conjectures* <note>original: "Conjecturis," referring to logical inferences or educated guesses used to determine intent when the literal meaning is unclear</note> are necessary to dig out the genuine sense if either individual words or a combination of words are ambiguous; or if certain parts of a speech seem to contradict each other, yet can be reconciled through a skillful explanation. For where there is a certain and manifest contradiction, the later statement will override the earlier ones.

§. 5. *Conjectures* regarding the speaker's will and the correct sense in ambiguous or intricate speech are sought primarily from the *underlying subject matter*, from the *effect*, and from *associated circumstances*. Regarding the subject matter, this is the rule: words are regularly to be understood according to the underly—

<header>OF BOOK I.</header>

...underlying subject matter. For it is always presumed that the subject matter about which the speech was initiated was before the speaker's eyes; and therefore, the meaning of the words must always be adjusted to that same matter.

§ 6. Regarding the *effect and consequences*, this is the rule: Where words, if taken bluntly and simply, would result in either no effect or some absurd effect, one must deviate slightly from the more common meaning, as far as the necessity of avoiding nullity <note>meaning a situation where the words have no legal force or meaning</note> or absurdity requires.

§. 7. The strongest inferences are drawn *from related parts* <term>Ex conjunctis: things joined together, such as surrounding sentences or other works by the same author</term>; because it is generally presumed that anyone wishes to be consistent with themselves. *Related parts* are so either by *location* or only by *origin*. Regarding the former (location), this is the rule: if the meaning in some part of the same speech is expressed clearly and transparently, the more obscure phrases are to be interpreted according to those clear ones. Neighboring this is another rule: In interpreting any speech, one must pay accurate attention to the *preceding and following parts* <term>antecedentia & conſequentia</term>, to which the intervening words are presumed to conform and correspond. Regarding the latter (origin), this rule is held: an obscure saying of one and the same person is to be interpreted from their own clearer sayings, even if they were uttered at another time and place; unless it clearly appears that the person changed their opinion.

# CHAPTER XVII.

...to look into the **reason of the law** <term>ratio legis: the underlying purpose, logic, or "soul" of a legal rule</term>, or that cause and consideration which moved the lawgiver to enact the law; especially where it is established that this was the sole reason for the law. Regarding this, the rule is as follows: *That interpretation of a law is to be followed which agrees with the reason of the law; and conversely, that which disagrees with it is to be rejected.* Likewise, when the sole and adequate reason for a law ceases, the law itself ceases. <note>original: "cessante ratione legis unica & adaequata, cessat ipsa lex." This is a fundamental principle of legal logic: if the purpose for a law no longer exists, the law should no longer be applied.</note> Truly, where there were several reasons for the same law, the whole law does not immediately cease upon the cessation of one; since the remaining reasons may suffice to sustain its force. Often also, the sole will of the legislator is sufficient, even if the reason for the law remains hidden from the subjects.

§. 9. Furthermore, it must be observed that many words have more than one *meaning*—one *broader* and another *stricter*. Additionally, one type of matter is *favorable*, another *odious*, and another *mixed*. *Favorable* is that which makes the condition of both parties equal, looks toward the common utility, preserves any acts <note>meaning it keeps legal agreements or contracts valid rather than overturning them</note>, promotes peace, and so forth. *Odious* <note>In this legal context, "odious" does not necessarily mean "hateful" in the modern sense, but rather "burdensome" or "restrictive," such as laws that impose penalties or take away rights.</note> is that which burdens only one party, or one more than the other, contains a punishment within itself, renders an act void, changes prior agreements, or promotes war. *Mixed* is, for example, if something changes prior agreements but does so for the sake of peace. Regarding these, the rule is: *Favorable things are to be interpreted broadly, odious things more strictly.*

<note>original vocabulary: reason of the law, interpretation, favorable, odious, mixed, legislator, when the reason ceases</note>

->### BOOK I.<-

§. 10. There are also <term>conjectures: inferences or logical deductions</term> derived from sources other than the words themselves, which cause an interpretation to be sometimes extended and sometimes narrowed. However, reasons are more easily provided to suggest that an interpretation should be narrowed rather than extended. A law can therefore be extended to a case that is not explicitly stated in the text, if it is certain that the reason which applies to that case was the sole reason that moved the legislator, and that this reason was considered by him in its general sense so as to include equivalent cases. Furthermore, a law must be extended to those cases which are devised by deviously clever men to <term>circumvent the law: originally "in fraudem legis," referring to actions that follow the letter of the law but violate its spirit</term>.

From a *lack of reason* <term>defectu rationis: the absence of the specific underlying logic or motive</term>, which alone moved his will. Thus, under a general statement, cases are not included to which the sole and adequate reason of the law does not apply. 3. §. 12. Now, the fact that a case which arises later might conflict with the will of the person who established the rule is discovered either *from natural reason* or *from some sign of the will*. The former occurs if equity would be abandoned unless certain cases were exempted from the general law. For indeed, <term>equity: (original: *æquitas*) the correction of a law where it is deficient due to its universal nature</term> is necessary. Because not all cases can be foreseen, nor can they all be expressed because of their infinite variety, it follows that when general words are to be applied to specific cases, those cases must be exempted which the lawmaker himself would have exempted if he had been consulted on such a case. 

However, one is not permitted to resort to this equity unless sufficient evidence compels it. Among these signs, the most certain is if it appears that <term>natural law: (original: *lex naturalis*) the inherent system of ethics and justice shared by all humans</term> would be violated if one insisted on strictly following the letter of human law. Next to this is if it is not indeed forbidden to follow the words of the law, but nevertheless, to a person judging with humaneness, it seems too harsh and intolerable—either for all people in general or for certain specific persons; or if the goal of the law does not seem important enough to be redeemed at such a high cost. <note>The author suggests that a law shouldn't be enforced if the "price" of following it—in terms of human suffering—outweighs the benefit the law was supposed to provide.</note>

§. 13. Finally, an exception must also be made from a universal statement if words placed in another location

<meta>This page concludes Book I of Pufendorf’s treatise by resolving conflicts between laws and introduces the start of Book II. The previous page discussed equity and the necessity of making exceptions to general laws when applying them to specific, unforeseen circumstances.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

if they <note>Continuing from the previous page: referring to specific actions or competing duties.</note> do not indeed conflict directly with the present law or agreement; but because of a specific circumstance of time, they cannot be observed here and now at the same time. Therefore, certain rules must be observed here, so that it can be understood, *which law ought to be preferred in that case where both cannot be satisfied at the same time.*

1. That which is merely permitted yields to that which is commanded. 

2. What must be done at a certain time is preferred over what can be done at any time.

3. An affirmative precept yields to a negative one; or, when an affirmative precept cannot be satisfied without violating a negative precept, the fulfillment of the former must be omitted for the present. <note>In legal logic, a "negative precept" is a prohibition (thou shalt not), which is generally considered more binding than an "affirmative precept" (thou shalt).</note>

4. Among agreements and laws that are otherwise equal, the <term>particular: (original: *peculiaris*) a specific law or clause tailored to a narrow situation</term> is preferred over the general.

5. Between two duties colliding at a certain point in time, of which one has more honorable or useful causes than the other, it is right for the latter to yield to the former.

6. An unsworn agreement yields to one made under oath, when both cannot be satisfied at once.

7. An <term>imperfect obligation: (original: *obligatio imperfecta*) a moral duty, such as charity, that cannot be legally forced</term> yields to a <term>perfect obligation: (original: *obligatio perfecta*) a duty that others have a right to demand and enforce, often by law or contract</term>.

8. The law of beneficence, all else being equal, yields to the law of gratitude. <note>Pufendorf argues that the duty to repay a kindness already received is more pressing than the duty to perform a new act of kindness for someone else.</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book II. <header>CHAPTER I.</header>

# CHAPTER I.

## ON THE NATURAL STATE OF HUMANS.

->§. 1.<-

Next, we must examine those *duties* that a person is required to fulfill based on the *different states* in which they are found to live in their shared life. We use the term *state* in a general sense to describe that condition in which people are understood to be placed for the purpose of carrying out a specific kind of action. This state is also usually accompanied by specific rights.

§. 2. *The state of humans is either natural or <term>adventitious: (original: adventitius) a state that is not innate but acquired later through human action, such as marriage, citizenship, or professional rank</term>. The natural state can be considered in three ways, guided by the light of reason alone: either in relation to God the Creator, or in the relation of individual humans to themselves, or in relation to other humans.*

§. 3. *Considered in the first way, the natural state of man is that condition in which he was placed by the Creator, who willed that he be an animal more excellent than all others. From this state it follows that man must acknowledge his author, worship Him, and admire His works; and he must conduct his life in a manner entirely different from that of the <term>brutes: (original: brutis) irrational animals or beasts</term>. Therefore, the life and condition of the brutes are the opposite of this state.*

§. 6. Furthermore, the character of this natural state can be considered either as it is represented *by fiction* or as it *actually exists*. The former occurs if we either conceive that from the beginning a certain multitude of humans existed together without any dependence of one upon another—as is told in stories about the <term>Cadmean brothers: (original: Cadmæis fratribus) a Greek myth where warriors sprang fully grown from dragon teeth sown in the ground, having no parents or childhood</term>—or if we imagine the whole human race to be so dissolved now that everyone governed themselves separately, and no one was joined to any other by any bond other than the likeness of nature. But the natural state that actually exists involves someone being joined with certain people in a particular society; however, with all others, they share nothing in common except for their human appearance and owe them nothing under any other title. Such a state now exists between different states and citizens of different <term>republics: (original: rerumpublicarum) in this context, any independent political community or commonwealth</term>, and formerly held sway among solitary <term>heads of households: (original: patresfamilias)</term>.

For the first of mortals, in order to fill the still-vast world and seek a more spacious habitation for themselves and their flocks, left their paternal homes and departed in different directions. Nearly every male established a family of his own. Among their descendants, who likewise scattered themselves, the specific bond of kinship and the affections arising from it gradually faded away; only that common bond, resulting from the similarity of our nature, remained. 

This continued until later, when the human race had multiplied significantly. Having realized the disadvantages of a solitary life, those living near one another gradually gathered into <term>states: (original: civitates) organized political communities or commonwealths</term>—first smaller ones, and then larger ones, formed from several smaller ones joined together either voluntarily or by force. Between these different states, as they are joined by no other bond than that of common humanity, a natural state certainly still exists today.

§. 8. For those who live in a natural state, the principal **right** is that they are subject and accountable to no one but God. In this respect, that state also goes by the name of **natural liberty**; by which anyone, without a prior human act <note>Meaning a person's status before entering into any legal contracts or political allegiances</note>, is understood to be under his own jurisdiction and power, and subject to the command of no other man. ### CHAPTER I.

...to have no subject <note>original: "subje-ctum" — completing the word from the previous page</note> status, and is considered an equal. Furthermore, since the light of reason is implanted in man, by which he may govern his actions as they are guided by that light, it follows that anyone living in natural liberty depends upon no other mortal in the management of his actions. Instead, he possesses the power to do all things that are consistent with sound reason, according to his own judgment and will.

And since man—owing to that common inclination implanted in all living creatures—cannot help but apply himself in every way to the **preservation of his own body and life** <note>original: "conservationem sui corporis vitaeq;"</note>, as well as to the driving away of those things which seem likely to destroy it, and to use the means toward that end; and since, in the natural state, no one has another person as a superior to whom he has submitted his will and judgment, it follows that in such a state, everyone decides by their own judgment regarding the fitness of the means—whether they lead to their own preservation or not. For even if he should listen to the advice of another, it remains within his own power whether he wishes to approve of that outside advice or not. However, for this self-governance to be conducted rightly, it is required that it be undertaken according to the dictates of **right reason** <note>The concept of "right reason" (recta ratio) refers to the human capacity to discern the objective moral laws of nature</note> and the natural law.

## BOOK II.

...most abundant [advantages], yet it has associated ***disadvantages***; whether we imagine every person existing in it individually, or we consider the condition of heads of households living in isolation. For if you imagine in your mind a human being, even of adult age, left alone in this world and deprived of all the comforts and supports with which human ingenuity <note>original: "solertia hominum" — the specialized skill and cleverness humans use to improve their environment</note> has rendered life more refined and gentle, you will see a naked, mute, and helpless animal. This person would ward off hunger with roots and herbs, thirst with whatever water is found, and the harshness of the weather with caves as best they can; they would be exposed to wild beasts and tremble at every encounter.

Life might have been slightly more refined among those who lived in isolated family groups. However, this could in no way be compared with civil life <note>original: "vita civilis" — life within a structured political community</note>. This is not so much due to a lack of necessities—which a family seems able enough to provide for by keeping desires in check—but because there is so little provision for security in that state. To put the matter briefly:

->**A Comparison of the Natural State and the Civil State**<-

| In the Natural State | In the Civil State |
| :--- | :--- |
| Everyone is protected only by their own strength. | Everyone is protected by the strength of all. |
| The fruit of one’s industry is certain for no one. | The fruit of industry is certain for everyone. |
| The rule of the passions, war, and fear. | The rule of reason <note>original: "imperium rationis"</note>, peace, and security. |
| Poverty, filth, solitude, and barbarity. | Wealth, splendor, society, and elegance. |
| Ignorance and wildness. | Science and benevolence. |

§ 10. Furthermore, in the natural state, if anyone...

### CHAPTER I.

...does not voluntarily provide to another what they owe by agreement <note>original: "ex pacto" — obligations arising from a formal contract or promise</note>, or if they have inflicted an injury <note>original: "injuriam" — in this context, a legal wrong or violation of rights</note>, or if some other controversy arises; there is no one who, by right of command, can compel the other to provide what is owed or to repair the injury, and decide the dispute. This is unlike the situation in states <note>original: "civitatibus" — organized political communities or sovereign nations</note>, where one is permitted to implore the help of a common judge.

Since, however, nature does not permit rushing into war for just any cause—even when someone is fully convinced of the justice of their own case—one must first attempt to see if the matter can be settled by a gentler way. This is achieved through a friendly discussion between the parties, and by a pure (that is, unconditional) compromise, or an **appeal to arbitrators**.

These arbitrators must conduct themselves fairly toward both parties. In delivering their judgment <note>original: "sententia" — the formal verdict or ruling</note>, they must give nothing to hatred or favor, but look solely at the merits of the case. For this reason, a person is not usually chosen as an arbitrator in a case where they might hope for greater personal advantage or special glory from the victory of one side over the other; or indeed, where it is in their own interest for one party to win the case by any means. Consequently, no agreement or promise should exist between the arbitrator and the parties that would force them to rule in favor of one side.

If the arbitrator is unable to determine the facts of the case either from the common admission of the parties, or from reliable documents, or from certain reasons and signs, they must determine it from the statements of ***witnesses***...

It emphasizes the necessity of an arbitrator's impartiality and the use of witnesses when facts are in dispute.</summary>

## BOOK II.

...to be recognized. Although natural law <note>original: "lex naturalis" — the fundamental moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</note> and, quite often, the religious obligation of an oath <note>original: "juramenti religio" — the sacred weight of swearing to tell the truth before God</note> may bind them to speak the truth, it is nonetheless safest not to admit those who are so biased toward one side or the other that their conscience must, in a sense, struggle against favor, hatred, a desire for revenge, or some other violent emotional impulse—or even some closer bond of necessity. Not everyone has enough moral strength <note>original: "constantiae" — steadiness or firmness of character</note> to overcome such feelings.

Sometimes disputes are also resolved through the intervention of mutual friends; this is rightly counted among the most sacred duties. Otherwise, in this state <note>the "state of nature," where no civil government exists to enforce laws</note>, where one person does not voluntarily provide what is owed, each individual carries out the enforcement of their own rights.

### §. 11.

Moreover, although nature itself intended there to be a certain kinship <note>original: "cognationem" — a sense of being related as fellow human beings</note> among all people, by the power of which it is a sin to harm another and instead right for everyone to look out for the well-being of others, this kinship exerts rather weak forces among those who live together in natural liberty <note>original: "naturali libertate" — the condition of living without being subject to a common political authority</note>. Consequently, any person who is not our fellow citizen—that is, anyone with whom we live in the state of nature—should be considered not exactly an enemy, but certainly an unreliable friend. The reason for this is that humans are not only very capable of hurting each other, but for various reasons, they often want to. For some, it is a depravity of character <note>original: "pravitas ingenij" — a fundamental flaw or wickedness in one's disposition</note>, or a lust for power and for possessing more than they need...

<summary>This page explores the limits of human conscience and kinship in the state of nature, arguing that without civil authority, individuals must enforce their own rights and treat others as "unreliable friends" due to human depravity.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, state of nature, human nature, conscience, oaths, kinship, natural liberty, conflict resolution</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "state of nature" and the inherent instability of human relations without civil authority, then transitions into the second book or major section regarding specific social institutions, beginning with the duties of marriage.</meta>

...drives them to harm others; others, though naturally modest in temperament, rush to arms out of a desire for self-preservation and to ensure they are not struck first <note>original: "praeveniantur" — to be anticipated or outmaneuvered by a preemptive strike</note>. Many are pitted against one another by a desire for the same thing, others by a clash of dispositions. Thus, in that state <note>the "state of nature," where no overarching government exists</note>, there thrive near-perpetual suspicions, distrust, a drive to undermine the strength of others, and a lust for preempting others or for increasing one's own power through the ruin of others. Therefore, just as it is the mark of an upright person to be content with their own things and not provoke others or covet what belongs to another, so it is the mark of a cautious person—and one who loves their own safety—to believe all people are friends in such a way that those same people could soon become enemies. Thus, one should maintain peace with everyone as if it could soon be changed into war. For this reason, a commonwealth <note>original: "respubl." (respublica) — an organized political community or state</note> is considered fortunate if it thinks of war even during times of peace.

BOOK II.

§ 8. There is also considered to be a moral impediment to legitimate marriage: *too close a connection of blood or affinity* <term>Affinity (original: *affinitas*): The relationship created by marriage between one spouse and the blood relatives of the other spouse.</term>. Under this heading, marriages between **ascendants and descendants** <note>Parents, children, grandchildren, etc.</note> are judged as wicked by natural law even to infinity. Furthermore, other marriages in the collateral line <note>original: "limite transverso" — referring to relatives who share a common ancestor but are not in a direct line of descent.</note>, for example with a paternal aunt, a maternal aunt, or a sister—likewise in affinity with a stepmother, mother-in-law, or stepdaughter—are detested not only by divine law, but also by the laws of civilized nations and the consensus of Christians. Indeed, the civil laws of many peoples have prohibited certain more remote degrees of relationship, so as to cast a sort of "fence" <note>This refers to the legal concept of creating a buffer zone around a core law to prevent even the possibility of a violation.</note> around the aforementioned more sacred degrees, lest one might so easily rush toward violating them.

§ 9. Furthermore, just as civil laws are accustomed to add certain requirements to other contracts and business dealings—which, if they are not observed, are not held as valid in a civil court—so it also happens regarding marriage. In various places, for the sake of decency and good order, *certain formalities are required* by civil laws. Although these requirements are outside of natural law, nevertheless, those who are subject to civil laws will not contract a legitimate marriage without them; or at least such a union will not have the legal standing and effects of a proper marriage within the state.

CHAPTER III.

§. 10. *The duty of the husband is to love, nourish, guide, and defend his wife. The duty of the wife is to love and honor her husband, and to be a help to him not only in producing and raising offspring, but also in taking up a share of domestic responsibilities. On both sides, however, the nature of such a close union requires that spouses be partners in both prosperity and adversity; if any calamity befalls either, they should be supported by the other. No less must they prudently temper their characters to maintain mutual harmony—though in this regard, it is more the wife's role to yield.* <note>original: "concedere" — to give way or compromise in the interest of domestic peace.</note>

### CHAPTER III.
# ON THE DUTIES OF PARENTS
## & children

->§. 1.<-

From marriage comes offspring, over whom is established *paternal power* <term>Paternal power (original: *potestas patria*): The legal and moral authority a father (or parents) holds over his children, considered in this period to be a fundamental building block of social order.</term>, a type of rule as ancient as it is sacred, by which children are bound to revere the commands of their parents and acknowledge their pre-eminence.

§. 2. *The authority of parents over their children arises primarily for two reasons: first, because natural law* <note>original: "lex naturalis" — the set of moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason.</note> *itself, while commanding that man be a social creature, has assigned the care of children to parents; so that this duty might be less neglected, nature has implanted in them at the same time a most tender affection for their offspring. To carry out this care, power is required.*

the actions of children must be directed toward their own well-being <note>original: "salutem" — in this context, it refers to the child's physical safety, moral health, and successful upbringing.</note>, which they themselves do not yet understand due to a lack of judgment. Furthermore, this authority rests upon the *tacit consent of the offspring* <note>original: "tacito quoque consensu prolis" — a legal concept where consent is assumed because any rational person would agree to the arrangement if they were able.</note>. For it is rightly presumed that if an infant, at the time they were born, had possessed the use of reason and perceived that they could not preserve their life without the care of parents and the authority joined to it, they would have gladly consented to it, and would have stipulated in return for a suitable education from them. However, in *actual practice*, authority over the offspring is established for parents when they take them up, nourish them, and undertake to shape them, as far as they are able, into a useful member of human society.

§. 3. Since, however, *the mother concurs no less than the father* in the generation of offspring, and thus physically the offspring is common to both, it must be examined: *which of the two has the superior right over the offspring?* On this matter, a distinction must be made. *For if the offspring is born outside of marriage*, they shall first belong to the mother; because in this case, the father cannot be known except by the testimony of the mother. Among those also who live *in natural liberty and above civil laws* <note>original: "in libertate naturali & supra leges civiles" — referring to a "state of nature" where individuals are not yet subject to the laws of a specific government or city-state.</note>, it can be agreed by a pact that the right should belong to the mother rather than the father. But *certainly in states established by men*, since the marriage contract regularly begins with the male and he is the head of the family, the father's right will be superior; so that although the offspring is indeed [indebted] to the mother...

### CHAPTER III.

However, if the father dies, his right over the offspring—at least those who are not yet adults—seems **to accrue to the mother**; and if she enters into a second marriage, this right passes to the **stepfather** <note>original: "vitrico"</note>, provided that he succeeds to the trust and care of the natural parent. Furthermore, anyone who takes up a child who has been abandoned or orphaned by their parents to be raised in a liberal manner <note>original: "liberaliter educandum" — referring to an education fit for a free person or a gentleman, rather than a servant.</note> can, by their own right, demand filial respect from that child.

§. 4. To accurately understand **how great parental power is over children**, a distinction must be made both between **heads of households living separately** <note>original: "patresfamilias segreges" — those living in a state of nature, independent of a larger government or state.</note> **and those who have entered into a civil state**; and also between **the power a parent has as such, and the power he has as the head of his family.** Since it is enjoined upon a father by nature, as a father, to educate his children well so that they may become useful members of human society until they are able to provide for themselves, it is understood that he is granted as much power over his children as is sufficient for this end. This power by no means extends so far that parents may destroy the offspring hidden within the mother's womb, or cast out and kill the child once born. For although the offspring is brought forth from the substance of the parents, they are nonetheless placed in an equal human condition with them, and are capable of receiving injury even from the parents themselves. Nor does this power extend to the right of life <note>original: "jus vitae" — a reference to the Roman legal concept of "jus vitae necisque," the power of life and death.</note>

...and the power of life and death <note>original: "necis"</note> does not seem to extend to the occasion of a crime, but only to moderate punishment; for this is concerned with a tender age, in which such atrocious crimes, which must be expiated by death, hardly occur. But if, nevertheless, beyond any hope of correction <note>original: "emendationis"</note>, the boy stubbornly scorns all discipline, he can be cast out from the father's house and disowned <note>original: "abdicari" — a formal legal rejection of a son.</note>.

During this time, if any goods are transferred from others to the child <term>impuberem: a child who has not yet reached the age of legal puberty or maturity</term>, the parent must accept and manage them on behalf of the son, provided however that the ownership <term>dominium: the legal right of ownership or property</term> is acquired for the son himself; even though it is most fair that the fruits <note>original: "fructus" — the income, interest, or produce generated by a property or asset</note> should go to the father until the son reaches full age. Likewise, the father rightly claims for himself whatever profit or gain comes from the son's labor; since, on the other hand, the burden of the son's nourishment and education falls upon him.

§. 6. *In adult age*, when children are indeed endowed with full judgment, yet still remain part of the father’s family, the power which the father has *as a parent* <note>original: "ut genitor"</note>, and that which he has *as the head of the family* <note>original: "ut caput familiæ"</note>, can be considered separately. ### CHAPTER III.

...they ought to follow the authority of their parents as those who are wiser. Furthermore, he who wishes to be supported by his father’s goods and to eventually inherit them must adapt himself to the rules of the father’s household, the management of which remains entirely with the father.

§. 7. Moreover, *heads of households* <note>original: "patresfamilias"</note> who had not yet joined into civil states <note>The author refers here to the "state of nature," a period before formal governments and cities were established.</note> acted as a kind of prince within their own homes. Consequently, the children still remaining within their household were required to respect their authority as supreme. However, this domestic authority (as with other rights) was later adapted to suit the needs and dignity of civil states; in some places, fathers retained much of this power, while in others, very little was left to them. Thus we see that in certain states, fathers held the <term>right of life and death: "jus vitæ et necis," the legal authority to execute a family member for a crime</term> over their children. In other states, this right was taken away from them, either to prevent parents from abusing their power to the detriment of the public good <note>original: "bonum publicum"</note> or for the unfair oppression of their children; or to ensure that vices which might lead to public ruin were not overlooked due to the tenderness of a father's love; or finally, to spare a father the necessity of passing such a grim sentence.

§. 8. But when the offspring has completely left the father’s household and has either established a new family of his own or joined another, then <term>paternal power: "potestas patria," the legal authority of a father over his descendants</term> is dissolved. Nevertheless, the duty of piety and respect...

->BOOK II.<-

...the debt remains; for it is founded on the merits <note>original: "meritis"</note> of the parents, with whom children are never or very rarely thought to be on equal footing. These merits consist not only in the fact that children owe their lives to their parents—which is the source of all blessings—but also in the fact that the parents undertook their laborious and expensive education. Through this, they shaped them into useful members of human society and often provided them with the means to live a comfortable and prosperous life.

§. 9. However, although the obligation to educate their offspring is imposed on parents by nature, this does not prevent the *administration of that education from being entrusted to another* when the child’s benefit or necessity requires it; provided, however, that the *parent reserves the right of supervision over the one to whom the child was delegated.* Thus, a father not only rightly commits the instruction of his son to suitable teachers, but he can also *give him to another for adoption*, provided that some benefit will come to the son from it. And if there is no other way to nourish the offspring, then rather than letting the child perish from poverty, a father can pledge or sell the child into a tolerable form of servitude, at least under the <term>right of recovery: "lex retractus," a legal condition allowing the father to buy back the child</term> should the father attain a more comfortable fortune, or should one of his relatives wish to redeem him. But if a parent, through inhumanity, should expose and cast away their offspring, the one who [takes them in]...

§. 10. *Just as a father ought not to cast his offspring out of his family while they still stand in need of education and assistance, except for the most serious reasons; so also the offspring should not leave the father's family without his gracious permission.* Furthermore, since children usually leave the paternal family on the occasion of entering into marriage; and since it certainly concerns the parents to whom their offspring is joined, and from whom their grandchildren will descend: hence filial duty clearly requires that *children follow their father's consent in this matter, and not be joined to a spouse against his will.* Yet if, in fact, children have contracted and consummated a marriage against their parents' wishes, it does not seem void by <term>natural law: "jus naturale," the universal principles of justice shared by all humanity</term>; especially when they no longer wish to be a burden to the paternal family, and the match is otherwise not unbecoming. # CHAPTER IV
## ON THE DUTIES OF MASTERS
### & servants.

->§. 1.<-

After the human race began to multiply, and it was discovered how conveniently domestic matters could be managed through the service of others, it was introduced at an early stage that *servants should be admitted into the family* <note>original: "in familiam adsciscerentur servi"</note> to perform domestic works. It is probable that in the beginning, they offered themselves of their own accord, driven by poverty or a sense of their own lack of skill <note>original: "propriæ hebetudinis sensu," referring to a perceived inability to manage one's own affairs</note>; they bargained for a perpetual provision of food and other necessities, [pledging] their services to their master forever—

Then, as wars became more frequent everywhere, it was accepted among many nations that those whose lives were spared after being captured in war should be bound to servitude, together with the offspring that would subsequently come from them. Although among many nations no such slavery is in use, but all domestic tasks are dispatched by **mercenaries** <term>mercenarios: here referring to wage-laborers or hired servants rather than soldiers</term> hired for a set time.

->§. 2.<-

However, just as there are different degrees, as it were, of *servitude*; so too does the power of the masters and the condition of the servants vary. To a *temporary hired worker*, the master owes the agreed-upon wage; the worker, in turn, owes the master the agreed-upon labor. And because in that **contract** <note>original: "contractu"</note> the condition of the master is superior, such a servant is also bound to show reverence to the master in proportion to his dignity; and where he has performed his work maliciously or negligently, he is subject to the master’s **correction** <note>original: "coercitioni"</note>; which, however, cannot proceed to severe bodily affliction, much less to death, by the master's own authority.

->§. 3.<-

Moreover, to such a *servant who has voluntarily bound himself to someone in perpetual service*, the master owes perpetual food and other necessities of life; the servant, in turn, must provide perpetual labors, whatever the master prescribes, and must faithfully hand over to the master whatever results from those labors. In which [tasks], however, the [measure] of strength and skill—

<header>BOOK II.</header>

This servant is also subject to the master’s **correction** <term>castigationi: disciplinary punishment intended to improve behavior</term> not only to drive out negligence in performing his work, but also so that his conduct may be shaped for the dignity and peace of the household. However, such a servant cannot be sold to another against his will; this is because he chose this master and no other of his own accord, and it is in his own interest whom he serves. If he commits a heinous crime against someone outside the family, in civil society he is subject to the power of the state; however, where the family lives in isolation <note>referring to a "state of nature" where families live independently without a shared government</note>, he may be expelled from it. But if the crime is committed against the isolated family itself, the master may punish him even to the extreme <note>original: "per extrema," implying capital punishment</note>.

->§. 4.<-

Regarding those *servants who were captured in war*, many began to treat them more harshly because some remnant of hostile anger remained against them, and because they themselves had intended the worst for us and our fortunes. Nevertheless, as soon as **mutual trust** <note>original: "mutua fides"</note> regarding their inclusion into the household has been established between such a victor and the vanquished, all previous hostility is considered forgiven. From that point on, a master does an injury even to a servant acquired in this way if he either fails to provide the necessities of life, or rages against him without reason—and even more so if he should kill him without a crime deserving of such a fate.

Regarding those **servants** <term>servos: here referring to enslaved persons or those in perpetual service</term> as well, who had been brought into that condition by the force of war, as well as those who are purchased for a price, it is accepted that they may be transferred to whomever one pleases and sold just like merchandise, no differently than our other possessions. Thus, it is understood that *the body of the servant belongs to the master*. However, where **humanity** <note>original: "humanitas"</note> commands, we must never forget that a servant is certainly a human being; and therefore we should by no means treat him like our other things, which we can use and abuse at our pleasure and even destroy. And when it is pleasing to alienate <note>to sell or transfer ownership of</note> such a servant, care should be taken that he is not, through deliberate effort or beyond his deserts, delivered to those under whom he will suffer inhuman treatment.

§. 6. Finally, it is also widely accepted that the **offspring** <term>proles: children born to servants</term> born from servant parents is itself of a servile condition, and belongs as a **chattel** <term>mancipium: a legal term for a person held as property</term> to the master of the mother. This is defended by the following argument: that he who owns the body should likewise rightly own the fruit that comes from that body. Furthermore, because that offspring would clearly not have been born at all if the master had exercised the **right of war** <term>jure belli: the legal justification used by victors to either kill or enslave the defeated</term> against the parent. And because the parent possesses nothing of their own, no means of nourishing such offspring remains except from the **master’s goods** <note>original: "bonis herilibus"</note>. Since, therefore, the master provides nourishment for such offspring long before their own labor can be useful, and the subsequent labors of their lifetime do not much exceed—

### CHAPTER V.
## ON THE IMPULSIVE CAUSE FOR ESTABLISHING THE STATE.

->§. 1.<-

Although there is hardly any pleasure or convenience that does not seem obtainable through the duties and statuses enumerated so far; yet it remains to be investigated why mortals, not content with those small, primary societies <note>referring to the family and household units discussed in previous chapters</note>, established the **large societies** which go by the name of **states** <term>civitatum: often translated as commonwealths or political communities</term>. For from these foundations must be derived the rationale for the duties that accompany the **civil state** of humans.

->§. 2.<-

*Here, therefore, it is not enough to have said that man is drawn to civil society by nature itself, so that without it he neither can nor wants to live. For indeed, since it is obvious that man is an animal of such a kind that he loves himself and his own advantage above all else; it is necessary that while he voluntarily aspires to civil society, he has looked toward some advantage that would come to him from it. And although from—*

...outside society with those like him, man would have been by far the most miserable animal; nevertheless, since the natural desires and necessities of man could be abundantly satisfied through those primary societies <note>such as the family or household</note>, and through duties performed out of humanity or contracts, it cannot be immediately inferred from man's social nature that his disposition is precisely geared toward **civil society** <term>societatem civilem: the political state or organized government</term>.

->§. 3.<-

This will become clearer if we consider: *what kind of condition arises among men from the establishment of states; what is required so that one may truly be called a political animal, that is, a **good citizen**; and finally, what is found in human nature that is at odds with the character of civil life.*

->§. 4.<-

He who becomes a **citizen** suffers the loss of his **natural liberty** <term>libertatis naturalis: the state of being subject to no one but God and natural law</term> and subjects himself to a governing power <term>imperio: supreme authority or sovereignty</term> which encompasses the **right of life and death** <note>original: "jus vitæ & necis"; the legal authority of a state to execute criminals or compel citizens to risk their lives in war</note>. By the command of this power, many things must be done which one otherwise shrank from, and things must be omitted which one vehemently desired. Most actions must also be directed toward the **public good** <term>bonum publicum: the collective welfare of the whole community</term>, which often seems to differ from the good of individuals. Yet, man is driven by his already-inborn inclinations to want to be subject to no one, to do everything by his own judgment, and to seek his own advantage in all things.

->§. 5.<-

We call him a *truly political animal, that is, a **good citizen***, who promptly obeys the orders of the rulers, and who [strives] for the public good with all his strength...

<meta>catchword: ...with [bus]</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

...strives <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "strives for the public good with all his might."</note> and holds that public interest more dearly than his own private benefit; indeed, he is one who believes nothing is good for himself unless that same thing is also good for the **state** <term>civitati: the political community or commonwealth</term>; and finally, he is one who conducts himself fairly toward his fellow citizens. Yet, the dispositions of only a few are found to be naturally tempered toward this end; the greatest part of humanity is restrained only by the fear of punishment; many remain bad citizens and **non-political animals** <note>original: "animalia non politica"; a reference to Aristotle's idea that man is a "political animal" by nature. The author here argues that many people never actually achieve the virtue required for political life.</note> throughout their entire lives.

->§. 6.<-

Finally, *no animal is more ferocious or more untamable than man, nor is any more prone to a greater number of vices apt to disturb the peace of society.* For besides the desires for food and sex—by which the **beasts** <note>original: "bruta"; irrational animals</note> are also typically driven—man is agitated by many vices of which the beasts are ignorant. Such is the insatiable desire for superfluous things and that most cruel of evils, **ambition** <term>ambitio: here used in the sense of a grasping lust for power and status</term>. Likewise, there is an overly vivid memory of injuries and a passion for **vengeance** <term>vindictae ardor: the heat of revenge</term>, which continues to smolder long after the event. Furthermore, there is an infinite variety of inclinations and appetites, and a stubbornness in each person to exalt his own particular interests. To this is added that man delights in venting his fury upon his own kind with such madness that the greatest part of the evils to which the human condition is subject proceeds from man himself.

->§. 7.<-

The genuine and principal cause, therefore, why **heads of households** <term>patresfamilias: the male leaders of independent domestic units</term>, having abandoned their **natural liberty** <term>libertate naturali: the state of total independence before the creation of laws</term>, stooped to the establishment of states, wa—

Indeed, those who have adopted this proverb have rightly judged the malice of men and its remedy: *unless there were legal judgments, one would devour the other.* Once men were organized into such an order through **states** <term>civitates: organized political communities or commonwealths</term> so that they could be safe from mutual injuries, it naturally followed that they would more abundantly enjoy the benefits that humans are suited to provide to one another. For example, they might be imbued with more refined manners from childhood, and they might discover and cultivate the various **arts** <note>This refers to "liberal arts" and technical skills like engineering, law, and medicine</note> by which human life has been rendered rich and comfortable.

->§. 8.<-

As a result, they focus only on the present and care little for the future, being moved only by what strikes their immediate senses. This is especially true when they see that the wicked often possess in abundance those things by which the common people measure happiness. ## CHAPTER VI.
## ON THE INTERNAL
### structure of states.

->§. 1.<-

Our next task is to investigate the manner in which states are constructed and how their *internal framework* <note>original: "interna compages"</note> holds together. In this, it is first of all evident that against the evils threatening from human wickedness, neither any location, nor weapons, nor brute animals could supply a more convenient and effective defense for individual humans than *other humans*. However, since their strength is not effective across long distances, it was necessary for those through whom this end was to be achieved to be *joined together*.

§. 2. Nor is it less certain that the agreement of two or three people cannot provide security of this kind against other men. This is because it is easy for enough people to conspire to crush these few as to [ensure] a most certain victory for themselves... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page; the catchword "am" indicates the next page continues the thought.</note>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

...be able to guarantee <note>original: "am spondere possint"; completing the word from the previous page</note>; to whom the hope of success and the confidence of impunity will provide the impulse to attack. Therefore, for this end, it is necessary that **a significant multitude of men join themselves together**; so that the addition of a few men to the enemy side is of no visible consequence to their victory.

   §. 3. Among these many people, coming together for this purpose, it is necessary that there be **agreement regarding the means to be employed that are suitable for that same end**. For even many people, unless they agree among themselves, but instead are pulled apart by different opinions and strive for different goals, will accomplish nothing. Or, though they might agree for a time, driven by some passing emotion: soon, however, just as human characters and inclinations are changeable, they will drift apart. And although they might promise to one another by a pact <term>pactum: a formal agreement or contract</term> that they will apply their strengths to the common defense: yet not even in this way will the longevity of that multitude be sufficiently secured. Rather, it is necessary that something more be added, so that those who have once agreed to peace and mutual aid for the sake of the common good <term>bonum publicum: the shared welfare of the community</term> may be prevented from disagreeing again later, when their private good seems to differ from the public good.

   §. 4. Furthermore, in the human character **two vices especially occur** which prevent many people, who are legally independent <term>sui juris: under their own legal authority, not subject to another</term> and not dependent on one another, from being able to work together toward some common end for a long time. §. 5. *The wills of many men can be united in no other way* than if each individual subjects his own will <term>voluntas: the faculty of choice or intent</term> to the will of one man, or of one council <term>concilium: an assembly or governing body</term>, so that from that point forward, whatever that authority decides concerning things necessary for the common security must be held as the will of all and of each individual.

§. 7. Furthermore, for a state to come together *in an orderly way* <note>original: "regulari modo"</note>, *two pacts and one decree* are required. *First of all, those many individuals who are understood to be existing in a state of natural liberty, when they gather to form a state, enter into a pact with one another. This agreement states that they wish to join into a single and perpetual assembly <term>cœtus: a gathering or social union</term>, and to manage the concerns of their safety and security by common counsel and leadership; in a word, they agree that they wish to become fellow citizens. It is necessary that every single person consents to this pact; and whoever does not consent remains outside the future state.*

§. 8. Following this pact, it is necessary that *a decree be made regarding what form of government <term>regimen: the system of rule or administration</term> is to be introduced. For until this has been decided, nothing can be established with any consistency that would serve the common welfare <note>original: "salutem"; meaning the health, safety, or well-being of the public</note>.*

§. 9. After the decree concerning the form of government, *there is a need for a second pact, when the person or persons are appointed upon whom the government of the emerging state is conferred. By this pact, these leaders bind themselves to the care of the common security and welfare, while the rest bind themselves to provide obedience to them. By this very act, everyone subjects their own will to the will of that person or those people, and at the same time...*

And when this pact has been duly put into effect, it is only then that a perfect and regular state <note>original: "civitas"; referring to the Commonwealth or body politic</note> emerges from it.

§. 10. A *State* thus established is conceived of in the manner of a *single person*. It is distinguished and recognized by a single name from all individual people, and it possesses its own specific *rights* and *property*. Neither individuals, nor groups, nor even everyone together can claim these for themselves without the one who holds the supreme power <term>imperium: the highest sovereign authority or right to command</term>, or to whom the government of the state has been entrusted. Thus, the state is *defined* as a composite moral person <term>persona moralis: a legal or collective entity, similar to a modern corporation, which can act with a single will</term>, whose will—intertwined and united from the pacts of many people—is considered the will of all. This allows it to use the strength and resources of every individual for the common peace and security.

§. 11. Furthermore, the *will of the state* reveals itself as the starting point of *public actions*. It acts either *through one man* or *through one council*, depending on whether the highest authority <note>original: "summa rerum"; literally "the highest of things" or the supreme power</note> was conferred upon the former or the latter. Where the government of the state lies with one man, the state is understood to "will" whatever that man decides (provided he is assumed to be of sound mind), specifically regarding those matters that concern the purpose of states.

§. 12. But where the government of the state has been conferred...

§. 14. However, these points regarding the origin of states do not prevent one from rightly saying that *civil authority comes from God*. For since He wills that the natural law be practiced by all men—and yet, after the human race had multiplied, such a horrific life would have arisen among men that there would be almost no place left for natural law—and since the practice of this law is greatly promoted by the establishment of states; therefore (since whoever commands an end is also deemed to command the means necessary for that end <note>The author uses the legal maxim "qui jubet finem, jubere etiam censetur media ad finem necessaria"</note>), it is understood that God, through the dictate of reason, previously commanded the human race as it grew that states should be established, which are given life, as it were, by **supreme authority** <term>summum imperium: the highest, sovereign power within a state</term>. He also explicitly approves the order of these states in the Holy Scriptures, confirms their sanctity with special laws, and testifies that they are a matter of His particular care.

§. 2. In a state <term>civitas: the organized political community or body politic</term>, everyone has submitted their own will to the will of the rulers <term>imperantes: those who hold the supreme power to command</term> regarding matters concerning the safety of the state, so that they wish to do whatever those rulers have willed. For this to be possible, it is necessary that *the rulers make known to the citizens what their will is regarding such matters.* They do this, therefore, not only *through commands directed at individuals* regarding specific affairs, but also *through general rules*, so that it remains clear to everyone for all time what must be done or avoided. Through these rules, it is also customary to define what should be considered one's own and what belongs to another; what is to be held as lawful and what <gloss>as</gloss> unlawful within the state, and what is <gloss>to be considered</gloss> honorable or dishonorable; what remains to each person from their natural liberty <term>libertas naturalis: the inherent freedom of individuals before the constraints of civil law</term>, or how each person ought to moderate the use of their rights for the peace <note>original: "tranquillitatem", referring to the internal order and security of the community</note> of the state; and finally, what and in what way each person can demand from another by right. Indeed, it contributes greatly to the dignity and peace of the state that all these matters be clearly defined.

§. 3. Furthermore, the chief purpose of states is that men, through mutual agreement and assistance, might be safe against the losses and injuries that men can and usually do inflict on one another. To obtain this from the men with whom we are joined in the same society <term>societas: the social bond or partnership of citizens</term>, it is not enough that

### CHAPTER VII. 187

<note>original: "mutuo inter se paciscantur de injuriis non inferendis"</note> they should mutually agree among themselves not to inflict injuries; nor is it even enough that the bare will of the superior be made known to the citizens: but there is a need for the fear of punishment, *and the power of administering the same.* This punishment, so that it may suffice for this purpose, must be so tempered that it is clearly a greater evil to have violated the laws than to have observed them; and so that the severity of the punishment outweighs the pleasure or profit that is taken or hoped for from the injury <term>injuria: a legal wrong or violation of another's rights</term>. For men cannot help but choose the lesser of two evils. Although many are not restrained from injuries by the threat of punishment, this must nevertheless be considered among the less frequent occurrences; which human condition simply does not allow us to avoid entirely.

§. 4. Because disputes also often tend to arise regarding the correct application of laws to specific cases, and many things must be carefully weighed if something is said to have been done against the laws: from this, so that peace <note>original: "tranquillitas", referring to internal social order</note> may be established among citizens, *it belongs to the supreme power to hear and decide the lawsuits of citizens,* and to examine the individual acts which are argued to be contrary to the laws, and to pronounce and carry out the punishment suitable to the laws.

§. 5. Moreover, so that those who have come together into a state <term>civitas: the organized political community</term> may be safe against foreigners, *it belongs to the supreme power to assemble, unite, and arm,* or in its place to hire, as many men as are needed—given the uncertain number and strength of enemies—for the common defense

# BOOK II.
### 188

[... as] it shall seem necessary; and again, when it is expedient, to make peace. And since treaties <term>fœdera: formal agreements or alliances between nations</term> serve both times of war and peace—so that the benefits of different states <term>civitatum: organized political communities</term> can be better shared, and a more powerful enemy repelled by joined forces, or brought into order <note>original: "in ordinem redigi queat" — implying subduing or disciplining a hostile power</note>—it will also be the right of the supreme sovereignty <term>summum imperium: the highest authority in a state</term> to enter into treaties serving both circumstances, to bind all subjects to observe them, and to simultaneously direct the resulting benefits back into the state.

§. 6. Since the affairs of a large state, occurring in both war and peace, cannot be carried out by one man without *ministers and magistrates*; it will be necessary for the supreme power to appoint men who, in his place, may examine the legal disputes of citizens, investigate the plans of neighbors, lead the soldiers, collect and distribute the state’s resources, and finally look out for the welfare of the state on all sides. The supreme ruler can and must compel these same individuals to perform their duty and demand accounts of their actions.

§. 7. Furthermore, because the business of the state in both war and peace cannot be conducted without expenses, it is the role of the supreme power to compel citizens to supply them. This happens in various ways; for example, if the citizens set aside some part of their goods or the products of the region they inhabit for...

This page concludes the discussion on taxation, moves into the state's role in public education and moral guidance, and argues that all these powers must be united in a single authority to ensure a stable society.</meta>

# CHAPTER VII.
### 189

[...of the] lands should be set aside for these uses; or that individual citizens should contribute from their own goods and, at the same time, offer their labor whenever it is needed; or that taxes <term>vectigalia: revenues or customs duties</term> be imposed on goods being imported and exported (the former of which weighs more heavily on citizens, while the latter burdens foreigners); or finally, if a modest portion is deducted from the price of goods that are consumed <note>This refers to what we would now call an excise tax or a sales tax on everyday items.</note>.

§. 8. Finally, since the actions of individuals are governed by each person's own opinion, and most people tend to judge matters according to habit or by what they see others commonly believing—since very few are able to discern what is true and honorable by their own intellect—it is therefore advantageous for the state <term>civitas: the organized political community</term> to resound publicly with the kind of doctrines that are consistent with the proper goal and utility of states. At the same time, the minds of the citizens should be imbued with these principles from childhood. Consequently, it belongs to the supreme sovereignty <term>summum imperium: the highest authority in a state</term> to appoint those who shall teach such doctrines publicly.

§. 9. Furthermore, these various parts of supreme sovereignty are naturally so *interconnected* that, if a regular form of state is to be maintained, all of them without exception must fundamentally belong to one person or assembly. For if one or another part is clearly missing, the government will be crippled and unfit to achieve the state's purpose. However, if these powers are divided—so that some belong fundamentally to one person and the rest to another—an irregular and poorly cohesive republic <note>original: "remp." or "rempublicam" — the public entity or state</note> must necessarily emerge.

<meta>catchword: CA- (likely beginning the word "Caput" or "Caritas" on the following page)</meta>

It concludes by asserting that sovereign power is indivisible; if the powers of law, tax, and education are split between different authorities, the state becomes "irregular" and unstable.</summary>

BOOK II. CHAPTER VIII.

# ON THE FORMS OF COMMONWEALTHS

->§. 1.<-

**S**upreme sovereignty <term>summum imperium: the highest or ultimate authority in a state</term>, according as it is found in *one man, or in one council consisting of a few, or of all citizens, is accustomed to produce different forms of commonwealths.*

§. 2. *Now, the forms of a state are either regular or irregular.* The regular forms are those where supreme sovereignty is so united in one subject <note>In this context, "subject" refers to the person or group holding the power, not the people being ruled.</note> that it distributes itself, undivided and unshaken, from a single will through all parts and affairs of the state. Where this unity is not found, the form of the state will be irregular.

§. 3. *There are three forms of a regular state*: the first is when supreme sovereignty belongs to one man, and it is called a **monarchy**. The second is when supreme sovereignty belongs to a council consisting only of select citizens, and it is called an **aristocracy**. The third is when supreme sovereignty belongs to a council consisting of all heads of households <note>original: "patribus familias." In 17th-century political thought, "the people" usually referred specifically to male heads of households rather than the entire population.</note>, and it is called a **democracy**. In the first, he who holds power is called the **monarch**; in the second, the **nobles** <term>optimates: the "best" or elite citizens</term>; in the third, the **people**.

§. 4. In all these forms, the power is indeed the same. However, **monarchy** has a notable *advantage* over the other forms in this respect: that in order for a matter to be deliberated and decided—that is,

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the classification of governments into "regular" and "irregular" forms, defining the three standard regular forms: monarchy, aristocracy, and democracy.</summary>
<keywords>monarchy, aristocracy, democracy, sovereignty, regular state, irregular state, political systems, heads of households</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the forms of government from Chapter VIII. The previous page established the definitions of the three regular forms of state: monarchy, aristocracy, and democracy. This page explains the practical functioning of these forms and begins to categorize the "diseases" or failings that can occur within them.</meta>

CHAPTER VIII.

...that sovereignty <term>imperium: the right to command or the highest authority</term> may be exercised in practice, there is no need for set times and places; instead, it can be deliberated and decided upon at any place and time. Thus, a monarch is always in the most immediate position to perform the acts of government. However, for the nobles <term>optimates: the ruling elite in an aristocracy</term> and the people—who are not a single natural person—to make decisions, it is necessary for them to meet at a designated place and time. There, they must deliberate and decide on public business, because the will of a council or the people (which arises from the shared <note>original: "conspirantibus," used here in the literal sense of "breathing together" or agreeing</note> opinions of the majority) cannot be known in any other way.

§. 5. Furthermore, as with other rights, it happens with supreme sovereignty that it is sometimes administered well, and at other times poorly or unwisely. This is why some states are called *healthy*, while others are described as *diseased and corrupt*: although it is not necessary to invent entirely new forms or species of government because of these "diseases." Of the sicknesses that plague states, some reside *in the men* <note>meaning the specific individuals holding office</note>, and others *in the system itself*. Thus, we hear of *vices of men* <note>original: "vitia hominum"</note> and *vices of the state* <note>original: "vitia status"</note>.

§. 6. *Vices of men in a monarchy* occur if the person who holds the throne lacks the skills of ruling, or if he feels no concern—or at least not enough—for the commonwealth <term>res publica: the "public thing" or the state</term>. In such cases, he abandons the state to be torn apart by the ambition or greed of wicked ministers. Other vices include cruelty

<keywords>sovereignty, monarchy, aristocracy, public deliberation, political corruption, vitia hominum, vitia status, governance</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "vices of men" (vitia hominum) within different forms of government. It completes the list of failings specific to a monarch and then categorizes the typical human failures found in aristocratic and democratic systems, concluding with those common to all political structures.</meta>

->BOOK II.<-

...and he is likewise terrifying in his anger; if he delights in putting the commonwealth <term>res publica: the state or public welfare</term> at risk without necessity; if he wastes the funds collected for the state’s expenses on luxury or through ill-advised gifts; if he accumulates money extorted from citizens beyond all reason; if he is abusive or unjust; and if there are other such traits through which the reputation of a "bad prince" is earned.

§. 7. *The vices of men in an aristocracy* occur if the path to the senate is opened to wicked or incompetent men through political corruption <note>original: "ambitum," referring to the unethical or illegal pursuit of office</note> and dishonest methods, while better men are excluded; if the nobles are pulled apart by factions; and if they seek to treat the common people like slaves and misappropriate the state's assets to grow their own private estates.

§. 8. *The vices of men in a democracy* occur if incompetent and rowdy men are permitted to defend their opinions in a disruptive and improper manner; if outstanding virtues—which are not a burden to the state—are suppressed; if laws are rashly enacted and repealed <note>original: "figuntur atq; refiguntur," literally "fixed and un-fixed," like Bronze tablets being posted and then taken down</note> out of fickleness, so that what was pleasing just a moment ago is soon rejected without reason; and if base and inept men are placed in charge of public affairs.

§. 9. *The vices of men that can occur in any kind of state* are these: if those responsible for the administration of sovereignty <term>imperium: the right to command</term> perform their duties negligently or dishonestly, and if the citizens (whose only remaining honor is that of obedience) "bite the bit" <note>original: "fraenum mordeant," a metaphor for resisting authority or being restive under control, like a horse struggling against its bridle</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: Vices of men, aristocracy, democracy, the state, sovereignty, the commonwealth.</note>

<summary>This page details the specific human failings that corrupt the three forms of government: corruption and factionalism in aristocracies, instability and the promotion of the unfit in democracies, and general administrative neglect or civil unrest in all states.</summary>
<keywords>political corruption, aristocracy, democracy, tyranny, rule of law, civil disobedience, sovereignty, vitia hominum</keywords>

It then begins to examine the loaded terminology used to describe corrupt governments, such as tyranny and oligarchy.</meta>

## CHAPTER VIII.

§. 10. But the **vices of the state** <term>vitia status: structural or systemic flaws in a government's design</term> occur when the laws or institutions of a community are not suited to the **character of the people** <note>original: "genium populi," referring to the unique spirit, culture, or temperament of a nation</note> or the region; or where these same laws predispose the citizens to internal disturbances, or to incurring the justified hatred of their neighbors; or if they make them unfit for performing the duties necessary for preserving the **commonwealth** <term>res publica: the state or public welfare</term>; for instance, if by the laws of the state they cannot help but dissolve into a weak idleness, or be rendered unsuited for maintaining peace; or if the **fundamental laws** <term>leges fundamentales: the core constitutional principles of a state</term> are so arranged that public business cannot be handled except slowly or with great difficulty.

§. 11. To such **diseased states**, many also apply specific names; thus, a *corrupt monarchy is called a tyranny; a corrupt state of the few is called an oligarchy; and a corrupt popular state is called an ochlocracy* <note>original: "ochlocratia," literally "mob rule" or government by the rabble</note>. Although it frequently happens that many use these terms *not so much to express a disease of the state as their own personal feeling or dissatisfaction with the current situation or those in power.* For often, one who dislikes a king or a monarchical system is accustomed to call even a legitimate and good prince a "tyrant," especially when he executes the laws strictly. Likewise, he who feels the sting of being excluded from the senate—even though he considers himself in no way inferior to the other senators—calls them "those few" <note>original: "ὀλίγους" (oligous), the Greek root for oligarchy, used here as a term of contempt for an exclusive elite</note> out of spite and envy, as if to say they are merely a few people who, though they are in no way [superior] to the others...
