...that they might stand out above others, yet they exercise power over their equals or superiors not without pride. Finally, men of a proud disposition, to whom popular equality is hateful, when they see everyone in a democracy casting votes regarding the **commonwealth** <term>res publica: the state or public affairs</term> with equal right—especially since in any state the number of the common people is the greatest—they call it an **ochlocracy** <term>ochlocratia: from the Greek for "mob rule," a government dominated by the "rabble"</term>; that is, a state where a vile crowd has power over things, and no special privilege is left for excellent men, such as they consider themselves to be.

§. 12. *An **irregular commonwealth** is one* <term>respublica irregularis: a state that deviates from the "pure" forms of monarchy, aristocracy, or democracy due to its unique legal structure</term> in which that union, in which the essence of a state consists, is not found so perfectly; and this is not by way of a disease or a vice clinging to the administration of the state, but rather because it has been accepted as if it were legitimate by public law or custom. Since, however, there can be infinite ways of deviating from the right path, certain and defined species of irregular states cannot be established. Its nature, however, can be clearly understood from one or two examples; for instance: if in some commonwealth the senate and the people handle public business with the highest right on both sides, so that neither is subject to the other. Or if in some kingdom the power of the nobles has become so strong that they are subsequently subject to the king in no other way than as **unequal confederates** <term>inaequales foederati: allies of lower status who are bound to a superior power but still retain some of their own sovereignty</term>.

§. 13. *We call them **systems of states**,* <term>systemata civitatum: a group of independent states joined together by a permanent bond, such as a confederacy or league</term> where several perfect states are joined by some particular bond...

Furthermore, these **systems of states** <term>systemata: a collection of states acting in concert</term> arise *primarily in two ways:* one through a common king, the other through a **treaty** <term>foedus: a formal agreement or league between nations</term>.

§. 14. *A system emerges through a common king* when several separate kingdoms, whether by agreement, by the occasion of marriage, inheritance, or victory, have one and the same king; yet they do not merge into a single kingdom because of this, but each is administered by the common king according to its own **fundamental laws** <term>leges fundamentales: the core constitutional principles of a specific territory</term>.

§. 15. *The other species of systems arises* when several neighboring states are so connected by a *perpetual treaty* that they wish to exercise certain parts of **sovereignty** <term>summum imperium: the highest power or supreme authority</term>—especially those regarding defense against outsiders—only by the consent of all, while the liberty and independence of the individual states remain intact in all other matters.

<page-num>196</page-num>
<header>BOOK II.</header>

exercising it, so that its acts cannot be rendered void by anyone acting as a superior.

§. 2. From this it also follows that the same power is <term>unaccountable</term> <note>original Greek: ἀνυπεύθυνον (anypeuthynon)</note>, or *bound to no mortal to render an account of its actions* with the effect that, if it does not prove those reasons to another, it would be subject to human punishments or coercion as if coming from a superior.

§. 3. Connected to this is the fact that this same **supreme power** <term>summum imperium: the highest authority in a state</term> *is superior to human and civil laws*, as such, and is therefore not directly bound by them. For these laws depend on the supreme power for both their origin and their duration. Hence, it is impossible for the power to be obligated by them; otherwise, the power would be superior to itself. Nevertheless, when the supreme ruler has enjoined certain things upon the citizens by law, the subject matter of which also applies to himself, it is fitting and beneficial for adding authority to the law that he should voluntarily conform himself to those same rules.

§. 4. Finally, **supreme power** <term>summum imperium</term> also possesses a peculiar *sanctity*, such that it is not only a sin to resist its lawful commands, but even its harshness must be endured patiently by the citizens, just as good children endure the irritability of their parents. Indeed, even where it threatens the most atrocious injuries, individuals should rather look to their safety by flight, or endure any calamity whatsoever, rather than [drawing] the sword

against a harsh, yet still "father of the country" <note>original Latin: "parentem patriae"</note>.

§. 5. Furthermore, **supreme power** <term>summum imperium: the highest authority in a state</term>, especially in monarchies and aristocracies, is found in some places to be *absolute*, and in others *limited*. A monarch is said to hold *absolute power* <term>absolutum imperium</term> when he can administer it according to his own judgment, not according to the standard of specific and permanent statutes, but as the present condition of affairs seems to require; and who thus, by his own discretion, provides for the welfare of the commonwealth <term>res publica</term> as the circumstances of the time demand.

§. 6. But because the judgment of a single man is not immune to error, and his will is prone to turn toward evil—especially in the midst of such great liberty—some peoples have seen fit to *circumscribe the exercise of this power within certain limits*. This was done by binding the king to specific laws regarding the administration of the various parts of the state at the time the kingdom was conferred. Furthermore, should matters arise concerning the "highest affairs of state" <note>original: "summam rerum"</note>, which cannot be defined in advance, they willed that these should not be undertaken except with the foreknowledge and consent of the people, or their deputies gathered in an assembly <term>comitia: a formal gathering of citizens or representatives for decision-making</term>, so that less opportunity is given to the king to deviate from the welfare of the kingdom.

§. 7. Finally, there usually occurs in kingdoms some distinction regarding the *manner of holding the kingdom*, which

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 197 regarding the nature of kingly power, specifically distinguishing between kings who own their kingdoms as private property and those who hold power as a public trust. It then transitions into Chapter X, which examines how such power is acquired.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>OF BOOK II.</header>

is not found to be equal in all cases. For some kings are said to hold their kingdom **as a patrimony** <term>patrimonium: property inherited from a father that can be managed or sold at will</term>, such that they are able to divide, alienate, or transfer it at their pleasure to whomever they wish. This happens most often to those who have sought a **kingdom by arms** <note>original Latin: "armis regnum"</note> and have created a people for themselves. However, other kings, who were voluntarily chosen by the people, even if they exercise sovereignty with the highest right, cannot divide, alienate, or transfer the kingdom at their pleasure. Instead, in passing it down to successors, they are bound to follow the fundamental law <term>lex fundamentalis: the basic constitutional principles of a state</term> or the custom accepted by the people. For this reason, they are compared by some in a certain respect to *usufructuaries* <term>usufructuarius: someone who has the right to use and enjoy the benefits of property owned by another, provided the property itself remains intact</term>.

### CHAPTER X.
# ON THE MODES OF ACQUIRING
## Sovereignty, especially monar-
## chical.

->§. I.<-

Although the *consent* of the subjects is required to establish any legitimate sovereignty <term>imperium: the legal power to command</term>, nevertheless, that consent is not elicited in the same way everywhere. For sometimes, *through the violence of war*, some are forced to consent to the rule of the victor; at other times, citizens *voluntarily* come together to appoint a certain prince.

§. 2. *The violent mode of acquiring sovereignty usu-*

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter X from page 198, finishing the explanation of sovereignty acquired through force (occupation) and beginning the explanation of sovereignty acquired through voluntary election and the nature of an interregnum.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X.</header>

...is said to be called **occupation** <note>original: *occupatio*</note>, namely when someone, supported by a just cause for making war, through the favor of the force of arms and fortune, reduces a people to such a state that they are thereafter compelled to submit themselves to his sovereignty <term>imperium: the legal power to command and govern</term>. The legitimate title of this sovereignty is derived not only from the fact that the victor, if he had wished to use the full rigor of the laws of war, could have taken the lives of the conquered entirely; thus, by allowing them to escape with a lesser hardship, he gains the additional praise of clemency. It is also justified because when a person enters into war with another whom he had previously injured, and to whom he refused to offer fair satisfaction, he exposes all his fortunes to the **gamble of war** <note>original: *alea martis*, literally "the dice of Mars"</note>. By doing so, he has already tacitly consented in advance to whatever condition the outcome of the war is to assign to him.

->§. 3.<-

Moreover, a kingdom is established by the **voluntary consent** of the people through the intervention of an **election**, by which a people about to be established, or one already established, voluntarily designates a certain man whom they judge capable of sovereignty. When the people’s decree has been made known to him and he has accepted it, sovereignty is conferred upon him by the promised obedience of the people.

->§. 4.<-

In a state already established, if an **election** is undertaken because the previous king has died, it is usually preceded by an **interregnum** <term>interregnum: the interval between the end of one king's reign and the beginning of the next</term>. In this period, although the state falls back into an imperfect form—while the citizens are connected to one another only by their original social contract <note>The author refers to the "first pact," which is the agreement between individuals to form a society, as opposed to the "second pact" between a people and their ruler</note>:

# BOOK II.

...However, the name and affection for a common fatherland, as well as the fortunes of most citizens which are generally tied to that place, still add much stability to it <note>Referring to the state during an interregnum</note>; these factors compel good citizens to voluntarily maintain peace among themselves for a time and to apply themselves as quickly as possible to restoring a full government. Nevertheless, it greatly helps in avoiding the troubles likely to arise from interregnums <note>original: *interregnis*</note> if it is decided in advance who should have the administration of the republic <term>respublica: the state or commonwealth</term> while the throne is vacant.

->§. 5.<-

Furthermore, in some places, a new election is held whenever an individual monarch dies. In other places, however, the kingdom is granted to another on the condition that it passes to others through succession without a new election taking place. This right of succession is established either by the *will of the king himself*, or by the *will of the people*.

->§. 6.<-

*Kings who hold their kingdom as a patrimony* <term>patrimonium: property held by right of private ownership rather than public office</term> *can arrange the succession as they please;* their arrangement will be honored just like the will of a private individual, especially where the kingdom was founded and acquired by that person. In such cases, it is permitted for them, if they wish, to divide the kingdom among several children, with daughters not being excluded; indeed, they may even appoint as heir an adopted son, a biological son, or even someone who is not related to them by blood at all.

->§. 7.<-

But where such a king [arranges] his succes-

# CHAPTER X.

...if he made no specific arrangements regarding the succession, *it is presumed, first, that he by no means wishes his kingdom to expire with him, but rather that it should pass entirely to his children due to the common affection of mortals* <note>original: *communem mortalium affectum*; referring to the natural love parents have for their offspring and the desire to provide for them after death</note> *for their own. Next, it is presumed that the monarchical form of government should be preserved after him, since he himself approved it by his own example. Then, that the kingdom should remain undivided; because division is joined with the disruption of both the kingdom and the royal family. Furthermore, that among those of equal degree, the male is preferred to the female, and the firstborn to those born later. Finally, that if children are lacking, the kingdom should pass to the next of blood relative.*

->§. 8.<-

In those kingdoms, however, which were established from the beginning by the voluntary will of the people, *the order of succession depends originally on the will of that same people.* Where the people have conferred upon the king, along with the supreme power <term>imperium: the legal authority to command and be obeyed</term>, the right to appoint a successor, then whoever he chooses shall succeed him. Where this was not done, the people are understood to have reserved that right for themselves. If it pleased the people to confer the kingdom upon an elected king by hereditary right, *they either made the order of succession similar to simple inheritances, insofar as the safety of the kingdom allows, or they tempered it by some specific reasoning.*

->§. 9.<-

Where *the people have simply ordered that the king hold the kingdom by hereditary right,* and have added nothing specifically; they indeed intended that the kingdom [should pass] to that person...

<sig>N 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: or-</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the legal presumptions for royal succession when a king dies without a will, emphasizing the preservation of the monarchy and the preference for primogeniture. It also distinguishes between patrimonial kingdoms and those where the power of succession remains rooted in the will of the people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of hereditary monarchies where the people have established the rules of succession. It moves from general principles of inheritance to the specific requirements for the stability of a state, concluding with an introduction to lineal succession.</meta>

# BOOK II.

...it should descend in the same order as private inheritances, but with some tempering or modification. For in these cases, the safety of states requires that succession in kingdoms depart from the rules of private inheritances in the following ways:

1. That the kingdom must be indivisible. <note>Unlike private property, which can be split among heirs, a state must remain a single unit to maintain its power and stability.</note>
2. That the succession must remain within those who descend from the first king.
3. That no one may succeed unless they were born according to the laws of the country; this excludes not only bastards but also adopted children.
4. That among those of equal degree of kinship, males are preferred to females, even if the females are older.
5. That the successor should acknowledge the kingdom as a <term>beneficium populi: a benefit or grant from the people</term>, rather than as a benefit from their predecessor.

->§. 10.<-

However, because inextricable controversies could easily arise regarding which member of the reigning family was the closest relative to the recently deceased king—especially when the lineage had moved far from the original founder of the stock—*lineal succession* was introduced among many peoples. This system consists in each person who descends from the founder of the reigning lineage forming a "line," as if it were a perpendicular path. From there, anyone is called to the kingdom according to whether their line precedes others. No transition is made from one line to another as long as anyone from the prior line still survives, even if there exists someone in another line who is closer in degree <note>Degree refers to the number of births separating two people; "line" refers to the specific branch of the family tree.</note> to the king who just died.

->§. 11.<-

The most common types of lineal succession are *cognatic* and *agnatic*. In the former <note>Cognatic succession, which includes relatives from both the male and female sides.</note>, women are not...

<meta>catchword: excluded</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the five specific rules that distinguish royal succession from private inheritance and introduces the concept of lineal succession as a solution to family disputes over the throne.</summary>
<keywords>succession, lineal succession, agnatica, cognatica, royal inheritance, legitimacy, primogeniture, sovereignty</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on rules of succession from Chapter X and begins Chapter XI, which focuses on the moral and practical duties of a sovereign. It transitions from the legal structure of the state to the personal character and education required of a ruler.</meta>

...[females] are not excluded, but are placed after males in the same line, so that a return is made to them if males of a superior or equal degree are lacking. This <note>The author is likely referring to "Agnatic" or Salic succession, which strictly forbids female inheritance.</note> system, however, excludes females and even the males born from them forever.

§. 12. Where a controversy arises regarding the succession in a <term>patrimonial kingdom: a state treated as the personal property of a ruler</term>, it would be best to turn to arbitrators from the royal family. If the succession was established by the will of the people, a declaration by the people will resolve the doubt.

BOOK II.

...men who are wise and experienced in human affairs; flatterers, triflers, and those who speak nothing but vanities must be kept far away. Indeed, so that he may know how to properly apply the general precepts of guiding <term>prudence: the practical wisdom required for effective leadership</term>, he must understand as deeply as possible the condition of his own state and the character of the people subject to him. Furthermore, he must especially study those virtues whose use is most evident in the administration of such a great weight of responsibility; and his conduct must be shaped to the dignity of such high rank.

§. 3. The general law of supreme rulers is this: *the welfare of the people is the supreme law.* <note>original: "salus populi suprema lex est"</note> For it was with that intention that <term>supreme power: "imperium," or the ultimate authority of the state</term> was conferred upon them: so that through it, the goal for which states were established might be secured. Hence, rulers ought to believe that nothing is privately beneficial to themselves which is not likewise beneficial to the state.

§. 4. For the internal tranquility of states, it is required that the wills of the citizens be so tempered and directed as is expedient for the welfare of the state. Hence, it is the duty of supreme rulers not only to prescribe *laws* suitable to that end, but also to establish *public discipline* in such a way that citizens conform themselves to the requirements of the laws not so much through fear of punishment as through habit. To this end, he must also take care that Christian doctrine, pure and sincere, flourishes in the state, and that in public schools such teachings are passed on which are consistent with the purpose of states.

<summary>This page defines the primary duty of a ruler as the "welfare of the people" and explains that laws should be supported by public discipline and education to ensure social harmony.</summary>
<keywords>welfare of the people, public discipline, education, Christian doctrine, duties of a ruler, state tranquility, legal compliance</keywords>

### CHAPTER XI.

§. 5. Toward the same end, it is beneficial to have laws written down that are *clear and plain* <note>original: "perspicuas & planas"</note> regarding those matters which most frequently arise among the citizens. However, no more should be decreed by <term>civil laws: the specific statutes enacted by a state to govern its people</term> than what contributes to the good of the state and its citizens. For since men more often deliberate on what they ought to do or not do through <term>natural reason: the innate human capacity for logical and moral judgment</term> rather than through a formal knowledge of the laws, wherever there are more laws than can easily be held in the memory, and where they prohibit things that reason itself does not forbid, it is inevitable that men will fall into these laws as if into snares, out of ignorance and without any evil intention. By this very act, an unnecessary hardship is imposed upon the citizens by the <term>rulers: those who hold supreme authority (Latin: "imperantes")</term>; this is contrary to the purpose for which states were formed.

§. 6. Furthermore, because laws are enacted in vain if the supreme rulers allow them to be violated with impunity, it is their duty to ensure their execution. They must see to it that everyone may enjoy their rights without slow delays, evasions, or harassments. They must also exact punishments according to the nature of each deed and the intention and malice of the offender. Nor should they grant a pardon without a sufficient reason; for it is unjust, and a most effective way to provoke the minds of citizens, to treat those of equal merit differently when all other factors are equal.

Because the expenses necessary for preserving the state must be collected from the goods of the citizens, and because the strength of a state consists in the virtue and the wealth of its citizens, rulers must provide, as far as they are able, that the private estates of the citizens flourish.

To this end, it serves to direct the citizens so that, as much as possible, they may reap a rich harvest from the land and the waters. They should be encouraged to apply their own industry to the raw materials produced among them, so that they do not have to purchase from others the labor they could conveniently perform themselves; this is achieved if the **mechanical arts** <term>mechanical arts: manual crafts, manufacturing, and technical trades (Latin: "artes mechanicæ")</term> are fostered. §. 13. Finally, since the relationship between different states is a rather untrustworthy peace, supreme rulers must ensure that the *virtue of the citizens and skill in arms* are nurtured. Furthermore, even if a **just cause for war** <term>just cause for war: a morally or legally recognized reason to go to war (Latin: "belli causa")</term> exists, no one should be provoked into conflict unless a very safe opportunity appears and the state’s condition can comfortably support it.

## CHAPTER XII.
# CONCERNING **CIVIL LAWS**
### in detail.

->§. 1.<-

It now remains to examine the parts of **supreme power** <term>supreme power: the absolute authority or sovereignty held by a ruler or governing body (Latin: "summi imperii")</term> in detail, and the specific things that must be observed regarding them. Here, **civil laws** <term>civil laws: the specific regulations enacted by a state for its citizens (Latin: "leges civiles")</term> occupy the first place. These are the decrees of the supreme civil ruler, by which citizens are instructed on what they ought to do or avoid in their lives as members of the state.

§. 2. Moreover, "civil laws" are so called primarily in two respects: either with respect to *authority* or with respect to *origin*. In the former sense, all laws according to which justice is administered in a **civil court** <term>civil court: the legal setting or "forum" where a state's laws are applied (Latin: "foro civili")</term> can be called civil laws, regardless of where they originally came from. <note>In this sense, even ancient religious or natural laws are considered "civil" if the state's courts use them to settle disputes.</note> In the latter sense, "civil laws" refers to those which proceeded first from the will of the supreme civil power and concern matters that are not already defined by **natural or divine law**, but are established for the particular benefit of individual states. <note>Natural law refers to universal moral principles (like "do not kill"), while civil law refers to specific state rules (like "drive on the right side of the road").</note>

§. 3. Furthermore, just as nothing is [holier] <meta>The original text ends with the catchword "fan-", likely for "sanctius" (holier/more sacred).</meta> for civil laws...

<summary>This page defines civil law as the primary tool of a sovereign ruler and distinguishes between laws defined by their court authority and those defined by their origin in the ruler's will.</summary>
<keywords>civil law, supreme power, sovereignty, natural law, divine law, legal authority, foreign policy, treaties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XII, which focuses on how the state adopts natural laws into its own legal system. The text transitions from the general definition of civil laws on the previous page to the practical necessity of enforcing them through state power.</meta>

BOOK II.

...[nothing should] be done that is not directed toward the benefit of the state. <note>original: "commodum reip[ublicae]"</note> Since it is highly beneficial for the decency and peace of social life that **natural law** <term>natural law: universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature (Latin: "lex naturalis")</term> be properly followed by citizens, it falls upon the **supreme rulers** <term>supreme rulers: those holding absolute authority in a state (Latin: "summis imperantibus")</term> to grant it the power and effectiveness of **civil law**. <term>civil law: the specific laws enacted by a state for its citizens (Latin: "lex civilis")</term> For there is such malice found among the majority of mortals that neither the obvious utility of natural law nor the fear of the divine Being is enough to restrain it. Thus, so that the integrity of civil life can be maintained in some way, the **supreme power** <term>supreme power: the absolute authority or sovereignty held by a ruler (Latin: "summum imperium")</term> can achieve this by giving natural laws the force of civil laws.

§. 4. The *force of civil laws* consists in this: that a *punitive sanction* <term>punitive sanction: a penalty or punishment established for breaking a law (Latin: "sanctio pœnalis")</term> is added to the commands regarding what must be done or avoided; or rather, that it defines what punishment shall await in the **court of the state** <term>court of the state: the legal jurisdiction of the community (Latin: "foro civitatis")</term> for the person who has failed to do what was required or has done what was forbidden. Those natural laws which lack this punitive sanction are violated with impunity in the *human court*, <term>human court: the legal system of mankind as opposed to God's judgment (Latin: "foro humano")</term> though the *vengeance of the divine tribunal* <term>divine tribunal: the judgment of God (Latin: "vindicta tribunalis divini")</term> remains. <note>The author is distinguishing between crimes the state can punish and sins that only God can judge.</note>

§. 5. Furthermore, because the nature of civil life does not permit every person to demand what they believe is owed to them through their own violence, the civil laws support natural law in this regard: they provide a *legal action* <term>legal action: the right to pursue a claim in court (Latin: "actionem")</term> for those things owed under it. By the force of this action, such claims can be pursued in a civil court through the assistance of a **magistrate**. <term>magistrate: a civil officer who administers the law (Latin: "magistratus")</term> Those things which have not received this force through civil laws [cannot] be [extorted] from the *unwilling*... <note>The text here breaks off with the catchword "tor," which would complete the word "extorqueri," meaning "to be forced" or "extorted."</note>

<summary>This page explains that because humans are often driven by malice, the state must turn moral "natural laws" into "civil laws" by adding specific punishments. It also notes that civil laws provide the official mechanism (legal actions) for people to claim what they are owed without resorting to private violence.</summary>

### BOOK II.

...cannot be enforced by coercion, but rest solely on the shame and conscience of those who owe them. Civil laws usually provide a legal **action** <term>action: the right to bring a lawsuit to enforce a legal claim (Latin: "actio")</term> primarily for those **obligations** <term>obligations: duties or legal ties between parties (Latin: "obligationibus")</term> which have been contracted through express agreements between people. For other duties, which are owed based on some indefinite obligation of **natural law**, the state has generally denied a legal action. This was done so that there might be a field in which the more upright of mortals could exercise their virtue; they might thus earn significant praise if they were seen to have done what is right without the threat of force. Often, too, a matter did not seem important enough that the **praetor** <term>praetor: a high-ranking Roman official responsible for the administration of justice and organizing the courts (Latin: "praetor")</term> should be troubled by it.

§. 6. Since many things are commanded by natural law in an indefinite way, with their application left to each person’s discretion, civil law—for the sake of the state's honor and tranquility—tends to assign the *time, manner, place, and persons* for such actions and determine other circumstances. Sometimes, it even entices people to undertake these duties by offering rewards. Furthermore, if anything in natural law is obscure, it is the role of civil law to *explain* <note>original: "explicare"</note> it. Citizens are bound to follow this explanation in their conduct, even if their private opinions might happen to differ.

§. 7. Since very many actions are left by natural law to each person's judgment and discretion, and yet it is in the interest of public peace and decency that they be regulated in a uniform manner within the state, civil laws [prescribe] for such actions...

### CHAPTER XII.

->O 3<-

...and they are accustomed to assign a *certain form* <note>original: "certam formam"</note> to business transactions; as happens in last wills, in contracts, and many others. For the same reason, **civil laws** <term>civil laws: the specific laws of a particular state or city-state (Latin: "leges civiles")</term> have also been accustomed to circumscribe the exercise of those rights which belong to us by nature.

§. 8. Citizens <term>citizens: members of a political community subject to its laws (Latin: "cives")</term> ought to *obey* <note>original: "obtemperare"</note> civil laws, insofar as they do not openly conflict with **divine law** <term>divine law: the law revealed by God (Latin: "jus divinum")</term>—not merely out of a bare fear of punishment, but from an intrinsic obligation confirmed by **natural law** <term>natural law: universal moral principles discoverable by human reason (Latin: "jus naturale")</term> itself; for among its precepts is also this: that one must obey those who command with legitimate authority.

§. 9. Here, however, it must be observed whether the sovereign ruler commands the citizen to do something as an *action proper to the citizen* <note>original: "actionem civi propriam"</note>; or whether he instead mandates that they undertake the bare *execution of an action*, which ought to be proper to the ruler. For in the latter case, if necessity is imposed by the ruler, a citizen can perform the act without committing a **sin** <term>sin: a moral or religious offense (Latin: "peccatum")</term> himself, while the ruler who commanded it is the one who commits the sin. But it cannot rightly happen that a citizen should commit a sin in his own name that is repugnant to natural and divine law. This is why, even in an **unjust war** <term>unjust war: a conflict that lacks a lawful or moral justification (Latin: "bellum injustum")</term>, if a citizen carries arms by order of the sovereign ruler, he does not sin. But if, by the same ruler's command, someone should condemn an innocent person, bear false witness, or ca- <note>The text breaks here; the following page likely continues with "calumniate" or "carry out a capital sentence."</note>

It makes a vital distinction between actions performed as a personal moral agent and those performed as a mere instrument of the state, such as serving in an unjust war.</summary>

### CHAPTER XIII.

## ON THE RIGHT OF LIFE AND DEATH.

->§. I.<-

The **sovereign civil power** <term>sovereign civil power: the highest authority within a state, such as a monarch or governing assembly (Latin: "summum imperium civile")</term> possesses a right over the lives of citizens in a twofold manner: *indirectly or directly; the former for the defense of the state, the latter for the suppression of crimes.*

§. 2. Since the force of outsiders must often be repelled by force, or our rights must be claimed from them by violence, the sovereign power is certainly permitted to compel citizens to this task. In such cases, it is not done with the intention that the citizens lose their lives, but rather that their lives are merely exposed to the danger of death. So that citizens might conduct themselves vigorously and skillfully in such dangers, the sovereign power ought to train and prepare them. However, no citizen is allowed to make themselves useless for **military duties** <term>military duties: obligations and services related to war and the defense of the commonwealth (Latin: "munia militaria")</term> out of fear of that danger. And whoever has been enrolled in the military shall by no means abandon their assigned post out of fear, but shall instead fight to their last breath—unless they understand it to be the ruler's will that they preserve their life rather than the position, or that the location itself is not as valuable to the state as the lives of those citizens.

§. 3. However, to directly take life from citizens...

<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

[The **sovereign power** <term>sovereign power: the supreme authority in a state (Latin: "summum imperium")</term>] can [take life] for atrocious crimes and by way of punishment; which, however, also falls upon other goods of a person. In this place, some things regarding the nature of punishment in general must be set forth.

§ 4. Therefore, **punishment** <term>punishment: a penalty for a crime (Latin: "pœna")</term> is an **evil of passion** <note>original: "malum passionis" — in this context, "passion" refers to something one suffers or undergoes (passive), as opposed to something one does (active)</note>, which is inflicted because of an **evil of action** <note>original: "malum actionis" — a wrongful deed or crime</note>; or some troublesome evil which is imposed on someone by way of **compulsion** <term>compulsion: the use of force or authority to make someone do or suffer something (Latin: "coactio")</term> and by authority, in view of a past crime. For although certain actions are often commanded in place of punishment, in these cases one considers the fact that they are laborious and troublesome to the one performing them, and that a certain suffering is brought upon the actor through them. Moreover, punishment must be inflicted upon the unwilling; otherwise, it would not achieve its purpose, which is to deter people from sins by its severity. Those things which one accepts willingly do not have this effect. Finally, those evils which are inflicted in war or in the heat of combat do not have the nature of punishment, because they are not done by authority; nor are those which one suffers through injury <note>i.e., a private wrong or assault</note>, because they are not inflicted in view of a past crime.

§ 5. Just as **natural liberty** <term>natural liberty: the state of being subject to no earthly authority but only to God (Latin: "libertas naturalis")</term> entails that anyone established in it has no superior besides God and is subject only to divine punishments, so too, with the introduction of **sovereign authority** <term>sovereign authority: the governing power over a people (Latin: "imperium")</term> among men, the safety of societies grants this to the rulers: that they may be able to restrain the **malice of subjects** <note>original: "malitiam subjectorum"</note> by the threat of evil, so that...

so that many people can live securely with one another.

§ 6. Moreover, although there seems to be nothing unfair in the idea that a person who has done evil should suffer evil, in human punishments we must not simply look at what evil was committed, but also at what **benefit** <term>benefit: the practical good or "utility" derived from a legal action (Latin: "utilitas")</term> might come from the punishment. Likewise, punishments should by no means be carried out for the purpose of allowing the injured party to "feed their soul" or take pleasure in the pain and suffering of the offender; for such pleasure is clearly inhuman and <note>original: "socialitati repugnans"</note> contrary to the social nature of mankind.

§ 7. The genuine goal of human punishment is the prevention of harms and injuries. This is achieved if either the one who sinned is corrected for the better, or if others are deterred by his example so that they do not wish to sin hereafter, or if the offender is so restrained that he cannot do harm again. This can also be expressed this way: in punishment, consideration is given either to the **benefit of the one who sinned**, or to the benefit of the one who had an interest in the crime not having happened (and was thus harmed by it), or to the benefit of everyone in general without distinction.

§ 8. Therefore, first, in applying punishment, the *benefit of the one who sinned* is considered, while his spirit is reformed through the pain of the punishment itself, and the desire to sin is extinguished by it. This kind of punishment is even left to **heads of households** <term>heads of households: the male leader of a family unit who held legal authority over children and servants (Latin: "patribusfamilias")</term> to exercise over their own domestic staff in most states. However, for this purpose alone <note>i.e., for the purpose of personal reform or correction</note>, one cannot proceed to the death penalty...

§ 9. Secondly, punishment involves the ***benefit of the one who was harmed***, so that they do not suffer such a thing in the future from the same person or from others. The first goal is achieved if the one who committed the wrong is either removed, or if their power to do harm is taken away while their life is spared, or if they are taught through their own suffering to stop committing crimes. The latter goal—deterring others—is achieved by a punishment that is public and visible, the display of which is designed to strike terror into others.

§ 10. Finally, the ***benefit of everyone is also sought*** in punishment. This is done to ensure that a person who has harmed one individual does not subsequently harm others, or so that others, terrified by the example, refrain from committing similar acts. This is achieved in the same manner as mentioned before.

§ 11. Furthermore, if we consider both the goals of punishment and the condition of the human race, it is clear that ***not all sins are of such a nature that it is appropriate for them to be punished at all in a human court.*** <term>human court: the legal jurisdiction of earthly authorities, distinguished from the "divine court" of God's judgment (Latin: "forum humanum")</term> Therefore, ***purely internal acts*** are exempt from human punishments; these include pleasant thoughts about a sin, cravings, desires, or a plan that is not carried out, even if these later come to the knowledge of others through a subsequent confession. For since no harm is inflicted on anyone by such an internal movement of the mind, it is in no person's interest that someone should be punished for it.

§ 12. It would also be far too harsh to subject the *most minute* <note>original: "minutissimos"</note> slips to human punishments, which, in this current condition of our nature, cannot be avoided no matter how much attention a person resolves to apply.

§ 13. § 14. Finally, it is also necessary to exempt from human punishment those *vices of the soul* <term>vices of the soul: internal moral failings or character flaws (Latin: "vitia animi")</term> *resulting from the common corruption* of mortals. These are found so frequently that there would be no one left to rule over if you wished to punish them with severe penalties, provided they have not broken out into egregious acts. Such vices include *ambition, greed, inhumanity, an ungrateful spirit, hypocrisy, envy, pride, anger, animosity, and the like.*

§ 15. Nor is it always necessary that a penalty be demanded even if sins have been committed that are suitable for human punishment. ### CHAPTER XIII.

Likewise, a pardon may be granted if the offender can point to his own exceptional services to the state <note>original: "rempublicam," referring to the commonwealth or organized political community</note>, or those of his kin, which are worthy of a special reward; or if he is recommended by some other notable quality, such as a rare skill; or if there is hope that the offense may be redeemed <note>literally "washed away"</note> by future virtuous deeds. This is especially true where some degree of ignorance—though not entirely blameless—played a part, or if the specific purpose behind the law no longer applies in a particular instance. Often, too, a pardon must be granted due to the sheer number of offenders, lest the state be drained by severe punishments <term>severe punishments: the Latin "suppliciis" often refers specifically to capital punishment or extreme physical penalties</term>.

§ 16. Furthermore, the gravity of crimes is weighed based on the object against which the offense was committed, depending on how noble or precious that object is held to be. It is also judged by its effect—that is, how much or how little damage flows to the state from the act. Finally, it is judged by the depravity of the intention, which is gathered from various indicators. For example, [we consider] whether the person could have easily resisted the impulses that drove them to sin; or whether, beyond general reason, there was some specific motive that should have deterred them from sinning; or whether unique circumstances make the act more serious; or whether the individual possessed a mind capable of resisting the temptations <note>original: "illecebris," charms or enticements</note> of vice. One also considers whether a person was the first to act, or was led astray by the example of others,

# BOOK II.

...whether they sinned once or repeatedly, and whether they did so after warnings had already been given in vain.

§ 17. However, it is within the power of the supreme civil authority <term>supreme civil authority: *summum imperium civile*, the sovereign power or governing body of a state</term> to define precisely what type and degree of punishment should be inflicted for individual offenses. In this matter, the authority must keep the utility of the state <note>original: "utilitatem reipubl."</note> as its sole focus. Thus, it is possible—and indeed common—for the same punishment to be imposed for two unequal crimes. For the "equality" that judges are commanded to observe regarding defendants is understood to apply to those who have committed the same *kind* of crime; in such cases, a crime punished in one person should not be pardoned in another without a very serious reason.

Although humans should be as lenient toward one another as possible, the safety of the state and the security of its citizens sometimes demand that punishments be made more severe. For instance, if a harsher "medicine" is required to combat spreading vices, or when a particular crime is exceptionally dangerous to the commonwealth. Regarding the magnitude of punishments, this rule must be strictly followed: they should be just severe enough to restrain the unruly desires <note>original: "libidinem," referring to the impulse or passion to break the law</note> of mortals that lead them to commit the very sins for which the punishments were established. Furthermore, punishments heavier than those defined by law should not be exacted unless particularly heinous <note>literally "atrocious"</note> circumstances aggravate the deed.

§ 18. However, because the same punishment does not affect everyone equally, and therefore does not produce an equal <meta>catchword: effect</meta>

### CHAPTER XIII.

...produces the effect of restraining the desire to sin <note>original: "peccandi libidinem"</note>. Therefore, both in the general designation of punishments and in their application to individuals, one must look at the **person of the offender themselves**, and those qualities in them which can increase or decrease the perception of the punishment; for example, age, sex, status, wealth, strength, and similar factors.

§ 19. Furthermore, just as no one can be subjected to punishment properly so called in a human court for the crime of another; so if a crime is *committed* by a *corporate body* <term>corporate body: *universitas*, a legal entity or community such as a city, guild, or corporation</term>, someone who did not consent to it will not be held responsible. Consequently, nothing can be taken away from that dissenting person which they did not hold in the name and benefit of the corporate body. This is true even though innocent people often feel the inconvenience when a corporate body is punished. The crimes of corporate bodies also expire when none of those remain who consented to or cooperated in the commission of that crime.

§ 20. However, it frequently happens that *the crime of another provides the occasion* for hardship to fall upon others, or for a previously hoped-for benefit to be cut off. For instance, when the parents' property is confiscated due to a crime, even innocent children are reduced to poverty. And when a defendant flees, a guarantor <term>guarantor: *fidejussor*, one who takes legal responsibility for another's appearance or debt</term> is forced to pay the fine, not because of a crime, but because he voluntarily bound himself to such an outcome.

## CHAPTER XIV.
# ON REPUTATION.
<note>original title: "DE EXISTIMATIONE"</note>

### § 1.

**Reputation** <note>original: "Existimatio"</note> in general is the value of persons in common life, according to which they are fit to be equated with or compared to other persons, and are either preferred over them or placed after them.

§ 2. It is divided into **simple** and **intensive**. <term>intensive: *intensiva*, referring to a higher degree of honor or rank beyond the basic status of a human being</term> Both are considered either among those living in **natural liberty** <term>natural liberty: a philosophical concept describing the state of humanity before or outside the bounds of organized government</term>, or those living together in a **civil state** <note>original: "statu civili," i.e., a society governed by laws and a sovereign</note>.

§ 3. **Simple reputation** among those living in natural liberty consists primarily in this: that one bears oneself as such, and is held to be such, that one can be dealt with as a good man, and as one who is prepared to live with others according to the prescription of the natural law.

§ 4. And this reputation is held to be **undamaged** <note>original: "integra"</note> as long as one has not yet, through a malicious or enormous deed, knowingly and willingly with evil intent <note>original: "dolo malo"</note> violated the law of nature against others. Hence, by nature, everyone is held to be a good man until the contrary is proven.

§ 5. The same reputation is **diminished** through enormous crimes maliciously committed against the natural law; these bring it about that greater caution is needed if anyone wishes to deal with such a person. Nevertheless, that stain can be washed away by providing

<meta>catchword: ul-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of 'Existimatio' or reputation, defining it as the social value of a person that allows them to be compared to others. It distinguishes between simple and intensive reputation and establishes that a person is naturally assumed to be good until they maliciously violate natural law.</summary>

<keywords>reputation, social value, natural liberty, civil state, natural law, simple reputation, intensive reputation, legal standing, presumption of goodness</keywords>

§. 6. *A way of life and profession that aims directly at harming others indiscriminately, and at making a profit from the manifest injuries of others, completely consumes this same reputation. Men of this sort <note>original: "Cujus farinæ homines," literally "men of this flour"</note>, as long as they refuse to reform, can be treated as common enemies by all those whom their malice can touch in any way. However, these men can repair their reputation <note>original: "existimatio"</note> when, having provided restitution for the damage or having obtained a pardon, they take up an honorable way of life and renounce their previous vice.*

§. 7. *Simple reputation among those living within states <note>original: "intra civitates," referring to organized political communities</note> is that by which one has not been declared a vicious member of the community according to the laws and customs of the state, and is understood to be counted as a person of some standing.*

§. 8. *This standing in a state is lost either from mere status or from a crime <term>delictum: a legal offense or wrongdoing</term>. The former happens in two ways: either that status itself has nothing naturally shameful about it, or it is joined with vice or at least the reputation of it. The first case occurs in certain states where slaves are of no account <note>original: "nullo numero sunt," meaning they have no legal personhood or standing</note>. The second case applies to pimps, prostitutes, and the like; as long as they are publicly tolerated in the state, they do indeed enjoy common protection, yet they are to be excluded from the company of honorable men. The same thing happens to certain cir—* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page; the catchword "ca" suggests the next word is "circumforaneis," referring to traveling peddlers or performers.</note>

§. 9. From a crime, however, this same standing is completely lost when someone is marked with <term>infamy: *infamia*, a legal loss of reputation or civic standing</term> according to civil laws due to a specific offense. This is done either so that the person is punished with death at the same time, thus branding their memory <note>meaning their name remains disgraced even after death</note>, or so that they are expelled from the state, or so that they are kept within the state as an infamous and rotten member.

§. 10. It is manifest, however, that <term>simple reputation: *existimatio simplex*, the basic moral standing of a person</term>, or *natural honesty*, cannot be snatched from someone by the mere whim of supreme rulers. Since doing so in no way serves the utility of the state, it cannot be understood that such power was ever granted to the rulers. Similarly, true infamy does not seem to be contracted when someone executes the orders of the state merely as a <term>servant: *nudus minister*, literally a "naked minister," meaning one who carries out orders without personal agency or moral responsibility for the command</term>.

§. 11. <term>Intensive reputation: *existimatio intensiva*, a person’s status or rank relative to others</term> is that by which persons—who are otherwise equal in terms of their simple reputation—are preferred to one another, according to the qualities present in one more than another, by which the minds of others are usually moved to show honor. **Honor**, however, is properly the expression of our judgment regarding another's excellence.

§. 12. This intensive reputation can be considered either *among those who live in natural liberty* <note>referring to the "state of nature" before or outside of organized government</note>, *or among citizens of the same state*. # CHAPTER XIV.

§. 13. The foundations of <term>intensive reputation: *existimatio intensiva*, the social rank or status a person holds relative to others</term> are generally considered to be all those things that possess remarkable perfection and excellence, or are judged to demonstrate such qualities—specifically those whose effects align with the purpose of <term>natural law: *lex naturalis*</term> or the needs of states. These include mental sharpness and the capacity for learning various sciences and arts; keen judgment in conducting affairs; a spirit that is firm and unshaken by external circumstances, rising above both temptations and fears; eloquence; physical beauty and dexterity; the gifts of fortune; and, above all, outstanding achievements.

§. 14. However, all these qualities produce nothing more than an <term>imperfect right: *jus imperfectum*, a moral claim to respect that lacks legal force</term>, or a fitness for receiving honor and veneration from others. Therefore, if someone denies honor even to those who are well-deserving, they do not commit a legal injury, but are merely spoken ill of for their lack of humanity and their incivility. But a <term>perfect right: *jus perfectum*, a legally enforceable claim</term> to receive honor from another, or to hold the symbols of that honor, arises either from the authority one holds over another, or from a pact entered into with that person regarding the matter, or from a law established or approved by a common ruler.

§. 15. Among <term>princes: *principes*, used here for sovereign rulers</term> and entire nations, for the sake of eminence and <term>precedence: *præcedentia*, the right to a higher rank or more honorable place in diplomatic settings</term>, it is customary to argue...

However, all these things by themselves do not produce a <term>perfect right: *jus perfectum*, a legally enforceable claim</term> to <term>precedence: *praecedentia*, the right to a higher rank in ceremonies or treaties</term> against other kings or peoples, unless that right has been acquired through a treaty or their consent. <note>In 17th-century diplomacy, "precedence" was a frequent cause of conflict, as rulers fought over who should walk first in processions or sign documents first.</note>

§. 14. Among citizens, however, it is the role of the <term>supreme ruler: *summus imperans*</term> to designate degrees of rank. In doing so, the ruler rightly considers each person's excellence and their capacity for contributing <term>meritorious service: *merita*</term> to the <term>commonwealth: *respublica*</term>. Whatever degree of rank the ruler assigns to a citizen, he rightly protects that citizen in that status against their fellow citizens; likewise, the citizen himself ought to be content with that same rank.

-># CHAPTER XV.<-

## ON THE POWER OF THE SUPREME AUTHORITY over the property contained within the state.

->§. I.<-

Just as it is at the discretion of supreme rulers to decide what rights citizens hold over property that was originally granted to them by those rulers, so too do rulers have three primary rights over the <term>property: *bona*</term> that citizens have fully acquired through their own industry or by some other means. These rights arise from the very nature of states and are necessary for achieving their purpose.

§ 2. The first right consists in this: that the rulers may <term>prescribe laws: *leges praescribere*</term> for the citizens regarding the use of those same goods...

<page-num>227</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER XV.</header>

...indeed, to temper it for the <term>utility of the state: *utilitas civitatis*</term>, whether concerning the manner and quality of <term>possessions: *possessiones*</term>, or the method of transferring goods to others, and other things of this kind.

§. 3. <note>The original text numbers this section as 3 and the following as 2, despite the text identifying them as the "second" and "third" rights respectively. This is likely a printer's error.</note> The second right consists in this: that the ruler may take some portion of the citizens' goods under the name of a <term>tax: *tributum*</term> or <term>duty: *vectigal*</term>. For since the lives and fortunes of citizens must be defended by the state, it is necessary that they contribute the funds from which the expenses required for that end may be sustained. Thus, he is truly shameless who wishes to enjoy the protection and convenience of the state, and yet contributes nothing of his labor or property toward its preservation. Nevertheless, in this matter, prudent rulers rightly adapt themselves to the complaining nature of the common people, and labor so that these funds may be collected with as little hardship as possible; they do this especially by maintaining equality, and by demanding moderate and varied payments rather than large and uniform ones.

§. 2. The third right is <term>eminent domain: *dominium eminens*</term>, which consists in this: that when the necessity of the <term>commonwealth: *respublica*</term> is urgent, the goods of any subject which are most needed at that time may be seized and applied to public use. This applies even if those goods far exceed the proportional share that the citizen was otherwise bound to contribute to the state's expenses. For this reason, however, whatever exceeds his fair share <term>ought to be refunded: *refundi debet*</term> to that citizen from the public treasury, or through a contribution from the other citizens, as far as is possible.

<sig>P 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: §. 5.</meta>

<summary>This page outlines two primary powers of the state over private property: the right of taxation to fund public defense and the right of eminent domain to seize private goods for urgent public needs, provided the owner is compensated.</summary>

<keywords>taxation, eminent domain, property rights, public utility, social contract, fiscal policy, state necessity, compensation</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sovereign's power over property from Chapter XV, specifically distinguishing between personal royal funds and public state funds. It then transitions into Chapter XVI, which examines the moral and legal foundations of war and peace.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>BOOK II.</header>

§. 5. In addition to these three rights <note>Referring to the rights over taxes and eminent domain discussed on the previous page</note>, in many states, specific **public goods** are granted, which are usually called the **patrimony of the state** or of the kingdom. In some places, these are again distinguished into the **patrimony of the sovereignty** and that of the **state**, or into the **fisc** and the **treasury**. <note>original: *fiscum & aerarium*. In this context, the author distinguishes between the ruler's personal budget and the public funds used for the realm's defense and administration.</note> The first of these is designated for the support of the king and his family; the second for the public utility of the kingdom. Regarding the first, the king holds the right of <term>usufruct: *usus fructus*</term> <note>The legal right to use and enjoy the profits or harvest of property, provided the property itself is not destroyed or changed</note>, and may dispose of the income arising from it as he sees fit. Regarding the second, however, he fulfills the role of an **administrator**, and must apply it to the specific uses for which it was intended. He cannot <term>alienate: *alienare*</term> <note>To transfer ownership or sell to another party</note> either one without the consent of the people.

§. 6. Much less can he who does not hold the kingdom as his own personal patrimony <note>That is, a ruler who holds his position by election or law rather than as a private inheritance</note> alienate the entire kingdom or any part of it, unless the consent of the people is granted—and in the latter case, the specific consent of the part being alienated is also required. Just as, conversely, no member of the state may break away against the state's will, unless it has been brought to such a condition by the force of external enemies that it can in no other way remain safe.

### CHAPTER XVI.

## ON WAR AND PEACE.

->§. I.<-

Although it is most consistent with <term>natural law: *lex naturalis*</term> that men should live in peace with one another, voluntarily providing those things which were
<meta>catchword: owed</meta>

<summary>This page defines the distinction between a ruler's private income (fisc) and the public treasury (aerarium), while establishing that national territory cannot be sold or abandoned without mutual consent. It begins a new chapter on the necessity of peace under natural law.</summary>
<keywords>public goods, state patrimony, fisc, treasury, usufruct, alienation of territory, sovereignty, natural law, peace</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the limits of sovereign power. It concludes a point about the preference for peace and begins Chapter XVI, which focuses on the transition from the law of property to the law of war and peace.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
# CHAPTER XVI.

...[they maintained]; rather, peace itself is the state peculiar to man insofar as he is distinguished from the beasts. Nevertheless, war is sometimes lawful even for man, and occasionally necessary—namely, when through the malice of another we cannot preserve our own property, or obtain our rights <note>original: *jus*</note> without employing force. In this situation, however, prudence and humanity advise that we should not resort to arms where more evil would result to us and our people from the pursuit of our injuries <note>original: *injuriarum*</note> than good would be gained.

§. 2. *The **just causes** for which war may be undertaken come down to these: that we preserve and protect ourselves and our property against the unjust invasion of others; or that we assert those things which, though owed to us, others refuse to provide; or that we obtain reparation for an injury already inflicted and security for the future. A war waged for the first cause is called **defensive war** <note>original: *bellum defensivum*</note>; that which is waged for the latter causes is called **offensive war** <note>original: *bellum offensivum*</note>.*

§. 3. Nor, however, must one immediately resort to arms as soon as they consider themselves wronged, especially where some doubt still exists regarding the law or the facts. Instead, it must be tried whether the matter can be settled in a friendly way; for instance, by a conference arranged between the parties, by a referral to **arbitrators** <term>arbitrators: *arbitros*</term> <note>Independent third parties chosen to settle a dispute outside of a court or battlefield</note>, or by leaving the matter to lot. This path should especially be attempted by the one making the claim; for indeed, the favor of the law certainly accompanies possession when it is held with some title <note>Meaning that the person currently in possession of a thing has the legal advantage over the person trying to take it away</note>.

<sig>P 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: §. 4.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the ethical boundaries of conflict, asserting that while peace is the natural human state, war is legally and morally permissible for self-defense or the recovery of rights when peaceful mediation fails.</summary>
<keywords>just war, defensive war, offensive war, peace, arbitration, legal rights, human nature, prudence</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the laws of war from Chapter XVI. Having established just causes for war on the previous page, the author now shifts to discussing the unjust motivations for conflict and the ethical limits of how war is conducted.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
<header>BOOK II.</header>

§. 4. *Furthermore, unjust causes of war are either openly so, or they admit some excuse, however thin.* These are primarily referred to two main sources: greed and ambition—or, the lust for possession and for ruling. These motivations are various; for example, fear arising from the wealth and power of a neighbor, utility <note>Meaning personal or national advantage</note> devoid of legal right, the desire to acquire better lands, the denial of things which were owed only out of some simple virtue <note>original: *simplici aliqua virtute*; this refers to moral duties like gratitude or kindness which, unlike legal duties, do not usually justify war</note>, the perceived "stupidity" of the current owner, the desire to extinguish another's legitimately acquired right because it seems a little troublesome to us, and similar things.

§. 5. *Although the most characteristic mode of acting in war is force and terror, it is nonetheless permitted to use fraud and deceit against an enemy, provided that a given promise is not violated.* <note>original: *fides data*; the concept that even in war, one's word or a signed treaty must be kept</note> From this, it follows that one may deceive an enemy through "assertive speech"—that is, through fabricated stories or misinformation—but absolutely never through broken promises or formal pacts.

§. 6. Regarding the force that is employed in war against the enemy and their property, a distinction must be made between *what the enemy can suffer without legal injury, and what we ourselves can inflict while still preserving our humanity.* <note>original: *humanitas*; the moral quality of being human and showing mercy</note> For a person who has declared himself my enemy has, by that very profession, signaled his intent to bring extreme evils upon me; therefore, as far as he is concerned, he grants me an almost infinite latitude to act against him. Nevertheless, humanity commands that, as far as the rush of arms allows, we should not inflict more evil on the enemy than is required for our defense, the vindication of our rights, or our security for the future.

<meta>catchword: S. 7.</meta>

unjust causes, greed, ambition, force, terror, fraud, deceit, humanity, defense, vindication of right

It argues that while force and even deception are permissible, one must never break a formal promise and should only inflict as much harm as is strictly necessary for security.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER XVI.</header>
->P 4<-
<meta>catchword: entia (likely part of the word 'patientia,' meaning 'suffering' or 'permitting')</meta>

§. 7. War is usually divided into *formal* and *informal* <note>original: *solenne* and *minus solenne*; these terms distinguish between "regular" wars between sovereign states and "irregular" conflicts like rebellions or raids</note>. For a war to be formal, it is required both that it be waged by the authority of the sovereign on both sides, and that it be preceded by a formal declaration. Informal war is that which has not been declared, or which is waged against private individuals. Civil wars also belong to this category.

§. 8. The *right to wage war* in a state belongs to the one who holds supreme power. Therefore, for a magistrate <note>In this context, a governor or local official</note> to seize this right without authority delegated by the sovereign is an overstepping of his office—even in cases where he suspects that the sovereign, if consulted, would have favored waging war then and there. While it is understood that everyone in charge of a province or a fortified place with military forces is tasked, by the very nature of their office, with repelling an invading enemy from the locations entrusted to them by any means possible, they should not, however, recklessly take the war into enemy territory.

§. 9. Furthermore, just as one living in natural liberty <note>The "state of nature," where there is no higher government</note> can be attacked in war only for wrongs they have personally committed, so too in a state, the *ruler of the state* or the entire state is often invaded in war, even if that ruler was not the author of the injury. But for this to be done justly, it is necessary that the injury has in some way passed over to them. Indeed, rulers of states participate in the wrongs committed by their former citizens, or those who have recently fled to them for refuge, if they allowed those wrongs to be committed, or if they offer them protection <note>original: *receptum*</note>. To permit...

...permission <note>The Latin *patientia* (sufferance) here refers to a ruler's failure to prevent a crime they are aware of, which makes them legally complicit</note> be considered blameworthy, it is required that one both knows a crime is being committed and is able to prevent it. Moreover, things that are done by citizens openly and frequently are presumed to be known by the ruler of the state. The power to prevent is always presumed, unless its absence is clearly proven. Nevertheless, the fact that someone who receives and protects a criminal <note>original: *noxium*; a harmful person or wrongdoer</note> fleeing to them merely for the sake of avoiding punishment can be attacked in war arises more from a specific treaty between neighbors and allies than from any common obligation; unless that fugitive <note>original: *profugus*</note> plots hostilities against the state they deserted while staying among us.

§. 10. But it is also accepted among nations <note>The *jus gentium* or Law of Nations</note> that for a state’s own debt, or for a debt which it has taken upon itself by failing to administer required justice, the goods of individual citizens are held liable to the extent that foreigners to whom the debt is owed may seize them <note>original: *manus injicere*; literally 'to lay hands upon,' a legal term for seizure</note> if found in their own territory. However, this must be done in such a way that reimbursement is provided to the citizens whose property is seized in this manner by those who originally contracted the debt. Such executions are usually called ***reprisals, which are frequently the preludes to wars.***

§. 11. ***Not only may anyone wage war for themselves, but also one for another.*** However, for this to be done rightly, a just cause is required on the part of the person for whom the war is waged; and on the part of the helper, there must be a justifiable reason <note>original: *probabilis ratio*; a plausible or valid ground for intervention, such as a treaty or kinship</note> why they may exercise hostilities against another on that person's behalf. # CHAPTER XVI.

...for whom we not only can, but also must take up arms. In the first place are our own subjects <note>original: *subditi*; those living under the legal authority of the state</note>, not only as a whole body, but also as individuals; provided that the state is not clearly plunged into greater evils by doing so. Next follow our allies, according to what is contained in their treaty <note>original: *fœdus*</note>. However, these allies must yield to our own citizens whenever we ourselves need help at the same time; furthermore, it is presupposed that the allies have a just cause for war and exercise some prudence in undertaking it. After these come our friends, even if no specific promise has been made to them. Finally, where no other reason exists, the mere bond of common kinship <note>original: *communis cognatio*; here meaning the shared identity of all humans as members of the same "race" or species</note> can be sufficient, so that we may go to defend one who is unjustly oppressed and who implores our help, as far as we can conveniently do so.

§. 12. *The license <note>original: *licentia*; legal or social permission</note> in war extends so far that, although someone may have advanced by committing slaughter or by wasting and seizing property beyond what humanity allows, they are still not considered infamous, or someone whom honest men should shun, according to the general opinion of nations.* The exception is that more civilized <note>original: *cultiores*; literally "more cultivated"</note> nations consider certain ways of harming enemies to be vile; for instance, using poison, or bribing another's citizens and soldiers to murder their masters.

§. 13. *Movable property <note>original: *res mobiles*; items that can be moved, such as equipment, livestock, or money</note> is understood to be captured in war only when it has escaped the enemy's pursuit; however, immobile property <note>original: *res immobiles*; such as land, buildings, or territory</note>, when*

<meta>This page continues the legal definition of captured property and transitions into the rules of sovereignty over defeated peoples and the nature of truces. ->### BOOK II.<-

...are held by us in such a way that we have the power to keep the enemy away from them. However, for the former owner's right <note>original: *jus*; a legal claim or entitlement</note> to recover those things to be completely extinguished, it is necessary that he has renounced every claim through a subsequent agreement <note>original: *pactum*</note>. Otherwise, it will be permitted to take back by force what was acquired by force. Furthermore, just as soldiers wage war by the authority of the state <note>original: *civitas*; the organized political community or body politic</note>, so what they take from the enemy they acquire properly for the state, not for themselves. Although it is commonly accepted everywhere that movable property <note>original: *res mobiles*; items that can be moved, such as equipment, livestock, or personal effects</note>, especially those not of great value, are left to the soldiers who captured them through indulgence, or as a reward, or sometimes in place of their wages; or to attract those who are willing to hire out their blood without being forced by necessity <note>a reference to mercenary service or volunteers seeking glory and profit</note>. When, however, captured things are taken back from the enemy again, immovable property <note>original: *res immobiles*; such as land, houses, or territory</note> indeed returns to the former owners; movable property should likewise return, although among most nations these too are granted to the soldiers as plunder.

§. 14. Finally, it is customary for sovereignty <note>original: *imperium*; the right to command and the authority to govern</note> to be acquired through war, both over individuals and over entire defeated peoples. However, for this to become legitimate and to bind the consciences of the subjects, it is necessary that the defeated have pledged their loyalty <note>original: *fidem dederint*; literally "given their faith"</note> to the victors, and that the victors have laid aside their hostile status and mindset toward them.

§. 15. Military actions are suspended through a truce <note>original: *induciæ*</note>, which is an agreement by which, for a set time, while the state of war and the dispute from which the war arose remains, one must refrain from offensive military acts.

### CHAPTER XVI.

...while they pass; when these have elapsed, unless peace has been struck in the meantime, a return is made to hostilities without a new declaration <note>original: *indictio*; a formal announcement of war required by international law to restart combat</note>.

§. 16. Furthermore, *truces can be divided* into those entered into while the armies remain in the field and the military apparatus <note>original: *apparatus bellicus*; the equipment, supplies, and readiness for war</note> is retained on both sides; these are usually taken for a very short duration. And then there are those in which the military apparatus is dissolved on both sides. These latter can be, and often are, entered into for a sufficiently long time and take on the appearance of full peace; sometimes they are even designated by the term "peace," with the addition of a specific timeframe. For otherwise, as a rule, all peace is eternal—that is, it extinguishes forever the controversies for which the war had been ignited. However, what are usually called "silent truces" <note>original: *induciae tacitae*; a de facto ceasefire where fighting stops without a formal agreement</note> contain no obligation; instead, the parties rest on both sides at their own pleasure and can rush back to acts of war whenever it pleases them.

§. 17. War is completely abolished, however, once *peace* has been ratified by the highest commanders <note>original: *summos imperantes*; the sovereign rulers or heads of state</note> on both sides. Just as it is within the power of the contracting parties to define its laws and conditions, so these must be faithfully carried out and observed at the agreed-upon time. To ensure the firmness of this arrangement, not only is an oath <note>term: *jusjurandum*; a religious and legal vow to keep one's word</note> usually offered and hostages <note>original: *obsides*; individuals held as security for the fulfillment of a treaty</note> given, but often others—especially those who intervened to reconcile the peace—take it upon themselves to guarantee its observation, promising aid to whichever party might be harmed by the other in violation of the laws of peace.

<gloss>truces, peace, war, oath, hostages, hostility, military apparatus</gloss>

<summary>This page distinguishes between short-term tactical truces and long-term truces that resemble peace, while defining "true peace" as the permanent resolution of conflict ratified by sovereign authorities and secured by oaths, hostages, or third-party guarantors.</summary>
<keywords>truces, peace treaties, military apparatus, silent truces, international law, oaths, hostages, guarantees of peace</keywords>
