BOOK II.

### CHAPTER XVII.
## ON TREATIES.

->§. 1.<-

In times of both war and peace, *treaties* <note>original: *fœdera*; formal agreements or alliances between sovereign powers</note>, or pacts entered into between the highest commanders on both sides, are of service. These can be divided based on their subject matter into those that establish some duty to be performed for one another which was already commanded by natural law <note>original: *lege naturali*; the universal moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason</note>; and those that add something further to the duties of natural law, or at least determine something specific where those laws seem indefinite.

§. 2. To the first class belong those treaties by which it is agreed only to exercise simple humanity <note>original: *humanitate*; the basic kindness or mutual respect owed between all people</note>, or to refrain from inflicting harm. This category also includes those by which simple friendship is sanctioned without any particular performance of services, or those by which the right of hospitality <note>term: *jus hospitij*; the legal protection and rights of passage granted to foreigners</note> or commerce is established, insofar as this is already owed by natural law.

§. 3. Treaties of the second class are either *equal* or *unequal*. The "equal" ones are those where both sides stand in the same position; that is, when not only are equal things promised on both sides (either simply or in proportion to their respective strength), but they are promised in an equal manner, so that neither party is in a worse condition than the other, or subordinate to the other.

§. 4. Treaties are *unequal* when either unequal performances are mutually exhibited, or when one party is in a worse condition. Unequal conditions are promised either by the more worthy ally or by the less worthy one. The former occurs if the more powerful party promises aid to the other without stipulating for it in return, or if they promise it in a greater proportion than the other. The latter occurs if the inferior ally is bound to perform more than they receive from the other.

§. 5. Of those things which are performed by the inferior ally,

CAPUT XVII.
### CHAPTER XVII.
## ON TREATIES.

<meta>This paragraph continues from the previous page's discussion on unequal treaties.</meta>

...some are joined with a reduction of supreme power <note>original: *summi imperii*; also known as sovereignty</note>; for instance, if it is agreed that an inferior ally should not exercise a portion of supreme power except with the consent of the superior party. Certain other treaties, however, *do not diminish supreme power*, even though they may include some temporary burden, or one that can be discharged all at once; for example, if by a peace treaty one party is bound to pay the wages of the other's soldiers, to restore the costs of the war, to pay a certain sum of money, to tear down city walls, to give hostages, or to hand over ships and arms, etc. Indeed, not even every perpetual burden diminishes supreme power. Such burdens include: having the same friends and enemies as the other party (but not reciprocally); being forbidden from building fortifications in certain places; or being forbidden from sailing to certain locations, and so on. Likewise, it does not diminish power if one of the allies is bound to courteously respect the majesty <note>original: *majestatem*; the high dignity and status of a sovereign state</note> of the other ally—that is, to show them a certain reverence and to modestly adapt themselves to their pleasure.

§ 6. Furthermore, both equal and unequal treaties are usually entered into for various reasons: among these, the closest kind of treaties are those that look toward some perpetual union of several states. However, the most frequent treaties are those that pertain to providing aid in defensive or offensive war, or to governing commerce <note>original: *commercia*; trade and mercantile relations</note>.

§. 7. Also famous is the division of treaties into *real* and *personal*. The "personal" ones are those entered into with a king in view of his own person, and they expire at the same time he dies. The "real" ones are those contracted not so much in view of the king himself, or the rulers of the people as such, but rather in view of the commonwealth <note>original: *reipubl.* or *reipublicae*</note> and the kingdom; these endure even if those who were their authors have died.

§. 8. Closely related to treaties are <term>engagements</term> <note>original: *sponsiones*; legal promises made on behalf of a state by an official who lacks the specific authority to do so</note>, which are properly called those pacts entered into by a minister of the supreme power concerning matters belonging to that power, but without having a mandate from the sovereign. To these, the sovereign [is] not...

BOOK II.

[The sovereign] is not bound, except after he has ratified <term>ratification: the formal validation of a treaty by a head of state</term> the same agreements: thus, if a minister has contracted them absolutely, and ratification has not followed, he himself must see how he may satisfy those who, having trusted his word, were deceived by empty agreements.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

## ON THE DUTIES OF CITIZENS.

The duty of citizens is either general or special. The former arises from the common obligation by which they are subject to civil authority. The latter, however, arises from a particular task and function which has been enjoined upon individuals by the supreme power.

§. 2. *The general duty of citizens regards either the rulers of the state, the state as a whole, or fellow citizens.*

§. 3. To the *rulers of the state*, the citizen owes reverence, loyalty, and obedience. Joined to this is the requirement that he acquiesce to the present state of affairs, neither turning his mind toward revolution <note>original: *res novandas*; literally "renewing things," a standard Latin idiom for political upheaval or revolution</note>, nor adhering more to any other party, nor admiring and venerating another; and that he think and speak well and honorably of the rulers and their actions.

§. 4. *Toward the whole state, the duty of a good citizen is* to hold nothing more dear than its safety and well-being; to willingly offer his life, wealth, and fortunes to preserve it; and to strain every nerve of his intellect and industry to bring it renown and promote its interests.

§. 5. *Toward fellow citizens, the duty of a citizen is* to live with them in a friendly and peaceful manner, and to show himself agreeable and easy to get along with; he should not cause disturbances through his own moodiness or stubbornness, nor should he envy the advantages of others or intercept them.

§. 6. *Special duties* either spread themselves throughout the entire commonwealth <term>commonwealth: translated from *remp.* (respublica); referring to the state or the public interest</term>, or are occupied only with a certain part of it. Concerning all of these, there is this general precept: that no one should aspire to or undertake any office in the commonwealth for which he finds himself unfit.

<summary>This page defines the ethical and legal obligations of citizens, categorizing them into general duties toward rulers and the state, and special duties associated with specific public offices.</summary>
<keywords>citizenship, duty, sovereignty, ratification, revolution, common good, public office, civil obedience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XVIII on the duties of citizens. While the previous page discussed the general obligations all citizens owe to the state and its rulers, this section examines the "special duties" associated with specific public offices and professions.</meta>

### CHAPTER XVIII.

§. 7. *Those who assist the rulers of states with their counsel* should turn the sharp focus of their minds toward every part of the commonwealth <note>original: *reip.*, an abbreviation for *res publica*, referring here to the state or public affairs</note>; they should explain what seems to be for the benefit of the state skillfully and faithfully, without personal passion or corrupt motives; they must hold the safety of the commonwealth as the goal of all their advice, rather than their own wealth or power; they should not sail with the wind by flattering the corrupt inclinations of princes; they must abstain from factions and illegal gatherings; they should conceal nothing that ought to be said, and leak nothing that ought to remain silent; they should show themselves impenetrable to the corruption of foreigners; and they must not prioritize their private business or pleasures over public affairs.

§. 8. *Those who are publicly appointed to perform sacred rites* should bring solemnity and attention to them; they should set forth true doctrines regarding the worship of the Divine <note>original: *cultu Numinis*</note>; they should show themselves to the people as a conspicuous example of what they themselves teach, lest through the depravity of their character they strip their office of its dignity and their doctrine of its weight.

§. 9. *Those whose public duty is to imbue the minds of citizens with the knowledge of various things* should teach nothing false or harmful: they should hand down truths in such a way that their listeners assent to them not so much out of a habit of hearing them, but because they have perceived the solid reasons for them; they should avoid all dogmas directed at disturbing civil society; and they should consider all human knowledge to be vain if no utility for human and civil life flows from it.

§. 10 *Those who are appointed to administer justice* <term>administer justice: from the Latin *juri dicundo*, referring to the judicial power to interpret and apply the law</term> should provide easy access to everyone and protect the common people from the oppressions of the powerful; they should deliver justice equally to the poor and humble as to the powerful and favored; they should not drag out lawsuits beyond what is necessary; they must abstain from bribery; they should apply diligence in investigating cases and set aside all emotions that corrupt the sincerity of judgment; and in doing what is right, they should fear no one.

§. 11. *Those to whom the care of arms is committed*, the soldier...

<summary>This page outlines the specific ethical and professional duties for various public roles—including political advisors, clergy, educators, and judges—emphasizing integrity, public service, and the rejection of personal gain.</summary>
<keywords>political ethics, counselors, religious duties, education, judicial integrity, public interest, anti-corruption, civil society</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page concludes Book II and the discussion on the duties of citizens. While the previous page addressed those in sacred and educational roles, this final section outlines the responsibilities of military commanders, soldiers, diplomats, and financial administrators, concluding with the conditions under which citizenship ends.</meta>

->## BOOK II.<-

[...commanders should] diligently and seasonably exercise their men, and strengthen them to endure the labors of military service; they must keep military discipline sound and well-protected; they should not rashly expose soldiers to be slaughtered by the enemy; they should provide provisions and pay as promptly as is in their power, and embezzle nothing from it; they should ensure that soldiers always favor the commonwealth <note>original: *reip.*, abbreviation for *res publica*</note>, and never win them over to themselves against the state.

§. 12. *Soldiers*, on the other hand, should be content with their pay; they must abstain from plundering and harassing peasants <note>original: *paganis*, referring to rural civilians</note>; they should undergo labors for the defense of the state willingly and vigorously; they should not invite dangers through recklessness, nor shun them through cowardice; they should display courage against the enemy, not against their fellow soldiers; they must manfully defend their assigned post; and they should prefer an honorable death to a shameful flight and life.

§. 13. *Those whose services the state employs among foreigners* <note>referring to ambassadors, envoys, or diplomats</note> should be cautious and circumspect; skilled in discerning the empty from the solid and the true from the fabulous; extremely tenacious of secrets; and obstinate against any corruptions for the benefit of their own state.

§. 14. *Those who are in charge of collecting or distributing the wealth of the state* <note>financial officers or tax collectors</note> should beware of all unnecessary harshness; they must not add any burden for their own gain, or out of insolence or malice of spirit; they must embezzle nothing of the public funds; and they should satisfy those to whom any public payment is due without unnecessary delays.

§. 15. The special duty of citizens lasts as long as they are established in that function from which it flows; when they leave that office, the duty also expires. Their general duty likewise lasts as long as they are citizens. *Now, they cease to be citizens either if they depart from the state with express or tacit consent and fix the seat of their fortunes elsewhere; or if they are cast into exile for a crime and are deprived of the right of citizenship; or if, overwhelmed by the force of an enemy, they are forced to yield to the victor's dominion.*

### THE END.

### GLORY TO GOD ALONE. <note>original: *Soli Deo Gloria*</note>

->## BOOK II.<-

[...commanders should] diligently and seasonably exercise their men, and strengthen them to endure the labors of military service; they must keep military discipline sound and well-protected; they should not rashly expose soldiers to be slaughtered by the enemy; they should provide provisions and pay as promptly as is in their power, and embezzle nothing from it; they should ensure that soldiers always favor the commonwealth <note>original: *reip.*, abbreviation for *res publica*</note>, and never win them over to themselves against the state.

§. 12. *Soldiers*, on the other hand, should be content with their pay; they must abstain from plundering and harassing peasants <note>original: *paganis*, referring to rural civilians or villagers</note>; they should undergo labors for the defense of the state willingly and vigorously; they should not invite dangers through recklessness, nor shun them through cowardice; they should display courage against the enemy, not against their fellow soldiers; they must manfully defend their assigned post; and they should prefer an honorable death to a shameful flight and life.

§. 13. *Those whose services the state employs among foreigners* <note>This refers to ambassadors, envoys, or diplomats.</note> should be cautious and circumspect; skilled in discerning the empty from the solid and the true from the fabulous; extremely tenacious of secrets; and obstinate against any corruptions for the benefit of their own state.

§. 14. *Those who are in charge of collecting or distributing the wealth of the state* <note>financial officers, treasurers, or tax collectors</note> should beware of all unnecessary harshness; they should not strive to increase the public treasury by plundering individuals; they should not convert any of the public funds to their own use; and they should pay those who are to be satisfied from the treasury without making them wait through unnecessary delays. <note>Pufendorf concludes here that the integrity of the state’s finances depends as much on the character of the administrators as on the law itself.</note>

<summary>This page contains the title of Samuel Pufendorf’s seminal work on natural law and concludes Book II by outlining the ethical duties of soldiers, diplomats, and financial administrators. It emphasizes the responsibility of public officials to prioritize the state's welfare over personal gain or glory.</summary>
